Light on Biological Systems: Luminescent
Transition Metal Complexes for Bioimaging and Biosensing Applications

## Abstract
 Luminescence imaging is a powerful and versatile technique
for
investigating cell physiology and pathology in living systems, making
significant contributions to life science research and clinical diagnosis.
In recent years, luminescent transition metal complexes have gained
significant attention for diagnostic and therapeutic applications
due to their unique photophysical and photochemical properties. In
this Review, we provide a comprehensive overview of the recent development
of luminescent transition metal complexes for bioimaging and biosensing
applications, with a focus on transition metal centers with a d6, d8, and d10 electronic configuration.
We elucidate the structure–property relationships of luminescent
transition metal complexes, exploring how their structural characteristics
can be manipulated to control their biological behavior such as cellular
uptake, localization, biocompatibility, pharmacokinetics, and biodistribution.
Furthermore, we introduce the various design strategies that leverage
the interesting photophysical properties of luminescent transition
metal complexes for a wide variety of biological applications, including
autofluorescence-free imaging, multimodal imaging, organelle imaging,
biological sensing, microenvironment monitoring, bioorthogonal labeling,
bacterial imaging, and cell viability assessment. Finally, we provide
insights into the challenges and perspectives of luminescent transition
metal complexes for bioimaging and biosensing applications, as well
as their use in disease diagnosis and treatment evaluation.

statusreleaseddisplay-pdfyesis-in-collection-domainyesis-olfnois-manuscriptnois-preprintnois-journal-matternois-scannednois-retractednoReceived 2023 Sep 5; Accepted 2024 Apr 11; Revised 2024 Apr 9; Collection date 2024 Aug 14.

1.

## Introduction
Luminescence imaging is
a powerful tool for studying biological
species and their biochemical processes at the subcellular level with
high spatial and temporal resolution. − It has provided valuable insights into the roles and functions of
biomolecules in various physiological and pathological processes.
The success of this imaging technique is attributed to the advances
in imaging instrumentation and the rapid development of fluorescent
probes. Recent breakthroughs in super-resolution microscopy techniques such as structured illumination microscopy (SIM), stimulated emission depletion (STED) microscopy, and stochastic optical reconstruction microscopy
(STORM), as well as the development of
small-molecule fluorescent probes with high brightness and photostability, have allowed the visualization of biomolecules
and cellular structures at the nanometer scale. This has enabled the interrogation of their spatial organization
and temporal dynamics in cells with unprecedented resolution, revolutionizing
our understanding of the biological systems. Further advancements
in luminescence imaging are focused on noninvasive in vivo imaging in small animals with subcellular resolution, which is instrumental
to life science research, clinical diagnosis, and drug development.
To achieve this goal, new designs have been introduced for both imaging
instrumentation and reagents to make them compatible with photoexcitation
using near-infrared (NIR) light, which effectively reduces cell autofluorescence
background and facilitates deeper penetration into biological tissues.
Additionally, NIR light excitation significantly minimizes photobleaching
of the probes and photodamage to biological samples, allowing for
long-term and real-time imaging. Two-photon microscopy that utilizes
NIR light as the excitation source and
fluorescent probes with large two-photon absorption (TPA) cross-sections has enabled cellular imaging in vivo with high contrast and resolution at increased tissue depths. Furthermore,
the development of fluorescent probes with absorption and emission
profiles in the NIR region opens up exciting opportunities for disease
diagnosis and treatment evaluation in preclinical and clinical settings. − 
Over the past decade, there has been a rapidly emerging interest
in luminescent transition metal complexes for diagnostic and therapeutic
applications. − In particular, luminescent complexes of transition metals such as
rhenium, ruthenium, osmium, iridium, platinum, and gold have received
the most significant attention. These heavy metal atoms induce significant
spin–orbit coupling (SOC) and facilitate intersystem crossing
(ISC) to access the triplet excited states, giving rise to their intriguing
photophysical and photochemical properties. Typically, these complexes
display strong phosphorescence in solution at room temperature with
lifetimes in the submicrosecond to microsecond time regime, which
are much longer than those of organic fluorophores that are on the
nanosecond timescale. The long emission lifetimes allow the use of
time-resolved techniques such as phosphorescence lifetime imaging
microscopy (PLIM) and time-gated luminescence microscopy (TGLM) to
reduce interference from the short-lived cellular autofluorescence,
enabling background-free imaging in living cells and animals. The emission of these complexes is also characterized
by large Stokes’ shifts, which can avoid self-quenching and
reduced brightness at high local concentrations, a problem that is
often encountered with organic fluorophores, and offer the possibilities
for multiplex imaging. Additionally, the metal–ligand coordination
leads to the formation of various excited states with charge-transfer
(CT) nature. These allow the complexes to exhibit enhanced nonlinear
optical (NLO) properties compared to organic chromophores, facilitating
their utilization in multiphoton luminescence imaging. Furthermore, many transition metal complexes
show high photostability, which enables their continuous exposure
to irradiation, promoting their applications in long-term and real-time
imaging in living systems. The long-lived triplet excited states of
these complexes also favor the photosensitization of reactive oxygen
species (ROS) such as singlet oxygen (1O2),
which has been exploited to induce cell death by causing oxidative
damage to adjacent biomolecules and subcellular organelles. This renders
them promising photosensitizers for photodynamic therapy (PDT), representing
a paradigm shift from diagnostics to therapeutics. − A prominent
example is TLD1433, a ruthenium(II)-based PDT agent undergoing phase
II clinical trials (NCT03945162) for treating nonmuscle invasive bladder
cancer. 
In this Review, we provide
a comprehensive overview of the recent
development in luminescent transition metal complexes for bioimaging
and biosensing applications. The scope of this Review is confined
to the triplet emitters derived from transition metal complexes with
a d6, d8, and d10 electronic configuration
due to their interesting photophysical and photochemical behavior.
We first provide a brief introduction to the photophysics and photochemistry
of luminescent transition metal complexes. We then describe the designs
of luminescent transition metal complexes with a d6 (rhenium(I),
ruthenium(II), osmium(II), rhodium(III), and iridium(III)), d8 (rhodium(I), platinum(II), and gold(III)), and d10 (copper(I) and gold(I)) electronic configuration for biological
imaging. We also discuss the structure–property relationships
of these complexes, with an emphasis on how the lipophilicity, formal
charge, and molecular size of the complexes affect their cellular
uptake and localization properties. We then introduce how the cellular
uptake, localization, biocompatibility, pharmacokinetics, and biodistribution
of transition metal complexes can be manipulated through conjugation
to biomolecules, polymers, and nanomaterials. Furthermore, we summarize
the various strategies used in the design of luminescent transition
metal complexes for a range of applications, including autofluorescence-free
imaging, multimodal imaging, organelle imaging, biological sensing,
microenvironment monitoring, bioorthogonal labeling, bacterial imaging,
and cell viability assessment. Finally, we discuss the current challenges
and future outlook of luminescent transition metal complexes as bioimaging
reagents for cell biology and chemical biology research, as well as
their use in disease diagnosis and treatment evaluation.

2.

## Photophysics and Photochemistry of Luminescent
Transition Metal Complexes
The photophysics and photochemistry
of transition metal complexes
have been an important area of research for decades. The diverse electronic
configurations, oxidation states, and coordination numbers of transition
metals and the various structural, electronic, and coordinating properties
of ligands allow the complexes to establish manifold excited states
such as metal-to-ligand charge-transfer (MLCT), ligand-to-metal charge-transfer
(LMCT), ligand-to-ligand charge-transfer (LLCT), intraligand (IL),
and intraligand charge-transfer (ILCT) states, as well as excited
states associated with metal–metal interactions such as metal–metal-to-ligand
charge-transfer (MMLCT) states, giving rise to their rich photophysical
and photochemical properties. Of particular
interest are the luminescent complexes of transition metals such as
rhenium, ruthenium, osmium, rhodium, iridium, platinum, and gold.
Compared to 3d transition metals, these 4d and 5d transition metals
typically induce a larger ligand field splitting and shift the nonradiative
metal-centered (MC) excited states to higher energies. The MC states
can be further destabilized through the strategic incorporation of
strong σ-donor and/or π-acceptor ligands to favor the
population of the low-lying CT and IL states, facilitating the formation
of luminescent and photostable complexes. Additionally, the presence
of a heavy metal center with a large SOC constant in these complexes
enables efficient ISC from the singlet excited states to the triplet
excited states upon photoexcitation ( ). The spin-forbidden transition between
the lowest triplet excited state (T1) and the singlet ground
state (S0) results in phosphorescence. Modulation of the
energy levels of the excited states allows efficient thermal upconversion
of excitons from T1 to a higher-lying triplet excited state
(T2) via spin-allowed reverse internal conversion (RIC)
or to the lowest singlet excited state (S1) via spin-forbidden
reverse intersystem crossing (RISC), realizing thermally stimulated
delayed phosphorescence (TSDP) , and thermally activated
delayed fluorescence (TADF), − respectively. Furthermore, the long-lived triplet excited states
of transition metal complexes enable them to function as catalysts
in various photochemical reactions, for example, the photosensitization
of 1O2. Through a rational molecular design
to manipulate their excited-state properties, luminescent transition
metal complexes have been exploited for a wide variety of light-driven
applications including organic light-emitting diodes, − light-emitting electrochemical cells, dye-sensitized solar cells, photoredox
catalysis, − chemosensing, bioimaging, − and PDT. − 
 
3.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes for Bioimaging
The development of luminescent transition metal complexes as cellular
probes and bioimaging reagents has focused on second- and third-row
transition metals with a d6, d8, and d10 electronic configuration. − The interest stems from the fact
that in d6 and d8 metal complexes, the nonemissive 3MC excited states can be easily shifted to higher energy levels
than the emissive 3CT and 3IL excited states,
while in d10 metal complexes, these nonemissive states
are absent due to the complete filling of the d orbitals, providing
a facile access to luminescent and photostable complexes for imaging
applications.
3.1.

## d6 Transition Metal Complexes
Since the initial investigation on the classical ruthenium(II)
complex [Ru(bpy)3]2+ (bpy = 2,2′-bipyridine), , extensive research has been conducted to study the photophysical
and photochemical properties of d6 transition metal complexes
such as rhenium(I), ruthenium(II), osmium(II), rhodium(III), and iridium(III)
complexes. The coordination of these heavy metal ions with strong
field ligands leads to the formation of low-spin octahedral complexes
characterized by low rates of ligand dissociation, rendering them
attractive luminophores for bioimaging applications.
3.1.1.

## Rhenium(I) Complexes
Among different
types of rhenium(I) complexes, rhenium(I) tricarbonyl diimine complexes
[Re(N∧N)(CO)3(L)]n (L = anionic or neutral monodentate ligand; n = 0 or +1) are the best studied and are known for their strong emission
in fluid solution. The emission primarily
originates from a 3MLCT emissive state, in which the lowest
π* orbital is localized on the diimine ligand. These complexes
have attracted significant interest as bioimaging reagents, as modulating
the functionalization of the monodentate ligand allows for tailored
cellular targeting and localization while retaining their favorable
photophysical behavior. − 
Coogan and co-workers have
developed a series of luminescent rhenium(I) complexes (1) ( ) as imaging
reagents for various biological models including mammalian cells (e.g., human breast adenocarcinoma MCF-7 cells) and microorganisms
(e.g., the diplomonad fish parasite (Spironucleus
vortens (S. vortens)) and yeasts (Saccharomyces cerevisiae and Schizosaccharomyces
pombe (S. pombe))). − As revealed
by laser-scanning confocal microscopy (LSCM), the cationic and lipophilic
complexes 1a–1g display efficient
cellular uptake and high affinities for hydrophobic organelles in
the cytoplasm such as the mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum (ER),
and Golgi apparatus. In contrast, the anionic complexes 1h–1j either localize in the exterior membrane
(1h and 1i) or exhibit negligible uptake
(1j). These findings indicate that the positive charge
of cationic complexes facilitates their interaction with the negatively
charged cell membrane, resulting in their enhanced cellular uptake
compared to neutral or anionic complexes. Similar subcellular distribution
has been observed for related rhenium(I) dipyrido[3,2-a:2′,3′-c]phenazine
(dppz) complexes (2). Wilson
and co-workers have examined the influence of the axial ligand on
the photophysical and biological properties of rhenium(I) complexes
coordinated with different monodentate nitrogen-donor ligands (3). While the axial ligand has
minimal impact on the photophysical behavior of these complexes, it
plays a significant role in their cellular uptake efficiencies. Among
these complexes, only the pyridine complex 3d is efficiently
taken up by live human cervical adenocarcinoma HeLa cells, suggesting
that the modification of the axial ligand holds promise for biological
applications of these complexes. Lo and co-workers have incorporated
an isothiocyanate moiety and a perfluoroalkyl pendant into the pyridine
ligand of rhenium(I) complexes to afford bifunctional reagents (4) for bioconjugation and cellular imaging. , These amine-reactive complexes have been applied to label biomolecules
such as bovine serum albumin (BSA) and glutathione (GSH) to prepare
luminescent bioconjugates. The perfluoroalkyl unit enables the facile
isolation of the resultant bioconjugates with fluorous solid-phase
extraction. Studies on the cellular uptake and intracellular localization
of the thiourea products in HeLa cells show that the complexes specifically
accumulate in the mitochondria due to their cationic charge and high
lipophilicity.
 Rajendran, Rajagopal, and co-workers have investigated
the biological
activity of rhenium(I) complexes appended with an alkyl chain. Among the complexes studied, complex 5 bearing long alkyl chains on both the monodentate and bidentate
ligands ( ) shows
the highest deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) affinity and cytotoxicity
toward MCF-7 cells, demonstrating that the hydrophobic alkyl chains
promote the cellular internalization of the complex and its interaction
with DNA, resulting in its high cytotoxic activity. Hallett, Dervisi,
Fallis, and co-workers have examined the uptake of rhenium(I) complexes
coordinated with a substituted imidazole or thiazole ligand (6) in S. vortens and S. pombe. The long alkyl chain on the imidazole
ligand of complexes 6b–6e assists
in their internalization across the plasma membrane via passive diffusion.
However, the cytotoxicity of the complexes also increases with the
length of the alkyl chain. Furthermore, the localization of these
complexes in S. vortens varies from the nucleus (6b) to the cell wall (6d) depending on their
lipophilicity. In contrast, complexes 6g–6i display efficient uptake with negligible cytotoxicity.
Carreño, Fuentes, and co-workers have also explored the use
of luminescent rhenium(I) complexes (7) as bioimaging
reagents for walled cells such as yeasts and bacteria. Notably, the cellular internalization of the
cationic complex 7f coordinated with an ancillary Schiff
base ligand is more efficient than its charge-neutral counterpart 7e. The strong colocalization with the nucleus stain 4′,6-diamidino-2-phenylindole
(DAPI) shows that complex 7f is preferentially enriched
in the cell nucleus. These findings illustrate that both the formal
charge and lipophilicity play crucial roles in the cellular uptake
and localization properties of transition metal complexes.
 Neutral rhenium(I) complexes such as [Re(N∧N)(CO)3(L)] (L = Cl or Br) have received
less attention compared
to their cationic counterparts due to their relatively low emission
quantum yields and short emission lifetimes. Rigby, Massi, and co-workers
have examined the photophysical and biological behavior of three neutral
rhenium(I) complexes coordinated with a thioalkyl-functionalized 1,10-phenanthroline
(phen) ligand (8) ( ). Notably, the tetrazolato
complex 8c exhibits more intense and longer-lived emission
as well as enhanced cellular uptake compared to the chloro complexes 8a and 8b. However, prolonged irradiation of
complex 8c leads to increased cytotoxicity due to the
generation of cytotoxic 1O2, resulting in extensive
membrane blebbing. The cellular behavior of rhenium(I) tetrazolato
complexes with different substituents attached to the phenyl ring
in conjugation to the tetrazole ligand (9) has also been
investigated. As revealed by flow cytometry
and LSCM, complex 9a shows a low degree of cellular internalization
and punctate staining in live murine macrophage RAW264.7 cells, whereas
complexes 9b–9d are efficiently taken
up by the cells and diffusely distributed in the cytoplasm, indicating
that the cellular uptake and localization properties of these complexes
are sensitive to the nature and position of the substituents on the
5-phenyltetrazolato ligand. Czerwieniec, Kowalski,
and co-workers have developed three neutral rhenium(I) carboxylato
complexes (10) with different emission properties as
cellular imaging reagents. Complexes 10a and 10b are strongly emissive in solution,
while complex 10c is nonluminescent due to the presence
of a ferrocene (Fc) moiety that quenches the emission. However, intense
intracellular emission has been detected in the mitochondria of live
HeLa cells for all three complexes. This observation suggests that
the ancillary carboxylate ligand dissociates from the complexes inside
cells, producing the cationic [Re(phen)(CO)3]+ species that reacts with cellular matrix molecules to yield luminescent
products targeting the mitochondria. Fernández-Moreira and
Sastre-Martín have utilized a ditopic mercaptopyridine
ligand to prepare luminescent rhenium(I) complexes (11) for cellular imaging. The neutral
complex 11b predominantly resides in the perinuclear
region of live human lung adenocarcinoma A549 cells, which appears
to be the mitochondria. Kowalski, Czerwieniec, and co-workers have
conjugated a rhenium(I) complex with a chromone derivative using a
strong σ-donor alkynyl ligand as a linker. The resulting neutral complex (12) efficiently
penetrates into mouse myoblast C2C12 cells and localizes in the perinuclear
region with negligible nuclear uptake. Additionally, the complex is
highly resistant to photobleaching in cells under continuous intense
irradiation (405 nm, 0.8 W cm–2, 30 min), highlighting
its applicability in long-term cellular imaging.
 Coogan, Pope, and co-workers have developed a
series of binuclear
rhenium(I) complexes (13) ( ) as cellular imaging reagents. These complexes are efficiently internalized
into live MCF-7 cells via an energy-independent pathway. Interestingly,
the cellular uptake and localization properties of the complexes are
influenced by the length and chemical nature of the linker. Among
these complexes, the cellular uptake of the diester complex 13c is the most efficient, giving rise to diffuse cytoplasmic
staining along with some localization in the nucleoli. Coogan and
co-workers have also designed a trinuclear rhenium(I) complex (14) that can serve as a molecular vessel for transporting
metal ions into cells. This neutral complex
is impermeable to the plasma membrane of live MCF-7 cells. However,
upon Ag+ sequestration, the resultant cationic adduct is
efficiently internalized into the cells and enriched in the nucleoli.
The complex can also encapsulate Cu2+, which makes bimodal
imaging with its radioactive isotope possible.
 Luminescent rhenium(I) tricarbonyl complexes
with tridentate ligands
[Re(N∧N∧N)(CO)3]+ are known for their interesting photophysical behavior and
high stability. One notable example is the rhenium(I) complexes coordinated
with a bis(2-quinolylmethyl)amine ligand. Metzler-Nolte and co-workers have developed
three rhenium(I) complexes coordinated with a phenanthridine-derived
tridentate ligand (15) ( ) as cellular imaging reagents. The symmetric complex 15a displays
both ligand-centered and 3MLCT emission, whereas the asymmetric
complexes 15b and 15c exhibit single 3MLCT emission but at longer wavelengths. All these complexes
show high cellular uptake efficiencies and specific accumulation in
the ER of various human cancer cell lines, including pancreatic ductal
adenocarcinoma (IMIM-PC2 and PT45), colorectal adenocarcinoma (HT-29),
hepatocellular carcinoma (HepG2), and breast adenocarcinoma (MCF-7),
highlighting their potential in examining the role of the ER in maintaining
the physiological functions of cancer cells.
 Studies on the photophysical properties of rhenium(I)
tricarbonyl
complexes have indicated that the high-frequency C≡O stretching
modes (> 2,000 cm–1) contribute to the rapid
nonradiative
decay from the lowest-lying 3MLCT state in these complexes. Thus, there has been increasing interest in
rhenium(I) dicarbonyl complexes such as cis-[Re(N∧N)(L∧L)(CO)2]+ (L∧L = neutral bidentate ligand) and trans,cis-[Re(N∧N)(L)2(CO)2]+ (L = neutral monodentate ligand), wherein the
removal of a carbonyl ligand results in emission at lower energies
with longer emission lifetimes. Liu, Tunik, Koshevoy, Chou, and co-workers
have designed a rhenium(I) dicarbonyl complex (16) ( ) for O2 sensing. Compared to its tricarbonyl
counterpart, the complex displays red-shifted emission with an increased
emission quantum yield and lifetime (λem = 583 nm, Φem = 0.34, τo = 4.05 μs) and possesses a larger
TPA cross-section (δ800nm = 300
GM) in aqueous solution. The anionic sulfonate groups on the phosphine
ligands allow the complex to interact with collagen in the extracellular
matrix after extravasation, facilitating the visualization of tissue
ischemia and tumor hypoxia in vivo using two-photon
excitation PLIM (TP-PLIM).
 
3.1.2.

## Ruthenium(II) Complexes
The photophysics
and photochemistry of ruthenium(II) polypyridine complexes have been
extensively studied. Manipulation of
their excited states, particularly the 3MLCT and 3MC states, has opened up diverse possibilities for their biological
applications. Luminescent ruthenium(II) complexes that exhibit emission
originating from a low-lying 3MLCT excited state have proven
valuable as bioimaging reagents. − However, the presence
of a close-lying 3MC state can lead to photosubstitution
reactions. This feature has been exploited to develop photoactivated
chemotherapy agents capable of releasing biologically active molecules
upon irradiation, thereby expanding the
applications of ruthenium(II) complexes from diagnostics to therapeutics.
Ruthenium(II) dppz complexes are well-known for their “light-switch”
effect. These complexes are nonluminescent
in aqueous solution but show intense emission when bound to double-stranded
DNA due to the shielding of the phenazine nitrogen atoms from water
molecules. Barton and Puckett have investigated the cellular uptake
behavior of ruthenium(II) dppz complexes with different formal charge,
size, and lipophilicity (17) ( ) in live HeLa cells using LSCM and flow
cytometry. The lipophilicity of the complexes
(log Po/w = −2.50 to 1.30) increases
with the hydrophobicity of the ancillary ligands. Among these complexes,
only the most lipophilic complex 17e (log Po/w = 1.30) is efficiently taken up by the cells, giving
rise to intense emission in the cytoplasmic region. Studies on the
cellular uptake mechanism reveal that the complex is internalized
into the cells through passive diffusion. Svensson, Lincoln, and co-workers have investigated the cellular
uptake and localization properties of ruthenium(II) dppz complexes
carrying alkyl chains of different lengths (18) in Chinese
hamster ovary (CHO)-K1 cells. The cellular
uptake of these complexes is limited in live cells but increases upon
irradiation. The least lipophilic complex 18a mainly
resides in the nucleus, while the most lipophilic complex 18c is preferentially enriched in membrane-rich structures such as the
ER. In contrast, the intermediate lipophilic complex 18b is distributed in both the nucleus and cytoplasm. The difference
in cellular localization of the complexes can be corroborated with
their binding preference, which is tuned from DNA (18a) to ribonucleic acid (RNA) and lipid membranes (18b and 18c) upon substitution of the dppz ligand with
alkyl chains of increasing length. The
diastereomeric interaction of the two enantiomers of complex 18b in cells have also been examined by LSCM and PLIM. Both isomers localize in the cell nucleus and
cytoplasm, but they demonstrate substantial differences in their emission
intensities and lifetimes. The Δ-isomer displays a higher emission
intensity and a longer emission lifetime in the nucleus compared to
the cytoplasmic region, while the Λ-isomer exhibits more intense
emission in the cytoplasm but shows a longer emission lifetime in
the nucleus. Since the affinities of both enantiomers for DNA are
similar, the large difference in cellular staining is associated with
the different emission quantum yields and lifetimes of the resultant
DNA-bound complexes.
 Vogel, Dyson, and co-workers have examined the
cellular uptake
and localization behavior of a series of ruthenium(II) complexes with
different lipophilicity (19) ( ) in human ovarian carcinoma A2780 and its
cisplatin-resistant variant A2780cisR cells. Among these complexes, the most lipophilic complex 19e (log Po/w = 0.55) preferentially accumulates
in the plasma membrane, while the other complexes are either membrane-impermeable
(19a; log Po/w = −1.21)
or internalized into the cells and enriched in small organelles in
the cytoplasm (19b–19d; log Po/w = −0.99 to −0.28). Additionally,
the cytotoxic activity of complex 19e is substantially
higher (IC50 < 1 μM) compared to the other complexes
(IC50 ≥ 85 μM), as its binding to the plasma
membrane triggers cell death via necrosis. Brindell and co-workers
have investigated the cellular uptake properties of nonintercalative
ruthenium(II) complexes (20). The dicationic complex 20d displays the most efficient
cellular uptake in live murine mammary carcinoma 4T1 cells, primarily
due to its high lipophilicity (log Po/w = 1.11). Surprisingly, despite having the highest lipophilicity
(log Po/w = 1.86), the monocationic complex 20c exhibits the lowest cellular accumulation. These findings
emphasize the important roles of both the formal charge and lipophilicity
in the cellular uptake of these complexes.
 The photophysical and biological behavior of
ruthenium(II) complexes
can be modulated by modification of the ligands with functional pendants.
Keyes and co-workers have developed ruthenium(II) complexes containing
an alkyl chain of different lengths (21) ( ) as membrane-permeable probes
for cellular imaging. These complexes
self-assemble into nanoaggregates in aqueous solution and enter mammalian
cells such as HeLa and CHO cells via endocytosis. Interestingly, the
intracellular localization of the complexes varies with the alkyl
chain length and cell lines used. Complex 21c preferentially
localizes in the Golgi apparatus and endosomes in HeLa and CHO cells,
respectively, while complex 21d is enriched in the hydrophobic
organelles such as mitochondria and lipid droplets (LDs). Estalayo-Adrián,
Gunnlaugsson, and co-workers have investigated the effect of alkyl
chain length on the photophysical, photochemical, and photobiological
properties of ruthenium(II) complexes modified with an alkyl chain
of different lengths (22). Notably, the presence of a heneicosanyl chain in complexes 22c and 22f promotes their self-aggregation into
micellar species, influencing their photophysical and 1O2-photosensitization behavior. Increasing the length
of the alkyl chain also enhances the lipophilicity and thereby the
cellular uptake efficiencies of the complexes. Among these complexes,
the most lipophilic complexes 22c and 22f (log Po/w = 1.07 and 0.49, respectively)
show the most efficient uptake in HeLa cells and significant cytotoxic
activity under both dark and light conditions. Williams, Quinn, Gunnlaugsson,
and co-workers have examined the photophysical and biological properties
of mono- and bis-1,8-naphthalimide-conjugated ruthenium(II)
complexes (23). Selective
excitation of the 1,8-naphthalimide moiety results in 3MLCT emission from the ruthenium(II) unit. The mono-1,8-naphthalimide
complex 23a binds to DNA and induces DNA photocleavage
upon irradiation, whereas the bis-1,8-naphthalimide complex 23b displays negligible interaction with DNA as the second
1,8-naphthalimide moiety displaces the complex from the
DNA backbone. Both complexes reside in the perinuclear region of HeLa
cells after uptake, and their differential DNA binding affinity leads
to different photocytotoxicity. Bhat, Revankar, and co-workers have
developed two ruthenium(II) complexes appended with a quinolinone
unit (24). These complexes
are rapidly internalized into live HeLa cells and distributed throughout
the cells. Núñez and co-workers have developed a bis(o-methylserotonin)-modified ruthenium(II)
complex (25) for cellular imaging. The emission intensity of the complex remains stable in
the presence of other metal ions. Its biodistribution has been studied
in live freshwater fish (Carassius auratus), revealing
that the complex localizes in the cytoplasm of erythrocytes, liver
cells, gills, and gonads.
 The use of N-heterocyclic carbene
(NHC) ligands
in the development of luminescent complexes is attractive, as their
strong σ-donor strength effectively destabilizes the nonemissive
MC states, facilitating the formation of stable complexes with high
emission quantum yields and long emission lifetimes. Also, the N-substituents of the NHC ligands
can be used to manipulate the physical, chemical, and biological behavior
of the resultant complexes. Chiu, Wong, and co-workers have developed
a ruthenium(II) NHC complex (26) ( ) as a cellular imaging reagent. This complex is essentially noncytotoxic toward
both MCF-7 and human retinal pigmented epithelium (RPE) cells. The
complex is internalized into the cells via endocytosis, which enables
the examination of the endocytic processes in live cells.
 Williams, Gunnlaugsson, and co-workers have examined
the photophysical
and biological properties of two binuclear ruthenium(II) complexes
featuring a 1,8-naphthalimide Tröger’s base ligand connecting
the two ruthenium(II) moieties (27) ( ). These complexes exhibit rapid cellular uptake in live HeLa cells,
primarily residing in the cytoplasm with negligible effect on the
cell viability. Similar cytoplasmic localization has also been observed
in other binuclear ruthenium(II) complexes (28 and 29). , Lincoln and co-workers have
studied the subcellular distribution of the stereoisomers of two binuclear
ruthenium(II) complexes (30) in fixed CHO-K1 cells and
found that while both the ΔΔ- and ΛΛ-isomers
of the complexes show prominent nuclear staining, the ΛΛ-isomers
exclusively accumulate in the nucleoli. However, no significant difference is observed in the cellular uptake
and localization of the complexes in live cells, where they are entrapped
in the endosomes after endocytic uptake. Chao and co-workers have
developed a series of binuclear ruthenium(II) complexes (31) as two-photon luminescence imaging reagents. These complexes possess large TPA cross-sections in the
NIR region (δ = 322 to 386 GM at 800 or 830
nm) due to the extensive π-conjugation of the bridging ligand.
Monitoring the cellular uptake of the complexes using two-photon microscopy
reveals that the complexes are taken up by live HeLa cells via an
energy-dependent, nonendocytic pathway and enriched in the cytoplasm.
Alexander and co-workers have examined the cellular localization of
mono-, di-, and trinuclear ruthenium(II) complexes (32) in live cells. These hydrophilic
complexes (log Po/w = −1.46 to
−0.53) display efficient cellular uptake and cytoplasmic localization.
Notably, the nuclear membrane penetration ability of the complexes
increases with their nuclearity. Furthermore, Chao and co-workers
have developed a series of multinuclear ruthenium(II) complexes (33–36) ( ) as trackable nonviral gene vectors. − These complexes can condense plasmid DNA, facilitating its internalization
into cells. Additionally, the intracellular trafficking process of
plasmid DNA can be monitored in real time using LSCM due to the luminescence
properties of the complexes.
 
3.1.3.

## Osmium(II) Complexes
Osmium(II)
complexes possess similar photophysical behavior as their ruthenium(II)
counterparts. These complexes typically
exhibit MLCT absorption and emission at lower energies due to the
higher-lying dπ orbital of the osmium(II) center. Additionally,
the increased ligand field splitting in osmium(II) complexes restricts
the thermal accessibility of the 3MC states. These properties
render osmium(II) complexes highly promising for applications as bioimaging
reagents.
Zhang, Zhang, Chao, and co-workers have developed
an NIR luminescent osmium(II) complex (37) ( ) for cellular imaging
and PDT applications. Compared to its
ruthenium(II) counterpart (log Po/w =
−2.23) that shows high mitochondria specificity, complex 37 (log Po/w = −1.24) preferentially
accumulates in the lysosomes of live A549 cells after cellular uptake,
suggesting that the lipophilicity of the complexes is influenced by
the nature of the metal centers. Additionally, complex 37 displays negligible dark cytotoxicity but high photocytotoxic activity
upon red-light irradiation (λex =
633 nm) due to 1O2 generation, highlighting
the potential applications of osmium(II) complexes in NIR imaging
and NIR light-induced PDT.
 Osmium(II) complexes with terdentate ligands
also exhibit luminescence
in solution, in contrast to the ruthenium(II) terpyridine analogues
that are nonemissive at room temperature due to the presence of low-lying 3MC states. Elliott and co-workers have developed a 1,2,3-triazole-based
bis(terdentate) osmium(II) complex (38) ( ). The use of 1,2,3-triazole-derived ligands allows for tailored derivatization
through click chemistry, enabling the optimization of the cellular
uptake and organelle specificity of the complexes. Complex 38 is preferentially enriched in the mitochondria of live HeLa and
human osteosarcoma U2OS cells with negligible dark cytotoxicity. Bryant,
Elliott, and co-workers have developed a series of luminescent 1,2,3-triazole-based
tris(bidentate) osmium(II) complexes (39) that show intense
emission in aqueous solution at room temperature. These complexes display efficient cellular uptake in live
HeLa and human bladder carcinoma EJ cells with apparent localization
in the endosomal/lysosomal compartments. Additionally, the complexes
exhibit low cytotoxic activity toward the cells under both dark and
light conditions. Notably, complex 39d shows antimicrobial
activity against methicillin- and ampicillin-resistant Staphylococcus
aureus (S. aureus), with the meridional
isomer displaying higher uptake and greater efficacy than its facial
isomer. Super-resolution imaging using
SIM reveals the binding of both isomers to intracellular DNA of the
bacteria.
 
3.1.4.

## Rhodium(III) Complexes
Rhodium(III)
complexes have received much less attention compared to other d6 transition metal complexes due to the lack of luminescence
at room temperature, resulting from the presence of a thermally accessible 3MC excited state. However, the
luminescence properties of these complexes can be significantly improved
by incorporating a strong σ-donor cyclometalating
ligand, which raises the energy level of the nonemissive 3MC state and introduces a lower-lying emissive 3IL state. 
Lo and co-workers have developed luminescent
cyclometalated rhodium(III) bis(pyridylbenzaldehyde)
complexes that exhibit extraordinarily long emission lifetimes of ca. 4.2–8.7 μs in solution at room temperature. These complexes have been exploited as biological
labeling reagents. Modification of these complexes with a biotin pendant
leads to the development of a luminescent biotinylation reagent (40) ( ). The cellular uptake and localization
properties of this complex have been studied in live HeLa cells, showing
that after cellular internalization, the complex resides in the perinuclear
region with negligible nuclear uptake. Torbati, Lutz, Amirnasr, and
co-workers have developed biscyclometalated
rhodium(III) complexes (41) using benzothiazole derivatives
as the diimine ligand. Among these complexes,
complexes 41a and 41c show emission in the
visible region, whereas complex 41b is nonluminescent.
Monitoring the cellular uptake of complex 41c in MCF-7
cells with LSCM reveals its efficient cellular internalization due
to its high lipophilicity (log Po/w =
8.28) and apparent localization in the perinuclear region. Additionally,
the complex displays relatively higher cytotoxicity toward MCF-7 cells
compared to the nonmalignant human embryonic lung fibroblast MRC-5
cells, suggesting its potential development as a theranostic agent.
 
3.1.5.

## Iridium(III) Complexes
Cyclometalated
iridium(III) complexes have gained significant attention for a range
of light-driven applications. Typically, these complexes exhibit strong
luminescence, primarily due to the limited thermal accessibility of
the nonemissive 3MC states caused by the presence of the
cyclometalating ligand. Furthermore, their emission color can be readily tuned from blue
to NIR through the utilization of ligands with different electronic
properties or degrees of π-conjugation while maintaining high
emission quantum yields. These attractive properties have led to their
rapid development as bioimaging reagents. − 
The seminal work on the utilization of luminescent biscyclometalated
iridium(III) complexes as bioimaging reagents was presented by two
independent research groups in 2008. , Li and co-workers
have examined the cellular uptake, intracellular localization, and
cytotoxic activity of two iridium(III) complexes (42)
( ). These complexes undergo facile internalization
into live HeLa cells, resulting in exclusive cytoplasmic staining
with negligible nuclear uptake. Additionally, the complexes are essentially
noncytotoxic (IC50 > 100 μM; 24 h) and highly
photostable,
suggesting their potential use for long-term tracking of cellular
events. Lo and co-workers have investigated the cellular uptake and
distribution of a series of iridium(III) complexes appended with an
alkyl chain (43). These
complexes are taken up by live HeLa cells via an energy-dependent
mechanism and enriched in the perinuclear region. The cellular uptake
efficiencies of the complexes follow the order: 43b (log Po/w = 5.34) > 43a (log Po/w = 2.01) > 43c (log Po/w = 9.89). The lowest cellular uptake of the
most lipophilic complex 43c is possibly due to its largest
molecular size and/or self-aggregation in aqueous solution. Lo and
co-workers have also examined the cellular uptake and localization
behavior of iridium(III) complexes carrying a hydrophobic indole pendant
(44). The most lipophilic
complex 44c shows the highest uptake in HeLa cells and
perinuclear localization, most likely resulting from its binding to
hydrophobic organelles such as the Golgi apparatus, ER, and mitochondria.
 Li and co-workers have developed a series of
biscyclometalated
iridium(III) complexes with different ancillary ligands (45) ( ) as multicolor
cellular probes. The emission energies
of the complexes can be readily tuned from blue to red by extending
the π-conjugation of the ancillary ligand. Notably, complex 45d emits in the NIR region (λem = 712 nm), making it particularly suitable for in
vivo applications. As revealed by LSCM, all the complexes
primarily localize in the cytoplasm of live HeLa cells with negligible
nuclear uptake. Li, Liu, Qiao, and co-workers have developed three
NIR luminescent iridium(III) complexes (46) using phenylbenzo[g]quinoline as the cyclometalating ligand. These complexes display NIR emission (λem = 698 nm) with moderate quantum yields
(Φem = 0.029 to 0.035). Modulating
the hydrophobicity of the ancillary diimine ligands allows the manipulation
of the lipophilicity and thus the cellular uptake efficiencies of
the complexes. Complexes 46a and 46b (log Po/w = 0.13 and 0.18, respectively) exhibit facile
cellular internalization and exclusive cytoplasmic staining in live
human embryonic kidney HEK293T cells. However, the cellular uptake
of complex 46c (log Po/w =
0.88) is negligible due to its high lipophilicity and poor solubility
in aqueous solution. Lima and co-workers have developed two red luminescent
iridium(III) complexes (47) as bioimaging reagents. The carboxylate groups on the diimine ligand
endow the complexes with high aqueous solubility and biocompatibility.
Live-cell imaging shows that the complexes are internalized into CHO-K1
cells and enriched in the cytoplasm. Li, Fan, and co-workers have
developed iridium(III) complexes appended with a triethylene glycol
pendant (48) for imaging Schistosoma japonicum. This modification endows the complexes
with high aqueous solubility while maintaining high membrane permeability.
Kozhevnikov, Bryant, and co-workers have designed a cationic binuclear
iridium(III) complex (49) using pyridazine as a bridging
ligand. Compared to mononuclear complexes,
multinuclear iridium(III) complexes usually display red-shifted absorption
and emission properties, which are advantageous to their use as cellular
imaging reagents with reduced autofluorescence background. The complex
is compatible with both one- and two-photon microscopy, showing that
the complex enters HeLa cells and accumulates in the mitochondria.
Su, Liao, and co-workers have developed a cationic iridium(III) complex
bearing dendritic carbazole moieties (50) that shows
aggregation-induced emission (AIE). This
complex is nonluminescent in solution but becomes strongly emissive
in the solid state due to restricted distortion of the excited-state
structure upon aggregation. Incubation of live human hepatocellular
carcinoma SMMC-7721 cells with the complex gives rise to intense emission
in the perinuclear region, indicating its aggregation in the intracellular
environment.
 Bian, Bian, and co-workers have studied the cellular
uptake properties
of a series of zwitterionic iridium(III) complexes (51) ( ) in both
live and fixed human cervical carcinoma KB cells. The cellular uptake efficiencies of the complexes increase
with their lipophilicity (log Po/w = −1.89
to 1.39) ( ),
which is in accordance with the hydrophobicity of the cyclometalating
ligands. Chao and co-workers have investigated the cellular uptake
and localization behavior of iridium(III) complexes (52) with different lipophilicity. The
less lipophilic complexes 52a–52c (log Po/w = −0.51 to −0.16)
are predominantly enriched in the lysosomes of HeLa cells, whereas
the more lipophilic complexes 52d and 52e (log Po/w = 0.70 and 1.09, respectively)
preferentially accumulate in the mitochondria. Temperature dependence
studies suggest the involvement of an energy-requiring pathway in
the uptake of complexes 52a–52d but
not for complex 52e. These findings highlight the significance
of lipophilicity in governing the cellular uptake and intracellular
localization properties of these complexes. Zhao, Zhang, and co-workers
have developed four iridium(III) complexes modified with an ester
or amide group (53) as cellular imaging reagents. These complexes have the same formal charge
(+1) and similar lipophilicity (log Po/w = 1.45 to 2.14) and molecular size, with structural variations limited
to one or two atoms. While complexes 53b–53d specifically accumulate in the mitochondria with negligible
nuclear uptake, the distribution of complex 53a between
the cell nucleus and mitochondria is similar. Additionally, the complex
localized in the nucleus emits with a longer emission lifetime (156
ns) than that in the cytoplasm (93 ns). Temperature dependence and
chemical inhibition experiments indicate that the nuclear uptake of
complex 53a is mediated by clathrin and microtubules,
and it is more efficient in the mitotic phase than in the interphase
during cell division.
 Mixed-ligand iridium(III) complexes [Ir(N∧N∧N)(N∧C)(L)]+ (L
= anionic
monodentate ligand) have emerged as an interesting class of luminophores. The incorporation of a tridentate ligand improves
the stability of the complexes, while the bidentate and monodentate
ligands allow independent tuning of the photophysical properties of
the complexes. Cao, Ye, Ward, and co-workers have developed two isomeric
luminescent iridium(III) complexes (54) ( ), where the ambidentate naphthalene-based
cyclometalating ligand is coordinated to the iridium(III) ion in a
five- or six-membered ring chelating mode. These complexes display distinct luminescence and self-assembly
properties. Complex 54a exhibits strong emission at 581
nm, whereas complex 54b shows weaker and red-shifted
emission at 612 nm. Furthermore, complex 54a undergoes
efficient self-assembly in aqueous solution via strong intermolecular
π–π interaction to form monodispersed nanoparticles
(NPs), which is not observed in complex 54b due to its
more twisted structure. These complexes have been used for cellular
imaging. A stronger emission signal is detected in HeLa cells treated
with complex 54a due to its higher emission quantum yield
and lipophilicity. Colocalization studies show that complex 54a preferentially accumulates in the lysosomes.
 Similar to diimine ligands, diamines can also
serve as a coordinating
ligand for the iridium(III) center, leading to the development of
robust complexes that display intense emission. The use of diamines
as a ligand offers a high degree of control over the emission energy
of the complexes since the π* orbitals are localized on the
cyclometalating ligand. Lo and co-workers have developed a series
of luminescent iridium(III) complexes coordinated with an ethylenediamine
ligand (55) ( ) as imaging reagents. The emission color of these complexes can be tuned across the visible
region from blue to red using different cyclometalating ligands. The
cellular localization properties of these complexes have been examined
using complex 55e as a model, which is specifically enriched
in the mitochondria of live HeLa cells. Additionally, these complexes
can be developed as transfection agents when poly(ethyleneimine) (PEI)
is used as the diamine ligand. Related iridium(III) ethylenediamine
complexes functionalized with polar ester or carboxylate groups (56) have also been developed. Among them, the cationic complexes 56a and 56b are readily taken up by live HeLa cells via an energy-requiring
process and enriched in the endosomes and mitochondria, respectively
( ). In contrast,
the anionic complexes 56c and 56d exhibit
limited cellular uptake, resulting from the charge repulsion between
the complexes and the phospholipid bilayers of the cell membrane that
restrains them from further internalization. Furthermore, complex 56c has been utilized as a visualization reagent for zebrafish.
The membrane impermeability of the complex has enabled efficient imaging
of the circulation system in zebrafish.
 Iridium(III) solvato complexes have been reported
as luminescence
turn-on probes for histidine (His) and His-rich proteins. Li and co-workers have developed a related
iridium(III) solvato complex [Ir(ppy)2(DMSO)2]+ (Hppy = 2-phenylpyridne) (57) ( ) as the first reaction-based
nuclear stain. The complex shows significant
emission enhancement (I/Io > 300) upon incubation with His and His-rich proteins. Upon cellular
internalization, the complex is enriched in the nucleus within minutes
and retained in this organelle for a long period of time (> 24
h).
The specific nuclear staining is due to the substitution reaction
of the complex with His or His-rich proteins inside the nucleus following
its cellular uptake and nuclear accumulation. Structure–property
relationship analysis on related complexes (58) shows
that variations in the counteranion (e.g., PF6– and CF3SO3–) and the solvent ligand (e.g., H2O,
DMSO, and CH3CN) of the complexes does not have a significant
effect on their cellular uptake and localization properties. However, increasing the length of the carbon
side chains or branched chains in the cyclometalating ligands leads
to the translocation of the complex from the nucleus (58a and 58b) to the cytoplasm (58d and 58e) ( ). These results suggest that the molecular size of the complexes
is critical to their nuclear uptake, which is controlled by the nuclear
pore complexes in the nuclear envelope. However, Ma and co-workers
have demonstrated a related iridium(III) complex (59a) that gives rise to diffuse cytoplasmic staining with negligible
nuclear uptake in both live and fixed cells. Zhao, Wong, and co-workers have developed iridium(III) solvato complexes
(59b–59h) as multicolor probes for
cellular imaging. All these complexes
are cell-permeable and localize in the cytoplasm after uptake via
passive diffusion.
 Zhou and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
NHC complexes (60) ( ) as cellular imaging reagents. The
lipophilicity of the neutral complexes 60a–60c (log Po/w = 1.28 to 1.57)
is higher than the cationic complexes 60d and 60e (log Po/w = 0.56 and 0.89, respectively).
These complexes enter cells via an energy-dependent pathway and reside
in the cytoplasm following cellular uptake. Che and co-workers have
developed a series of stable luminescent cyclometalated iridium(III)
complexes using bis-NHC as the ancillary ligands. These complexes display intense emission in
solution at room temperature with emission quantum yields up to 0.89
and emission lifetimes up to 96 μs. Furthermore, these complexes
are highly photostable under continuous visible-light irradiation.
Live-cell imaging shows that complexes 61a–61e exhibit efficient cellular uptake and specific ER accumulation
in various cell lines including HeLa, human lung adenocarcinoma NCI-H1975,
and normal human colon mucosal epithelial NCM460 cells. Barnard and
co-workers have developed various bidentate NHC ligands based on imidazolylidene
and triazole/triazolylidene using copper(I)-catalyzed azide–alkyne
cycloaddition (CuAAC) reaction to prepare luminescent iridium(III)
complexes. Two of the complexes (62) have been studied in a biological context due to their
good aqueous solubility. These cationic complexes are efficiently
taken up by live A549 cells and specifically enriched in the mitochondria.
 Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have developed neutral
iridium(III)
complexes (63a–63c) ( ) using 3-hydroxypicolinic
acid as the ancillary ligand. Notably,
the introduction of a BF2 moiety to the 3-hydroxypicolinic
acid ligand significantly perturbs the excited-state properties of
the complexes. The resultant heteronuclear complexes (63d–63f) display similar cellular uptake efficiencies
but substantially improved biocompatibility compared to their mononuclear
counterparts. Zheng, He, and co-workers have developed two iridium(III)
complexes incorporating l-alanine as the ancillary ligand
(64). These complexes exhibit
high aqueous solubility while retaining high emission quantum yields.
Both complexes show efficient cellular uptake in human gastric cancer
SGC-7901 cells and primarily localize in the cytoplasm. Chowdhury,
Laskar, and co-workers have developed an AIE-active neutral iridium(III)
complex (65) as bioimaging reagents. The intense emission observed in the mitochondria of human
osteosarcoma cells indicates the specific accumulation and aggregation
of the complex in the mitochondria after cellular uptake. Palamà,
Di Maria, and co-workers have demonstrated that neutral iridium(III)
complexes such as 63a, 66a, and 66b can self-assemble into supramolecular nanostructures through nanoprecipitation. Compared to the free complexes, these NPs exhibit
red-shifted emission with longer emission lifetimes. Furthermore,
the NPs show reduced cytotoxic activity and enhanced cellular uptake
efficiency in both 2D monolayer cells and 3D spheroids.
 Plush, Brooks, Massi, and co-workers have
studied the intracellular
localization and cytotoxic activity of a series of iridium(III) tetrazolato
complexes (67) ( ). Among these complexes,
the neutral complexes 67a–67e preferentially
reside in hydrophobic organelles such as the ER and LDs of rat embryonic
cardiomyoblast H9c2 cells, while the cationic analogues 67f, 67g, and 67i specifically accumulate
in the mitochondria. The high mitochondria specificity of complex 67g has enabled its use for staining the mitochondria in cardiac
and skeletal muscle tissue samples. The
complex has also been utilized as a cathodoluminescent probe for cathodoluminescence
imaging of cellular structures. Complexes 67b and 67d have also been demonstrated as bacterial
imaging reagents, displaying rapid internalization in Bacillus
cereus (B. cereus) and high specificity
for lipid vacuoles without inhibiting the bacterial growth. 
 Triscyclometalated iridium(III) complexes such
as [Ir(ppy)3] are attractive as biological probes due to
their high emission
quantum yields. However, these charge-neutral
complexes usually exhibit limited solubility in aqueous solution.
Chen, Zhao, and co-workers have developed a water-soluble triscyclometalated
iridium(III) complex (68) ( ) for cellular imaging. The complex forms NPs that show intense red emission (Φem = 0.13) and TPA (δ800nm = 33 GM) in aqueous solution, facilitating two-photon
luminescence imaging at an ultralow effective concentration (0.5 μM).
 Iridium(III) porphyrins represent an interesting
class of triplet
emitters. These complexes typically display intense red to NIR phosphorescence
in solution at room temperature, with emission lifetimes in the microsecond
time regime. Unlike square-planar palladium(II)
and platinum(II) porphyrins, the octahedral geometry of the iridium(III)
center allows the introduction of two axial ligands in iridium(III)
porphyrins, offering possibilities to manipulate their photophysical
properties and biological behavior. However, iridium(III) porphyrins
remain scarce as the synthesis and chemistry of these complexes are
more difficult than those of the corresponding palladium(II) and platinum(II)
porphyrins. Hua, Lo, and co-workers have developed a series of iridium(III)
porphyrins (69) ( ) as NIR luminescent biological probes. The cationic complexes 69c and 69d exhibit emission properties similar to their neutral counterparts 69a and 69b, but their emission maxima are blue-shifted
due to the axial pyridine ligands. Additionally, the cationic complexes 69c and 69d show higher cellular uptake efficiencies
compared to the neutral complexes 69a and 69b, and they are enriched in the perinuclear region of HeLa cells with
negligible nuclear uptake after cellular internalization. Furthermore,
the carboxyl-containing complex 69d has been used to
label BSA via an amidation reaction to produce a luminescent protein
conjugate. The conjugate displays similar photophysical properties
and cellular behavior to its parent complex. These results suggest
that cationic iridium(III) porphyrins are promising candidates as
NIR luminescent probes for bioimaging applications.
 The strategic modification of iridium(III) complexes
can lead to
the development of photofunctional complexes for various biological
applications. Dual-emissive complexes are highly attractive for the
development of ratiometric probes for bioimaging and biosensing in
living systems. Lo and co-workers have developed a series of iridium(III)
complexes (70) ( ) that exhibit interesting environment-sensitive dual
emission under ambient conditions. , The dual-emissive
behavior of the complexes results from the interruption of the electronic
communication between the higher-lying 3IL and the lower-lying 3CT states by a triplet amine-to-ligand charge-transfer (3NLCT) state. These complexes have been utilized as ratiometric
biological probes and bioimaging reagents. The intracellular localization
properties of these complexes vary depending on their lipophilicity:
the less lipophilic complexes primarily localize on the surface of
HeLa cells, whereas the more lipophilic complexes reside in the perinuclear
region. The cellular uptake and localization
properties of related iridium(III) fluorous complexes (71) have also been examined. Interestingly,
despite their similar cellular uptake efficiencies, the biotin complexes
show punctate staining while the biotin-free complexes display diffuse
staining in the perinuclear region. These findings suggest that the
intracellular localization of the complexes is influenced not only
by their lipophilicity but also by their molecular structures. Weinstein,
Ward, and co-workers have developed a heterobimetallic iridium(III)–europium(III)
complex (72), where the iridium(III) unit functions as
a sensitizer for the emissive 5D0 excited state
of europium(III) ion. Upon two-photon
excitation of the iridium(III) unit (λex = 780 nm), the complex undergoes partial d → f energy
transfer, resulting in both iridium(III)-based green phosphorescence
(τo = 1,070 and 380 ns) and europium(III)-based
red luminescence (τ = 0.46 ms) in aqueous solution.
The dual emission with different lifetimes of the complex has enabled
the use of LSCM and PLIM, revealing both punctate and diffuse cytoplasmic
staining in human dermal fibroblast (HDF) cells.
 Sun, Tian, and co-workers have designed a noncentrosymmetric
iridium(III)
complex with a donor–acceptor structure (73) ( ) for application
in second-harmonic generation (SHG) imaging. In SHG imaging, two incident photons are converted to a single photon
of twice the frequency after interacting with the probe. Since SHG
only involves virtual energy transition, the probes are not excited
and thus the problem of photobleaching can be effectively circumvented. Crystallization of the complex produces two
types of single crystals with distinct morphology and photophysical
and NLO properties, resulting from their different intermolecular
packing. The yellow and red crystals exhibit an emission band at 500–600
nm and 600–700 nm, respectively. Both crystals show two-photon-induced
emission upon photoexcitation at 820 nm; however, the yellow crystal
displays an additional sharp SHG emission peak at 410 nm (bandwidth
= ca. 7 nm), and the emission wavelength varies with
the excitation wavelength in the range of 800–900 nm. Live-cell
imaging shows that the complex is enriched in the lysosomes at low
concentrations (e.g., 5 μM) and in the nucleus
at high concentrations (e.g., 30 μM). The tunable
excitation wavelength and narrow emission wavelength of SHG emission
of the complex have facilitated multicolor imaging, enabling the simultaneous
detection of the complex with intracellular DNA (DAPI), RNA (SYTO
9), and mitochondria (MitoTracker Red) with no signal crosstalk ( ). The strong SHG
signals of the complex have also allowed imaging of mouse liver tissues
with a higher contrast compared to two-photon luminescence imaging
at a depth down to 200 μm.
 Yi, Tian, and co-workers have developed two iridium(III)
complexes
conjugated with a photochromic diarylethene (DAE) moiety (74) ( ) as photoswitchable
luminescent probes for cellular imaging. Under visible-light excitation, the DAE moiety adopts a ring-opened
form that exhibits weak absorption in the visible region, and thus
complexes 74a and 74b show intense yellow
emission (λem = 568 and 574 nm,
respectively). However, upon UV irradiation, the DAE moiety is converted
to the ring-closed form, which displays increased absorption in the
visible region (λabs = 480–647
nm). The increased spectral overlap with the emission of the iridium(III)
unit facilitates Förster resonance energy transfer (FRET),
resulting in efficient emission quenching. The photoswitchable emission
behavior of the complex is retained in biological contexts, highlighting
that the complex can be used for super-resolution microscopy such
as STORM that depends on stochastic switching of single molecules.
 

3.2.

## d8 Transition Metal Complexes
Compared to octahedral d6 transition metal complexes
that are coordinatively saturated, d8 transition metal
complexes such as rhodium(I), platinum(II), and gold(III) complexes
adopt a square-planar molecular geometry and possess vacant axial
coordination sites that allow for supramolecular self-assembly to
occur via noncovalent metal–metal interactions, leading to
the formation of excimers or aggregates with interesting spectroscopic
and luminescence properties. This provides an additional and orthogonal
dimension to tune their excited-state behavior for the construction
of luminescent supramolecular self-assembled functional materials
for various applications. 
3.2.1.

## Rhodium(I) Complexes
Square-planar
rhodium(I) complexes are known to exhibit interesting photophysical
properties attributed to the presence of Rh···Rh interaction. A typical example of such complexes is the
homoleptic tetrakis(isocyanido)rhodium(I) complexes, in which the
short Rh···Rh distance is crucial to the formation
of oligomers showing interesting spectroscopic behavior. − 
Lu, Che, and co-workers have demonstrated that the amphiphilic
tetrakis(2,6-xylylisocyanide)rhodium(I) complexes such as 75 ( ) can self-assemble
in water through Rh···Rh and hydrophobic interactions
into ultralong crystalline nanowires, and in turn hierarchically organize
into various ordered micro- and macrostructures. These solution-processable superstructures are luminescent
in the NIR region, originating from an excited state associated with
close Rh···Rh contacts. Yu, Bu, and co-workers have
utilized complex 75 to fabricate stable rod-like polymeric
nanomicelles with the negatively charged block copolymer poly(ethylene
oxide)-block-poly(sodium acrylate) through electrostatic
interaction. The nanomicelles are stable
in solution and display intense NIR phosphorescence, and the emission
intensity increases with the increasing molecular weight of the poly(sodium
acrylate) block, which assists in the hierarchical organization of
the rhodium(I) complex through extended Rh···Rh interaction.
The NIR emission has allowed in vivo imaging in mice
bearing 4T1 tumor xenografts, which shows that the nanomicelles selectively
accumulate at the tumor sites with a long retention time (> 48
h),
enabling the precise induction of cancer cell apoptosis in the tumor
tissues without injuring other healthy organs.
 
3.2.2.

## Platinum(II) Complexes
The development
of luminescent platinum(II) complexes has been largely focused on
those coordinated with a rigid multidentate ligand containing strong
σ-donor atoms. The strong σ-donor
strength and high rigidity of the ligand scaffold can effectively
raise the energy of the dissociative 3MC states and thereby
minimize excited-state structural distortion and nonradiative decay.
One of the typical examples is the platinum(II) complexes supported
by a tridentate ligand derived form 1,3-di(2-pyridyl)benzene (Hdpyb), which show intense emission in solution at
room temperature, rendering this class of complexes highly suitable
for the development of light-emitting materials and bioimaging reagents. 
Haycock, Weinstein, Williams, and co-workers have utilized the luminescent
cyclometalated platinum(II) complex [Pt(dpyb)Cl] (76a) ( ) in live-cell
imaging. The complex preferentially
resides in the cell nucleus in a range of mammalian cell lines including
HDF, human melanoma C8161, and CHO cells. It is retained in this cellular
compartment for several hours, which could be associated with its
covalent binding to nuclear structures. High-resolution cellular imaging
under two-photon excitation reveals the specific accumulation of the
complex in the nucleoli. Additionally, the long emission lifetime
of the complex has allowed the use of time-resolved emission imaging
microscopy (TREM) to remove the short-lived emission from cell autofluorescence
or other fluorescent labels to enhance the signal-to-noise ratio.
Since coordination with a thiolate ligand would shift the emission
of the complexes to lower energies, the intracellular emission of
complex 76a in mouse embryonic fibroblast NIH/3T3 cells
in both the green and red channels has been monitored, confirming
the reaction of the complex with the protein thiols in the mitochondria. Background-free, lifetime-based imaging of
live cells and histological tissues with complex 76a has
been demonstrated using two-photon TREM. The emission lifetime of the complex is highly sensitive to its
local environment and varies from nanoseconds to several microseconds
due to the varying levels of protection from O2 quenching.
Thus, the complex has been used to monitor changes in the cellular
viscosity upon induction of DNA damage by UV-A irradiation or X-ray,
showing that the viscosity in the cell nucleus changes more significantly
than that in the cytoplasm. A related
platinum(II) complex (76b) has been used for lifetime
mapping of live cells and histological tissues over a timeframe of
50 microseconds using a laser on/off “beam blanking”
approach, which allows the cell nuclei and matrix proteins to be readily
distinguished in both structured and homogeneous tissue sections. Complex 76b has also been used
as a photosensitizer to induce irreversible DNA single strand breaks
that are unable to be repaired in cells upon visible-light irradiation,
causing significant cell death of a range of cancer cell lines including
cisplatin-resistant bladder carcinoma EJ-R cells. Haycock and co-workers have utilized the water-soluble
analogue (76c) to examine the variations in O2 concentration in 3D multicellular tumor spheroids (MCTSs) using
PLIM, allowing real-time O2 mapping across live human melanoma
C8161 MCTSs with high spatial resolution. Dragonetti, Mauro, De Cola, and co-workers have developed three
cyclometalated platinum(II) complexes (77) bearing a
hydrophilic (oligo)ethylene glycol chain to increase their aqueous
solubility and biocompatibility. These
complexes are rapidly internalized into live HeLa cells via a diffusion-controlled
uptake mechanism. The uptake of complex 77b is fastest
(< 1 min) among the three complexes, probably due to its smallest
molecular size, indicating that the internalization kinetics depends
on both the length of the ethylene glycol chain and the ancillary
ligand. Upon internalization, the complexes are distributed throughout
the cells with localization in both the cytoplasm and nucleus.
 Che and co-workers have developed a cyclometalated
platinum(II)
complex (78) ( ) for protein staining and cell imaging. This complex is weakly emissive in aqueous
solution, but exhibits a 26-fold increase in emission intensity upon
binding to BSA. Treatment of HeLa cells with the complex leads to
intense emission in the perinuclear region, suggesting that the complex
interacts with hydrophobic organelles such as the ER and Golgi apparatus.
Lam and co-workers have developed a bioaccumulative cyclometalated
platinum(II) complex (79) for live-cell imaging. The strong two-photon-induced emission of the
complex has enabled time-lapse imaging of its cellular internalization
process using two-photon microscopy, revealing its rapid accumulation
in live HeLa and NIH/3T3 cells. Yang and co-workers have developed
a platinum(II) complex (80) displaying remarkable three-photon
absorption properties (σ1400nm =
3 × 10–81 cm6 s2 photon–2). Additionally, the
complex exhibits selective cytotoxicity toward cancerous HeLa cells
(IC50 = 7.2 μM) over normal human embryo liver fibroblast
(HELF) cells (IC50 = 52 μM), attributed to its specific
localization in the lysosomes after cellular uptake.
 Platinum(II) complexes chelated by a tetradentate
ligand usually
show higher emission quantum efficiencies due to increased molecular
rigidity. Huang, Sadler, and co-workers have studied the photophysical
and biological properties of a series of platinum(II) complexes supported
by an N,N’-bis(salicylidene)phenylenediamine
(salophen) ligand (81) ( ). These complexes
display a broad absorption band in the visible region (at ca. 400–600 nm) and intense phosphorescence at ca. 625 nm. The emission intensities and lifetimes of these
complexes are sensitive to O2 and viscosity, suggesting
that the complexes can be used to examine intracellular microenvironment.
As revealed by LSCM, the complexes localize in the cytoplasm of A549
cells, with higher affinities for the mitochondria and lysosomes but
not the nucleus. Additionally, the complexes are noncytotoxic in the
dark, but exhibit significant photocytotoxicity upon green-light irradiation
due to 1O2 generation.
 Shi, Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have examined
the photophysical
properties and cellular uptake of a series of cyclometalated platinum(II)
β-diketonate complexes (82) ( ). The emission color of the complexes can be tuned from blue-green
to yellow by changing the degree of conjugation of the β-diketonate
ligands due to their low triplet-state energy levels. All the complexes
show low cytotoxic activity, high membrane permeability, and exclusive
cytoplasmic staining. Shafikov, Czerwieniec, and co-workers have developed
a cyclometalated platinum(II) complex (83) that displays
both green fluorescence (λem = 495
nm) and red phosphorescence (λem = 660 nm) upon photoexcitation at a single wavelength (λex = 445 nm) due to slow ISC. The interesting dual emission can be detected in HeLa cells, showing
that the complex resides in the perinuclear region. Additionally,
the complex exhibits efficient 1O2 photosensitization
(ΦΔ = 0.84) upon irradiation.
The interesting dual emission behavior and high 1O2 generation efficiency of the complex are expected to offer
new opportunities for theranostic applications.
 Square-planar platinum(II) complexes have been
demonstrated to
self-assemble into supramolecular nanostructures via Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions, which is accompanied by the
emergence of the characteristic MMLCT absorption and emission bands
at lower energies. The unique supramolecular
self-assembly behavior of platinum(II) complexes has provided a new
paradigm for the construction of functional supramolecular architectures
with interesting spectroscopic and photophysical properties, where
Pt···Pt interaction plays a key role in guiding the
self-assembly process and maintaining the assembled structures. These supramolecular assemblies can allow the
molecules to be closely packed in a fixed structure and provide a
better shielding from the environment, while shifting both the excitation
and emission to the biological windows, specifically the red to NIR
regions, which are advantageous to bioimaging and biosensing applications. 
Che and co-workers have examined the
self-assembly behavior of
a cationic alkynylplatinum(II) complex (84) ( ) in water. This complex forms supramolecular structures
in aqueous solution through Pt···Pt and π–π
interactions, which gives rise to a new absorption band at ca. 500 nm and an emission band at 677 nm. It can also form
stable nanoaggregates in the intracellular environment, resulting
in intense red emission in the cytoplasmic region of live HeLa cells. Mauro, De Cola, and co-workers have demonstrated
that neutral platinum(II) complexes (85) can self-assemble
into nanosized aggregates, showing the characteristic 3MMLCT emission in live cells. These
luminescent aggregates primarily localize in the cytoplasm of HeLa
cells, but those formed from complex 85b also display
a high tendency to reside in the nucleus, in particular the nucleoli,
indicating that the tridentate ligand of the complex plays a role
in dictating the affinities of the assemblies toward different cellular
compartments. A related platinum(II) complex (86) has
been encapsulated into a redox-sensitive, disulfide-bridged organosilica
nanocage to control its dynamic self-assembly process. This nanocage can serve as a carrier to facilitate
the transport of the complex into live HeLa cells. Upon cellular internalization,
the nanocage slowly decomposes due to the reductive intracellular
environment and releases the platinum(II) complex, which self-assembles
into thermodynamically stable, blue-emissive microscopic fibers. Song,
Qu, Wong, and co-workers have developed an AIE-active alkynylplatinum(II)
complex (87), where an AIE luminogen tetraphenylethylene
is introduced to strengthen its intermolecular interaction in the
aggregated state, and an oligo(ethylene glycol) (OEG) pendant to increase
its solubility and biocompatibility. This complex self-assembles into nanoaggregates in aqueous solution
through Pt···Pt and π–π interactions,
giving rise to intense emission in the NIR region (λem = 730 nm). The complex has been encapsulated with a
nonionic copolymer Pluronic F127 to increase its stability, and the
resultant NPs are enriched in the lysosomes of HeLa cells after uptake.
 Bonnet and co-workers have examined the intracellular
dynamics
of two platinum(II) complexes (88) ( ), which form stable, deep
red-emissive nanorods via Pt···Pt interaction in serum-containing
media. Most of the complexes are internalized
into live A549 cells in the form of NPs via clathrin- and dynamin-mediated
endocytosis and reach the mitochondria without being trapped in the
lysosomes, while a smaller portion of the complexes pass through the
cell membrane as monomeric species via an energy-independent pathway
(e.g., passive diffusion), accumulate in the mitochondria,
and self-assemble to form larger and emissive nanostructures.
 Xu, Xie, Zhao, and co-workers have developed
two biscyclometalated
platinum(II) complexes (89) ( ) for cell imaging. These complexes are nonluminescent in solution but exhibit
intense red emission (λem = ca. 630 nm) upon aggregation. Cellular imaging shows that
the complexes are specifically enriched in the LDs of live HeLa cells.
Qian, Wong, and co-workers have developed a luminescent soft salt
(90) based on two cyclometalated platinum(II) complexes
of opposite charges. The soft salt shows
intense NIR emission (λem = 718
nm, Φem = 0.17) in the solid state,
where Pt···Pt and π–π interactions
take place to bring the two complexes into close proximity. The favorable
NIR emission has allowed the complex to be used in in vivo imaging.
 
3.2.3.

## Gold(III) Complexes
Compared to
the isoelectronic d8 platinum(II) complexes, luminescent
gold(III) complexes have received comparatively less attention in
the field of bioimaging, since many gold(III) complexes are nonluminescent
in solution due to rapid nonradiative decay via the low-lying 3MC states. However, through
the strategic incorporation of strong σ-donating alkyl and aryl
ligands into the complexes to raise the energy of the 3MC states, gold(III) complexes can
be made luminescent in solution at room temperature, providing new
opportunities for the development of luminescent gold(III) complexes
for diverse applications. 
Che
and co-workers have developed a cyclometalated gold(III) complex functionalized
with a biotin pendant (91) ( ) for coupling to avidin. The resultant luminescent gold(III)–avidin conjugate
displays strong interaction with DNA and BSA, which results in a up
to 45-fold increase in emission intensity. The cellular uptake of
the conjugate has been examined in live HeLa cells, showing that the
conjugate localizes in the cytoplasm with negligible nuclear uptake.
Che and co-workers have also developed a luminescent gold(III) complex
modified with a poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) pendant (92). This amphiphilic complex self-assembles
into nanosized micelles with a long emission lifetime of 84 μs
in aqueous solution. Live-cell imaging shows that the complex primarily
resides in the cytoplasm of HeLa cells. Notably, the complex exhibits
low dark cytotoxicity, high photocytotoxic activity, and selective
tumor accumulation in vivo.
 

3.3.

## d10 Transition Metal Complexes
In d10 transition metal complexes, the complete filling
of the d orbitals eliminates the possible emission quenching pathways
associated with the dissociative MC states. Similar to the d8 transition metal complexes, the low-dimensional coordination geometry
of d10 transition metal complexes allows for weak metallophilic
interactions between closed-shell d10 metal centers. Thus,
there has been significant interest in polynuclear d10 metal
complexes, in which metal–metal interactions are often observed
and play an important role in the luminescence properties of these
complexes. 
3.3.1.

## Copper(I) Complexes
Copper(I) complexes
are attractive in the development of bioimaging reagents due to their
high abundance and low cost. Copper(I) complexes such as [Cu(N∧N)2]+ and [Cu(N∧N)(P∧P)]+ with sterically bulky substituents
on the bidentate ligands have been demonstrated to show emission with
a long lifetime of several microseconds at room temperature due to
minimized Jahn–Teller distortion in the excited state. 
Li, Li, and co-workers have developed
four binuclear copper(I) complexes (93) ( ) for cellular imaging. While these complexes display very weak emission
in organic solvents such as dichloromethane, they exhibit intense
and long-lived phosphorescence (τ = 2.51 to
9.26 μs) in the solid state. Thus, upon the addition of an aqueous
buffer to the DMSO solution, the complexes form aggregates and show
aggregation-induced phosphorescence. Incubation of live HeLa cells
with the complexes results in punctate intracellular staining, indicating
that the luminescent aggregates can be internalized into the cells.
 
3.3.2.

## Gold(I) Complexes
Luminescent gold(I)
complexes have attracted significant attention for supramolecular
and optoelectronic applications due to their aurophilicity. The low
dimensionality of gold(I) complexes allows for weak intermolecular
attractive interaction between the gold(I) centers. Manipulation of
the Au···Au interaction in these complexes enables
a high degree of control over their emission properties.
Baker,
Berners-Price, and co-workers have studied the intracellular distribution
