of a luminescent binuclear gold(I) NHC complex (94a)
( ). The rigid cyclophane-derived bidentate NHC
ligands support a close intramolecular Au···Au contact
(ca. 2.9 Å), resulting in emission in the visible
region (λex = 355 nm, λem = 496 nm). The complex exists in both cis and trans configurations, depending on the localization
of the phenyl rings. Both isomers display identical absorption and
emission profiles. The cis-isomer has been used for
cellular imaging due to its higher aqueous solubility. Live-cell imaging
shows that complex cis-94a is preferentially
enriched in the lysosomes of RAW264.7 cells. Related binuclear gold(I)
complexes (94b–94d) featuring a short
intramolecular Au···Au distance (ca. 2.9–3.3 Å) have also been developed. Compared to complex cis-94a, complex cis-94c exhibits more efficient
cellular uptake and accumulation due to its higher lipophilicity.
Monitoring the characteristic emission resulting from Au···Au
interaction in complex cis-94c in human
breast adenocarcinoma MDA-MB-231 cells shows that the complex is highly
stable within the cellular environment for a period of at least 6
h.
 The d10···d10 metallophilic
interaction between the nonemissive cations [Au(NHC)2]+ and anions [MX2]− (M = Au or
Cu; X = halide, cyanide, or arylacetylide) has shown to drive the
formation of luminescent complex salts [Au(NHC)2][MX2]. The emission originates from
an emissive state of ligand (anion)-to-ligand (cation) charge-transfer
character. Chen, Wang, Zhou, and co-workers have exploited the d10···d10 metallophilic interaction
to induce the assembly of a cationic gold(I) complex (95) ( ) with
an anionic copper(I) (96a) or gold(I) complex (96b) inside cancer cells for cancer-selective imaging and
PDT. The cationic complex 95 is loaded onto hyaluronic acid (HA) for targeting the HA receptors
overexpressed on cancer cells. Thus, the bioorthogonal assembly between
complexes 95 and 96a specifically occurs
in cancer cells (A549 and HeLa) but not in normal cells (human liver
LO2 and human ovarian epithelial IOSE80) ( ), switching on both the luminescence and 1O2-generation properties of the assembles to realize
cancer-selective imaging and PDT.
 Liu, Zhang, and co-workers have demonstrated aggregation-induced
conversion from TADF to phosphorescence in gold(I) complexes (97) ( ). These complexes display weak blue
TADF with lifetimes of 31.79–64.26 μs in CH3CN, but exhibit intense orange-red phosphorescence with lifetimes
up to 2.31–3.40 ms upon aggregation in aqueous solution. The
complexes have been applied in cellular imaging, and all of them give
intense intracellular emission in A549 cells. Additionally, complexes 97b and 97c show high cytotoxicity toward A549
cells, suggestive of their potential development as theranostic agents.
 

4.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Conjugates for
Bioimaging
Luminescent transition metal complexes have demonstrated
great
success as cellular probes and bioimaging reagents. However, these
complexes often encounter challenges such as low aqueous solubility,
nonspecific in vivo biodistribution, and potential
systemic toxicity, which impede their effectiveness in clinical applications.
Thus, various strategies have been developed to improve the performance
of transition metal complexes for bioimaging and biosensing. Among
these strategies, the conjugation of transition metal complexes to
biomolecules, polymers, and nanomaterials has emerged as the most effective approach, offering promising solutions
to overcome these limitations.
4.1.

## Transition Metal–Biomolecule Conjugates
Biomolecules are the fundamental building blocks of living organisms,
playing indispensable roles in various life processes. Given their
natural occurrence, the conjugation of luminescent transition metal
complexes to biomolecules offers significant advantages such as enhanced
aqueous solubility, cellular uptake efficiency, and biocompatibility,
while preserving the favorable photophysical properties of the complexes.
Furthermore, luminescent labeling of biomolecules allows for the tracking
and visualization of these substances in living systems, providing
valuable information about their localization, activities, and functions
in diverse biological processes. This advancement is crucial to the
development of innovative diagnostic and therapeutic agents.
4.1.1.

## Nucleosides
Nucleosides are involved
in various important biological processes, ranging from cellular metabolism
to signaling. Serving as precursors
for nucleotides, nucleosides are essential for nucleic acid synthesis.
Synthetic fluorescent nucleobases, which can be incorporated into
nucleic acids alongside their natural counterparts, have emerged as
a valuable tool for probing nucleic acid structures, activities, and
interactions, facilitating the development
of nucleoside and nucleotide analogues as therapeutics for treating
cancer and viral infections. 
Chattopadhyay,
Das, and co-workers have designed two ruthenium(II) complexes conjugated
with the nucleobase uracil (98a) or its derivative 5-fluorouracil
(98b) ( ) as cellular imaging reagents. These luminescent conjugates are readily internalized into live
MCF-7 cells via endocytosis and enriched in lipid dense regions such
as the ER, which is different from their nucleobase-free counterpart
[Ru(bpy)3]2+ that displays poor cellular uptake
and nonspecific staining. Notably, upon cell fixation that dissipates
the lipid bodies, the conjugates migrate to the nucleus due to their
high affinities for DNA, enabling the visualization of nuclear DNA
at various stages of cell division.
 Berdis, Gray, and co-workers have modified an
iridium(III) complex
with a deoxyribose to afford a luminescent nucleoside analogue (99) ( ) for investigating enzymatic nucleoside transport in cells. , Competition assays and gene knockdown experiments reveal the involvement
of human equilibrative nucleoside transporters (hENTs), in particular
hENT1, in the cellular uptake of the conjugate in KB-3-1 cells, suggesting
that the conjugate can serve as a substrate for hENT1 and monitor
its activity in real time. After cellular internalization, the conjugate
primarily localizes in the cytoplasm, and it migrates to the nucleus
and mitochondria after 24 h. The specific accumulation of the conjugate
in these two organelles induces cell cycle arrest in the G2/M phase
and mitophagy, respectively, suggesting that the conjugate can function
as both an imaging tool and an anticancer agent.
 Doyle, Zobieta, and co-workers have studied the
cellular behavior
of a luminescent rhenium(I)–thymidine conjugate (100) ( ). Blocking and temperature dependence experiments
indicate that the cellular uptake of the conjugate is not mediated
by the thymidine receptor, and it primarily occurs via passive diffusion.
However, the involvement of nonspecific endocytosis cannot be ruled
out, given the localization of the conjugate in the endosomal/lysosomal
compartments after cellular internalization. Kowalski and co-workers
have utilized a binuclear rhenium(I) 1,2-diazine complex as a linker
to replace the canonical phosphodiester backbone of dinucleoside phosphates. The resulting luminescent dinucleoside phosphate
mimics (101) predominantly reside in the membrane-rich
compartments of HeLa cells. The intracellular localization properties
of the conjugates have also been examined in bacterial cells, demonstrating
uniform staining in both Gram-positive S. aureus and
Gram-negative Escherichia coli (E. coli), but with more efficient cellular uptake in the former species.
 
4.1.2.

## Amino Acids, Peptides, and Proteins
4.1.2.1.

## Amino Acids
Amino acids are essential
building blocks of proteins and play pivotal roles in various biological
processes. The incorporation of fluorescent
amino acids into proteins provides opportunities to examine protein
localization, trafficking, and interactions in live cells. Amino acids have also been utilized in the
development of prodrugs with improved physicochemical properties such
as enhanced solubility, permeability, and metabolic stability. 
Velders and co-workers have conjugated
[Ir(ppy)3] with three different amino acids including glycine
(Gly), alanine (Ala), and lysine (Lys). The cellular uptake of the resulting amino acid conjugates (102) ( ) varies with the number of amino acid substituents, with the monosubstituted
conjugates 102a, 102d, and 102g showing the highest cellular uptake efficiencies due to their higher
lipophilicity. Among the three types of amino acids, the Lys conjugates 102g–102i display the most efficient cellular
uptake and thus higher cytotoxic activity. In particular, the monosubstituted
Lys conjugate 102g is internalized into live 4T1 cells
via an energy-dependent pathway and diffusely distributed in the cytoplasm.
In contrast, the bi- and trisubstituted Lys conjugates 102h and 102i primarily localize in the lysosomes after
cellular uptake. The viability of the cells decreases with the number
of Lys substituents from 71 and 70% to 39%. Gimeno and co-workers
have developed a series of pyridyl amino acid derivatives for direct
coordination to the rhenium(I) core to produce luminescent amino acid
conjugates (103). These
conjugates are rapidly taken up by live MCF-7 cells and mainly enriched
in the cytoplasm with partial mitochondrial accumulation. Notably,
the para-substituted conjugates 103a–103c can induce the formation of amino acid
radicals, resulting in significant damage to the cell structures and
cell death.
 Glutamine is crucial to cancer cell survival
and growth by serving
as an alternative energy source, supporting macromolecular synthesis,
and maintaining redox homeostasis. Yapp,
Storr, and co-workers have prepared two rhenium(I)–glutamine
conjugates (104) ( ) to investigate glutamine uptake in live HT-29 cells,
a cell line that depends on both glucose and glutamine metabolism
for proliferation. Compared to conjugate 104a, conjugate 104b exhibits more efficient
cellular uptake and it is evenly distributed throughout the cells
after internalization. The inhibited cellular uptake observed in the
presence of excess glutamine suggests that the conjugate is primarily
taken up by the cells via a receptor-mediated uptake pathway. Remarkably,
both conjugates are essentially noncytotoxic toward the cells (IC50 > 1,360 μM; 48 h), offering opportunities for long-term
imaging of glutamine metabolism in cells.
 Tryptophan serves as a precursor for serotonin,
a neurotransmitter
involved in a wide range of neurological processes, and its metabolism
has implications in various neurodegenerative disorders and cancer. Colombo and co-workers have designed three
iridium(III) complexes appended with a tryptophan moiety (105) ( ) for
neuroimaging. These tryptophan conjugates
preferentially stain neuronal cells in ex vivo thin
sections of rat cerebellum tissues. Among the three complexes, complex 105c shows the highest affinity for cerebellar granule and
purkinje cells, and it is mainly distributed in the cytoplasmic region
after cellular uptake.
 
4.1.2.2.

## Peptides
Peptides are versatile
biopolymers composed of about 2 to 50 amino acid residues, showing
a wide range of biological functions and holding great potential as
therapeutics for numerous diseases. Their
ease of synthesis, efficient tissue penetration, and high binding
affinity and specificity toward their targets make them attractive
candidates in drug development.
Cell-penetrating peptides (CPPs)
are a class of peptides that can penetrate cellular membranes and
facilitate the transmembrane transport of bioactive cargos such as
oligonucleotides, proteins, and therapeutics into cells for various
biological and medical applications. The most extensively studied CPPs are the cationic Tat peptides,
which are derived from the human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)-1 Tat
protein. Zhou, Fei, and co-workers have
modified the Tat49–57 peptide with a His residue
at the N-terminus (HRKKRRQRRR) for conjugation with the iridium(III)
solvato complex [Ir(ppy)2(CH3CN)2]+. The resultant luminescent
peptide conjugate (106) ( ) is efficiently internalized into live
HeLa cells and primarily enriched in the nucleoli and vesicular structures.
Che and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex featuring
an electron-deficient alkynoic amide moiety for selective conjugation
to cysteine (Cys)-containing peptides and proteins. The complex has been tethered to a Tat48–60 peptide modified with a Cys residue at the C-terminus (GRKKRRQRRRPPQC),
producing a luminescent peptide conjugate (107) that
displays efficient cellular uptake and cytoplasmic localization. Tzakos
and co-workers have also developed a ruthenium(II) complex conjugated
with a Tat48–60 peptide (108) to promote
its cellular internalization, resulting in punctate cytoplasmic staining
in live HeLa cells. 
 Given the high abundance of basic amino acid
residues in the protein-derived
CPPs, homopolymers of arginine (Arg), Lys, ornithine, and His have
been examined as CPPs. Among these cationic
homopeptides, polyarginines, specifically those composed of 7 to 20
Arg residues, exhibit the highest cellular uptake. Keyes and co-workers
have conjugated a membrane-impermeable ruthenium(II) complex with
an octaarginine peptide to increase its cellular uptake for cellular
imaging. The resulting peptide conjugate
(109a) ( ) efficiently enters murine nonsecreting myeloma SP2/0 cells
and human blood platelets via an energy-dependent pathway. Notably,
the length of the polyarginine peptide is critical to transmembrane
transport, as the octaarginine conjugate shows more efficient cellular
uptake compared to its pentaarginine counterpart. A related osmium(II)–octaarginine
conjugate (109b) has been developed for cellular imaging. This conjugate has a higher propensity to penetrate
the nuclear membrane compared to its ruthenium(II) counterpart and
enter the nucleoli upon irradiation. Importantly, both conjugates
display negligible cytotoxicity toward SP2/0 and CHO cells under both
dark and light conditions. A ruthenium(II) dppz complex has been conjugated
with an octaarginine peptide to afford a cell-permeable probe (109c) for multimodal luminescence and resonance Raman imaging. Incubation of SP2/0 cells with the conjugate
results in intense emission in both the outer and internal membrane
structures, attributed to the emission enhancement brought about by
the hydrophobic local environment. This argument is supported by resonance
Raman spectroscopy, which indicates the preferential accumulation
of the conjugate inside the cells rather than in the membranes.
 Yoshihara and co-workers have designed a series
of NIR luminescent
iridium(III) complexes conjugated with an oligo(arginine) peptide
of different lengths (110) ( ) for in vivo vascular
imaging. Interestingly, while the free
complex and the tetraarginine conjugate 110a reside in
the intracellular space, conjugates 110b–110d with a longer Arg chain exhibit selective staining of
the vascular endothelium due to increased interaction with the proteoglycans
on the surface of vascular endothelial cells. Compared to the fluorescein
isothiocyanate (FITC)-modified lectin that circulates in the bloodstream,
the oligo(arginine) conjugates show higher retention, enabling long-term
imaging of the vascular network. Additionally, the long emission lifetimes
of the conjugates have allowed imaging of the blood vessel structures
without interference from background autofluorescence using time-gated
measurements. These properties have enabled in vivo imaging of the microvasculature in various organs and tissues including
the kidney, liver, pancreas, and tumor tissues. Dual-color imaging
of normal and fatty liver in live mice with a LD-specific fluorescent
probe PC6S has revealed distortion of the sinusoid network in the
hepatic tissue of mice fed with a high-fat diet.
 Barton and Puckett have conjugated a ruthenium(II)
complex with
a d-octaarginine peptide, which displays higher metabolic
stability than its l-counterpart. The resultant peptide conjugate (111a) ( ) exhibits punctate cytoplasmic
staining in HeLa cells at low concentrations (≤ 10 μM).
However, it preferentially accumulates in the nucleus, specifically
in the nucleoli at 20 μM. The nucleolar staining is further
enhanced when a lipophilic fluorescein unit is introduced to the C-terminus
of the peptide. The increased nucleolar localization of the resulting
conjugate (111b) results from its enhanced interaction
with the plasma membrane, which facilitates its cellular uptake via
a nonendocytic pathway and thus promotes its access to the nuclear
import machinery in the cytosol. In contrast, no comparable nuclear
staining is observed when a shorter d-tetraarginine peptide
is used, and the conjugate remains confined to vesicular structures
within the cytoplasm. Keyes and co-workers
have modified a membrane-impermeable osmium(II) complex with two d-tetraarginine or d-octaarginine peptides to increase
its membrane permeation for cellular imaging. The resultant bis-tetraarginine conjugate 112a shows efficient cellular uptake and accumulation in live
cells via an energy-dependent pathway. Interestingly, it primarily
resides in the lysosomes of cancerous A549 cells, while it is found
in the cytoplasm and nucleoli of noncancerous CHO cells. Furthermore,
the conjugate displays efficient membrane permeation in human pancreatic
adenocarcinoma cell MCTSs with intense emission being observed deep
into the spheroid core. In contrast, the bis-octaarginine
conjugate 112b remains impermeable to the cell membrane
in both 2D monolayer cells and 3D multicellular spheroids. These findings
indicate that the high membrane permeability of the octaarginine peptide
does not require a contiguous arrangement of the amino acids, and
a longer Arg chain does not further improve membrane permeation.
 Peptide nucleic acids (PNAs) are synthetic counterparts
of DNA
and RNA, featuring a unique pseudo-peptide backbone composed of N-(2-aminoethyl)glycine units replacing
the conventional phosphate-ribose structure. This substitution endows PNAs with enhanced chemical and thermal
stability and increased resistance to nucleases and proteases, while
maintaining high binding affinity and specificity toward complementary
nucleic acid strands. These remarkable characteristics have facilitated
their successful application in nucleic acid sensing and cancer therapy. D’Alfonso, Licandro, and co-workers
have conjugated a neutral binuclear rhenium(I) complex with a homothymine
PNA decamer. The resultant conjugate
(113a) ( ) can recognize and bind to the complementary homoadenine
single-stranded DNA oligonucleotide, forming stable PNA–DNA
duplexes. Treatment of HEK293 cells with the conjugate gives rise
to intense emission in the nuclear region. Notably, the nuclear emission
is blue shifted with respect to that in the cytoplasm due to the reduced
mobility and increased hydrophobicity of the nuclear environment.
A related PNA conjugate (113b) also exhibits preferential
enrichment in the cell nucleus. However,
the introduction of four additional Lys residues at the C-terminus
of the homothymine PNA decamer leads to the localization of the conjugate
(113c) in small globular endosome-like compartments with
negligible nuclear uptake. Gasser, Metzler-Nolte, and co-workers have
conjugated a rhenium(I) complex with a PNA oligomer complementary
to the messenger RNA expressing the enhanced green fluorescent protein
(EGFP). The resultant luminescent conjugate
(114) primarily resides in the perinuclear region of
HeLa cells upon electroporation. Notably, incubation of EGFP-expressing
HeLa cells with the conjugate reduces EGFP expression by 18%, suggesting
the potential use of the conjugate as an antisense agent.
 
4.1.2.3.

## Proteins
Proteins, as the fundamental
building blocks of life, are involved in a multitude of essential
cellular functions. For example, serum proteins are crucial to the
transport of various endogenous and exogenous compounds, serving as
vital carriers within the intricate network of biological processes.
Owing to their high biocompatibility and biodegradability, excellent
pharmacokinetic properties, and efficient accumulation in cancer tissues,
serum proteins have been exploited as carriers to deliver diagnostic
tools and therapeutic drugs to cancer cells. 
Albumin is the most abundant protein in blood plasma, playing
an important role in maintaining the plasma oncotic pressure and in
the transport of hydrophobic molecules such as fatty acids, hormones,
bilirubin, and drugs. Shcheslavskiy,
Tunik, and co-workers have utilized the cyclometalated platinum(II)
complex [Pt(ppy)(PPh3)Cl] (115a) ( ) for site-selective
labeling of human serum albumin (HSA). The complex is nonluminescent in solution but shows emission turn-on
upon substitution of the chloride ligand with His via imidazole coordination,
offering a new route for peptide and protein modification that is
orthogonal to the common bioconjugation techniques that target the
Lys or Cys residues. Labeling of HSA with the complex at a stoichiometric
ratio of 1:1 yields a water-soluble, nonaggregated luminescent conjugate
with a long emission lifetime (15.6 μs) in aerated aqueous solution.
Simultaneous mapping of the autofluorescence from cellular metabolic
components such as nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (phosphate) (NAD(P)H)
using fluorescence lifetime imaging microscopy (FLIM) and the phosphorescence
of the platinum(II)–HSA conjugate using PLIM allows monitoring
changes in both cellular metabolism and microenvironment. The results
show that upon cellular internalization, the platinum(II)–HSA
conjugate is distributed in the cytoplasm of live HeLa cells with
negligible effect on the cell metabolism. Related platinum(II) complexes
(115b–115d) have also been developed
for preparing luminescent protein conjugates with different photophysical
properties. Among the various platinum(II)–HSA
conjugates formed, the one derived from complex 115b displays
both strong TPA and high O2 sensitivity, facilitating its
application in O2 sensing using TP-PLIM. The substantial
differences in the emission lifetimes recorded within a single cell
indicate significant variations in the O2 levels across
different cellular compartments. Chelushkin and co-workers have designed
a water-soluble platinum(II) complex (116) for the preparation
of nonaggregated HSA conjugates with enhanced cellular internalization
capabilities. 
 Zhang, Zhang, and co-workers have utilized BSA
as a platform to
prepare a biocompatible and biodegradable protein hydrogel for imaging
and therapeutic applications. The hydrogel
is synthesized by mixing BSA with a carboxyl-bearing ruthenium(II)
complex (117) ( ), followed by crosslinking of the ε-amino groups of the Lys residues of BSA using glutaraldehyde. The
hydrogel shows BSA-based green fluorescence and ruthenium(II)-based
red phosphorescence upon excitation at 405 and 488 nm, respectively.
The hydrogel enters cells via endocytosis and, upon enzymatic digestion
in the lysosomes, releases the ruthenium(II) complex to the mitochondria.
Remarkably, the incorporation of the complex into the hydrogel results
in selective cytotoxic activity toward liver cancer HepG2 cells (IC50 = 15.6 μg mL–1) over normal liver
LO2 cells (IC50 = 106.2 μg mL–1).
 Transferrin is a glycoprotein that is involved
in mediating the
transport of iron through blood plasma. Donnelly and co-workers have developed ruthenium(II) and iridium(III)
complexes functionalized with a thiol-reactive maleimide moiety (118) ( ) for conjugation to transferrin. Treatment
of HeLa cells that overexpress transferrin receptors with the luminescent
transferrin conjugates results in punctate staining throughout the
cytosol. The strong colocalization of the conjugates with early endosomal
antigen 1 but not with the late endosomal mannose-6-phosphate receptor
indicates that the conjugates are internalized into the cells via
a pathway that is similar to native transferrin, which is trafficked
to early endosomes to release iron, suggesting that the luminescent
transferrin conjugates can be used to monitor transferrin uptake and
trafficking and examine cellular iron metabolism.
 

4.1.3.

## Carbohydrates
Carbohydrates are
essential for key biological processes including cell proliferation,
differentiation, and immune response. , Monitoring
the cellular uptake and distribution of carbohydrates is important
to disease diagnosis. For example, the 18F-labeled 2-deoxy-d-glucose (18F-FDG) is used with positron emission
tomography in clinical settings for diagnosing, staging, and monitoring
the recurrence of cancer based on the increased glucose metabolism
of cancer cells. 
Gottschaldt,
Hilger, and co-workers have modified [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with six d-glucose, d-galactose, or d-mannose moieties, and investigated the cellular uptake of the resultant
conjugates in live HepG2 cells using LSCM and flow cytometry. Among the three sugar conjugates, the d-glucose conjugate 119 ( ) displays the most efficient cellular internalization
and accumulation. Lo and co-workers have designed iridium(III) complexes
appended with an N-methylaminooxy group as luminescent
labeling reagents for reducing sugars. The resulting β-glucose conjugate (120a) is taken
up by live HeLa cells via an energy-requiring pathway and enriched
in the perinuclear region. Notably, the β-glucose conjugate
exhibits higher cellular uptake efficiency compared to its β-galactose
(120b), α-galactose (120c), β-lactose
(120d), and β-maltose counterparts (120e). These findings suggest the involvement of glucose transporters
(GLUTs) in the uptake of the glucose conjugates. Stagni, Plush, and
co-workers have developed a neutral rhenium(I) complex bearing an
alkyne handle for conjugation with azide-modified glucose, galactose,
mannose, and maltose using click chemistry. The cellular uptake and localization of the resulting conjugates
(121) have been studied in H9c2 cells, a cell line known
for its high dependence on carbohydrate metabolism and proliferation.
These conjugates are primarily enriched in the late endosomes/lysosomes
of the cells. Among the four conjugates, the glucose conjugate 121a shows the highest cellular uptake. Interestingly, the
addition of d-glucose does not affect the uptake of the conjugates,
indicating that the sugar conjugation does not specifically target
the conjugates to GLUTs but rather influence their localization following
uptake. Mauro, Panigati, and co-workers have investigated the cellular
uptake and localization properties of luminescent glyco-functionalized
neutral binuclear rhenium(I) complexes (122). Conjugates 122a–122c are readily internalized into live HeLa cells and enriched in the
ER. Interestingly, the more hydrophilic maltose conjugate 122c displays higher cellular uptake efficiency compared to the glucose
conjugates 122a and 122b, suggesting that
the uptake of conjugates is associated with an active transport mechanism.
However, incubation of the cells with the trivalent glycoconjugate 122d results in negligible intracellular emission.
 Glucose plays a critical role in cellular metabolism,
serving as
a vital energy source for cell growth. The transportation of glucose across the plasma membrane is mediated
by the membrane-bound GLUTs. The overexpression
of GLUTs has been linked to the increased glucose uptake and metabolism
in cancer cells. Lo and co-workers have
developed a rhenium(I)–glucose conjugate (123)
( ) as a luminescent
glucose uptake indicator to examine glucose uptake in cells. The conjugate exhibits similar and competitive
uptake behavior to d-glucose and accumulates in the mitochondria
after cellular internalization. Remarkably, the conjugate shows more
efficient uptake in transformed cell lines (HeLa and MCF-7) compared
to nontransformed cell lines (HEK293T and NIH/3T3) due to the overexpression
of GLUTs in transformed cells. A related iridium(III)–glucose
conjugate (124) also displays similar cell line-dependent
uptake and selective cytotoxicity toward transformed cells. This conjugate can serve as a luminescent glucose
uptake indicator that is responsive toward hormonal and metabolic
stimulation and inhibition, allowing for monitoring cellular metabolism.
Related rhenium(I) (125) and iridium(III)–glucose conjugates (126) also exhibit efficient cellular uptake in cancer
cells.
 The members of GLUTs show different substrate
specificity, transport
kinetics, and tissue expression profiles. Among them, GLUT5 is recognized
for its high specificity for fructose and overexpression in breast
cancer tissues. Lo and co-workers have
designed a rhenium(I) complex bearing a d-fructose unit (127) ( ) as a fructose uptake indicator and a selective breast cancer imaging
reagent. This conjugate displays selective
accumulation in human breast adenocarcinoma cell lines (MCF-7 and
MDA-MB-231) while exhibiting limited uptake in nonbreast cancer (A549
and HepG2) and nontransformed cell lines (NIH/3T3 and HEK293T) ( ). The inhibited
cellular uptake of the conjugate in the presence of exogenous fructose
but not glucose or glucose-uptake inhibitors indicates the involvement
of fructose transporters in the uptake process. Furthermore, the conjugate
shows higher cytotoxic activity toward breast cancer cells (MCF-7
and MDA-MB-231 cells; IC50 = 9.6 and 4.9 μM, respectively)
compared to nonbreast cancer cells (A549 and HepG2 cells; IC50 = 26.8 and 33.9 μM, respectively). Related iridium(III)–fructose
conjugates (128) display increased uptake in MCF-7 cells
and exhibit high photocytotoxicity (IC50 = 5.0 and 11.2
μM) due to photoinduced 1O2 generation. Additionally, a related ruthenium(II)–fructose
conjugate (129) shows selective enrichment in the plasma
membrane of cancerous MCF-7 and HeLa cells while displaying minimal
uptake in noncancerous Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK) and 3T3 cells
due to its binding to the overexpressed GLUT5. These findings highlight the potential of fructose conjugates
as innovative diagnostics and therapeutics for breast cancer.
 O-Linked-N-acetylglucosaminylation
(O-GlcNAcylation) is a posttranslational modification
that involves the addition of O-linked β-N-acetylglucosamine (O-GlcNAc)
to the serine or threonine residues of nuclear or cytoplasmic proteins
by O-GlcNAc transferase. It is invovled in the regulation of diverse cellular processes including
transcription, translation, signal transduction, and metabolism. Xiao
and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II) complex conjugated with
an O-GlcNAc derivative (130) ( ) for cellular imaging. The conjugate localizes in the cytoplasm of
live MCF-7 cells with negligible nuclear uptake, suggesting its potential
use to examine the expression and dynamics of O-GlcNAc-modified
proteins in cells.
 
4.1.4.

## Fatty Acids and Lipids
Fatty acids
are essential components of lipids and play important roles in biological
systems. They serve as a major source of energy and are integral to
the structure of membrane lipids. Additionally, fatty acids function
as signaling molecules, exerting significant influence on the development
and progression of metabolic disorders and cancer. Massi and co-workers have studied the photophysical and
biological behavior of neutral iridium(III) tetrazolato complexes
modified with a fatty acid of different chain lengths and saturation
(131) ( ). The length and degree of
unsaturation of the fatty acid chains do not significantly impact
the photophysical properties, lipophilicity, and cytotoxic activity
of the conjugates. Upon cellular internalization, conjugates 131a–131c reside in the ER of live HeLa
cells. In contrast, conjugates 131d and 131e exhibit much less efficient cellular uptake due to their longer
chains that lead to a higher degree of aggregation.
 Cholesterol is an essential lipid aiding in maintaining
the stability
and permeability of cell membranes. Additionally, it acts as a building
block for hormones and is involved in signal transduction and lipid
metabolism. Disruptions in cholesterol
homeostasis have been linked to various human diseases. Brooks and co-workers have studied the biodistribution
of a rhenium(I) complex conjugated with a cholestanol unit (132) ( ) in four prostate cell lines including a nonmalignant cell line
(PNT1a) and three malignant cell lines (22Rv1, LNCaP, and PC3) that
show distinct lipid profiles and lipid metabolism. In nonmalignant PNT1a cells, the conjugate primarily accumulates
in late endosomal/lysosomal compartments, whereas in prostate cancer
cells, it is enriched in early endosomes and multivesicular bodies.
Notably, the accumulation of the conjugate in androgen-independent
PC3 cells is higher compared to androgen-sensitive 22Rv1 and LNCaP
cells, which is associated with the increased lipid metabolism and
altered cholesterol biology in malignant cells.
 Squalene is a natural, polyunsaturated triterpene
that acts as
a biochemical precursor for the synthesis of cholesterol and other
steroids. Dosio and co-workers have
conjugated a ruthenium(II) complex with squalene to afford a self-assembling
lipophilic probe (133) ( ) for cellular imaging. The conjugate self-assembles into nanosized aggregates
in aqueous solution. These nanoaggregates are efficiently transported
across the plasma membrane of HT-29 and MCF-7 cells and distributed
throughout the whole cells including the cell nucleus. The nanoaggregates
also exhibit effective penetration into MCTSs. Interestingly, the
squalene conjugate can undergo coassembly with drugs such as gemcitabine
and paclitaxel, suggesting its potential application as carriers for
drug delivery.
 
4.1.5.

## Estrogens
Estrogens are steroid
hormones that have a significant impact on the development and maintenance
of normal sexual and reproductive function. The sex hormone 17β-estradiol is of particular interest due
to its crucial role in the growth and progression of estrogen receptor-positive
breast cancer cells. 
Lo and co-workers
have examined the cellular uptake of a ruthenium(II) complex conjugated
with a 17α-ethynylestradiol moiety (134) ( ) in live cells. This conjugate shows enhanced emission upon
binding to the estrogen receptor. The conjugate is taken up by HeLa
cells via an energy-requiring process and enriched in the perinuclear
region following cellular uptake. Panigati, Sungi, and co-workers
have studied the cellular uptake of binuclear rhenium(I) 17α-ethynylestradiol
conjugates (135) in MCF-7 and MDA-MB-231 cells that exhibit
different expression levels of estrogen receptors. The similar cellular uptake efficiency of conjugate 135a in MCF-7 (estrogen receptor-positive) and MDA-MB-231
cells (estrogen receptor-negative) suggests passive diffusion as the
major mechanism responsible for the cellular internalization of the
conjugate. The conjugate has also been used to stain Ciona
intestinalis embryos and larvae at different stages of development.
 
4.1.6.

## Vitamins
Vitamins such as biotin
(vitamin B7), folic acid (vitamin B9), and cobalamin
(vitamin B12) are essential for cell growth, proliferation,
and differentiation. Cancer cells often display elevated expression
of receptors for these vitamins to meet their increased demands for
rapid cell proliferation. 
Lo and
co-workers have examined the cellular uptake and localization properties
of a rhenium(I) complex conjugated with a biotin unit (136) ( ). The conjugate is taken up by HeLa cells via
an energy-dependent pathway and enriched in the perinuclear region,
which appears to be the Golgi apparatus. Related iridium(III) (137) and rhodium(III) complexes (40) also localize
in the perinuclear region with minimal uptake in the nucleus, but
their internalization occurs via energy-independent passive diffusion. However, rhenium(I) (138) and iridium(III) complexes (139) bearing multiple biotin moieties
exhibit more punctate cytoplasmic staining.
 Doyle and co-workers have investigated the cellular
uptake of a
rhenium(I) complex conjugated with a folic acid moiety (140) ( ). This conjugate is efficiently internalized
into A2780/AD cells that overexpress folate receptors (FRs). However,
no cellular uptake of the conjugate is observed when an excess of
folic acid is added to the cell culture medium or when CHO cells that
do not express FRs is used. Related rhenium(I) complexes have also
been conjugated with cobalamin (vitamin B12) to afford
luminescent probes (141) targeting the cubilin receptor. , The cellular uptake of these conjugates occurs via a cubilin receptor-mediated
pathway, facilitated by the presence of intrinsic factor. This unique
cellular uptake mechanism allows for the detection of the cubilin
receptor in live cells and enables the examination of its expression
in both cancerous and normal cell types, providing a valuable tool
for characterizing the expression of the cubilin receptor in various
cellular contexts.
 

4.2.

## Transition Metal–Polymer Conjugates
When developing imaging reagents using transition metal complexes,
their low aqueous solubility and high cytotoxicity often pose challenges
to their use in living systems. Thus, there has been significant interest
in attaching these complexes to hydrophilic polymers. This modification
provides a facile means to enhance their aqueous solubility and biocompatibility,
expanding their potential applications in bioimaging and clinical
diagnosis.
4.2.1.

## Poly(ethylene glycol)
Poly(ethylene
glycol) (PEG) is a synthetic polymer that has found important applications
in pharmaceuticals due to its high hydrophilicity and biocompatibility. The attachment of PEG chains to bioactive molecules
such as peptides, proteins, and antibodies offers several benefits
by minimizing their nonspecific interactions with biological components,
prolonging their blood circulation time, and reducing their clearance
rate. These properties render PEG a valuable tool in optimizing the
performance of pharmaceutical compounds.
Lo and co-workers have
designed a series of iridium(III) complexes appended with a PEG pendant
(142) ( ). This modification substantially
increases the aqueous solubility of the complexes (> 0.3 M) while
significantly reducing their cytotoxic activity (IC50 =
287 to 1,180 μM) without compromising their high cellular uptake
efficiencies. The aldehyde-bearing complex 142c has been
utilized as a luminescent PEGylation reagent for modification of BSA
and PEI. The resulting PEGylated PEI conjugate shows efficient condensation
of plasmid DNA, allowing it to serve as a transfection reagent. Related
iridium(III) complexes modified with a nondiscrete PEG pendant (143) have also been developed. Similar to their discrete PEG counterparts, these complexes display
negligible cytotoxicity toward HeLa cells in the dark (IC50 > 300 μM). However, upon continuous irradiation, complexes 143a–143d exhibit significantly increased
cytotoxic activity (IC50 = 3.4 to 23.2 μM) due to
the photoinduced generation of 1O2, resulting
in efficient oxidative damage to the mitochondria and induction of
necrosis. Additionally, the in vivo toxicity of the
PEG complexes is substantially reduced compared to their PEG-free
counterparts, making them suitable for use as visualizing reagents
for zebrafish ( ). Related rhenium(I) complexes (144) also display increased
aqueous solubility and biocompatibility upon the covalent attachment
of a PEG pendant. Additionally, the
amino group of these complexes can be activated with thiophosgene,
producing amine-reactive isothiocyanate derivatives for PEGylation
purposes. Furthermore, Lo and co-workers have developed photosensitive
(145) and thiol-responsive
PEG complexes (146) as
activatable cytotoxic agents. The incorporation of a 2-nitrobenzyl
moiety or a disulfide linkage between the iridium(III) core and the
PEG pendant enables the complexes to exhibit substantially increased
cytotoxicity in response to light and biothiols, respectively, due
to the release of the biocompatible PEG unit. Sun, Li, and co-workers
have demonstrated that iridium(III) PEG complexes (147) self-assemble into nanosized micelles. The introduction of a longer PEG chain reduces the nonspecific interactions
of the complexes with proteins, prolonging their blood circulation
time and increasing their tumor accumulation in vivo, enabling tumor-specific imaging and PDT.
 
4.2.2.

## Poly(amidoamine)
Poly(amidoamine)s
(PAMAMs) are a class of amphoteric polymers synthesized through the
polyaddition of amines to bisacrylamides. They show remarkable properties such as high aqueous solubility,
biocompatibility, and biodegradability. Additionally, PAMAMs with
different charge distribution profiles display distinct structural
and conformational behavior depending on the pH of their environment.
These unique attributes render PAMAMs useful for a wide range of biomedical
applications.
Maggioni, Ranucci, and co-workers have incorporated
rhenium(I) or ruthenium(II) complexes into a zwitterionic PAMAM to
afford water-soluble and biocompatible metallopolymers (148a and 148b) ( ). The ruthenium(II)-containing
polymer 148b is internalized into live HEK293 cells via
an endocytic pathway and distributed in the cytoplasm. A related iridium(III)-containing
polymer (148c) has been developed for imaging and PDT
applications. Compared to the monomeric
complex, the polymer exhibits similar subcellular distribution but
remarkably lower (photo)cytotoxicity. Salvati, Maggioni, and co-workers
have utilized a cationic PAMAM as the backbone for attaching ruthenium(II)
complexes for PDT applications. , The resultant
metallopolymer (149) shows more efficient cellular uptake
in live HeLa cells compared to its zwitterionic counterpart 148b. It enters cells via an active uptake mechanism and undergoes
trafficking along the endosomal/lysosomal compartments. Upon irradiation,
the photogenerated 1O2 triggers membrane damage,
enabling the polymer to escape from the endosomes/lysosomes and gain
access to the cell nucleus, facilitating the induction of cell death.
 Furthermore, PAMAMs can be prepared as dendrimers
with branched
and layered architectures. The high degree of control over the size,
shape, branch length and density, and surface amino groups of PAMAM
dendrimers enables them to hold significant potential in various biomedical
applications including gene transfection, drug delivery, and imaging. Lo and co-workers have utilized a generation
1 PAMAM dendrimer as the backbone to develop two dendritic, octanuclear
iridium(III) complexes (150) ( ). At equimolar
concentrations, these dendritic complexes display higher emission
intensities compared to their monomeric counterparts, distinguishing
them from dendrimers decorated with organic fluorophores that often
experience self-quenching effects. However, the dendritic complexes
exhibit reduced cellular uptake efficiencies due to their higher cationic
charge and larger molecular size. These complexes are internalized
into live HeLa cells via an energy-requiring pathway and enriched
in the Golgi apparatus ( ), whereas the mononuclear complexes are taken up by the cells
through an energy-independent pathway and enriched in the perinuclear
region. Velders and co-workers have developed two dendritic ruthenium(II)
complexes of opposite charges (151) using a generation
2 PAMAM dendrimer that possesses a cystamine core and sixteen amino
groups on the surface. These dendritic
complexes localize in the lysosomes of live murine colon carcinoma
CT26 cells after cellular internalization via endocytosis. Upon continuous
irradiation, the cationic complex 151a escapes from the
lysosomes to the cytoplasm and migrates to the nucleoli. In contrast,
the anionic complex 151b is retained in the lysosomes
upon irradiation, suggesting a lack of photocytotoxicity.
 

4.3.

## Transition Metal–Nanomaterial Conjugates
Over the past two decades, there have been significant advancements
in the development of nanomaterials for the diagnosis and treatment of cancer and other diseases. The size, shape, and surface chemistry
of nanomaterials play a crucial role in determining their cellular
interaction as well as pharmacokinetic and biodistribution profiles.
Furthermore, nanomaterials often show prolonged circulation times
in the bloodstream, enabling their preferential accumulation in tumor
tissues due to the enhanced permeability and retention (EPR) effect.
4.3.1.

## Polymeric Nanomaterials
Polymer-based
nanomaterials have been developed as promising nanocarriers for biomedical
applications. The high diversity of
polymers allows for the construction of nanomaterials with different
architectures such as polymeric nanoparticles (PNPs), polymeric micelles,
and polymersomes. The encapsulation of therapeutic agents in polymeric
nanocarriers can significantly prolong their circulation times, thereby
reducing the side effects of the treatment without compromising the
therapeutic efficacy.
Shi, Li, and co-workers have developed
a simple and effective method to prepare luminescent PNPs for cellular
imaging. A hydrophobic osmium(II) complex
(152) ( ) is embedded into hydrophilic polyacrylamide-coated polystyrene
through emulsion polymerization. The resultant PNPs display efficient
cellular uptake and exclusive staining of the cytoplasm in live KB
cells, in sharp contrast to the free complex that is impermeable to
the plasma membrane of live cells. This indicates that the incorporation
of the complex into PNPs alters its cellular uptake behavior. Using
the same preparation method, water-insoluble iridium(III) (153) and platinum(II) complexes (154) with interesting AIE behavior
have also been encapsulated into polyacrylamide-coated polystyrene
to develop luminescent PNPs for imaging applications. Further modification
of the amino groups on the surface of these PNPs with targeting moieties
such as folic acid enables selective targeting of cancer cells that
overexpress FRs. Pu, Li, and co-workers
have utilized the emulsion polymerization method to incorporate a
luminescent iridium(III) complex (155) and a photochromic
DAE derivative (DAE-CHO) into PNPs to afford photoswitchable
luminescent PNPs for bioimaging applications. The PNPs show photoswitchable emission both in solution
and in live cells upon sequential exposure to UV- and visible-light
irradiation. Cellular imaging shows that the PNPs are internalized
into live KB cells via an energy-dependent pathway and enriched in
the cytoplasmic region. Hildebrandt, Charbonnière, Reisch,
and co-workers have coencapsulated a phosphorescent rhenium(I) complex
(156) and an NIR fluorescent cyanine derivative 1,1′-dioctadecyl-3,3,3′,3′-tetramethylindodicarbocyanine
(DiD) as a FRET pair into PNPs to prepare NIR luminescent PNPs for
cellular imaging with high constrast. 
 Choudhury, Alemany, Jana, Laskar, and co-workers
have utilized
a biodegradable block copolymer PEG–PLA (PLA = poly(lactic
acid)) to encapsulate an AIE-active monocyclometalated iridium(III)
complex (157) ( ) for cellular imaging. The amphiphilic block copolymer self-assembles into micelles in
aqueous solution, with the hydrophobic PLA block located inside and
hydrophilic PEG block outside. The complex aggregates inside the micelles,
resulting in intense green emission. Live-cell imaging in HeLa cells
reveals the binding of the PNPs to the cell surface and their cytoplasmic
localization after uptake. Lepeltier, Monnereau, and co-workers have
encapsulated an AIE-active carbazole-substituted iridium(III) complex
(158) into PNPs for two-photon luminescence imaging of
cerebral vasculature. Feng, Wang, Zeng,
and co-workers have utilized an amphiphilic block copolymer PDMAEMA-b-poly(PEGMA)-b-PDMAEMA (PDMAEMA = poly(2-(dimethylamino)ethyl
methacrylate); PEGMA = poly(ethylene glycol) methacrylate) as an encapsulating
agent to deliver a water-insoluble rhenium(I) complex (159) into live cells. The resulting core–shell
structured PNPs are permeable to HEK293T cells and distributed throughout
the whole cells. Additionally, the PNPs display strong interaction
with DNA, which allow them to serve as gene transfection agents. Chai,
Zhang, Tang, and co-workers have utilized polymeric nanocapsules to
encapsulate the iridium(III) complex 66b for two-photon
luminescence imaging of neural stem cells. The complex is loaded into the PEG–PLGA (PLGA = poly(lactic-co-glycolic acid)) nanocapsules using a double emulsion
method. The resultant luminescent nanocapsules efficiently stain mouse
neural stem cells, and the labeled stem cells can be monitored in
live mice for up to 21 days after subcutaneous injection, offering
exciting opportunities for real-time tracking of the transplanted
stem cells in vivo.
 Yang, Yang, and co-workers have developed a three-component
self-assembly
approach to prepare water-soluble luminescent PNPs using an iridium(III)
complex (160) ( ), the block copolymer P4VP-b-PEG
(P4VP = poly(4-vinylpyridine)), and methanesulfonic acid. Methanesulfonic acid is introduced to form
the pyridinium methanesulfonate salt with P4VP-b-PEG
through proton transfer, facilitating hydrogen bonding formation with
the hydroxyl groups of the iridium(III) complexes. The resultant PNPs
show exclusive cytoplasmic staining in live 4T1 cells, and induce
significant cell death upon continuous irradiation. Core-stabilized
luminescent PNPs have also been developed by complexation of the pyridine
blocks in P4VP-b-PEG with chloride-bridged iridium(III)
dimers (161). These PNPs
display high stability in aqueous solution and cell media for at least
7 days. They also exhibit efficient cellular uptake with minimal dark
cytotoxic activity, enabling effective cellular imaging and PDT. Modification
of P4VP-b-PEG with a folic acid moiety allows the
resultant PNPs to selectively target HeLa cells that overexpress FRs. Importantly, upon cellular internalization,
the intracellular His can react with the iridium(III) moieties in
the PNPs, triggering their release from the PNPs and facilitating
their nuclear entry. This innovative mechanism offers a controlled
delivery approach for iridium(III) complexes to reach the cell nucleus.
 Massi, Lowe, and co-workers have prepared luminescent
metallopolymers
(162) ( ) using well-defined tetrazole-functionalized acrylamide copolymers
as a macromolecular ligand for iridium(III) and rhenium(I) complexes. The incorporation of a macromolecular ligand
does not significantly affect the photophysical properties of the
iridium(III) tetrazolato complexes. However, FRET occurs in the copolymers
containing both the iridium(III) and rhenium(I) species. The metallopolymers
have been used for lipid imaging in brain tissues. The strong affinity
of the metallopolymers for the myelin lipids leads to intense staining
of the cerebellar white matter tracts, while their interaction with
intracellular lipids within individual granule neurons results in
the staining of the granular layer of the cerebellum.
 Shakirova, Tunik, and co-workers have designed
a series of rhenium(I)
dicarbonyl complexes (163) ( ) as a hydrophobic block for the synthesis
of water-soluble metal-containing copolymers. These complexes feature an alkenyl group on the phosphine
ligand, which allows copolymerization with poly(N-vinylpyrrolidone) (PVP) via reversible addition–fragmentation
chain-transfer polymerization. Compared to the monomeric complexes,
the resultant metallopolymers show blue-shifted emission, longer emission
lifetimes, and reduced cytotoxicity. Furthermore, while the monomeric
complexes display high specificity for the mitochondria, the metallopolymers
primarily reside in the endosomal/lysosomal compartments of live CHO-K1
cells after uptake due to their different internalization pathways.
Additionally, the emission of the metallopolymers is relatively insensitive
to O2 due to the effective shielding provided by the polymeric
matrix, minimizing the production of ROS upon irradiation. Proetto,
Gianneschi, Strassert, and co-workers have developed a norbornene-modified
platinum(II) complex (164) as a monomer for direct incorporation
into amphiphilic block copolymers via ring-opening metathesis polymerization. This method provides precise control over the
position of the complexes, either distal or adjacent to the hydrophilic
block, allowing their selective loading into the core or the shell
of the resultant micelles, respectively. Interestingly, when the platinum(II)
complexes are confined to the shell, the smaller volume of the shell
compared to the core promotes Pt···Pt interaction,
resulting in the characteristic 3MMLCT emission. These
luminescent micelles have been used for bimodal imaging of HeLa cells
with transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and LSCM. Tunik, Tennikova,
and co-workers have designed a platinum(II)–Cys conjugate (165) for the synthesis of amphiphilic polypeptides. This conjugate acts as an initiator for the
ring-opening polymerization of α-amino acid N-carboxyanhydrides to attach the luminescent
platinum(II) complex to the C-terminus of the polypeptide chain. The
resultant polypeptdies have been utilized to prepare luminescent PNPs
using the phase inversion method. Live-cell imaging in human histiocytic
lymphoma U937 cells shows that the PNPs are rapidly taken up by the
cells due to their small size and cationic surface charge, and they
are primarily distributed in the cytoplasm after cellular internalization.
 Tunik and co-workers have examined the cellular uptake of a series
of NIR luminescent core–shell structured PNPs in different
cell lines. These PNPs are prepared
by the nanoprecipitation of hydrophobic polymers such as poly(d,l-lactic acid), poly(ε-caprolactone),
and poly(ω-pentadecalactone), followed by the
covalent modification with poly(l-lysine) labeled with iridium(III)
and platinum(II) complexes bearing an amine-reactive N-hydroxysuccinimide ester moiety (166) ( ). The
resultant PNPs are stable in aqueous solution and show NIR emission
characteristic to the unconjugated complexes. Furthermore, the PNPs
display efficient cellular internalization in various cell types,
including cancer cells (A549), stem cells (human adipose tissue-derived
mesenchymal stem cells), and fibroblasts (NIH/3T3). Notably, the cellular
uptake efficiency of the PNPs increases when the surface of the PNPs
is further modified with heparin to reverse their surface charge from
positive to negative, which reduces their nonspecific interactions
with proteins in the culture medium.
 
4.3.2.

## Glyconanomaterials
Glyconanomaterials
derived from glycopolymers offer unique advantages for biomedical
applications such as biocompatibility, biodegradability, and cellular
recognition due to their resemblance to natural glycoproteins. 
Sun, Huang, and co-workers have developed
water-soluble glyconanoparticles by self-assembling a luminescent
iridium(III) complex (167) ( ) with a water-soluble glycopolymer (poly(glucose))
or polysaccharide (β-1,3-glucan). These glyconanoparticles are efficiently internalized into live
HepG2 cells and distributed throughout the whole cytoplasm. In vivo biodistribution studies in live mice reveal significant
accumulation of the NPs in the liver, indicating their potential as
luminescent probes for tissue imaging. Furthermore, the NPs induce
effective apoptosis of cancer cells upon continuous irradiation, highlighting
their theranostic capabilities. Luminescent core–shell structured
glyconanoparticles have been developed by crosslinking an amine-reactive
glycopolymer with an amine-modified ruthenium(II) terpyridine complex
(168). The complex is confined
to the crosslinked core of the resultant NPs, while the glycopolymer
forms the hydrophilic corona. The glyconanoparticles have
been used for one- and two-photon luminescence imaging of live KB
cells, revealing their localization in both the cytoplasm and nucleus.
 Cyclodextrins (CDs) are water-soluble carbohydrate
oligomers consisting
of six to eight glucose units arranged in a ring structure. The amphiphilic cup-like structures of CDs
allow them to form host–guest inclusion complexes with hydrophobic
guest molecules such as pharmaceuticals, improving their stability
and bioavailability. Liu, Feng, and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex conjugated with a β-CD moiety (169) ( ) as a luminescent
DNA carrier. Upon interaction with DNA,
the complex induces DNA aggregation, hindering DNA topoisomerization
and cleavage by enzymes. The complex has been used to deliver a plasmid
DNA encoding EGFP to live HEK293T cells, with its luminescence properties
enabling real-time tracing of DNA translocation into the cells. Liu
and co-workers have leveraged the strong host–guest interaction
between β-CD and adamantane to develop photoresponsive NPs.
These NPs are constructed by the supramolecular assembly of a β-CD-modified
ruthenium(II) complex (170) and an adamantane-modified
DAE derivative (DAE-Ad) in the presence of two other
amphiphilic substances, Pluronic F127 and cetrimonium bromide. The strong spectral overlap between the emission
of the ruthenium(II) complex and the absorption of the ring-closed
form of DAE-Ad (λabs = 366 and 620 nm) but not the ring-opened form (λabs = 317 nm) results in photocontrollable FRET, enabling
the NPs to show photoswitchable emission. These NPs have been used
as a photoresponsive label for live A549 cells.
 Zysman-Colman, Ravoo, and co-workers have utilized
β-CD vesicles
as a carrier to deliver iridium(III) complexes (171)
( ) to live
cells for imaging applications. Complexes 171a and 171b are bound to the surface of the
CD vesicles with high affinities (Ka =
0.9 and 2.8 × 106 M–1 s–1, respectively), where the less polar local environment results in
an increase in their emission intensities and a blue shift in their
emission maxima. Live-cell imaging shows that the complex-containing
β-CD vesicles are internalized into human umbilical vein endothelial
cells (HUVECs) via an endocytic pathway. Importantly, the inclusion
of complex 171b into CD vesicles significantly enhances
its cellular uptake. By labeling the CD vesicles with rhodamine, it
is observed that complex 171b dissociates from the CD
vesicles upon entry into the cells. Monitoring the intracellular dynamics
of complex 171b reveals that the complex primarily localizes
in the early endosomes during endosomal maturation and then migrates
to the mitochondria due to its cationic charge and high lipophilicity.
 
4.3.3.

## Lipid Nanoparticles
Lipid-based
NPs such as liposomes and phospholipid micelles are important delivery
systems used for biomedical applications. Liposomes are vesicles with
an aqueous core surrounded by a phospholipid bilayer. They were the
first nanocarriers used in clinical settings. The covalent conjugation of polymers such as PEG to phospholipids
can lead to the formation of phospholipid micelles with a protective
hydrophilic layer on the surface, preventing self-fusion and nonspecific
interactions with other particles.
Prasad and co-workers have
developed an optical probe for in vivo tumor imaging
by encapsulating PtTPTNP (H2TPTNP = meso-tetraphenyl-tetranaphthoporphyrin)
(172) ( ) into phospholipid micelles. These micelles are formed through the self-assembly of 1,2-distearoyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine and the PEGylated 1,2-distearoyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphorylethanolamine (DSPE-PEG2000). Upon intravenous injection into live mice bearing human pancreatic
cancer PANC-1 tumor xenografts, these nanomicelles display efficient
tumor accumulation, allowing for the effective visualization of the
tumors even during their early stages of development (i.e., one week after cancer cell inoculation). Xie and co-workers have
utilized folic acid-conjugated liposomes as a carrier for [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ for cancer imaging and photoinduced inactivation. The specific interaction of folic acid with
FRs enables selective targeting of the liposomes to FR-overexpressing
cancer cells and their enrichment in the plasma membrane. Upon exposure
to laser irradiation, the liposomes rupture and release the complex
into the cells. The complex then migrates to the nucleus and intercalates
into DNA, leading to photoinduced cell death. Franchini and co-workers
have designed a cholesterol-based nanocarrier for specific imaging
of glioma cells using an iridium(III) complex (173). The nanocarrier is formed through the self-assembly
of a PEGylated cholesterol using a nanoprecipitation method. The complex
is either physically entrapped in the core or covalently linked to
the shell of the nanocarrier. To enable specific targeting, the surface
of the nanocarrier is further modified with chlorotoxin, a peptide
known for its specific binding to matrix metalloproteinase-2 (MMP-2)
overexpressed in glioma cells. Both types of nanocarriers exhibit
efficient internalization into Uppsala 87 malignant glioma (U-87 MG)
cells, with no significant difference observed for their cellular
uptake efficiencies and cytotoxic activity.
 Tunik, Urtti, and co-workers have developed
two iridium(III) complexes
containing a long aliphatic chain (174) ( ) as lipid mimics to prepare
luminescent metalloliposomes for imaging retinal pigment epithelium in vitro and in vivo. The metalloliposomes are formulated using 1,2-dipalmitoyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine, DSPE-PEG2000, and the iridium(III) complexes. Notably, the incorporation
of the complexes into the rigid bilayer structure of the liposomes
enables effective shielding of the excited states from interaction
with O2 and partial suppression of nonradiative decay,
resulting in improved luminescence properties. These luminescent liposomes
show efficient cellular uptake and cytoplasmic localization in human
retinal pigment epithelium ARPE-19 cells. The liposomes have also
been administered into rat eyes through intravitreal injection and
imaged using optical coherence tomography and funduscopy. Luminescent
metalloliposomes derived from ruthenium(II) complexes
such as 175( ) and 176( ) have also been developed as nanocarriers
for therapeutic agents and genetic materials.
 
4.3.4.

## Graphene Oxide
Graphene has gained
significant attention in the field of nanomedicine. It is an ideal carrier for efficient drug delivery due
to its high drug loading capability and minimal cytotoxicity. Among
the different graphene-based nanomaterials, reduced graphene oxide
(rGO) has received increasing attention as it can absorb NIR light
and generate localized heat, rendering them highly effective photothermal
therapy (PTT) agents.
Tan, Mao, and co-workers have loaded a
ruthenium(II) PEG complex (177) ( ) onto rGO sheets for lysosome-targeted
imaging and phototherapy. The rGO sheets
function as both a carrier to deliver the complex to cancer cells
and a photothermal transduction agent to convert light to heat for
PTT. The release of the complex from the rGO sheets is pH-sensitive
and accelerated under acidic conditions (pH = 5) due to the protonation/deprotonation
of carboxyl groups on rGO. The resultant nanohybrid is efficiently
taken up by live A549 cells and enriched in the lysosomes, facilitating
the release of the complex. The nanohybrid is also compatible with
photothermal imaging, showing that it specifically accumulates at
the tumor site of mice bearing A549 tumor xenografts after subcutaneous
injection. Furthermore, since the nanohybrid exhibits photothermal
effect and 1O2 generation upon irradiation at
808 and 450 nm, respectively, it effectively inhibits the tumor growth
through a combined PTT–PDT treatment.
 
4.3.5.

## Carbon Dots
Carbon dots (Cdots)
are luminescent nanomaterials with particle sizes of less than 10
nm. These nanomaterials show intrinsic
upconversion luminescence (UCL) properties, enabling the use of NIR-light
irradiation for imaging or therapeutic purposes. Additionally, they
display excellent photostability, high aqueous solubility and biocompatibility,
and ease of surface modification.
Wu, Zhu, and co-workers have
developed a facile approach for the preparation of ruthenium(II)-containing
Cdots using an amine-modified ruthenium(II) complex (178) ( ) and
citric acid as the starting materials. The ruthenium(II)-modified Cdots exhibit high solubility and stability
in aqueous solution and show intense red phosphorescence (Φem = 0.21, τ =
0.65 μs) upon photoexcitation with NIR light. These remarkable
properties have enabled the application of the modified Cdots as probes
for bioimaging in live HeLa cells and zebrafish embryos. Additionally,
the modified Cdots induce DNA cleavage upon irradiation through the
generation of ROS, highlighting their potential as PDT agents.
 
4.3.6.

## Silica Nanoparticles
Silica nanoparticles
(SiNPs), particularly mesoporous silica nanoparticles (MSNs), have
attracted considerable interest due to their straightforward synthesis,
tunable pore size, high surface area, large pore volume, and facile
surface modification. Furthermore, SiNPs
display high biocompatibility and stability in cellular environments,
making them promising candidates for various biomedical applications.
Besic Gyenge and co-workers have examined the cellular uptake and
intracellular behavior of a series of [Ru(bpy)3]2+-encapsulating SiNPs with distinct surface modifications in head
and neck squamous cell carcinoma. Bimodal
imaging with LSCM and TEM shows that both the positively and negatively
charged SiNPs are efficiently internalized into human head and neck
squamous cell carcinoma UMB-SCC 745 cells and preferentially enriched
in the membrane-bound vesicles in the cytoplasm. Notably, the SiNPs
are propagated during cell division, allowing them to be traced over
five cell passages without significant impact on cell morphology and
viability. The long intracellular retention of these SiNPs can be
exploited to improve cancer diagnosis and treatment strategies. Huang
and co-workers have utilized [Ru(bpy)3]2+ in
the development of aptamer-labeled luminescent SiNPs for specific
imaging of cellular prion protein. The
ruthenium(II) complex is incorporated into the SiNPs using the reverse
microemulsion method. The SiNPs are then labeled with an aptamer that
binds to prion protein with high affinity and selectivity. This modification
allows the luminescent SiNPs to selectively stain human neuroblastoma
SK-N-SH cells that express high levels of prion protein on their cell
surface, while exhibiting negligible staining of HeLa cells with minimal
prion protein expression. Che and co-workers have incorporated a platinum(II)
pincer complex (179) ( ) into MCM-41-type MSNs for two-photon luminescence
imaging of cancer cells. The complex
shows favorable photophysical properties such as good TPA and intense
emission (Φem = 0.99); however,
its poor aqueous solubility due to the hydrophobic pincer ligand has
limited its biological applications. The incorporation of the complex
into MSNs allows it to enter live HeLa cells for cellular imaging
with LSCM, showing that the complex-loaded MSNs are entrapped in the
endosomes following their uptake via endocytosis.
 Yuan, Guan, and co-workers have prepared luminescent
SiNPs using
a ruthenium(II) complex modified with three alkoxysilyl units (180) ( ). The alkoxysilyl moiety enables the
covalent immobilization of the complex in the silica matrix of the
SiNPs, minimizing its leakage. The covalent conjugation also results
in a significant increase in the emission lifetime of the complex
(from ca. 0.77 to 2.28 μs), making the SiNPs
suitable for time-gated luminescence imaging. The luminescent SiNPs
have been conjugated to streptavidin and utilized for the detection
of Giardia lamblia. Menu and co-workers have incorporated
an alkoxysilyl-modified ruthenium(II) complex (181) into
SiNPs of various sizes and surface modifications to examine their
penetration and distribution in Pseudomonas aeruginosa biofilms. As revealed by LSCM, the
hydrophilic SiNPs penetrate into the biofilm regardless of their sizes,
with smaller SiNPs displaying greater diffusion compared to the larger
ones; whereas the hydrophobic SiNPs preferentially accumulate at the
periphery of the biofilm. These findings suggest that the spatial
distribution of SiNPs in bacterial biofilms is more influenced by
their hydrophilicity/hydrophobicity rather than their size. Zhao and
co-workers have utilized a similar approach to immobilize iridium(III)
complexes (182) in MSNs. The surface of the MSNs is then modified with folic acid for targeting
cancer cells. The resultant MSNs exhibit efficient cellular uptake
and cytoplasmic localization in live HeLa cells that overexpress FRs.
Larráyoz, García-Martínez, Berenguer,
and co-workers have developed a one-pot synthetic approach for the
preparation of well-defined luminescent MSNs with different surface
functionalizations. This method utilizes
an alkoxysilyl-modified iridium(III) complex (183) as
the precursor, which not only allows for efficient and homogeneous
incorporation of the complex into the silica matrix but also enables in situ surface functionalization of the MSNs. The resultant
MSNs show spherical structures with high specific surface area (up
to 1,120 m2 g–1) and enhanced emission
compared to MSNs with the iridium(III) complex grafted onto their
surface. These MSNs are internalized as aggregates into live A549
and HeLa cells and enriched in the cytoplasmic region.
 Zhao, Shi, Huang, and co-workers have developed
a luminescent nanoprobe
by immobilizing alkoxysilyl-modified iridium(III) (184a) and europium(III) complexes (184b) ( ) in porous SiNPs. The appropriate energy gap between the T1 state of the iridium(III) complex and the 5D0 level of Eu3+ enables efficient energy transfer,
resulting in intense europium(III)-based luminescence in aqueous solution
upon visible-light excitation. The resultant nanoprobe is efficiently
internalized into live HeLa cells via an energy-dependent pathway,
resulting in intense intracellular emission in the cytoplasmic region
with a high signal-to-noise ratio of up to 400. Additionally, the
long lifetime of lanthanide luminescence has enabled time-gated luminescence
imaging with a time delay of 1,500 ns, effectively eliminating interference
from the short-lived autofluorescence background. Sábio and
co-workers have developed a dual-emissive nanoprobe by covalently
immobilizing an alkoxysilyl-modified heterobimetallic ruthenium(II)–ytterbium(III)
complex (185) onto the surface of SiNPs. Upon photoexcitation at 455 nm, the nanoprobe
exhibits both ruthenium(II)- and ytterbium(III)-based luminescence
centered at ca. 610 and 980 nm, respectively. The
nanoprobe has been used for luminescence imaging of murine melanoma
B16-F10 cells and neonatal HDF cells, demonstrating their efficient
cellular uptake in both cell lines. Shi, Li, and co-workers have utilized
an in situ coordination approach to prepare europium(III)-based
luminescent NPs. The in situ coordination reaction between the precursor iridium(III) complex
and Eu3+ yields a water-insoluble heterobimetallic iridium(III)–europium(III)
complex (186) within MSNs, significantly increasing the
loading efficiency compared to physical adsorption. These NPs show
intense europium(III)-based red luminescence in aqueous solution with
a high quantum yield (Φem = 0.16)
and a long lifetime (τ = 904.7 μs), facilitating
their use for luminescence imaging of live HeLa cells and lymphatic
imaging in live mice. The radioactive 153Sm3+ can also be incorporated into the NPs due to its similar atomic
radius and chemical properties to Eu3+, allowing for single-photon
emission computed tomography (SPECT)/CT imaging.
 Lemercier, Gary-Bobo, Cunin, and co-workers have
conjugated an
alkyne-appended ruthenium(II) complex (187) ( ) to porous SiNPs
for two-photon-induced luminescence imaging and PDT of cancer cells. The complex is covalently attached to the surface
of the SiNPs through hydrosilylation of its terminal alkyne moiety
with the Si–H groups on the surface of the SiNPs. The surface
of the SiNPs is also modified with PEG and mannose moieties to enhance
their biocompatibility and cancer-targeting ability, respectively.
The modified SiNPs are efficiently taken up by live MCF-7 cells and
effectively induce cancer cell death upon two-photon excitation (λex = 800 nm). Li, Li, and co-workers have
modified fluorescent SiNPs with a carboxyl-bearing ruthenium(II) complex
(188) for NIR light-mediated cancer imaging and PDT. The SiNPs display UCL at 490 nm upon NIR-light
irradiation; thus, modification of the SiNPs with the ruthenium(II)
complex allows them to exhibit intense red phosphorescence and generate 1O2 upon photoexcitation at 800 nm due to FRET from
the SiNPs to the complex. The SiNPs have been further modified with
folic acid on the surface to enable selective targeting of FR-overexpressing
HeLa cells both in vitro and in vivo. Remarkably, the SiNPs induce a remarkable decrease in HeLa cell
viability upon irradiation, highlighting their potential as cancer
theranostics.
 
4.3.7.

## Polyhedral Oligomeric Silsesquioxanes
Polyhedral oligomeric silsesquioxanes (POSSs) are nanosized materials
characterized by their cage-like structure and well-defined chemical
formula (RSiO1.5)n (n = 8, 10, 12). Among them,
cubic octasilsesquioxane is the most extensively
studied due to its high symmetry and ease of modification with various
corner functional groups. In addition to their common advantages shared
with dendrimers such as multiple functionalities and 3D macromolecular
scaffolds, POSSs possess unique properties including high thermal
stability, rigidity, and hydrophobicity. These distinctive characteristics
make them highly versatile as building blocks for innovative hybrid
nanocomposites, supramolecular assemblies, and functional materials.
Tang, Lo, and co-workers have developed a series of luminescent
molecular hybrids (189) ( ) through grafting eight iridium(III) complexes
onto a POSS unit. The attachment of
the iridium(III) complexes to a POSS unit renders them AIE-active,
showing increased emission intensities upon increasing water fraction.
The modification also substantially reduces the dark cytotoxic activity
and photocytotoxicity of the complexes. Live-cell imaging shows that
these octanuclear complexes are internalized into live HeLa cells
via caveolae-mediated endocytosis and enriched in the cytoplasm, in
contrast to their POSS-free counterparts that specifically accumulate
in the mitochondria. Molecular hybrids composed of an iridium(III)
core and a POSS pendant (190) have also been developed. These complexes display distinctive internalization
mechanisms compared to their POSS-free counterparts and target different
intracellular organelles such as the mitochondria, LDs, and lysosomes
( ). In particular,
the bipyridine complex 190a is taken up by HeLa cells
via a pathway involving both clathrin-mediated endocytosis and macropinocytosis
and enriched in the mitochondria, whereas its POSS-free counterpart
is internalized into the cells through passive diffusion and is enriched
in the ER. Additionally, the POSS complexes are relatively noncytotoxic
in the dark, but exhibit high photocytotoxic activity under both normoxia
and hypoxia due to their capabilities to generate both 1O2 and superoxide anion radical (O2•−) upon irradiation. These findings indicate that the conjugation
of a POSS unit to transition metal complexes can modulate their cellular
uptake, organelle specificity, and (photo)cytotoxicity.
 
4.3.8.

## Nanoscale Metal–Organic Frameworks
and Nanoscale Coordination Polymers
Nanoscale metal–organic
frameworks (NMOFs) and nanoscale coordination polymers (NCPs) are
hybrid nanomaterials constructed through the coordination of metal
ions or clusters to organic linkers. Generally, NMOFs are referred to as porous crystalline nanomaterials,
while NCPs are considered as amorphous NPs. The high chemical flexibility
and synthetic tunability of these nanomaterials allow for facile manipulation
of their properties such as size, shape, and loading capacities. Additionally,
the relatively labile metal–ligand coordination bonds endow
the nanomaterials with intrinsic biodegradability, making them attractive
for biomedical applications.
Lee and co-workers have incorporated
[Ru(bpy)3]2+ into zirconium-based UiO-67 NMOFs
to develop a theranostic nanoplatform for two-photon-induced imaging
and PDT. Loading of the complex into
NMOFs substantially enhances the emission quantum yield and lifetime,
TPA cross-section, 1O2 generation efficiency,
and photostability of the complex. The modified NMOFs also show high
water dispersibility and stability in biologically relevant media.
Two-photon luminescence imaging of live A549 cells shows that the
luminescent NMOFs are internalized into the cells via endocytosis
and enriched in the cytoplasm. Furthermore, these NMOFs display high
biocompatibility in the dark and induce significant cell death upon
white-light irradiation due to 1O2 generation.
Lin and co-workers have developed luminescent NCPs using Zr2+ as the connecting point and a luminescent dicarboxylic acid-modified
ruthenium(II) complex (191) ( ) as the bridging unit. The NCPs are further stabilized with a silica coating functionalized
with PEG and anisamide as a tumor-targeting unit. Live-cell imaging
shows that the resultant nanomaterials exhibit efficient uptake in
human non-small-cell lung cancer H460 cells, highlighting the potential
of functionalized NCPs for targeted imaging and drug delivery.
 
4.3.9.

## Gold Nanoparticles
Gold nanoparticles
(AuNPs) are widely used in biological and medical applications due
to their advantageous properties such as high biocompatibility and
optical absorption tunability. Luminescent
labeling of AuNPs allows for multimodal detection, combining optical
signatures and gold-based density detection. Additionally, AuNPs can
serve as an excellent scaffold for sensing applications, offering
stability, tunability, and multifunctionalization
for selective binding and detection of targets. These interesting
properties make them valuable tools in biomedical research and diagnostics.
Williams, Gunnlaugsson, and co-workers reported on the first application
of ruthenium(II) complex-functionalized AuNPs as cellular imaging
reagents. The ruthenium(II) complexes
(192) ( ) are modified with a terminal alkyl thiol group to facilitate
their conjugation to the surface of AuNPs. The AuNPs are synthesized
and functionalized with the ruthenium(II) complexes using the two-phase
Brust–Schiffrin method, which gives rise to small, spherical
NPs with an average diameter of 3–4 nm. Monitoring the uptake
of the AuNPs in HeLa cells using LSCM and TEM shows that the AuNPs
localize in both the cytoplasm and nucleus. The ruthenium(II) complexes
have also been tethered to AuNPs with a larger diameter of ca. 15–20 nm. The larger
size of the AuNPs allows their cellular uptake to be followed by TEM,
which shows that these AuNPs accumulate in single membrane vesicles
in the cytoplasm. This, together with the LSCM images, shows that
the AuNPs are internalized into the cells via endocytosis and migrate
to the nuclear membrane over time. The ruthenium(II) complexes have
also been conjugated to silver nanoparticles (AgNPs) to develop lysosome-targeting
theranostic agents. The AuNPs can also
be functionalized using binuclear ruthenium(II) complexes that are
linked with a disulfide bond (193). The resultant nanoconjugates show higher retention in HeLa
cells than the free complexes after cellular internalization.
 Hodges, Pikramenou, and co-workers have demonstrated
the coating
of AuNPs of a diameter of ca. 100 nm with ca. 105 ruthenium(II) complexes. The nanoconjugates are prepared using a ruthenium(II)
complex that contains two thiol groups (194) ( ) for attachment
to the AuNP surface that is precoated with a nonionic fluorinated
