surfactant. The use of a surfactant enables a high loading of metal
complexes on AuNPs of different sizes while inhibiting flocculation
induced by the cationic complexes. The high loading of complex has
allowed luminescence imaging of the interaction of the NPs with nuclear
chromatin in A549 cells at single-nanoparticle resolution. Furthermore,
the luminescence properties of the complexes have allowed quantification
and tracking of the cellular uptake and localization of the AuNPs
in cells. Bimodal imaging with LSCM and TEM reveals that the complex 195-labeled AuNPs are enriched in the early endosomes after
uptake, and at later time points, in the lysosomes and autophagosomes. This accumulation leads to organelle swelling
and breakdown of the surrounding membranes, resulting in the endosomal
release of the NPs into the cytoplasm without inducing detectable
cytotoxicity. Pikramenou and co-workers have also exploited the characteristic
emission lifetimes of an iridium(III) complex (196) and
AuNPs for multiphoton lifetime imaging with multichannel detection. The TPA cross-section of the complex (δ760 nm = up to 2 × 103 GM) is significantly enhanced by the surface plasmon resonance of
AuNPs. The intense two-photon-induced emission has facilitated the
use of two-photon excitation FLIM and PLIM to monitor the surface
plasmon resonance of the AuNPs and the emission of the iridium(III)
complex, which are in the tens of picosecond and hundreds of nanosecond
range, respectively. Additionally, the long-lived iridium(III) emission
is sensitive to the cellular environment, providing local information
that can be used to examine biomolecular interactions. The AuNPs mainly
reside in the cytoplasm of the cells after cellular uptake, with some
of them also accumulating in the nucleus. Related AuNPs coated with
an iridium(III) complex (197) have been used for the
visualization and monitoring of microvascular blood flow. 
 La Deda and co-workers have encapsulated transition
metal complexes
into gold–silica nanoparticles (GSNPs) using a one-pot microemulsion
method. The GSNPs doped with a hydroxy-containing
ruthenium(II) complex (198) ( ) exhibit efficient uptake and cytoplasmic
localization in cancer cells. Remarkably, the GSNPs show a larger
increase in cytotoxicity upon irradiation compared to the free complex,
highlighting their enhanced therapeutic potential.
 
4.3.10.

## Upconversion Nanoparticles
Upconversion
luminescence (UCL) is an anti-Stokes process that emits high-energy
photons by absorbing two or more low-energy photons. It can be achieved through two main approaches: lanthanide-based
upconversion and triplet−triplet annihilation (TTA)-based upconversion.
Lanthanide-based upconversion requires high excitation intensity and
suffers from low upconversion efficiencies in aqueous solution due
to the low absorption of lanthanide ions and water quenching. On the
other hand, TTA-based upconversion involves energy transfer from a
sensitizer with high molar absorptivity in the biological windows
to an annihilator, enabling efficient upconversion even under low
excitation intensity.
Li and co-workers have developed water-soluble
TTA-based upconversion nanoparticles (UCNPs) by embedding PdOEP (H2OEP = octaethylporphyrin) (199) ( ) and 9,10-diphenylanthracene into
SiNPs. Upon selective photoexcitation
of PdOEP (λex = 532 nm), the UCNPs
display blue UCL at 433 nm (Φem =
0.045) in aqueous solution, originating from 9,10-diphenylanthracene
via TTA. The efficient UCL has allowed imaging in live HeLa cells
with negligible background interference. The UCNPs have also been
used for real-time UCL imaging of lymph node in live Kunming mice
with a high signal-to-noise ratio at low excitation power density
(8.5 mW cm–2). Nagai, Siegwart, and co-workers have
developed a UCL system (200) using cellulose as a template. The sensitizer, PtTPP (H2TPP = meso-tetraphenylporphyrin),
and the emitter, rhodamine B, are functionalized with an alkyne moiety
for conjugation to the azide-modified cellulose using the CuAAC click
reaction. The attachment of PtTPP on cellulose induces the formation
of J-aggregates, giving rise to a broad absorption band in the NIR
region (λabs = ca. 800 nm). Selective excitation of the J-aggregate absorption band
with NIR light (λex = 850 nm) results
in orange fluorescence (λem = 595
nm) with a large anti-Stokes’ shift (> 250 nm). This system
has been used for tumor imaging in vitro and in vivo through formulation into NPs. Li and co-workers
have examined the in vivo biodistribution and toxicity
of TTA-based upconversion nanocapsules (UCNCs), which are prepared
by coloading PtTPTBP (H2TPTBP = meso-tetraphenyl-tetrabenzoporphyrin)
(201) and a BODIPY derivative (BODIPY-TPS) into BSA-stabilized soybean oil droplets. The UCNCs exhibit excellent stability under physiological conditions
with no apparent leakage of the dyes, and show efficient UCL at 558
nm upon excitation at 635 nm. Whole-body UCL imaging shows that the
UCNCs are mainly enriched in the liver and spleen of mice after intravenous
injection, and rapidly excreted from the mice within 24 h. Importantly,
analyses on the body weight, histology, hematology, and blood biochemistry
of the mice indicate that the UCNCs display no apparent toxicity at
a dose as high as 1,200 mg kg–1 over a period of
60 days. Nevertheless, TTA-based upconversion suffers from triplet
excited state quenching by O2, which leads to the loss
of UCL. Bonnet and co-workers have demonstrated the use of biocompatible
antioxidants to reduce the O2 sensitivity of TTA-based
UCNPs. The UCNPs are prepared by doping
PdTPTBP and 2,5,8,11-tetra(tert-butyl)perylene
into the hydrophobic membrane of polymersomes derived from an amphiphilic
block copolymer poly(isobutylene)-block-poly(ethylene
glycol). The UCNPs can efficiently convert red light into blue light
under aerobic conditions in the presence of O2 scavengers.
 

5.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes for Autofluorescence-Free
Imaging
Luminescence imaging has offered exciting opportunities
to study
biological processes at the cellular level with unprecedented spatial
and temporal resolution. However, conventional imaging techniques
rely on external light sources for real-time excitation of the probes,
restricting the effectiveness of luminescence imaging in vivo due to the unavoidable background autofluorescence and limited light
penetration depth in biological tissues. Thus, there has been emerging
interest in autofluorescence-free imaging modalities that utilize
self-illuminating probes to eliminate the need for real-time excitation. This approach allows for the sensitive detection
of biological events in deep tissues with minimal background interference,
overcoming the challenges associated with conventional luminescence
imaging techniques.
5.1.

## Cherenkov Luminescence Imaging
Cherenkov
luminescence imaging is an imaging technique that utilizes the Cherenkov
radiation emitted from high-energy charged particles during the decay
of clinical radionuclides (e.g., 18F, 64Cu, 68Ga, 89Zr, 90Y, and 131I) in biological tissues as an internal excitation source. This self-illuminating phenomenon eliminates
the need for real-time external excitation, enabling in vivo luminescence imaging with a high signal-to-noise ratio.
Wang,
Yang, Yang, and co-workers have utilized 18F-FDG as an
internal radioactive source to excite an iridium(III) complex (202) ( ) for radiopharmaceutical-excited phosphorescence imaging (REPI). The iridium(III) complex acts as a transducer
to convert the UV-to-blue Cherenkov radiation emitted by 18F-FDG into orange-red phosphorescence. This method overcomes the
limited penetration depth of Cherenkov radiation, allowing background-free
imaging with improved penetration depth in vivo.
As shown in , REPI has enabled in vivo tumor imaging with a
significantly improved signal-to-noise ratio compared to traditional
phosphorescence imaging and Cherenkov luminescence imaging.
 
5.2.

## Chemiluminescence Imaging
Chemiluminescence
is a phenomenon in which light is generated as a result of chemical
reactions. Since chemiluminescence does
not require an external excitation source, it does not suffer from
interference caused by autofluorescence or light scattering, and thus
it can offer a high signal-to-noise ratio and deep imaging depth that
are difficult to achieve with conventional luminescence imaging techniques.
Common chemiluminescent substrates such as luminol, Cypridina luciferin analogues, and peroxyoxalates generate light following
reaction with an oxidant. In contrast, Schaap’s dioxetanes
undergo a chemically initiated electron exchange luminescence (CIEEL)
process via phenol deprotection, yielding an electronically excited
benzoate ester that emits light upon returning to the ground state.
The ease of incorporating various stimuli-responsive moieties as a
protecting group for the phenol allows Schaap’s dioxetanes
to detect a wide range of analytes in living systems. Lippert and co-workers have developed chemiluminescent
iridium(III) complexes (203) ( ) by conjugating the iridium(III) polypyridine
core with an acrylic acid-modified 1,2-dioxetane via a piperazine
linker. Upon CIEEL under physiological
conditions (pH = 7.4), chemiluminescence resonance energy transfer
(CRET) takes place from the chemiexcited benzoate unit to the O2-sensitive iridium(III) polypyridine core, enabling excitation-free,
ratiometric O2 sensing in vitro and in vivo ( ). Two related chemiluminescent iridium(III) complexes (204) have also been developed, with complex 204b displaying an unusual sulfur ligation. The direct conjugation of the 1,2-dioxetane scaffold to iridium(III)
complexes allows more efficient energy transfer, giving rise to single
red chemiluminescence. The cationic complex 204b has
been used for in vivo chemiluminescence imaging due
to its higher aqueous solubility.
 Transition metal complexes such as [Ru(bpy)3]2+ can generate chemiluminescence upon reduction
reactions. Eppinger, Reiner, and co-workers
have utilized
[Ru(bpy)3]2+ as a chemiluminescent agent for
intraoperative chemiluminescence imaging in live mice. In the presence of an oxidizing agent such
as ceric ammonium nitrate, [Ru(bpy)3]2+ is oxidized
to [Ru(bpy)3]3+, which can then be reduced by
biological tissues or serum to [Ru(bpy)3]2+*,
resulting in the characteristic red phosphorescence upon returning
to the ground state. Biodistribution studies with intraoperative chemiluminescence
imaging reveal that the complex primarily accumulates in the kidneys
and liver, suggesting renal clearance as the major elimination pathway.

5.3.

## Afterglow Luminescence Imaging
Afterglow
luminescence imaging has emerged as a promising technique for in vivo imaging. It monitors
the persistent luminescence emitted from chemical defects after the
cessation of light irradiation. This imaging approach effectively
reduces autofluorescence interference from biological tissues, enabling
deep tissue imaging with high detection sensitivity.
Typically,
afterglow luminescent probes consist of three components: a 1O2 photosensitizer as an afterglow initiator, a 1O2-reactive molecule as an afterglow substrate, and a
luminophore as an afterglow emitter. However, the physical mixing
of these components often leads to inefficient intermolecular energy
transfer, resulting in weak afterglow luminescence. Liu, Xu, Li, and
co-workers have leveraged both the luminescence and 1O2-photosensitization behavior of iridium(III) complexes to
design a unimolecular afterglow luminescent probe (205) ( ) for
autofluorescence-free imaging in vivo. The probe features an iridium(III) complex
conjugated with the afterglow substrate 1,4-oxathiin, serving as both
an afterglow initiator and an afterglow emitter. Upon short-term irradiation,
the iridium(III) moiety sensitizes the production of 1O2, oxidizing the 1,4-oxathiin unit to form an unstable 1,2-dioxetane
derivative that rapidly decomposes to yield an electronically excited
dicarbonyl species. This CIEEL process activates the iridium(III)
moiety via intramolecular CRET, giving rise to iridium(III) emission
persisting for ca. 3 s after ceasing the excitation
light source. The complex has been encapsulated into Pluronic F127
for in vivo imaging, realizing autofluorescence-free
imaging with a signal-to-noise ratio of up to 189 ( ).
 

6.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes for Multimodal
Imaging
While luminescence imaging is a powerful technique
for direct visualization
of cells and subcellular structures with high resolution, it poses
challenges in providing quantitative information due to its high sensitivity
to light scattering, instrumental parameters such as laser power,
and local concentration and environment of the probes. Additionally,
luminescence imaging in biological tissues is limited by the penetration
depth. Thus, the combination of luminescence imaging and other molecular
imaging modalities with complementary advantages can allow biological
imaging at increased tissue penetration depth while maintaining high
sensitivity and spatial resolution, improving disease diagnosis and
treatment. 
6.1.

## Vibrational Imaging
Vibrational imaging
is a label-free and noninvasive method for direct visualization of
molecular species inside cells through probing the inherent vibrations
of chemical bonds in forms of infrared (IR) absorption and Raman scattering.
This imaging approach enables the mapping of different molecular species
such as nucleic acids, proteins, and lipids that are characterized
by a specific set of chemical bonds without the need for exogenous
labels, offering opportunities to elucidate their intricate interactions
in the complex biological milieu. 
Rhenium(I) tricarbonyl complexes are promising probes for IR imaging
due to their characteristic C≡O absorption bands at ca. 1,920 (antisymmetric stretching, E band) and 2,020 cm–1 (symmetric stretching, A1 band), a specific
region that is free from interference caused by other functional groups
commonly found in biological molecules. , Policar and co-workers have designed a luminescent rhenium(I) tricarbonyl
complex featuring a 4-(2-pyridyl)-1,2,3-triazole ligand (206) ( ) as the
first single-core multimodal probe for imaging (SCoMPI) that can be
visualized inside cells using both fluorescence microscopy and synchrotron
radiation Fourier transform IR (SR-FTIR) microspectroscopy. The mapping of the E and A1 bands
of the complex with the asymmetric stretching of phosphate at 1,240
cm–1, one of the IR signatures of the cell nucleus,
indicates the enrichment of the complex in the perinuclear region
of MDA-MB-231 cells ( ). These observations coincide with those obtained in luminescence
imaging. Further colocalization studies confirm that the complex specifically
accumulates in the Golgi apparatus. This bimodal imaging approach
has also been used to examine the effect of alkyl chain lengths on
the cellular uptake and cytotoxicity of rhenium(I)-based SCoMPIs (207). Quantification of the
cellular uptake of the complexes based on their characteristic IR
absorption reveals that a longer chain can enhance the cellular permeation
and cytotoxic activity of the complexes. The cellular uptake of complex 207c in live MDA-MB-231 cells has been monitored in real time
using a microfluidic device to limit water absorption while keeping
the cells alive, showing that the intracellular concentration of the
complex reaches a plateau within 1 h. This lipophilic complex has also been incorporated into bicosomes
to promote its skin penetration for dermatological applications. A related rhenium(I) 1-(2-pyridyl)-1,2,3-triazole
complex (208) has also been developed for cellular imaging. This complex displays stronger emission compared
to its 4-(2-pyridyl)-1,2,3-triazole counterpart, facilitating its
sensitive detection in cells.
 Policar and co-workers have conjugated a SCoMPI
to mestranol, a
prodrug of 17α-ethynylestradiol and an estrogen component of
some oral contraceptive formulations, to monitor its uptake and localization in two breast cancer cell
lines (MDA-MB-231 and MCF-7 cells) using a correlative multimodal
imaging approach. The resultant conjugate
(209) ( ) enters cells via an active transport mechanism that involves
mestranol recognition, and specifically localizes in the Golgi apparatus
after uptake. Hyaluronic acids (HAs), a natural glycosaminoglycan
that can be used as a dermal filler to reduce the appearance of facial
wrinkles, have also been modified with a SCoMPI to examine their penetration
into human skin biopsies. Bimodal imaging
of the resulting conjugates (210) shows that the skin
penetration of HAs is dependent on their size and confined to the
upper layer of the skin. Additionally, SCoMPIs have been used to label
a nonaarginine peptide to study its interaction with membranes both in vitro and in live cells. Conjugate 211a exhibits substantially higher emission
intensity in the presence of lipid vesicles compared to conjugate 211b due to the different sensitivity of the SCoMPIs to their
local environment. Thus, incubation of CHO cells with conjugate 211a leads to more intense intracellular emission than with
conjugate 211b. The comparable intracellular IR signals
observed from the conjugates suggest that their concentrations are
similar within cells. A related nonaarginine conjugate (211c) has been utilized for imaging of human skin biopsies using the
bimodal approach. Quantification of
the conjugate in skin biopsies indicates that its transdermal penetration
is time-dependent and the diffusion of the conjugate occurs via the
extracellular matrix. Furthermore, Zobi and co-workers have investigated
the uptake and localization of a rhenium(I)–cyanocobalamin
conjugate (212) in 3T3 cells using FTIR spectromicroscopy. The conjugate is unsuitable for luminescence
imaging as its emission is quenched by FRET from the rhenium(I) core
to the cyanocobalamin moiety. However, the IR signatures of the rhenium(I)
core allow the examination of its cellular localization by FTIR spectromicroscopy,
which shows that the conjugate resides in the cytoplasm in close proximity
to the nucleus but not to lipid-rich organelles.
 
6.2.

## X-Ray Fluorescence Imaging
Synchrotron
radiation X-ray fluorescence (SXRF) imaging is a noninvasive imaging
technique that allows for the quantitative determination of trace
elements in biological samples. This
method enables the simultaneous mapping of multiple elements with
high accuracy and specificity, providing valuable insights into the
concentration and distribution of both essential and exogenous metals
in cells and tissues.
Rhenium(I) complexes are suitable for
SXRF imaging due to the low natural abundance of rhenium, which results
in a high signal-to-noise ratio. The rhenium center shows two characteristic
signals at ca. 8.6 and 10.28 keV, corresponding to
its L-bands. Harris, Massi, and co-workers have examined the cellular
uptake and localization properties of a luminescent rhenium(I) tetrazolato
complex (213) ( ) using epifluorescence and SXRF microscopy. The iodo substituent is introduced onto the
axial tetrazole ligand to investigate the stability of the complex
in the intracellular environment via elemental mapping. The strong
colocalization of rhenium and iodine in the XRF elemental distribution
map confirms that the tetrazole ligand remains bound to the rhenium
center within the cellular context after uptake. The complex is preferentially
enriched in the perinuclear region but not inside the nucleus, based
on the high cell-to-nuclear content ratios for both rhenium and iodine.
Additionally, quantitative measurements of cellular elemental contents
suggest that the complex disrupts the homeostasis of several biologically
relevant elements such as selenium, chlorine, zinc, and potassium.
Wilson and co-workers have utilized a similar approach to examine
the intracellular stability of a rhenium(I) isonitrile complex that
induces cancer cell death via ER stress. 
 
6.3.

## Nuclear Imaging
Nuclear imaging is
a medical technique that utilizes a radioactive tracer to observe
structures deep inside tissues. Technetium-99m is the most commonly
used radioisotope in clinical diagnosis, as it is a pure γ-emitter
(Eγ = 140 keV) with a half-life
of 6 h. This half-life is long enough
for the preparation of radiopharmaceuticals and in vivo accumulation in target tissues for image acquisition, while also
being short enough to minimize radiation dose to the patient. These
characteristics make technetium-99m ideal for nuclear medical scans
that use SPECT to detect gamma rays.
Since technetium-99m is
a congener of rhenium, both of them share similar coordination chemistry,
allowing for the use of the same set of ligand scaffolds to prepare
isostructural complexes with similar physical and chemical properties.
By combining the use of these compounds, it is possible to bridge
the gap between cellular imaging and in vivo imaging,
obtaining information on the cellular uptake, intracellular localization, in vivo biodistribution, and pharmacokinetics of the complexes.
Zubieta, Valliant, and co-workers have designed a bifunctional single
amino acid chelate using a Lys derivative. The chelate contains: (1) a tridentate bis(2-quinolylmethyl)amine
ligand at the side chain for coordination to the [M(CO)3]+ core to prepare stable rhenium(I) and technetium-99m
complexes; and (2) a Fmoc protecting group at the N-terminus that
can be cleaved for incorporation into short peptide sequences during
solid-phase peptide synthesis. The resultant luminescent rhenium(I)
conjugate (214) ( ) displays both ligand-centered fluorescence (λem = 425 nm) and metal-based phosphorescence
(λem = 580 nm), and has been conjugated
with the peptide fMLF to target the formyl peptide receptors (FPRs)
expressed on neutrophils for monitoring the trafficking of white blood
cells. Luminescence imaging shows that conjugate 214 localizes
in the cytoplasm of human leukocytes after uptake. The conjugate has
also been modified with the CPP GRKKRRQRRR derived from the Tat48–57 domain to enhance its cellular uptake. The resulting peptide conjugate (215a) mainly resides in the cell nucleus, with significant association
with the nuclear envelope. Its radioactive technetium-99m counterpart
(215b) has also been prepared and utilized to label neurospheres
and monitor cell transplantation in mice.
 Valliant and co-workers have developed isostructural
luminescent
and nuclear probes based on the [M(N∧N)(CO)3(L)]+ scaffold, which allows for the facile preparation
of the complexes in high yields using a two-step procedure. Structure–property relationship analysis
indicates that the plasma stability of the technetium-99m complexes
increases with the basicity of the monodentate ligand. By using N-alkylated imidazole
derivatives as the monodentate ligand, stable rhenium(I) and technetium-99m
complexes (216–218) ( ) have been developed for
luminescence and nuclear imaging. As
revealed by SPECT/CT imaging, the sulfonate complex 216b is rapidly excreted through the kidneys and bladder with negligible
liver uptake, while the bisphosphonate complexes 217b and 218b are enriched in regions of high calcium turnover
such as the knee and shoulder joints. However, complex 217b exhibits more significant liver accumulation compared to complex 218b, which is attributed to the dissociation of the monodentate
imidazole ligand. Related rhenium(I) and technetium-99m complexes
(219) have been prepared using bathophenanthroline
disulfonate (bpds) as the bidentate ligand to reduce nonspecific
binding in vivo. Luminescence imaging indicates that
the rhenium(I) complex 219a is predominantly distributed
in the cytoplasm of MCF-7 cells. Furthermore, SPECT/CT imaging shows
that the radioactive technetium-99m complex 219b is distributed
in the gall bladder and intestine along with the knee and shoulder
joints at 6 h postinjection ( a,c), and its accumulation in the joints and spine
is still evident at 24 h ( b,d).
 Benoist and co-workers have designed a rhenium(I)
complex featuring
a tridentate chelating iminodiacetic acid clamp for coordination to
the [M(CO)3]+ core to construct bimetallic complexes
(220) ( ) as multimodal imaging reagents. The ester group is introduced for conjugation to a targeting vector.
The homobimetallic rhenium(I) complex 220a shows efficient
uptake by A549 cells with negligible cytotoxicity (IC50 > 300 μM), highlighting the potential use of its radioactive
counterpart 220b in in vivo imaging.
 
6.4.

## Magnetic Resonance Imaging
Magnetic
resonance imaging (MRI) is a noninvasive imaging technique that produces
three dimensional anatomical images with high spatial resolution based
on differences in longitudinal (T1) and
transverse (T2) relaxation and proton
density in tissues under a magnetic field. It allows for the visualization of organs and deep-seated tissue
structures without using ionizing radiation, making it a useful tool
for medical diagnosis in clinics. However, its intrinsically low sensitivity
requires the use of contrast agents for improved detection. Positive
contrast agents reduce the T1 relaxation
time of protons to give a brighter image in T1-weighted MRI, whereas negative contrast agents shorten the T2 relaxation time of protons to produce a darker
image in T2-weighted MRI. Combining luminescence
imaging and MRI offers complementary advantages, with luminescence
imaging providing high sensitivity while MRI offering depth penetration
and spatial visualization.
Gadolinium(III) chelates are commonly
used as positive contrast agents for MRI due to the large magnetic
moment arising from the seven unpaired electrons and long electron
spin relaxation time of Gd3+. Li, Yang, and co-workers have designed a heterobimetallic iridium(III)–gadolinium(III)
complex (221) ( ) as a bimodal probe for luminescence imaging and MRI. The intense emission from the iridium(III)
unit allows the visualization of the cellular uptake and localization
of the complex using LSCM, revealing the specific accumulation of
the complex in the mitochondria. Additionally, the complex displays
high T1 relaxivity (r1 = 3.36 mM–1 s–1),
which is comparable to the gadolinium(III) chelates such as [Gd(DTPA)(OH2)]2– and [Gd(DOTA)(OH2)]− (H4DTPA = diethylenetriaminepentaacetic
acid; H4DOTA = 1,4,7,10-tetraazacyclododecane-1,4,7,10-tetraacetic
acid) (r1 = 3 to 5 mM–1 s–1) that are commonly used for clinical MRI,
facilitating its application in T1-weighted
MRI in live mice. As shown in , the complex serves as an excellent positive contrast
agent for liver imaging, highlighting its potential use for diagnosing
liver diseases. A related heterobimetallic ruthenium(II)–gadolinium(III)
complex (222) has been developed for luminescence imaging
in live cells and Daphnia magna (D. magna) and T1-weighted MRI in live mice. The complex exhibits high aqueous solubility
and biocompatibility, and more importantly, it has a long retention
time in living bodies, facilitating its use as a bimodal imaging agent
for tracking biological targets in vitro and in vivo. Ward and co-workers have designed a rigid ligand
framework for the construction of stable heteronuclear d–f
complexes (223 and 224) for dual modal imaging. − This design effectively inhibits the free rotation of Gd3+ and reduces the water exchange rates, resulting in increased relaxivities
(r1 = 6.2 to 14.0 mM–1 s–1). Monitoring the cellular uptake of these
complexes using LSCM and PLIM reveals that the complexes are efficiently
taken up by cancer cells such as HeLa and MCF-7 cells and enriched
in the perinuclear region. The conjugated ligand architecture also
facilitates efficient d → f energy transfer to sensitize the
luminescence of lanthanide ions such as Nd3+, Eu3+, and Yb3+. This gives rise to both transition metal-
and lanthanide-based luminescence under single-wavelength excitation,
which can be exploited for the development of ratiometric probes.
Pope and co-workers have also developed a water-soluble heterobimetallic
platinum(II)–gadolinium(III) complex (225) as
a bimodal imaging reagent. Notably,
the conjugation of the gadolinium(III) complex with the platinum(II)
complex results in a remarkable increase in the T1 relaxivity (r1 increases
from 3.8 to 7.1 mM–1 s–1). The
favorable luminescence and relaxivity properties of the complex render
it an excellent candidate for luminescence imaging and MRI.
 Compared to designing a single molecule that integrates
both luminescence
imaging and MRI capabilities, which typically requires multi-step
organic synthesis, the use of nanomaterials to accommodate different
metallic species enables the independent tuning of their concentrations
to optimize the imaging sensitivity for the corresponding imaging
technique. Seshadri, Prasad, and co-workers have designed a luminescent
nanomicelle for bimodal imaging of head and neck tumor xenografts. The NIR luminescent platinum(II) porphyrin
PtTPTNP is encapsulated in a phospholipid nanomicelle with the surface
being modified with gadolinium(III) chelates. The nanomicelle is small
in size (ca. 100 nm) and specifically accumulates
in the tumors due to the EPR effect. The strong absorption and emission
in the NIR window (λabs = ca. 700 nm, λem = ca. 900 nm) and high T1 relaxivity
(r1 = 10.4 mM–1 s–1) of the nanomicelle have facilitated tumor imaging
with a high optical and MR contrast, respectively. Yang, Yang, and
co-workers have developed magneto-luminescent NCPs via the self-assembly
of magnetic Gd3+ with dicarboxylic acid-functionalized
luminescent iridium(III) complexes (226 and 227) ( ). , Additionally, PVP is introduced to endow the NCPs with high dispersity,
aqueous solubility, and biocompatibility. These NCPs exhibit intense
red emission, high T1 relaxivity (r1 = 8.0 and 29.5 mM–1 s–1), efficient 1O2 photosensitization,
and rapid cellular internalization, highlighting their potential use
as a three-in-one theranostic platform for luminescence imaging, MRI,
and PDT.
 Lechevallier and co-workers have grafted gadolinium(III)
chelates
on the surface of luminescent SiNPs incorporating a silylated ruthenium(II)
complex (228) ( ) for the development of bimodal imaging agents. The content of both metallic species can be
adjusted to optimize the luminescence and magnetic properties of the
NPs for cell tracking both in vitro and in
vivo. The preferential localization of the NPs in the lysosomes
of live human colorectal carcinoma HCT-116 cells suggests the possible
involvement of an endocytic pathway in their cellular internalization.
Yi, Li, and co-workers have designed a multifunctional nanoprobe with
an iridium(III) complex (229), a gadolinium(III) chelate,
and an RGD peptide conjugated onto SiNPs for targeted luminescence
imaging and MRI. The RGD peptide allows
the nanoprobe to specifically target integrin-overexpressing cancer
cells. The more intense emission and MR signals at the tumor site
in tumor-bearing mice highlight the potential of the nanoprobe for
cancer diagnosis via bimodal luminescence imaging and MRI. Yin and
co-workers have developed MSNs that are codoped with Gd3+ and Al3+ and loaded with [Ru(bpy)3]2+ for in vivo bimodal imaging. The mesoporous structure of MSNs provides a large surface
area for loading of [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and improves
the water exchange rate, while codoping with Al3+ enhances
the loading of Gd3+ onto the MSNs. In vivo luminescence imaging and T1-weighted
MRI in Kunming mice show that the nanoprobe is enriched in the liver
and spleen after intraperitoneal or intravenous injection, and eliminated
via renal excretion. Fedorenko and co-workers have prepared SiNPs
codoped with a paramagnetic manganese(II) complex and [Ru(bpy)3]2+ as a dual contrast agent. The incorporation of the manganese(II) complex into the
shell of the SiNPs substantially enhances its magnetic relaxivity
by 10 fold, while codoping [Ru(bpy)3]2+ into
the core of SiNPs effectively minimizes the leaching of the manganese(II)
complex. The SiNPs show intense red emission and high magnetic relaxivity,
enabling the investigation of their biodistribution in mice via ex vivo luminescence imaging and in vivo MRI. Hsiao, Chou, and co-workers have developed uniform NPs comprising
a manganese oxide nanocrystal core and a mesoporous silica shell functionalized
with an iridium(III) complex (230). The worm-like nanochannels in the mesoporous silica shell
not only enable the free diffusion of O2 for effective
PDT but also increase water permeability to the interior hollow MnO
core for T1 signal enhancement. Functionalization
of the mesoporous silica shell with folic acid allows specific targeting
of cancer cells that overexpress FRs (HeLa) over those that are FR-deficient
(human pancreatic carcinoma MIA PaCa-2), enabling localized PDT of
targeted cells while minimizing collateral damage to nontargeted cells. 
 Iron oxide NPs have attracted significant attention
as negative
contrast agents for MRI due to their superparamagnetism and biocompatibility. Zeng, Sun, and co-workers have coupled a ruthenium(II)
complex (231) ( ) to Fe3O4 NPs using a block
copolymer to afford a bimodal probe for biological imaging. The polymer coating separates the ruthenium(II)
complex from the magnetic Fe3O4 NPs to minimize
emission quenching by the Fe3O4 NPs. Lu and
co-workers have developed luminescent magnetic NPs for targeted imaging
of human breast cancer. The core of
the NPs is composed of Fe3O4 and coated with
a silica shell doped with [Ru(phen)3]2+, while
the surface of the NPs is modified with the RGD peptide for specific
targeting of cancer cells overexpressing integrin αvβ3 (e.g., MDA-MB-231). The high T2 relaxivity (r2 = 61.67 mM–1 s–1) of the NPs
has enabled their use as a negative contrast agent for T2-weighted MRI. The specific tumor accumulation of the
NPs in MDA-MB-231 tumor-bearing mice after tail vein injection indicates
their potential for breast cancer detection. Xu, Luo, Ma, and co-workers
have developed a dual-modal nanoprobe based on attapulgite nanorods. The surface of the attapulgite nanorods is
loaded with Fe3O4 NPs and coated with PEI for
covalent conjugation with an aldehyde-bearing ruthenium(II) complex
(232). The nanocomposite can function as a negative contrast
agent due to its high T2 relaxivity (r2 = 120.81 mM–1 s–1) and distinguish between tumor and normal tissues in vivo due to its efficient tumor accumulation. Chou, Chi, Hsiao, and co-workers
have designed iridium(III) complex-functionalized Fe3O4/SiO2 core–shell structured NPs as a trifunctional
agent for luminescence imaging, MRI, and PDT applications. The iridium(III) complex (233)
is encapsulated within the porous SiO2 nanoshell, which
provides sufficient space for O2 diffusion for efficient 1O2 photosensitization to induce apoptosis. Maggioni
and co-workers have developed a multifunctional nanocomposite consisting
of a Fe3O4 core and a compact silica shell functionalized
with a luminescent rhenium(I) complex (234). The nanocomposite is readily taken up by A549
cells and localized in the perinuclear region, effectively inducing
cell death upon continuous irradiation.
 Paramagnetic lanthanide(III) ions such as Dy3+ and Ho3+ have been utilized to develop efficient
contrast agents
for T2-weighted MRI at high magnetic fields
due to their large magnetic moments and short electronic relaxation
time. Du, Yang, and co-workers have
developed hollow bimetallic iridium(III)–holmium(III) NCPs
for trimodal imaging and PDT. These
NCPs are prepared by the coordination assembly of an iridium(III)
dicarboxylate complex (235) ( ) and holmium acetate in the presence of
PVP. The luminescence nature and high T2 relaxivity (r2 = ca. 160 mM–1 s–1) of the NCPs allow
for luminescence imaging in live cells and T2-weighted MRI in vivo, respectively. The
NCPs are also compatible with ultrasound imaging due to their stable
cavity structure, demonstrating their potential for multimodal image-guided
PDT. Zhou, Liao, and co-workers have utilized NaDyF4 nanorods
to develop multifunctional contrast agents that enable both efficient T1- and T2-weighted
MRI. The oleic acid-coated NaDyF4 nanorods are self-assembled with α-CD for conjugation
with gadopentetic acid, and the luminescent iridium(III) complex 236 is loaded into the resultant NPs through hydrophobic interaction.
The resultant NPs display intense orange emission, good X-ray attenuation
(Hounsfield unit = 158 at 10 mg mL–1), and high T1 and T2 relaxivities
(r1 = 4.65 mM–1 s–1, r2 = 7.68 mM–1 s–1). These remarkable features have enabled luminescence
tracking of the NPs in live HeLa cells, as well as CT imaging and
both T1- and T2-weighted MRI of the tumor in mice bearing HeLa tumor xenografts,
providing complementary information that is not achievable with a
single type of contrast agent.
 

7.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Probes
for Specific Organelles
The cell serves as the fundamental
unit responsible for the structure
and function of living organisms. Eukaryotic cells are surrounded
by a plasma membrane and characterized by the presence of a nucleus
and other membrane-bound organelles such as the mitochondria, lysosomes,
ER, Golgi apparatus, and LDs in the cytoplasm, all of which are held
in place by the cytoskeleton. These organelles are essential for cellular
processes and their malfunctions are frequently linked to a range
of disorders and diseases. Thus, the development of luminescent transition
metal complexes that can target specific organelles will enhance our
understanding of their biological roles and improve therapeutic outcomes. 
7.1.

## Nucleus
In eukaryotic cells, the
nucleus is separated from the cytosol by the nuclear envelope, a double
lipid bilayer decorated with nuclear pore complexes that regulate
the transport of substances between the nucleus and the cytoplasm. , The nucleus contains most of the genetic material (i.e., DNA) of the cell and plays a crucial role in controlling gene expression
to regulate various cellular processes. 
One common approach for staining the nucleus is to target
specific biomolecules that are abundant in this organelle. Li and
co-workers have demonstrated that the iridium(III) solvato complex 57 can serve as a reaction-based nuclear stain through targeting
nuclear His or His-rich proteins. Upon
internalization into cells, the complex is rapidly enriched in the
nucleus and retained in this organelle for a long period of time (>
24 h), allowing for the long-term monitoring of nuclear dynamics in
live cells. Notably, the complex displays selective staining of the
nucleus of live cells over fixed cells, enabling the rapid and sensitive
differentiation between live and dead cells. Yang, Gao, and co-workers
have developed mono-, di-, and tetranuclear iridium(III) complexes
(237) ( ) that can stain the nucleus with two different emission colors. These complexes undergo hydrolysis in aqueous
solution, releasing the solvato complex [Ir(ppy)2(OH2)2]+ that can coordinate with His or
His-rich proteins. The iridium(III)–His coordination leads
to a change in the emission color of the complexes from orange to
green, allowing the real-time tracking of ligand release in live cells.
Monitoring the two emission channels reveals that the complexes are
taken by the cells via an energy-dependent pathway, initially localizing
in the cytoplasm and gradually migrating to the nucleus over time.
Notably, the release of ligand can be accelerated by GSH, and thus
the intracellular ligand release kinetics is faster in cancerous HeLa
cells than in normal human lymphatic fibroblast (HLF) cells due to
the more reductive intracellular environment of cancer cells. An alternative
target for staining the nucleus is nuclear DNA, which will be discussed
in a separate section (see below).
 Another effective approach to target the cell
nucleus involves
the conjugation of the complexes with a nuclear localization sequence
(NLS) to promote their nuclear uptake. Devocelle, Keyes, and co-workers
have conjugated membrane-impermeable ruthenium(II) complexes with
the peptide sequence VQRKRQKLMP, which
is an NLS responsible for transporting nuclear factor-κB (NF-κB)
into the nucleus. The resulting peptide
conjugates (238) ( ) exhibit efficient cellular and nuclear
uptake in live CHO cells. While both conjugates specifically accumulate
in the cell nucleus, the more lipophilic conjugate 238b preferentially localizes in the nucleolar region ( ). Pope and co-workers have
conjugated a deep red luminescent iridium(III) complex with the NLS
PAAKRVKLD, which is critical for the
nuclear localization of the human c-Myc protein. This modification enhances the nuclear accumulation of
the conjugate (239) in live human skin fibroblast cells
compared to its peptide-free counterpart, which exclusively resides
in the cytoplasm. In addition to the
protein-derived NLS, the short synthetic peptide RrRK has also shown
high effectiveness in delivering the attached cargos to the cell nucleus. Barton and Puckett have conjugated a ruthenium(II)
dppz complex with this short peptide. The resultant peptide conjugate (240) displays punctate
cytoplasmic staining in live HeLa cells at low concentrations (≤
20 μM) but preferential accumulation in the nucleus at higher
concentrations (e.g., 100 μM).
 Despite these strategies, other transition metal
complexes have
also been reported to specifically target the cell nucleus. Sun, Mei,
and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II) complex (241) ( ) that
shows enantioselective cellular staining. The Λ-isomer of the complex specifically stains the nucleus
of MDA-MB-231 cells, whereas the Δ-isomer exclusively resides
in the cytoplasmic region ( ). The specific nuclear uptake of the Λ-isomer
occurs via an adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-dependent active transport
mechanism following cellular internalization through endocytosis.
Additionally, both enantiomers can bind to and stabilize G-quadruplex
DNA. , However, they exhibit enantioselectivity
in the self-assembly of c-myc G-quadruplex DNA. The
Λ-isomer promotes the self-assembly of c-myc G-quadruplex DNA into a nanowire structure, while the
Δ-isomer induces DNA condensation into G-quadruplex aggregates.
These enantiomers have been utilized as luminescent nonviral DNA carriers
to deliver the c-myc G-quadruplex structures into
different cellular compartments of HepG2 cells. In particular, the
nucleus-targeting Λ-isomer can facilitate the transportation
of DNA to the nucleus of live cells, enabling DNA release and functional
expression. Ruthenium(II) complexes coordinated with a 6-chloro-5-hydroxylpyrido[3,2-a]phenazine ligand (242) also display efficient
cellular uptake and nuclear staining in live cells. These complexes have been used to track the dynamic changes
in the nucleus of cancer cells including HepG2, HeLa and MCF-7 cells.
Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex
(243) that exhibits specific nuclear staining in a wide
range of cell lines, in a sharp contrast to its binuclear counterpart
that shows diffuse cytoplasmic staining. Since the emission intensity and lifetime of the complex is sensitive
to O2, the complex has been used for monitoring the O2 levels in the nucleus. Laskar and co-workers have developed
an AIE-active cyclometalated platinum(II) diphosphine complex (244a) that displays exclusive staining of the nucleus of live
human hepatocellular carcinoma Hep3B cells. Its water-soluble ethylenediamine counterpart (244b) has also been developed for bioimaging. The complex is specifically enriched in the nucleus of both cancer
(HeLa, U-87 MG, and mouse neuroblastoma Neuro-2a) and normal cells
(CHO-K1 and 3T3-L1), with more efficient cellular uptake observed
in cancer cells compared to normal cells.
 
7.2.

## Nucleolus
Nucleolus is the largest
substructure in the nucleus, serving as the site of ribosome biogenesis. It is also involved in the regulation of mitosis,
cell cycle progression, stress response, and biogenesis of ribonucleoproteins.
Lo, Yam, and co-workers have developed a guanidinium-functionalized
alkynylplatinum(II) complex (245) ( ) for the specific imaging
of nucleolus through targeting RNA. The
guanidinium moiety is essential to facilitate hydrogen bonding and
electrostatic interactions between the complex and RNA, which bring
the complex monomers into close proximity. This strengthens the Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions between the monomers and induces
their self-assembly into supramolecular nanostructures, switching
on the characteristic 3MMLCT emission (λem = 670 nm). In comparison, the complex shows much smaller
emission changes upon treatment with single- and double-stranded DNA.
The specific interaction of the complex with RNA has enabled the selective
visualization of nucleolus in HeLa and CHO-K1 cells. Monitoring changes
in the nucleolar dynamics reveals that the RNA synthesis inhibitor
actinomycin D induces significant nucleolar reorganization, suggesting
that the complex can be utilized for the development of cell-based
luminescence assays for inhibitor screening.
 Lam, Lo, and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
dipyridoquinoxaline
complexes (246) ( ) that display emission turn-on upon binding
to proteins. These complexes specifically
localize in the nucleoli of MDCK cells. The specific nucleolar staining
is attributed to their binding to nucleolar proteins, as evidenced
by the fact that the complexes do not show any significant change
in their emission profiles in the presence of RNA, and pretreatment
of the cells with ribonuclease (RNase) does not significantly affect
the nucleolar staining of the complexes. Lam, Lam, and co-workers
have also developed a two-photon-active cyclometalated platinum(II)
complex (247) that exhibits specific enrichment in the
nucleoli of HeLa and 3T3 cells due to its interactions with nucleolar
proteins. However, this complex induces
rapid cell death in live cells due to potent transcription inhibition.
Nevertheless, the complex has been used as a nuclear stain for various
thick mouse tissues. Imaging the mouse ileum samples at different
depths with multiphoton confocal microscopy has enabled the 3D reconstruction
of optical sections and visualization of the structures in detail.
 
7.3.

## Mitochondria
Mitochondria play a
pivotal role in producing energy in the form of ATP via oxidative
phosphorylation for various cellular processes such as biomolecular
synthesis, metabolism, and cell proliferation and differentiation. These organelles are also involved in the production
of ROS for intracellular signaling. Furthermore, mitochondria play
an important role in the regulation of apoptosis, the programmed cell
death that eliminates damaged or unwanted cells. 
One common approach to direct transition metal complexes
to the mitochondria is to functionalize them with a cationic and lipophilic
triphenylphosphonium (TPP+) moiety, which aids in the transport
and accumulation of the complexes in the mitochondria resulting from
the negative membrane potential across the inner mitochondrial membrane
(ca. −180 mV). Zhou and co-workers have developed
two iridium(III) complexes modified with a TPP+ unit (248) ( ) for targeted imaging of the mitochondria. These complexes specifically localize in the mitochondria of live
HeLa cells, which is different from their TPP+-free counterparts
that are diffusely distributed in the cytoplasm without any apparent
organelle specificity. Additionally, these complexes exhibit higher
photostability than commercial mitochondrial stains such as MitoTracker
Green FM and MitoTracker Red FM, indicative of their potential in
long-term imaging in live cells. García, Bertrand, and
co-workers have developed three TPP+-appended rhenium(I)
complexes (249) as multimodal probes for specific imaging
of the mitochondria. The introduction
of methyl groups to the TPP+ unit enhances the lipophilicity
of the complexes. Luminescence and XRF imaging in A549 cells shows
that complexes 249b and 249c preferentially
reside in the mitochondria, suggesting that these complexes can be
used as bimodal imaging probes for the mitochondria. Quantification
of the rhenium content in the stained cells using XRF spectroscopy
shows that the cellular uptake of the complexes increases with increasing
lipophilicity, with complex 249c showing ca. 2.5-fold higher accumulation in cells than complex 249b. Chan and co-workers have developed a series of cationic platinum(II)
complexes coordinated with a triphenylphosphine ligand as live-cell
imaging reagents. Two of these complexes
(250a and 250b) display high lipophilicity
(log Po/w = 4.0 and 5.5, respectively),
rapid cellular internalization through an energy-dependent pathway,
and specific enrichment in the mitochondria.
 Another approach is to conjugate transition metal
complexes with
a mitochondria-targeting peptide sequence, which can efficiently direct
the attached cargos to the mitochondria due to its cationic charge
and lipophilic character. Zhou, Fei, and co-workers have modified
an iridium(III) complex with a CPP RKKRRGRRR derived from the Tat49–57 domain to increase its cellular uptake, and a
mitochondria targeting sequence MLAKGLPPKSVLVKG derived from the mitochondrial
protein cytochrome P450 to facilitate its mitochondrial accumulation. The resultant peptide conjugate (251) ( ) is
readily taken up by HeLa cells and enriched in the mitochondria. Keyes
and co-workers have conjugated a membrane-impermeable osmium(II) complex
with two mitochondria-penetrating peptides (MPPs) FrFKFrFK for imaging
and therapeutic applications. The resulting
conjugate (252) primarily localizes in the mitochondria
of HeLa and MCF-7 cells at low incubation concentrations (≤
30 μM), but becomes diffusely distributed throughout the cells
including the nucleus and nucleoli upon increasing the concentration.
This shift in intracellular localization results in a change in the
mechanism of inducing apoptosis, switching from causing mitochondrial
membrane depolarization to activating caspases.
 Cationic and lipophilic transition metal complexes
can also be
directed to the mitochondria without the need of introducing a targeting
vector. Chao and co-workers have developed a series of cationic and
lipophilic ruthenium(II) (253) and iridium(III) complexes (254–259) − ( ) that
specifically target the mitochondria while exhibiting high resistance
to changes in mitochondrial membrane potential, enabling the real-time
tracking of the morphological changes of mitochondria over long periods
of time. These interesting features have been exploited to visualize
the mitochondrial morphological changes that occur during early apoptosis
induced by carbonyl cyanide m-chlorophenylhydrazone
(CCCP), revealing the gradual transformation of mitochondria from
their typical reticular structure into numerous smaller, scattered
fragments ( ). Cationic mixed-ligand iridium(III) complexes (260) also show high mitochondria specificity in a range of mammalian
cell lines. These complexes possess
large TPA cross-sections (δ750nm = 84 to 450 GM), enabling the effective visualization of mitochondria
in HeLa MCTSs with a penetration depth of up to ca. 120 μm using two-photon microscopy (λex = 750 nm). Additionally, complex 260b has
been used for real-time tracking of mitochondrial fission and fusion
in live cells and Caenorhabditis elegans (C. elegans) ( ). Furthermore, luminescent transition metal complexes
have been developed as mitochondria-targeting theranostic agents (261–266) ( ) that can simultaneously induce mitochondrial
dysfunction in cancer cells and monitor mitochondrial morphological
and metabolic changes. − For example, Tan, Mao, and co-workers have developed two iridium(III)
complexes (263) that specifically reside in the mitochondria
and induce mitophagy. Monitoring the
mitochondrial morphological changes at different time intervals has
revealed the autophagosomal engulfment of the damaged mitochondria
and the fusion of the autophagosome-engulfed mitochondria with lysosomes.
 To facilitate imaging with lower background interference,
Chao
and co-workers have designed a series of iridium(III) complexes (267) ( ) featuring NIR emission (λem =
672 to 702 nm) with large TPA cross-sections (δ730nm = 69.4 to 279.6 GM) for imaging the mitochondria
in live cells, MCTSs, and hippocampus slices. Super-resolution imaging of the mitochondria with complex 267c using SIM has allowed the clear visualization of their
ultrastructures in live cells with resolution up to 80 nm. It shows that the length and width of mitochondria
are ca. 2.0 and 0.7 μm, respectively, whereas
the lamellar cristae are visible with a thickness of ca. 105 nm. The high photostability of the complex has also enabled
the monitoring of the mitochondrial dynamics, in particular, the interactions
between mitochondria and lysosomes in live cells under SIM. Shen,
Pei, Chao, and co-workers have designed two mitochondria-targeting
iridium(III) complexes (268a and 268b) with
interesting three-photon absorption properties (σ980nm = 1.88 and 2.63 × 10–78 cm6 s2 photon–2, respectively). The use of three-photon excitation (λex = 980 nm) allows imaging at deeper
penetration, resulting in a significant increase in the maximum imaging
depth for zebrafish and hippocampus slices to ca.
1,000 and 500 μm, respectively, compared to ca. 200 and 250 μm with two-photon excitation (λex = 750 nm). These enhanced imaging capabilities have
enabled superior penetration depth and resolution for in vivo imaging of mouse brain vasculature ( ). Liu, Song, and co-workers have developed
three AIE-active iridium(III) complexes (269) for specific
mitochondria imaging and tracking. Their
long emission lifetimes in the solid state (τ = 3.23 to 7.07 ms) are advantageous to avoid interference from the
background autofluorescence via time-resolved luminescence imaging.
 The immobilization of luminescent probes in the
mitochondria allows
monitoring of the mitochondrial microenvironment under different conditions
irrespective of changes in the mitochondrial membrane potential. Lo, Lam, Cheng, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III) isothiocyanate complex (270) ( ) for labeling
mitochondrial proteins. The introduction
of an isothiocyanate unit enables modification of mitochondrial proteins
under the alkaline mitochondrial environment (pH ≈ 8.0). Incubation
of live mammalian cells such as 3T3, HeLa, and RPE cells with the
complex results in efficient and specific labeling of the mitochondria.
Proteomic analyses indicate the covalent conjugation of the complex
to mitochondrial proteins such as voltage-dependent anion channel
1, 14-3-3 protein ε, and peroxiredoxin 1. Coogan and co-workers
have developed a thiol-reactive rhenium(I) complex featuring a chloromethyl
unit (1g) that undergoes nucleophilic substitution with
the reactive thiol groups present in mitochondrial proteins for specific
mitochondria targeting. Cao, Mao, and
co-workers have demonstrated that the mitochondrial immobilization
of a related rhenium(I) complex (271) results in selective
and significant cancer cell death. The
O2-sensitive emission lifetime of the complex has allowed
the real-time monitoring of the mitochondrial metabolism using PLIM,
revealing that the complex induces more effective repression of mitochondrial
respiration than its mitochondria-nonimmobilized counterparts. Related
iridium(III) complexes (272) have also been developed
as theranostic agents targeting mitochondrial metabolism. 
 
7.4.

## Lysosomes
Lysosomes are membrane-bound
organelles that contain numerous hydrolytic enzymes for the degradation
of macromolecules. They are involved
in various crucial cellular processes such as endocytosis, autophagy,
and plasma membrane repair. Lysosomes also engage in interactions
with other organelles such as mitochondria, ER, and LDs to regulate
cellular homeostasis. Additionally, lysosomes serve as signaling centers
that govern cell growth, division, and differentiation. Lysosomal
dysfunction has been associated with the development of various diseases
such as lysosomal storage disorder, cancer, neurodegenerative diseases,
and aging.
Since lysosomes are characterized by an acidic internal
environment (pH ≈ 4.5–5.5), modification of transition
metal complexes with a pH-sensitive ligand is a common approach to
achieve effective lysosomal targeting. A typical example is morpholine
(pKa = 5–6), which is protonated
in lysosomes to facilitate the retention of the attached cargos inside
these acidic organelles. Chao and co-workers have developed a triscyclometalated
iridium(III) complex modified with three morpholine moieties (273) ( ) for specific imaging of the lysosomes. The emission intensity of the complex increases with decreasing
pH due to inhibition of photoinduced electron transfer (PET) quenching
by the morpholine unit. Additionally, the complex possesses a large
TPA cross-section (δ750nm = 69.5
GM), allowing for the visualization of lysosomes in live cells and
MCTSs using two-photon microscopy (λex = 750 nm). Notably, the complex is specifically enriched in the
lysosomes for more than 4 days without escape, compared to LysoTracker
Red that loses its lysosomal specificity after 48 h. The excellent
lysosomal specificity and high photostability of the complex have
enabled long-term lysosomal tracking during cell migration and apoptosis
( ). Pu, Ma,
and co-workers have also developed an iridium(III) complex bearing
a morpholine moiety (274) for specific lysosome staining. The introduction of an additional pH-sensitive
hydroxyl group to the complex enables a larger increase in emission
intensity under acidic conditions. Furthermore, a water-soluble iridium(III)
complex carrying two morpholine units (275) has been
developed for lysosome-targeted imaging and PDT. Kielar and co-workers have demonstrated that iridium(III)
complexes containing a pH-sensitive aminoalkyl moiety (276 and 277) can also specifically target the lysosomes. − 
 Transition metal complexes containing N-heterocycle-based
ligands such as imidazole, pyrrole, and pyridine exhibit acid–base
equilibrium in aqueous solution, influencing the photophysical properties
of the complexes in response to pH. Shandala, Massi, and co-workers
have examined the intracellular localization of a neutral rhenium(I)
tetrazolato complex (278) ( ) in live human adipose 3T3-L1 cells and
insect Drosophila larval adipose tissues. The protonatable pyridine ring endows the complex
with high affinity for organelles such as the late endosomes and lysosomes
that are characterized by an acidic environment. Williams and co-workers
have studied the photophysical properties and cellular behavior of
two iridium(III) complexes coordinated with a 2-pyridylbenzimidazole
ligand (279). These complexes
are a pair of conjugate base and acid, showing green emission at high
pHs and red emission at low pHs. Cellular imaging shows that these
complexes predominantly localize in the lysosomes of live CHO cells.
Solomatina, Porsev, Tunik, and co-workers have developed a series
of pH-sensitive iridium(III) complexes bearing two phenantroimidazole-based
cyclometalating ligands (280). The imidazole rings in these complexes remain uncoordinated and
accessible for protonation in acidic environments, enabling the complexes
to display emission changes in response to pH. Cellular imaging shows
that complexes 280c and 280d primarily reside
in the endosomes/lysosomes. Ma, Lam, and co-workers have designed
two binuclear platinum(II) complexes (281) as lysosome-specific
two-photon luminescence imaging probes. These complexes feature a bridging diphosphine ligand connecting
the two platinum(II) units, which promotes close intramolecular Pt···Pt
contact and thus facilitates 3MMLCT emission. However,
the deprotonation of the pyrazole ring in the terdentate ligand at
high pHs induces electrostatic repulsion between the ligands, disrupting
Pt···Pt interaction and diminishing 3MMLCT
emission. The pH-sensitive 3MMLCT emission has enabled
the real-time tracking of the rapid transportation of lysosomes between
the cell body and axons in live primary adult mouse dorsal root ganglion
neurons and acute brain slices ( ).
 Tan, Mao, and co-workers have developed two theranostic
iridium(III)
complexes coordinated with a pH-sensitive pyridyl-β-carboline
ligand (282) ( ) that can simultaneously induce autophagy and monitor
autophagic lysosomes. The emission intensities
of complexes 282a and 282b are pH-sensitive
due to deprotonation/protonation of the indole ring in the β-carboline
ligand, and they increase by ca. 35 and 30 fold,
respectively, upon decreasing the pH from 8.2 to 3.0. Complexes 282a and 282b also exhibit increased TPA under
acidic conditions (δ810 nm =
958 and 972 GM at pH 3.4, respectively). Remarkably, these complexes
specifically accumulate in the lysosomes and induce autophagy. Real-time
tracking of the lysosomes using complex 282b has revealed
their fusion with the autophagosomes during autophagic processes ( ). Complex 282b has also been loaded onto polydopamine NPs functionalized
with cyclic RGD peptides for targeted multimodal imaging and therapy. The pH-sensitive emission lifetime of complex 282b has enabled the monitoring of the specific lysosomal
damage induced by the NPs in integrin-overexpressing U-87 MG cells
using TP-PLIM. Related iridium(III) β-carboline complexes (283 and 284) have been developed for cancer-
and lysosome-targeted imaging and PDT. , These complexes
show higher 1O2 generation efficiencies under
acidic conditions and thus higher photocytotoxicity toward cancer
cells over normal cells. The high lysosome specificity of these complexes
also offers opportunities to monitor the therapeutic outcomes in situ by real-time tracking the changes in lysosomal integrity
during photoinduced cell death. Tan, Mao, and co-workers have conjugated
a pH-sensitive ruthenium(II) β-carboline complex (285) to Cdots for lysosome-targeted imaging and therapy. This modification results in increased cellular
uptake of the complex and TPA cross-section (δ810nm = 1,118 GM), which allows the imaging of cancer
cells, MCTSs, and zebrafish at a higher penetration depth as well
as the more effective inhibition of cancer cell growth in both 2D
cells and 3D MCTSs upon two-photon excitation.
 The manipulation of the self-assembly/disassembly
processes of
transition metal complexes in response to pH has been demonstrated
as an effective means for lysosomal imaging. Yam and co-workers have
studied the pH-induced self-assembly/disassembly behavior of alkynylplatinum(II)
terpyridine complexes featuring a pH-responsive phenol or carboxyl
group (286) ( ). , In aqueous solution at low pHs,
these complexes undergo efficient self-assembly via Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions, resulting in intense 3MMLCT emission in the NIR region (λem = 760 to 795 nm). However, the emission intensity decreases
with increasing pH due to the deprotonation of the phenol or carboxyl
groups on the alkynyl ligand, which reduces the propensity of the
complexes to aggregate by increasing their hydrophilicity and favors
emission quenching by PET. The significant differences in their NIR
emission intensities (up to 1,350-fold) at different pHs have enabled
the facile differentiation of acidic lysosomes from other cellular
compartments. Che and co-workers have developed a series of cyclometalated
platinum(II) isocyanide complexes (287) that display
pH-dependent emission properties. At
high pHs, the deprotonated pyrazole ring in the terdentate ligand
inhibits intermolecular Pt···Pt interactions. Increasing
the pH induces self-assembly of the complexes and thus a drastic change
in their emission colors from green to orange. These complexes specifically
target the lysosomes and induce cell death by increasing lysosomal
membrane permeability, resulting in potent cytotoxic activity against
a range of cancer cell lines including primary bladder cancer cells.
Chao and co-workers have conjugated an iridium(III) complex with two
pH-sensitive self-assembling peptides Nap-FFK-taurine (Nap = naphthalene)
for long-term tracking of lysosomes. The resultant conjugate (288) undergoes self-assembly
in aqueous solution, and the resulting self-assemblies transform from
large nanoaggregates (at pH 7–8) to smaller particles (at pH
6) and jointed networks (at pH 4–5) upon decreasing the pH
from 8 to 4 due to increased lipophilicity. This morphological transformation
has led to the long-term retention of the conjugate in acidic lysosomes
for more than 15 cell generations. The strong lysosomal retention
ability and high photostability of the conjugate have facilitated
the monitoring of lysosome dynamics using SIM, enabling the clear
visualization of mitochondria–lysosome contacts under starvation
conditions with high resolution ( ).
 Zhang, Tian, Tian, and co-workers have developed
three cyclometalated
platinum(II) complexes (289) ( ) that specifically target lysosomes through
interacting with lecithin, one of the major components of lysosomal
membrane. These complexes feature strong
four-photon absorption with a large four-photon absorption cross-section
of up to 1.52 × 10–81 cm8 s3 photon–3 at 1,600 nm. Live-cell imaging
shows that the complexes exhibit weak emission in normal cells (HELF)
but show intense intracellular emission in cancer cells (A549, HeLa,
and HepG2). Furthermore, the complexes specifically stain the lysosomes
of cancer cells, attributed to their enhanced emission upon binding
to lecithin. Notably, complex 289b exhibits selective
cytotoxicity toward HeLa cells (IC50 = 0.63 μM) over
HELF cells (IC50 > 50 μM), suggesting the potential
development of these complexes as cancer theranostics.
 
7.5.

## Endoplasmic Reticulum
The ER is an
interconnected network of flattened sacs and branching tubules that
spreads throughout the cytoplasm. It
is responsible for protein synthesis, folding, modification, and transportation
to the Golgi apparatus. Additionally, it plays a critical role in
maintaining calcium homeostasis, synthesizing lipids and steroid hormones,
and metabolizing xenobiotics.
Battaglia, Smythe, Thomas, and
co-workers have developed a binuclear ruthenium(II) complex (290) ( ) that shows specific staining of the ER. The complex is nonluminescent in aqueous solution but displays substantial
emission enhancement upon binding to DNA or interacting with liposomes.
The high lipophilicity of the complex (log Po/w = 1.52) facilitates its interaction with the hydrophobic
membranes of the ER, resulting in intense intracellular emission and
specific ER staining. Similar observations have been found for related
binuclear iridium(III) complexes (291). Ray, Gupta, and co-workers have reported an iridium(III)
complex (292) that exhibits specific ER staining in MCF-7
cells. Structure–property relationship
analysis indicates that the high ER specificity of the complex is
associated with: 1) the strong intramolecular hydrogen bonding interaction
between the hydroxyl group in the appended phenyl ring and the amino
group in the imidazole ring of the diimine ligand; and 2) intermediate
lipophilicity (log Po/w = 1.12) of the
complex. Brooks and co-workers have developed a neutral rhenium(I)
tetrazolato complex (293) that is enriched in the ER
and nuclear membrane of cells after cellular uptake through passive
diffusion. The high photostability and
low cytotoxic activity of the complex have allowed real-time monitoring
of biological events that occur in these cellular compartments, including
the formation of nuclear membrane-derived phagosomes and the release
of small vesicles from the cell surface. Related neutral iridium(III)
tetrazolato complexes (67a–67e) also
preferentially localize in the ER of cells. 
 Keyes and co-workers have conjugated a membrane-impermeable
ruthenium(II)
complex with penetratin (RQIKIWFQNRRMKWKK) to afford a luminescent
probe (294) ( ) for the ER. Penetratin
is a CPP derived from the DNA-binding domain of Antennapedia, and
it can facilitate the membrane translocation of the attached cargos
and deliver them to the ER. Thus, the
peptide conjugate specifically resides in the ER after cellular internalization,
enabling super-resolution imaging of the tubular structure of smooth
ER using STED microscopy. Lo and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)–peptide
conjugate containing a peptide sequence KDEL (295), a specific signal that causes proteins to be
retained in the ER. The peptide conjugation
directs the localization of the complex to the ER, resulting in increased
photocytotoxicity compared to the conjugate lacking the KDEL sequence.
 
7.6.

## Golgi Apparatus
The Golgi apparatus
is a membrane-bound organelle composed of a stack of flattened cisternae.
It is responsible for the processing and packaging of proteins and
lipids into vesicles for transporting to their targeted destinations. 
Lo and co-workers have reported two dendritic
iridium(III) complexes (150) that show specific accumulation
in the Golgi apparatus of live HeLa cells following their cellular
internalization. Ho, Wong, and co-workers
have developed a neutral iridium(III) complex (296) ( ) that displays
strong two-photon-induced emission due to its large TPA cross-section
(δ750nm = 340 GM). The complex is specifically enriched in the Golgi apparatus
and remains confined to this organelle for a long period of time (>
6 h) without migration to other cellular compartments.
 Wu and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II)
complex modified
with a sphingosine unit (297) ( ) as a luminescent ceramide mimic for Golgi
apparatus-targeted imaging and PDT. This
complex is taken up by cells via an energy-dependent pathway and specifically
enriched in the Golgi apparatus in multiple cancer cell lines (HeLa,
A549, and MCF-7). Additionally, the complex is noncytotoxic toward
HeLa cells in the dark (IC50 > 100 μM) but exhibits
high photocytotoxic activity (IC50 = 3.45 μM) upon
prolonged irradiation due to 1O2 generation.
Monitoring the morphological changes of the Golgi apparatus during
PDT treatment reveals Golgi apparatus dispersal.
 
7.7.

## Lipid Droplets
Lipid droplets (LDs)
are dynamic subcellular organelles composed of a neutral lipid core
surrounded by a phospholipid monolayer. They are crucial for intracellular lipid storage and metabolism
and are involved in various physiological processes such as energy
homeostasis, lipid metabolism, and protein degradation. The dysfunction of LDs can lead to several
diseases including obesity, diabetes, neurodegenerative disorders,
and cancer.
Shandala, Massi, and co-workers have developed a
neutral rhenium(I) tetrazolato complex (298) ( ) that specifically
accumulates in the LDs of live 3T3-L1 cells and insect Drosophila larval adipose tissues. Measurement
of the IR signatures of the complex using FTIR microspectroscopy reveals
that the complex is preferentially enriched in the core of LDs due
to its higher affinity for polar lipids such as phosphatidylcholine,
phosphatidylethanolamine, lysophosphatidic acid, sphingosine, and
sphingomyelin, compared to neutral lipids like triglycerides. The complex has been used to monitor the trafficking
of polar lipids in Drosophila larval adipose tissues,
which shows that polar lipids migrate from the LDs to the autophagic
compartments under complete starvation conditions and during larval
to adult metamorphosis. The strong association
of the complex with polar lipids has also enabled its use with other
lipid-specific fluorescent stains such as Filipin III for cholesterol
and BODIPY 493/503 for cholesteryl esters and triglycerides to examine
the lipid profile of prostate cancer cells. The elevated staining of polar lipids in human prostate cancer cell
lines (DU145, 22RV1, and LNCaP) compared to the nonmalignant human
prostatic cell line (PNT1a) highlights a significant difference in
the lipid profile of prostate cancer cells. These probes can also
facilitate the identification of alternations in lipid content and
distribution in cells. Neutral iridium(III) tetrazolato complexes
(67a–67e) also show a high affinity
for LDs. 
 Mao and co-workers have developed two lipophilic
neutral iridium(III)
β-diketonate complexes (299) ( ) for LDs imaging. The neutral charge and lipophilic nature (log Po/w = 2.15 and 2.19, respectively) of complexes 299a and 299b have led to their specific accumulation
in the LDs in a wide range of cell lines such as A549, HepG2, HeLa,
and LO2 cells. Additionally, the complexes possess large TPA cross-sections
(δ810nm = 232 and 220 GM, respectively),
which allows for the visualization of LDs and examination of their
dynamics in live cells and in vivo using two-photon
microscopy (λex = 810 nm). Complex 299a has been used to monitor oleic acid-induced LDs accumulation
in live cells and lipid metabolism in live zebrafish larvae ( ). Related iridium(III)
complexes (300) have been developed as LD-targeting luminescent
probes and PDT agents. These complexes
efficiently generate 1O2 upon continuous visible-light
irradiation, which causes elevated levels of lipid peroxidation inside
cells, resulting in high photocytotoxicity.
 Tang, Lo, and co-workers have developed three neutral
iridium(III)
complexes featuring a hydrophobic POSS unit (301) ( ) for specific
staining of the LDs. Complex 301c has been utilized to examine various LDs-related biological events
in live cells including the accumulation of LDs under oleic acid stimulation,
the movement of LDs, and the differentiation of 3T3-L1 preadipocyte.
The complex has also been used to visualize LDs in different cell
lines, revealing that the number of LDs is upregulated in cancer cells
(HeLa, MCF-7, MDA-MB-231, HepG2, A549, and Neuro-2a) compared to normal
cells (HEK293T, MRC-9, and 3T3-L1) ( ). The differential staining preference
of the complexes indicates their potential applications in cancer
diagnosis. Additionally, complex 301c exhibits negligible
dark cytotoxic activity (IC50 > 200 μM) but very
high photocytotoxicity (IC50 = 2.1 μM) toward HeLa
cells due to the induction of apoptosis and lipid peroxidation-mediated
ferroptosis upon continuous irradiation.
 
7.8.

## Cytoskeleton
The cytoskeleton of
eukaryotic cells is a complex and interconnected network of filamentous
proteins that are critical for the mechanical properties and shapes
of cells. Two major components of the cytoskeleton are microtubules
and actin filaments, which have unique roles in various cellular processes. Microtubules are assembled from dimers of α-
and β-tubulin and they play a crucial role in the intracellular
transport of vesicles and organelles, as well as in the separation
of chromosomes during mitosis; while actin filaments are formed in
a helical arrangement by the polymerization of monomeric globular
actin and they are responsible for controlling cell shape and movement.
Zhang, Tian, Battaglia, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex (302) ( ) for imaging microtubules. Binding of the complex to tubulin αβ and βα
sites induces a ca. 8-fold increase in its emission
intensity, which is not observed in the presence of other biomolecules
such as actin. The emission enhancement not only allows real-time
monitoring of tubulin polymerization into microtubules but also specific
staining of the microtubules in both live and fixed cells. Probing
the microtubule dynamics throughout mitosis reveals that microtubules
polymerize through the centriole (in prophase) to mitotic spindles
(in metaphase). Correlative light–electron microscopy and energy-filtered
transmission electron microscopy indicate a periodic distribution
of the iridium element along the microtubule structures with an average
interspace of 4.1 nm. Additionally, the complex has been utilized
to image the microtubule networks in the hippocampus region of brain
tissues using STED microscopy, which has enabled the visualization
of neuronal subunits including the nascent neuritis, growth cone,
and spines at high spatial resolution.
 Ruiz-Pérez, Battaglia, Tian, and co-workers
have utilized
a related iridium(III) solvato complex (303) ( ) for imaging
intracellular actin. The complex specifically
binds to the exposed His40 residue of the actin protein through iridium(III)–His
coordination, resulting in ca. 10-fold emission enhancement.
This emission response has facilitated the imaging of actin in cytoskeleton-rich
human lung fibroblasts HFL1 and mouse heart tissues. The complex can
label different actin species such as β- and γ-actin that
are mainly located in the cell boundary and cytosol, respectively,
as well as actin in different intracellular compartments including
lamellipodium, filopodium, and stress fibers. The high photostability
of the complex has enabled the use of STED nanoscopy, allowing the
visualization of actin with higher resolution (fwhm = ca. 60 nm) than conventional LSCM (fwhm = ca. 160
nm). Additionally, the complex is compatible with the use of correlative
light–electron microscopy and liquid-phase electron microscopy,
revealing the component unit and periodicity of actin filaments at
spatial resolution of ca. 8 nm.
 
7.9.

## Plasma Membrane
The plasma membrane
is a phospholipid bilayer embedded with proteins, which separates
the interior of a cell from the extracellular environment. It is involved in various cellular processes,
including nutrient transport, signal transduction, and cell recognition. , Abnormalities in the plasma membrane, such as overexpression of
specific cell-surface proteins, are potential biomarkers for certain
diseases such as cancer. 
The introduction
of long aliphatic chains to cationic transition metal complexes can
facilitate their enrichment in phospholipid membranes through assisting
hydrophobic interaction with the hydrophobic tails of phospholipids.
Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have examined the plasma membrane staining
properties of iridium(III) complexes appended with two alkyl chains
of different lengths (304) ( ). The affinities
of the complexes for the plasma membrane increase with the length
of the aliphatic chains, indicating that a longer alkyl chain can
more effectively inhibit the internalization of the complexes into
the cells due to increased hydrophobic interaction with the lipid
bilayer. Complex 304c has been used to detect and distinguish
between exogenous and endogenous analytes due to its similar distribution
in the plasma membrane and cytoplasm. Iridium(III) complexes carrying
two decyl chains (305a–305c) also
display exclusive plasma membrane staining, which is not observed
for their counterparts that contain shorter isopentyl pendants (305d and 305e). The differential staining further supports the importance of a long
alkyl chain in facilitating the accumulation of the complexes in the
plasma membrane. Zhao and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex featuring two nonyl chains (306) for targeting
the plasma membrane. Interestingly,
the complex exhibits selective staining of the plasma membrane of
cancer cells (A549) over noncancerous cells (human skin fibroblast
and 3T3), indicating that the complex can distinguish cancer cells
that are characterized by negative surface charges from normal cells.
Li, Tian, Li, and co-workers have developed a platinum(II) complex
bearing two long alkyl chains (307) as a two-photon probe
for the plasma membrane. Upon two-photon
excitation (λex = 860 nm), the complex
shows intense emission at 627 nm, which originates from the 3MMLCT state where Pt···Pt interaction plays an important
role. Lam and co-workers have designed an amphiphilic cyclometalated
platinum(II) complex (308) with two-photon-induced emission
properties for plasma membrane staining. The complex features a long hydrophobic octadecanyl chain for inserting
