into the cellular membrane, and an anionic p-trisulfonated
triphenylphosphine ligand for preventing cellular internalization.
It is rapdily and efficiently enriched in the plasma membrane of live
HeLa cells and retained in the membrane without internalization over
a long period of time (> 3 h).
 van Leeuwen and co-workers have designed a luminogenic
probe (309) ( ) that allows specific membrane staining with fluorescence
turn-on. The complex is nonluminescent
in aqueous solution
due to efficient energy transfer from [Ir(ppy)3] to Cy5
and from the singlet excited state of Cy5 to its triplet manifold
as facilitated by the SOC effect induced by the iridium atom. However,
interaction of the complex with the plasma membrane of cells induces
a conformational change that makes SOC less efficient, selectively
switching on Cy5 fluorescence. Shao, Chen, and co-workers have developed
a ruthenium(II)–coumarin complex (310) to prepare
a nanoprobe for cell membrane imaging. The complex self-assembles into NPs with a spherical structure and
an average diameter of 56 nm upon nanoprecipitation. The resultant
NPs show dual emission at 520 and 660 nm in aqueous solution. However,
the NPs disassemble upon interacting with the plasma membrane, accompanied
by the disappearance of the red emission and thus a change in the
emission color from red to green. The NPs show long retention in the
plasma membrane and high photostability, enabling long-term membrane
imaging.
 Furthermore, specific staining of the plasma
membrane allows for
the examination of various biological events that occur at the membrane
interface. Wang, Li, and co-workers have reported a heterobimetallic
ruthenium(II)–rhenium(I) complex (311) ( ) that displays
different emission response toward pH and anions such as F–, OAc–, and H2PO4–. The complex resides in the plasma
membrane of live HeLa cells, offering the possibilities to examine
related biological events at the membrane interface. Pan, Shen, Su,
and co-workers have developed a metal–organic cage (MOC) by
coordinating Pd2+ with a pH-responsive ruthenium(II) complex
(312) for cellular tracking. The MOC is retained on the plasma membrane of various cell lines
through electrostatic and hydrophobic interactions. This membrane
retention has enabled the monitoring of changes in extracellular pH,
which is useful for cancer cell detection. Additionally, the MOC has
been used for two-photon-induced cell membrane imaging in both live
cells and tissues due to its large TPA cross-section (δ820nm = 798 GM). Zhao, Liu, Huang, and co-workers have
developed conjugated polyelectrolytes (CPEs) (313) that
can be used for ratiometric and lifetime sensing of heparin. , The CPEs exhibit specific plasma membrane staining with high contrast
in difference cell lines such as KB and A549 cells, providing opportunities
to examine heparin interaction with the plasma membrane and internalization
using LSCM and PLIM.
 

8.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Probes
for Biological Species
Biological systems encompass a diverse
range of chemical entities,
including metal ions, anions, carbohydrates, thiols, reactive oxygen
and nitrogen species (RONS), nucleic acids, and proteins. These biological
species play distinct roles in regulating normal cellular functions,
and thus their aberrant expression and functions are often linked
to the onset and progression of diseases. Therefore, the development
of transition metal complexes that allow for the sensitive and selective
detection of specific biological species in the complex cellular milieu
will comprehend their respective roles in various physiological and
pathological processes, and foster the development of innovative approaches
for disease diagnosis and treatment. 
8.1.

## Metal Ions
Metal ions play crucial
roles in a wide range of physiological and pathological processes.
Sodium and potassium ions are essential to osmoregulation, the generation
of action potentials, and signal transduction, while calcium ion is vital for intracellular signaling. Heavy metal ions such as iron(II)/iron(III),
zinc(II), and copper(I)/copper(II) ions are involved in modulating
intracellular signaling and contribute to the production of ROS. However, certain heavy metal ions such as mercury(II),
cadmium(II), and lead(II) ions that are widely used in industries
are toxic to living organisms. The disruptions in metal ion homeostasis
can lead to aging and various disorders including cancer, neurodegenerative
diseases, and diabetes.
8.1.1.

## Iron(II) and Iron(III) Ions
Iron
is the most abundant transition metal in the human body. It primarily
exists as Fe2+ and Fe3+, and plays a crucial
role in the functions of numerous proteins and enzymes that are involved
in a wide range of metabolic processes including O2 transport,
cellular respiration, and DNA synthesis. Additionally, it can generate ROS such as hydroxyl radicals via
Fenton reaction, which cause oxidative damage to proteins, lipids,
and nucleic acids. Thus, the dysregulation
of iron homeostasis has been associated with various diseases including
anemia, atherosclerosis, neurodegenerative diseases, and cancer. 
He, Wang, and co-workers have developed
a manganese dioxide (MnO2) nanosheet-based sensor that
enables sensitive and label-free detection of Fe2+ using
[Ru(bpy)3]2+ as a signaling unit. The emission of [Ru(bpy)3]2+ is efficiently quenched when the complex is loaded onto single-layer
MnO2 nanosheets via electrostatic interaction. However,
it is rapidly restored upon reduction of MnO2 nanosheets
by Fe2+ to Mn2+. The nanosensor has been used
to detect Fe2+ in live HeLa cells, where the cells are
pretreated with N-ethylmaleimide (NEM) to remove
GSH that can also reduce MnO2 and interfere with the detection
process. A related nanoplatform based on an amphiphilic iridium(III)
complex (314) ( ) has been utilized to detect Fe2+ in drugs
and live cells. 
 Chen, Chao, and co-workers have designed a lysosome-targeting
iridium(III)
complex (315) ( ) as a Fe3+-activatable theranostic agent
for imaging and treatment of gastric cancer. Reaction of the complex with Fe3+ induces the oxidative
hydrolysis of the catechol moiety, releasing a highly cytotoxic iridium(III)
complex [Ir(ppy)2(bpy-NH2)]+ (bpy-NH2 = 4-amino-2,2′-bipyridine) and a ROS generator 2-hydroxybenzoquinone.
Complex 315 displays high cytotoxicity toward human gastric
adenocarcinoma cells (AGS and MKN-28) (IC50 = 9.2 and 11.3
μM, respectively) due to the high levels of labile iron pools
present in the lysosomes of these cells. Furthermore, the reaction
with Fe3+ leads to a substantial increase in the emission
intensity of the complex as PET from the catechol unit is inhibited.
The emission changes have enabled the monitoring of the intracellular
reaction using LSCM, revealing that the complex is selectively activated
in the lysosomes of AGS cells and gradually migrates to the mitochondria.
Remarkably, the selective activation of the complex in mice bearing
AGS tumor xenografts results in effective inhibition of tumor growth
with minimal side effects.
 
8.1.2.

## Zinc(II) Ion
Zinc is the second
most abundant transition metal within living organisms. The majority
of Zn2+ is bound to proteins, where it plays an important
role in maintaining structural stability and regulating catalytic
activity. A smaller fraction of Zn2+ exists as labile ions, participating in signaling events
that are associated with various physiological and pathological processes. Aberrant levels of Zn2+ have been
implicated in pathological conditions such as diabetes, immune dysfunction, and neurological disorders. 
Lo and co-workers have designed three rhenium(I) complexes appended
with a di-(2-picolyl)amine (DPA) moiety (316) ( ) for detecting
Zn2+. The binding of Zn2+ to the DPA unit inhibits PET from the DPA moiety, resulting
in increased emission intensities and lifetimes of the complexes.
Similar changes are not observed in the presence of other transition
metal ions such as Fe2+ and Cu2+ that are also
abundant in biological systems, enabling the specific imaging of Zn2+ in live HeLa cells. These complexes can also detect Cd2+ due to its similar binding properties as Zn2+. Iridium(III) complexes functionalized with a DPA unit (317) have also been developed for Zn2+ sensing. The direct attachment of the DPA moiety to
the phen ligand leads to more drastic photophysical changes upon Zn2+ binding. Live-cell imaging shows that complex 317c is enriched in the perinuclear region of HeLa cells. You, Nam, Lippard,
and co-workers have developed a related iridium(III) complex (318a) as a luminescent probe for Zn2+, where the
DPA moiety is introduced to the 4-position of the phen ligand via
a methylene linker. The binding of Zn2+ to the DPA unit suppresses PET and destabilizes the nonemissive 3ILCT (π(DPA) → π*(phen)) state, allowing
for efficient radiative decay from the emissive 3MLCT (dπ(Ir)
→ π*(phen)) state. The substantial increase in the emission
intensity and lifetime of the complex has enabled the visualization
of Zn2+ in A549 cells using LSCM and PLIM. Structure–property
relationship analysis of related complexes (318b–318g) indicates that PET plays a dominant role in the emission
response of the complexes toward Zn2+, and the use of cyclometalating
ligands with a high oxidation potential would allow for a greater
emission enhancement. Complex 318b has been used to visualize intracellular Zn2+ distribution in HeLa cells using LSCM and PLIM. Zhang, Liu, He,
and co-workers have utilized an iridium(III) DPA complex (319) for phosphorescence lifetime imaging of endogenous labile Zn2+ in live cells. The high mitochondria
specificity of the complex has enabled the monitoring of changes in
the labile Zn2+ levels in the mitochondria of MCF-7 cells
using PLIM, which shows that S-nitrosocysteine stimulates
intracellular Zn2+ release in mitochondria. Ma, Wong, and
co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex (320) that shows ratiometric response toward Zn2+. Upon Zn2+ binding, the intensity
of the emission band at 435 nm decreases whereas a new band at 495
nm appears, increasing the ratio I495nm/I435nm by ca. 6 fold.
This ratiometric response is specific toward Zn2+ over
other metal ions including Cd2+, enabling the specific
visualization of Zn2+ distribution in live zebrafish.
 Tang and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex modified
with a tetraazamacrocycle (321) ( ) for Zn2+ recognition. Similar to the DPA-based probes, the coordination
of Zn2+ to the tetraazamacrocycle inhibits PET quenching
and induces emission turn-on. This complex shows high sensitivity
and selectivity toward Zn2+, enabling the visualization
of Zn2+ in live HepG2 cells.
 However, the Zn2+ chelators such as
DPA and tetraazamacrocycles
could also bind to other metal ions such as Cu2+, interfering
with Zn2+ detection. As an alternative approach, Li, Xu,
and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex (322) ( ) that
features two phosphonate groups as the recognition units for Zn2+. The interaction between the
phosphonate group and Zn2+ reduces the solubility of the
complex and induces the aggregation of the complex in aqueous solution,
resulting in emission enhancement with a blue shift in the emission
maximum from 590 to 550 nm. The emission response is highly specific
toward Zn2+ over other metal ions including Cu2+, and it is reversible upon the addition of the Zn2+ chelator N,N,N’,N’-tetrakis(2-pyridylmethyl)ethylenediamine
to remove the bound Zn2+ from the complex. The complex
has been used to monitor endogenous Zn2+ release upon hydrogen
peroxide (H2O2) stimulation, revealing an increase
in the cytosolic Zn2+ levels under oxidative stress.
 
8.1.3.

## Copper(II) Ion
Copper, as a redox-active
metal, serves as a key catalytic cofactor for numerous enzymes involved
in cellular metabolic processes. However,
an excessive accumulation of Cu2+ can induce oxidative
stress and perturb normal cellular functions. It can also trigger
cuproptosis upon binding to lipoylated proteins in the tricarboxylic
acid cycle. Thus, the dysregulation
of Cu2+ homeostasis has been associated with various pathological
conditions such as Menkes and Wilson diseases, neurodegenerative disorders,
cardiovascular disease, and cancer. 
You, Nam, Lippard, and co-workers have modified a dual-emissive triscyclometalated
iridium(III) complex [Ir(ppy)2(btp)] (Hbtp = 2-(2′-benzo[b]thienyl)pyridine) with a DPA unit to afford
a ratiometric probe (323) ( ) for Cu2+ detection. In the absence of Cu2+, the complex
exhibits dual phosphorescence at 486 (τ = 0.53
μs) and 648 nm (τ = 4.16 μs), originating
from the emissive states associated with the ppy and btp ligands,
respectively. However, the binding of Cu2+ to the DPA unit
selectively quenches the red phosphorescence, increasing the ratio I486nm/I648nm by ca. 4 fold. The ratiometric response is highly specific
toward Cu2+ over other metal ions, allowing ratiometric
imaging of Cu2+ in HeLa cells ( ). Yu and co-workers have also developed
biscyclometalated iridium(III) DPA complexes (324) for
the detection of Cu2+ in live HeLa cells. , The binding of Cu2+ to the DPA unit on the diimine ligand
results in efficient emission quenching. Li and co-workers have utilized
a related ruthenium(II) complex (325) for the sequential
and specific detection of Cu2+ and Cys/His in live cells
and zebrafish. The in situ generated nonluminescent complex 325–Cu2+ ensemble can function as a sensor for Cys and His, which
induce emission turn-on by displacing Cu2+ from the ruthenium(II)
complex due to their higher affinities for Cu2+. This “On–Off–On”
response has allowed sequential imaging of Cu2+ and Cys/His
both in live bone mesenchymal stem cells and zebrafish. Wu and co-workers
have embedded an NIR luminescent ruthenium(II) DPA complex (326) into large-pore mesoporous silica nanoparticles (LPMSNs)
for the sensitive detection of Cu2+ in live HeLa cells
and zebrafish. The introduction of the
complex to the pores of LPMSNs can protect the complex from O2 quenching, resulting in emission enhancement and thus a more
drastic emission response upon Cu2+ recognition. The nanoprobe
shows different tissue affinities in zebrafish and has been used to
monitor Cu2+ distribution in vivo.
 Chao and co-workers have developed a luminescent
binuclear ruthenium(II)
complex (327) ( ) as a two-photon probe for Cu2+ detection. This complex features a 2,6-bis(imidazol-2-yl)pyridine
unit that serves as both a linker between the two ruthenium(II) moieties
[Ru(bpy)2(phen)]2+ and a recognition site for
Cu2+. Additionally, the extended π-conjugation in
the bridging ligand results in enhanced TPA (δ850nm = ca. 400 GM), enabling imaging
in live HeLa cells and zebrafish at increasing depths using two-photon
microscopy (λex = 850 nm). Ruthenium(II)
complexes bearing an imidazole-derived ligand such as 328( ) and 329( ) have been used for imaging Cu2+ in live A549
cells and pea aphids, respectively. Wang, Xu, and co-workers have
designed an iridium(III) complex bearing a 3-(2-pyridyl)pyrazole ligand
(330) that enables the rapid and reversible detection
of Cu2+ and Cys in the mitochondria of live HeLa cells
and zebrafish. Anandan and co-workers
have developed a binuclear ruthenium(II) complex (331) for sensing Cu2+ and sulfide. The two ruthenium(II) units are connected to a p-tert-butyl calix[4]arene through a triazole linker.
The emission of the complex is quenched upon binding of Cu2+ to the N2O2 core of the triazole-modified
calix[4]arene, and is restored in the presence of sulfide which displaces
Cu2+ to form stable CuS, allowing the sequential imaging
of these two species in live A549 cells. A triazole-appending ruthenium(II)
complex (332) has been used to detect Cu2+ in live MCF-7 cells. Kou, Xiao, and
co-workers have incorporated a salen ligand into [Ru(bpy)2(phen)]2+ to develop a luminescent probe (333), where the N2O2 core of the salen ligand
is involved in Cu2+ recognition, for sequential imaging
of Cu2+ and His in live B16 cells. Yu and co-workers have also developed a ruthenium(II) complex
with a Schiff base ligand (334) that enables rapid and
sensitive sensing of Cu2+ in live rat adrenal medullary
pheochromocytoma PC-12 cells. 
 Yu and co-workers have designed a ratiometric
nanoprobe for Cu2+ detection using SiNPs doped with [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and coated with PEI, which serves as a template
for in situ synthesis of silver nanoclusters (AgNCs)
on the
NPs. The nanoprobe exhibits dual emission
bands at 500 and 602 nm, which originate from the AgNCs and [Ru(bpy)3]2+, respectively. The binding of Cu2+ to PEI induces efficient quenching of the green emission of AgNCs
and thus a decrease in the ratio I500nm/I602nm, facilitating the ratiometric
imaging of Cu2+ in HeLa cells.
The development of
luminogenic probes for Cu2+ based
on Cu2+-binding is a challenging task due to the paramagnetic
nature of Cu2+, which facilitates emission quenching through
electron or energy transfer. Sun and
co-workers have modified [Ru(phen)3]2+ with
a phenylazo moiety and an amino group to develop a phosphorogenic
probe (335) ( ) for detecting Cu2+ taking advantage of
its oxidizing capability. The complex
is nonluminescent in solution due to the electron-deficient azo unit.
However, upon the addition of Cu2+, the azo unit is converted
to a nonquenching benzotriazole derivative through a Cu2+-mediated oxidative cyclization reaction, resulting in ca. 80-fold emission turn-on. This remarkable enhancement has enabled
the visualization of Cu2+ in live cells and pea aphid.
 
8.1.4.

## Aluminum(III) Ion
Aluminum is one
of the most abundant metals in the Earth’s crust and has found
applications in numerous aspects of life, ranging from food packaging
and kitchen utensils to transportation and construction work. However,
Al3+ can compete with essential metal ions such as Ca2+, Mg2+, and Fe3+ due to their similar
atomic size and electric charge, and
thus excessive exposure to Al3+ can lead to its accumulation
in various organs, causing harmful effects on human health. In particular,
elevated levels of Al3+ within brain tissues can cause
damage to the central nervous system (CNS), contributing to the development
of Alzheimer’s disease (AD). 
Leung, Ma, and co-workers have designed an iridium(III) complex (336) ( ) that incorporates an o-phenolsalicylimine moiety
as a receptor for detecting Al3+. The complex is weakly emissive in solution (Φem = 0.0082), but its binding to Al3+ induces
a 13.5-fold increase in emission intensity. The emission enhancement
is specific toward Al3+ over other common metal ions, enabling
the sensitive and selective detection of Al3+. The complex
has been used to detect Al3+ in live HepG2 cells. The increased
intracellular emission intensity observed upon exposure of the cells
to Al3+ indicates that the complex is membrane-permeable
and can react with Al3+ in the intracellular environment.
 
8.1.5.

## Mercury(II) Ion
Mercury(II) ion
is one of the most toxic environmental pollutants that poses a significant
threat to human health. The high toxicity
of Hg2+ arises from its high affinity for sulfhydryl groups. Elevated levels of Hg2+ can result
in irreversible damage to multiple vital systems in the body including
the digestive, endocrine, immune, nervous, and cardiovascular systems. 
Li and co-workers have introduced a Hg2+-responsive ruthenium(II) complex (337) ( ) to the surface
of an upconversion nanophosphor NaYF4:Yb,Er,Tm to develop
UCNPs as a ratiometric nanoprobe for Hg2+. The coordination of the NCS group of the complex
to Hg2+ induces a significant blue shift in the absorption
maximum from 541 to 485 nm. The increased spectral overlap enhances
the efficiency of FRET from the nanophosphor to the complex, reducing
the intensity of the UCL at 541 nm. The UCL from Tm3+ at
801 nm is insensitive to Hg2+ and used as a reference signal
to generate ratiometric UCL. The nanoprobe has been used to monitor
changes in Hg2+ in live HeLa cells. A related nanoprobe
has been developed using the core–shell structured UCNPs NaYF4:Yb,Er,Tm@NaGdF4 and the Hg2+-responsive
ruthenium(II) complex 338 for UCL imaging in live cells
and mice. The probe can also function
as a T1-weighted contrast agent for MRI in vivo due to the Gd3+ doped in the NaGdF4 shell.
 Zhao, Li, and co-workers have developed a neutral
iridium(III)
complex [Ir(bt)2(acac)] (Hbt = 2-phenylbenzothiazole; Hacac
= acetylacetonate) (339) ( ) as a ratiometric probe for Hg2+. The binding of Hg2+ to
the sulfur atom on the bt ligand rapidly induces the departure of
the acac ligand and the formation of the solvato complex [Ir(bt)2(solv)2]+. This structural transformation
leads to a significant blue shift in the emission maximum from ca. 560 to 520 nm and an increase in the ratio I520nm/I560nm, enabling the
ratiometric detection of Hg2+. The complex has been used
to detect Hg2+ in live KB cells, where it is distributed
in the cytoplasm. Incubation of the cells with Hg2+ increases
the ratio I515nm/I570nm from ca. 0.1 to > 0.6 ( ).
 Cho, Kim, and co-workers have developed a platinum(II)
complex
conjugated with a rhodamine derivative (340) ( ) for detecting
Hg2+ in live cells. The Hg2+-induced desulfurization triggers the opening of the rhodamine
spirolactam, resulting in 23-fold emission enhancement. Additionally,
the TPA cross-section of the complex increases in the presence of
Hg2+ (δ780nm = 55 GM).
These properties have enabled two-photon luminescence imaging of Hg2+ in live HeLa cells. Tang, Wang, Liu, and co-workers have
developed ruthenium(II) (341) , and iridium(III) complexes (342) , bearing a 1-(2-aminoethyl)-3-phenylthiourea pendant for the detection
of Hg2+. Upon the Hg2+-induced desulfurization
of the thiourea unit, an intramolecular cyclization reaction occurs,
giving rise to an imidazoline derivative. This structural transformation
substantially increases the emission intensities and lifetimes of
the complexes. Interestingly, the iridium(III) complex 342a, where the thiourea-containing pendant is introduced to the 4-position
of the phen ligand, displays ratiometric response toward Hg2+. The emission intensity of the complex
at 560 nm decreases while that at 610 nm increases in the presence
of Hg2+. These photophysical changes are attributed to
the difference in emissive states between the thiourea complexes and
their imidazoline counterparts. The complexes have been used to image
Hg2+ in live SMMC-7721 cells.
 
8.1.6.

## Silver(I) Ion
Silver has gained
widespread usage in modern industries, encompassing fields such as
electronics and pharmaceuticals. However, the release of Ag+ into the environment has led to significant environmental pollution.
Since Ag+ can deactivate sulfhydryl enzymes and form complexes
with a range of metabolites containing amino, carboxyl, and imidazole
groups, excessive exposure to Ag+ can result in various
health issues. 
Khatua and co-workers
have developed a ruthenium(II) complex coordinated with a benzothiazole-substituted
pyridyl-1,2,3-triazole ligand (343) ( ) that enables specific detection
of Ag+. The complex exhibits
weak emission in solution due to the presence of a low-lying nonemissive 3MC excited state. However, the binding of Ag+ to
the triazole ring forms a silver(I)–triazolide complex, which
destabilizes the 3MC state and facilitates the population
of the emissive 3MLCT state, resulting in 80-fold emission
enhancement. The binding occurs faster at pH > 9 as basic media
can
assist in the deprotonation of the acidic triazole C–H proton
for the formation of the silver(I)–triazolide complex. The
complex has been used to image Ag+ in live HeLa cells.
 
8.1.7.

## Gold(III) Ion
Gold has found important
applications in diverse fields such as catalysis and nanomaterials. While
metallic gold is stable and biocompatible, it can be oxidized to Au+ and Au3+ in the presence of strong oxidants in
biological environments. These ions are highly reactive and can cause
damage to organs such as the liver, kidneys, and peripheral nervous
system. 
Hong, Sun, and co-workers
have developed a luminescent ruthenium(II) complex (344) ( ) for
detecting Au3+. The emission
of the complex is quenched upon binding to Au3+, which
is not observed in the presence of other metal ions. The complex has
been used to monitor changes in Au3+ levels in inflamed
RAW264.7 cells and zebrafish, revealing the generation of Au3+ as an intermediate metabolite of the anti-rheumatoid agent sodium
aurothiomalate in inflamed models. Wang, Wang, Leung, and co-workers
have designed an NIR luminescent iridium(III) complex functionalized
with an alkynyl moiety (345) for imaging of Au3+ in live cells. The alkyne unit facilitates
the reduction of Au3+ into zerovalent AuNPs and the simultaneous
anchoring of the complex to the surface of the AuNPs during their
formation, which leads to efficient emission quenching. Upon cellular
internalization, the complex specifically accumulates in the mitochondria
of HeLa cells. Incubation of the stained cells with Au3+ reduces the intracellular emission intensity, indicating that the
complex can specifically image mitochondrial Au3+ in live
cells.
 
8.1.8.

## Palladium(0) and Palladium(II) Ion
Palladium is widely used in catalysis to promote a myriad of organic
transformations that are important to the synthesis of natural products,
pharmaceuticals, and agrochemicals. − However, its extensive
use has resulted in environmental and health concerns. Inorganic palladium
can disrupt mitochondrial respiration in the liver and kidney, while
palladium ions can bind to biomolecules such as DNA and proteins,
impairing normal cellular function and causing diseases. 
Wang, Leung, Wang, and co-workers have
developed an NIR luminescent iridium(III) complex (346) ( ) for
detecting palladium species. The emission
of the complex is quenched in the presence of palladium species including
palladium(0) and palladium(II) ions. The complex has been used to
detect palladium species in live HeLa cells. The high mitochondria
specificity of the complex has allowed the imaging of various palladium
species in the mitochondria.
 

8.2.

## Anions
Anions are crucial components
in living organisms, playing fundamental roles in various biological
processes such as intracellular pH homeostasis, cell volume regulation,
and cellular signaling. However, certain anions such as cyanide and
perchlorate are toxic and pose significant threats to human health.
Thus, accurate detection and monitoring of anions are essential for
understanding their roles and assessing their impact on human health. 
8.2.1.

## Chloride
Chloride is the most abundant
anion in living organisms and plays a crucial role in numerous physiological
processes such as the regulation of ion balance, cell volume, and
membrane potential. Imbalances in Cl– homeostasis have been implicated in several diseases
including myotonia, osteopetrosis, and cystic fibrosis. 
Cao, Mao, and co-workers have utilized
the cyclometalated platinum(II) complex [Pt(ppy)(bpy)]+ (347) ( ) as a phosphorogenic probe for halide ions. This complex is nonluminescent in aqueous solution;
however, the addition of halide ions (e.g., Cl–, Br–, and I–)
to the solution triggers its self-assembly via Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions, switching on the 3MMLCT emission in the deep red region (λem = 655 nm). The emission turn-on has allowed the complex
to monitor changes in intracellular Cl– levels in
live MCF-7 cells. Dorazco-González and co-workers have also
developed a cyclometalated platinum(II) complex (348)
for selective sensing of Cl–. The Cl– recognition involves the formation
of a Pt–Cl bond and two convergent C–H···Cl
interactions, which rigidify the complex and induces emission enhancement.
The cooperative three-point recognition results in higher binding
affinity and selectivity toward Cl– (Kb = 1.5 × 105 M–1) over
other halide ions (Kb = 5.75 × 102, 3.67 × 103, and 1.42 × 104 M–1 for F–, Br–, and I–, respectively). Treatment of Jurkat cells,
an immortalized cell line derived from human acute T cell leukemia,
with the chloro-substituted complex results in intense emission in
the nuclear region.
 Wang and co-workers have developed a nanosensor
for quantitative
imaging of intracellular Cl– concentration. The nanosensor is constructed using core–shell
structured SiNPs, where [Ru(phen)3]2+ serves
as a reference dye and is physically entrapped in the SiNPs, while
the Cl– indicator lucigenine is covalently attached
on the surface of the SiNPs. The nanosensor shows efficient uptake
and specific localization in the lysosomes of live HeLa cells, primarily
attributed to the residual positively charged amino groups present
on the surface of the SiNPs. The long emission lifetime of [Ru(phen)3]2+ has enabled the quantification of intracellular
Cl– levels using a time-domain dual-lifetime referencing
approach, unveiling that the Cl– concentration in
lysosomes exceeds 80 mM. The nanosensor has been further utilized
for long-term monitoring of changes in intracellular Cl– levels in response to alternations in extracellular Cl– concentration.

8.2.2.

## Fluoride
Fluoride is well-known
for its use as an additive in drinking water and toothpastes to prevent
dental caries. However, excessive intake
of F– can result in the development of dental and
skeletal fluorosis and damage to nonskeletal tissues in the gastrointestinal,
cardiovascular, and nervous systems. 
Liu, Zhao, and co-workers have developed a luminescent iridium(III)
complex modified with two F–-sensitive tert-butyldiphenylsilyl (TBDPS) moieties
(349) ( ) for detecting F– in live cells. Reaction of the complex with F– induces the cleavage of the TBDPS unit, generating an electron-rich N-(4-hydroxyphenyl)amino group that efficiently quenches
the emission of the complex through PET and intramolecular rotation.
This leads to a decrease in the emission intensity and lifetime of
the complex, enabling the monitoring of changes in intracellular F– levels using LSCM and PLIM, respectively. A related
TBDPS-modified iridium(III) complex has been incorporated into the
polymer backbone of a fluorescent polyfluorene-based CPE
for ratiometric detection of F–. The modified CPE (350) self-assembles into
well-dispersed and ultrasmall semiconducting polymer dots (Pdots)
with a diameter of < 10 nm in aqueous solution due to its amphiphilic
nature. The Pdots display both polyfluorene fluorescence (λem = ca. 420 nm) and
iridium(III) phosphorescence (λem = ca. 600 nm). Reaction of the Pdots with F– selectively reduces the intensity and lifetime of
the red phosphorescence. These photophysical changes have enabled
the visualization of F– in live HeLa cells using
LSCM and PLIM, showing that incubation of the stained cells with F– reduces the ratio Ired/Iblue from 1.12 to 0.29 ( a) and the emission lifetime
from 36.0 to 6.8 ns ( b).
 
8.2.3.

## Bisulfite and Sulfite
Sulfur dioxide
(SO2), which dissociates into bisulfite and sulfite in
water, is an air pollutant that is detrimental to human health through
causing DNA damage in multiple organs. However, SO2 can be generated from the metabolism of
sulfur-containing amino acids in mammals, functioning as a gaseous
signaling molecule to regulate various physiological and pathological
processes in the cardiovascular system. 
Transition metal complexes have been developed as probes for
detecting HSO3–/SO32– through nucleophilic addition reaction. Chao and co-workers have
designed binuclear iridium(III) complexes (351) ( ) featuring an
electron-deficient azo bridge as both a linker and a recognition site
for HSO3– and SO32–. , The nucleophilic addition of HSO3–/SO32– to the azo
unit inhibits PET quenching, resulting in a substantial increase in
the emission intensities (I/Io = 8 to 27) and lifetimes of the complexes. Among these complexes,
complex 351d exhibits the strongest two-photon-induced
emission in the presence of SO32– due
to the large TPA cross-section (δ750nm = 141.0 GM) and high emission quantum yield (Φem = 0.28) of the resultant adduct, facilitating the detection
of HSO3– and SO32– in live cells using two-photon microscopy. Owing to the cationic
charge and high lipophilicity, the complex shows high mitochondria
specificity, enabling the specific detection of these SO2 derivatives in the mitochondria ( a). It has been used to visualize the endogenous
production of HSO3– and SO32– mediated by thiosulfate sulfurtransferase (TST),
which catalyzes sulfur transfer from thiosulfate to GSH and cyanide,
respectively, in the mitochondria of live HepG2 cells ( b), as well as the production
of HSO3–/SO32– in live RAW264.7 cells upon stimulation with the bacterial endotoxin
lipopolysaccharide (LPS) ( c). The complex has also been used for two-photon luminescence
imaging of HSO3–/SO32– in C. elegans and rat hippocampal tissue slices.
 Li and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex conjugated
with an indolium unit (352) ( ) for the sensitive detection of HSO3–. The 1,4-addition
of HSO3– to the unsaturated C=C bond
of the complex disrupts the π-conjugation between the iridium(III)
polypyridine core and the indolium moiety, resulting in an increase
in emission intensity. The emission enhancement has facilitated the
visualization and tracking of HSO3– in
live HepG2 cells. Wang, Li, Cao, and co-workers have developed an
iridium(III) complex functionalized with two malononitrile units (353) as a two-photon probe for HSO3–. The high mitochondria specificity
of the complex has enabled the specific detection of mitochondrial
HSO3– in live A549 cells. A related iridium(III)
complex (354) has also been developed for two-photon
luminescence imaging of SO2 derivatives. The complex displays a substantial increase in the emission
intensity (I/Io = 69)
and lifetime (from 110 to 254 ns) upon reaction with HSO3–, enabling the sensitive detection of mitochondrial
HSO3– in live cells and zebrafish using
LSCM and PLIM.
 Qi, Qi, and co-workers have utilized an aldehyde-bearing
iridium(III)
complex (355) ( ) for the detection of HSO3–. Incubation of the complex with HSO3– results in an increase in emission intensity
due to inhibited PET in the resultant aldehyde–HSO3– adduct, enabling the imaging of HSO3– in live MCF-7 cells. Liu, Ma, and co-workers
have used a related iridium(III) complex bearing two aldehyde units
(356) to visualize HSO3– in live LO2 cells and zebrafish. Since
treatment of the aldehyde–HSO3– adduct with H2O2 can regenerate the aldehyde
unit, Qi and co-workers have utilized a related iridium(III) complex
(357) to monitor the HSO3–/H2O2 redox cycles in live HeLa cells with
a reversible “Off–On–Off” emission response. 
 Luo, Yang, Gao, and co-workers have developed
cyclometalated palladium(II)
complexes that undergo self-assembly through Pd···Pd
interaction in the presence of specific anions driven by electrostatic
interaction. The cyclometalating ligand
of the complexes plays an important role in anion recognition. One
of the complexes (358) ( ) has been demonstrated to exhibit emission
turn-on (I/Io = ca. 31.8) in the presence of HSO3–. It primarily resides in the lysosomes of live HeLa cells following
uptake via endocytosis.
 
8.2.4.

## Pyrophosphate
Pyrophosphate (PPi),
the dimeric form of inorganic phosphate, is a by-product of cellular
hydrolysis of ATP. It plays a crucial role in biological processes
such as gene regulation, energy storage, and signal transduction.
Aberrant intracellular PPi levels have been associated with several
diseases including calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystal deposition
disease, chondrocalcinosis, arthritis, and cancer. 
Khatua and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex coordinated with a benzimidazole-substituted 1,2,3-triazole
ligand (359) ( ) for sensitive, selective, and rapid detection of
PPi. The emission of the complex is
enhanced in the presence of PPi, as the multiple hydrogen bonding
interactions between the benzimidazole-substituted 1,2,3-triazole
ligand and PPi increases the energy gap between the 3MC–3MLCT states. The emission response is rapid (< 24 s) and
selective toward PPi over other phosphates with similar structures
(e.g., H2PO4– and adenosine mono-, di-, and triphosphate). The complex has been
used to image PPi in live HeLa cells. A ruthenium(II) complex bearing
two aminoethanol pendants (360) that can detect PPi through
a similar recognition mechanism has also been used to visualize PPi
in live HeLa cells. 
 
8.2.5.

## Cyanide
Cyanide is one of the most
toxic anions to living organisms. It shows a strong binding affinity
for the heme unit in cytochrome c oxidase, an important
enzyme in the mitochondria responsible for the electron transport
chain, inhibiting cellular respiration. Additionally, CN– has been associated with the
generation of ROS within the mitochondria, which leads to oxidative
damage.
Li and co-workers have designed an iridium(III) complex-modified
UCNP for ratiometric detection of CN–. This nanoprobe is comprised of a lanthanide-doped
upconversion nanocrystal NaYF4:Yb,Er,Tm and a CN–-responsive iridium(III) complex (361a) ( ). The nanocrystal
displays two UCL bands at 540 and 800 nm, with the green UCL exhibiting
substantial spectral overlap with the absorption of the complex (λabs = 505 nm), resulting in efficient
FRET. However, the addition of CN– to the α,β-unsaturated
carbonyl unit of the complex reduces its visible absorption and suppresses
FRET, allowing for the ratiometric detection of CN– using the UCL at 800 nm as a reference signal. The complex has been
used for ratiometric imaging of CN– in live HeLa
cells. A related complex (361b) has been loaded into
the hollow space of UCNPs with a yolk–shell structure, consisting
of NaLuF4:Yb,Er,Tm as the inner yolk and porous silica
as the shell. The resultant nanocomposite
has been used to detect intracellular CN– in live
KB cells. Xu, Jin, Yao, and co-workers have utilized the iridium(III)
complex 355 to detect CN– through a
cyanohydrin reaction. The reaction converts
the aldehyde moiety to a nonquenching cyanohydrin derivative, resulting
in a 15-fold increase in emission intensity. The complex has been
used to detect CN– in live A549 cells.
 Ghosh, Das, and co-workers have developed a heterobimetallic
iridium(III)–copper(II)
complex (362) ( ) for the detection of CN– based
on a metal displacement approach. The
complex is weakly emissive (Φem =
0.0047) in aqueous solution due to efficient quenching by the paramagnetic
Cu2+. However, the addition of CN– to
the solution induces the decomplexation of the copper(II) DPA unit
and the formation of copper(II) cyanide complexes [Cu(CN)n](n−2)– (n = 2 or 4) due to the strong affinity of CN– for Cu2+, resulting in emission turn-on.
The complex has been used to monitor the uptake of CN– in live HeLa cells. It has also been utilized to develop a luminescence
assay to examine the activity of hydroxynitrile lyase, an enzyme that
catalyzes the conversion of cyanohydrins into their corresponding
aldehyde or ketone derivatives and CN–.
 Leung, Ma, and co-workers have utilized an iridium(III)
complex
(363) ( ) and Ag@Au core–shell structured NPs to develop a
sensing platform specific for CN–. The Au shell protects the Ag core from interactions with
other possible interferants (e.g., I–). The complex is adsorbed onto the Au surface via electrostatic
interaction, which induces efficient emission quenching. However,
upon the addition of CN–, the Au shell of Ag@Au
NPs dissolves and the complex is released from the Ag@Au NPs, resulting
in substantial emission enhancement. The sensing platform has been
applied to monitor CN– in drinking water and in
live HeLa cells.
 
8.2.6.

## Perchlorate
Perchlorate is one
of the major environmental pollutants due to the widespread industrial
and commercial use of perchlorate salts. Its presence in the environment
can be harmful to human health as it can substitute iodide in the
body and inhibit iodine uptake in the thyroid gland due to their similar
ionic radii. 
Zhu, Su, Bryce, and
co-workers have developed an AIE-active binuclear iridium(III) complex
(364) ( ) that enables specific detection of ClO4–. The complex is nonluminescent in
aqueous solution (Φem = 0.29 ×
10–3); however, the addition of ClO4– to the solution leads to a 430-fold increase in the
emission quantum yield (Φem = 0.12)
with a blue shift of 25 nm in the emission maximum. These changes
are attributed to the specific interaction between the complex and
ClO4–, which induces trans-to-cis isomerization of the complex and subsequent
aggregation of the cis-isomer due to its lower aqueous
solubility. The complex has been used to detect ClO4– in live HeLa cells. Chao and co-workers have also
developed two AIE-active iridium(III) complexes (365 and 366) that can selectively detect ClO4– in aqueous solution and in live cells, where the presence of ClO4– induces the formation of emissive NPs
and thus substantial emission enhancement. , 
 
8.2.7.

## Periodate
Periodate is a versatile
oxidizing agent used in organic synthesis for various chemical transformations. It is particularly useful for modifying biomolecules
like carbohydrates due to its unique reactivity and selectivity toward
1,2-diols. However, excess exposure to IO4– can be toxic, causing kidney toxicity and uremia. Additionally,
IO4–-induced iodine increase in the thyroid
may lead to thyrotoxicosis.
Leung, Wong, Ma, and co-workers
have developed an iridium(III) complex bearing a sulfide group (367) ( ) for the detection of IO4–. Oxidation of the sulfide group by IO4– to a sulfoxide derivative induces significant
emission quenching of the complex. The emission response is specific
toward IO4– over other iodine sources
(e.g., I– and IO3–) and common oxidizing agents (e.g., H2O2 and hypochlorous acid (HClO)). The complex
has been used to image IO4– in live HeLa
cells.
 

8.3.

## Amino Acids
Amino acids possess diverse
functional side chains that contribute to their various biochemical
properties and functions. In addition
to their involvement in protein synthesis, amino acids play crucial
roles in gene expression, cell signaling, neurotransmission, redox
balance, and immune response. Aberrant amino acid metabolism has been
linked to various diseases such as diabetes, neurological disorders,
cardiovascular disease, and cancer.
8.3.1.

## Histidine
As an essential amino
acid, His is crucial for human growth and development. Additionally, it serves as a precursor for
histamine that plays an important role in mediating immune response and neurotransmission. Abnormal levels of His and His-rich proteins have been
associated with pathological conditions including rheumatoid arthritis,
liver cirrhosis, thrombotic disorders, and chronic kidney disease.
Tian, Tian, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex
(368) ( ) as a two-photon probe for His sensing. The complex shows a weak emission band centered at 593
nm (Φem < 0.01) in aqueous solution.
However, a strong emission band emerges at 493 nm upon the addition
of His or His-rich proteins such as BSA. The substantial emission
enhancement is attributed to the secondary bonding interactions between
the complex and His that effectively inhibit nonradiative decay. The
complex has been used to visualize His in HepG2 cells ( ). Incubation of the cells
with the complex gives intense nuclear emission, which disappears
when the cells are pretreated with histidine decarboxylase (HDC) and
is restored when the cells are further incubated with His. Similar
nuclear staining is observed in other cancer cell lines such as A549
and HeLa cells but not in normal HELF cells, suggesting that the complex
can differentiate between cancer cells and normal cells based on its
specific recognition of nuclear His. The complex has also been utilized
to image His using STED microscopy, which enables the visualization
of nuclear His at a high signal-to-noise ratio.
 Ye and co-workers have designed a heterobimetallic
ruthenium(II)–nickel(II)
complex (369) ( ) as a selective probe for His. This complex displays weak emission due to the paramagnetic
nature of Ni2+. Since His exhibits high binding affinity
for Ni2+, treatment of the complex with His triggers the
release of Ni2+ from the complex, resulting in an 11-fold
increase in emission intensity that is not observed for other amino
acids and His-containing proteins such as BSA. The complex has been
applied to visualize endogenous His in live HeLa cells. The intense
emission observed in the nuclear region indicates the high specificity
of the complex toward nuclear His. The complex has also been used
to image His in living organisms such as zebrafish larvae and D. magna.
 Iridium(III) solvato complexes have been demonstrated
as promising
probes for His detection through iridium(III)–His coordination. − Wang and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) solvato complex
(370) ( ) as a phosphorogenic probe for His or His-containing proteins. This complex shows substantial emission enhancement
upon specific coordination with His or His-rich proteins. The complexes
have been used to visualize endogenous His or His-containing proteins
in live HepG2 cells and rat brain tissue slices. Hu and co-workers
have utilized a related iridium(III) solvato complex (371) for tracking His or His-containing proteins in fixed HeLa cells,
which gives rise to intense emission in the nuclear region. 
 You and co-workers have developed a platinum(II)
solvato complex
(372) ( ) that displays ratiometric response toward histamine. Treatment of the complex with histamine induces
a large blue shift in the emission maximum from 635 to 567 nm, resulting
in a substantial increase in the ratio I567nm/I635nm from 0.26 to 1.90. The lifetime
of the emission at 567 nm also increases from 21 to 435 ns in the
presence of histamine. These photophysical changes are specific toward
histamine over His as the binding affinity of the complex for histamine
(Kd > 1.2 pM) is 6 orders of magnitude
higher than His (Kd > 1.2 μM),
enabling
the selective visualization of histamine in live RAW264.7 cells. Furthermore,
the complex has been utilized to monitor the progress of the de novo synthesis of histamine in RAW264.7 cells ( ).
 Cash and Clark have utilized the platinum(II) porphyrin
PtTPFPP
(373) (H2TPFPP = meso-tetrakis(2,3,4,5,6-pentafluorophenyl)porphyrin)
( ) and diamine
oxidase to develop a phosphorescent nanosensor for tracking histamine
levels in vivo. The
enzyme catalyzes the oxidative deamination of histamine to imidazole-4-acetaldehyde,
ammonia, and H2O2, leading to a decrease in
the local O2 concentration and thus an increase in the
emission intensity of PtTPFPP. The nanosensor has been used to monitor
changes in histamine levels in live mice, revealing rapid histamine
clearance with an elimination half-life of ca. 7.6
min.
 
8.3.2.

## Tryptophan
Tryptophan is an essential
amino acid that is needed for protein synthesis in humans. Additionally,
its metabolite serotonin plays an important role in neurotransmission
and immune response. The metabolism
of tryptophan has been implicated in a wide range of diseases, including
neurodegenerative diseases such as AD and Parkinson’s disease
(PD), infectious diseases, autoimmune diseases, and cancer.
Song and co-workers have reported a luminescent iridium(III) complex
(374) ( ) that enables the specific detection of tryptophan via noncovalent
interactions. The emission of the complex
is effectively quenched in the presence of tryptophan due to PET.
This emission quenching is not observed for other amino acids, highlighting
the selectivity of the complex toward tryptophan. The complex has
been used to detect tryptophan in live HepG2 cells. The complex is
distributed throughout the cells, and the intracellular emission intensity
diminishes upon increasing tryptophan concentration. These findings
indicate that the complex can specifically recognize and interact
with tryptophan in the intracellular environment.
 
8.3.3.

## Glutamine
Glutamine is a vital
amino acid that plays multiple roles in living organisms. It is involved
in protein synthesis, regulation of intracellular processes, support
of cell proliferation, and maintainance of acid-base balance. Glutamine also serves as a versatile nutrient,
contributing to energy production, macromolecule synthesis, and signaling
in cancer cells.
Yang, Chen, Mao, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III) solvato complex [Ir(pba)2(DMSO)2]+ (Hpba = 4-(2-pyridyl)benzaldehyde) (375) ( ) for
detecting glutamine. This complex undergoes
solvent ligand substitution upon binding with glutamine, leading to
the formation of a glutamine-bound iridium(III) complex. The interaction
between the aldehyde unit of the pba ligand and the amide group of
glutamine stabilizes the complex through hydrogen bonding. Furthermore,
this intramolecular interaction perturbs the p−π conjugation
within the pba ligand, resulting in 200-fold emission enhancement.
The emission response is specific toward glutamine over other amino
acids and glutamine-containing peptides such as GSH, allowing for
the selective imaging of glutamine in live HeLa cells. Additionally,
the complex preferentially accumulates in the mitochondria, providing
opportunities to investigate mitochondrial glutamine metabolism.
 
8.3.4.

## Proline
Proline is a nonessential
amino acid that can be endogenously produced from glutamate (Glu)
or ornithine. It plays an important role in energy production, redox
balance, and apoptosis. Proline metabolism
is also closely linked to normal brain function and various neurological
and psychiatric disorders. For example,
impaired proline metabolism leading to the accumulation of proline
in the blood can result in hyperprolinemia, which is associated with
symptoms such as seizures, intellectual disability, and schizophrenia.
Proline metabolism has also been implicated in cancer cell survival,
proliferation, and metastasis. 
Liu, Ma, and co-workers have utilized the iridium(III) complex 355 to detect proline through a proline-mediated aldol reaction. In the presence of proline, the quenching aldehyde
unit reacts with acetone to form a nonquenching aldol unit, resulting
in ca. 8-fold emission enhancement. No similar increase
in emission intensity is observed for other amino acids. The complex
has been used to detect proline in live A2780 cells, and the intense
intracellular emission observed when the cells are treated with acetone
indicates that the complex can detect endogenous proline.

8.3.5.

## Tyrosine
Tyrosine is categorized
as a nonessential amino acid as it can be endogenously synthesized
from phenylalanine. It is the precursor for the production of catecholamines
such as dopamine, norepinephrine, and epinephrine that play important
roles as neurotransmitters in the nervous system and as hormones in
the endocrine system. Tyrosine is also a precursor for the production
of melanins for skin and hair pigmentation. 
Tian, Tian, and co-workers have developed an AIE-active heterobimetallic
iridium(III)–organotin(IV) complex (376) ( ) that can recognize
endogenous tyrosine. The complex shows
multiphoton absorption behavior (δ920nm = 9.95 GM, σ1500nm = 2.29 ×
10–81 cm6 s2 photon–2) and significant emission enhancement upon interaction with tyrosine
through secondary bonding interactions. The complex has been used
to visualize endogenous tyrosine in live A549 cells. The intense nuclear
emission observed indicates that the complex specifically recognizes
tyrosine or tyrosine-containing proteins in the cell nucleus. The
complex has also been applied to super-resolution imaging due to its
high photostability, which allows the visualization of the nucleus
structure with an increased signal-to-noise ratio using STED microscopy.
 

8.4.

## Carbohydrates
Carbohydrates are vital
biomolecules that play crucial roles in essential biological processes
such as cell proliferation and differentiation. , As a result, their detection is of high importance for disease diagnosis
and treatment. One notable example is the association between abnormal
glucose levels in the blood and diabetes. Accurate detection of blood
glucose levels is useful for the effective management of diabetes
and improving patient outcomes.
Phenylboronic acid (PBA) derivatives
have received significant attention as recognition units for carbohydrates
due to their rapid and reversible reaction with 1,2- or 1,3-diols
at physiological pH. Pikramenou and
co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex modified with two
PBA moieties as the recognition sites (377) ( ) for detecting
saccharides. Binding of the complex
to glucose and fructose via phenylboronate ester formation results
in a substantial decrease in its emission intensity. Live-cell imaging
shows that increasing the glucose or fructose concentration in the
cell culture media leads to a reduction in the intracellular emission
intensity, which is ascribed to both the lower emission intensities
and cellular uptake efficiencies of the resultant sugar conjugates
compared to the free complex.
 Sialic acids are a family of negatively charged,
nine-carbon monosaccharides
derived from neuraminic acid. They are
commonly located at the terminal position of N- and O-linked glycans attached to the cell surface, playing an
important role in mediating cell–cell interactions, signaling,
and immune response. Increased sialylation on the surface of cancer
cells has been linked to the progression, invasion, and metastasis
of cancer. Lo and co-workers have designed
three iridium(III) complexes functionalized with a PBA moiety (378) ( ) as luminescent probes for sialic acids. The PBA moiety recognizes N-acetylneuraminic acid
(Neu5Ac), the most prevalent form of sialic acid, through phenylboronate
ester formation with the vicinal diol of Neu5Ac. Furthermore, the
introduction of an aminomethyl group at the meta position
of the PBA unit facilitates electrostatic interaction with the carboxylate
group of Neu5Ac, enhancing the stability of the resultant conjugate
under physiological conditions. This cooperative binding mode significantly
increases the binding affinities of the complex for Neu5Ac (log Kb = 3.61 to 3.85), surpassing those for other
monosaccharides that are commonly found in glycoproteins such as d-glucose, d-galactose, and d-mannose (log Kb = 1.68 to 3.34). Among the three complexes,
complex 378c can specifically recognize the sialic acid
residues on the surface of live HepG2 cells ( ), as the ionized carboxylate groups efficiently
suppress the internalization of the complex and facilitate its reaction
with sialic acids. The absence of plasma membrane staining in cells
coincubated with exogenous Neu5Ac or pretreated with neuraminidase
further confirms the specific interaction of the complex with sialic
acids. Remarkably, the specific recognition of cell-surface sialic
acid residues has enabled the complex to distinguish between cancerous
HepG2 cells and noncancerous HEK293T cells, highlighting its potential
use in cancer diagnosis.
 
8.5.

## Nicotinamide Adenine Dinucleotide
Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD) and its phosphate ester (NADP)
serve crucial roles as coenzymes in intracellular redox reactions. The NAD+/NADH and NADP+/NADPH redox couples are important to maintaining intracellular redox
homeostasis and regulating various cellular processes such as energy
metabolism, calcium homeostasis, gene expression, and immunological
functions. Imbalances in these two redox couples have been linked
to various pathological conditions including aging, neurodegenerative
diseases, cardiovascular diseases, and cancer.
Suresh, Maiti,
and co-workers have designed an iridium(III) complex featuring two N-methylquinolinium moieties (379) ( ) as an NADH-activatable
theranostic agent. Reduction of the
electron-deficient quinolinium moiety by NADH through hydride transfer
increases the emission intensity of the complex due to inhibited PET
quenching. Additionally, the reaction can weaken the interaction between
the complex and AuNPs, leading to a reduction in the surface-enhanced
Raman scattering signal. These signal changes offer the possibilities
for the detection of NADH using a bimodal approach. The complex has
been used to detect endogenous NADH in cancer cells under different
metabolic stimulation. Furthermore, the intracellular reaction of
the complex with NADH not only induces NADH depletion but also increases
the efficiency of the complex in generating 1O2 and NAD* upon irradiation, resulting in redox imbalance to trigger
apoptosis.
 
8.6.

## Biothiols
Biothiols are crucial in
preventing oxidative stress and maintaining intracellular redox homeostasis
for normal cellular functions. Alternations in intracellular biothiol
levels can serve as an indicator of different pathological states.
For example, Cys regulates protein structure and function, and its
dysregulation has been associated with various syndromes such as neurotoxicity,
liver damage, muscle and fat loss, skin lesions, and slowed growth. Homocysteine (Hcy), an intermediate from the
metabolism of methionine, is a risk factor for cardiovascular and neurodegenerative disease when its levels are elevated. The most abundant biothiol
in mammalian cells is GSH (ca. 1–10 mM), which
is crucial in maintaining redox homeostasis, metabolizing xenobiotics,
and modulating immune response. Increased
intracellular GSH levels have been implicated in cancer development
and resistance to chemotherapy. 
Transition metal complexes have been designed as phosphorogenic probes
for biothiols based on nucleophilic aromatic substitution, which separates
an emission quencher from the luminophore to induce phosphorescence
turn-on. Zhao and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II)-based phosphorogenic
probe (380) ( ) for biothiols through the functionalization of [Ru(phen)3]2+ with a 2,4-dinitrophenyl (2,4-DNP) moiety via
a thiol-responsive sulfonamide linker. This complex is nonluminescent in solution due to efficient emission
quenching by PET from [Ru(phen)3]2+ to the electron-deficient
2,4-DNP unit. However, it displays up to 90-fold emission enhancement
upon reaction with biothiols that cleaves the sulfonamide linker and
separates the 2,4-DNP moiety from the [Ru(phen)3]2+ core. The complex has been used to detect endogenous biothiols in
human small-cell lung carcinoma NCI-H446 cells, and its specific reaction
with intracellular biothiols is supported by the reduced emission
intensity when the cells are pretreated with the thiol scavenger NEM.
Lo and co-workers have designed a series of rhenium(I) complexes appended
with a 2,4-DNP moiety (381) as thiol-activatable probes
and photosensitizers. The intracellular
reaction of the complexes with biothiols switches on their phosphorescence
and 1O2-photosensitization properties, resulting
in higher emission intensity and photocytotoxic activity in cells
with a higher concentration of biothiols (e.g., HeLa
cells). Additionally, complex 381c is specifically enriched
in the mitochondria of the cells after celluar uptake due to the cationic
charge and high lipophilicity, while complex 381d preferentially
localizes in the ER due to the appended tosylamide moiety.
 Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers have also developed
a ruthenium(II)
complex (382) ( ), where the luminophore is conjugated with the 2,4-DNP
unit via a sulfonate ester linkage, for the detection of intracellular
thiols. Modification of a related ruthenium(II)
complex with a morpholine unit yields a phosphorogenic probe (383) that enables the specific detection of biothiols in the
lysosomes of cells. Furthermore, the
complex has been used to image biothiols in D. magna. The complex is transported to their body through feeding, and its
reaction with biothiols present in the digestive system results in
intense emission in the esophagus and gut regions. Zheng, Ge, and
co-workers have utilized 2,4-DNP-functionalized mono- and binuclear
ruthenium(II) complexes such as 384( ) and 385( ) that
exhibit rapid and selective emission turn-on in response to biothiols
for imaging endogenous biothiols in live Glioma cells. The steric
interactions between the 1H-imidazo[4,5-f][1,10]phenanthroline ligand and the 2,4-DNP moiety facilitate the
reaction with thiols, allowing the reaction to complete within 10
min. Zhao, Li, Huang, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
2,4-DNP complex (386) that shows high selectivity toward
Cys and Hcy over GSH. The emission intensity
of the complex significantly increases in the presence of Cys and
Hcy, while incubation with GSH results in negligible changes due to
the steric hindrance around its sulfhydryl group that hinders its
reaction with the complex. Ruthenium(II) (387) and iridium(III) complexes (388) functionalized with a 2,4-DNP unit
via a (thio)ether linker have also been developed as thiol-responsive
probes and used for imaging biothiols in live cells and organisms.
 Zhang, Yuan, Zhang, and co-workers have designed
a ruthenium(II)
complex (389) ( ) that can detect total biothiols and differentiate
between Cys and Hcy from GSH both in vitro and in vivo. The molecular design
features the conjugation of [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with
7-nitro-2,1,3-benzoxadiazole (NBD) via an ether linkage, which enables
efficient quenching of the luminescence of both [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and NBD through PET and O-substitution at the 4-position,
respectively. The reaction of complex 389 with Cys and
Hcy produces a red phosphorescent ruthenium(II) complex (λem = 628 nm) and a green fluorescent NBD derivative NBD-NHR
(R = Cys and Hcy) (λem = 540 nm).
The amine-substituted NBD derivative NBD-NHR is generated from the
intermediate NBD-SR through an intramolecular S → N rearrangement. However, reaction of the complex with GSH
only switches on the red phosphorescence as the intramolecular rearrangement
reaction is not feasible in NBD-S(GSH). The drastic difference in
the emission colors of the reaction products has facilitated the facile
discrimination of Cys and Hcy from GSH in live cells, zebrafish, and
mice ( ).
Furthermore, the long emission lifetime of the complex (> 300 ns)
has allowed for the detection of changes in total biothiols in live
cells with increased sensitivity by using time-gated techniques to
remove the shorter-lived fluorescence from the cells and NBD-NHR (0.80
ns).
 Liu, Huang, Zhao, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III)
complex (390) ( ) that enables selective GSH sensing. The introduction of two electron-deficient N-alkylpyridinium moieties enables efficient emission quenching by
PET. However, the electron transfer from GSH to the pyridinium moiety
increases its susceptibility to nucleophilic attack and promotes the
substitution reaction by hydroxide in aqueous solution, resulting
in substantial emission enhancement (I/Io = 12.5) and lifetime extension (from 84.7 to 690.5 ns)
that are not observed when the complex is incubated with Cys, Hcy,
or H2S. The high selectivity of the complex toward GSH
has allowed for the differentiation of cancer cells (HeLa, A549, and
HepG2) from noncancerous cells (3T3-L1 and HL-7702) by detecting intracellular
GSH levels using LSCM and PLIM ( ). Additionally, the reaction with GSH increases
the 1O2 photosensitization efficiency of the
complex, resulting in higher photocytotoxicity toward cancer cells
than normal cells due to different intracellular GSH levels.
 Phosphorogenic probes that detect biothiols based
on azo or disulfide
reduction have also been developed. Chao and co-workers have designed
an azo-bridged binuclear iridium(III) complex (391) ( ) to visualize
biothiols in live cells. The complex
is nonluminescent due to the quenching azo unit. However, reduction
of the azo unit by biothiols via a two-electron transfer process yields
a nonquenching azo anion, resulting in substantial emission enhancement
(I/Io = 30 to 38). Interestingly,
the mononuclear complex does not exhibit a similar reaction, implying
that the reactivity and selectivity of the recognition unit can be
significantly altered by conjugation to a metal center. A related
binuclear ruthenium(II) complex (392) has been developed
as a thiol-activatable photosensitizer for two-photon-induced PDT
in cancer cells and MCTSs. The complex
shows strong TPA (δ810nm = 210 GM),
increased emission intensity (I/Io = 50), and high 1O2 generation
efficiency (ΦΔ = 0.40) upon
reduction of the azo bridge by GSH to a hydrazo derivative.
 Yu, Lo, and co-workers have designed a rhenium(I)
complex conjugated
with a perylene diimide (PDI) moiety through a disulfide linker (393) ( ) for detecting biothiols in live cells. The complex displays very weak emission in aqueous solution due
to efficient quenching of rhenium(I) phosphorescence (λem = 557 nm) by FRET and PDI fluorescence (λem = 661 nm) by aggregation. Reductive cleavage of the
disulfide bond by thiols separates the two luminescent entities, resulting
in a substantial increase in the intensity (I/Io = 14.7 to 29.2) and lifetime (from < 10
ns to 1.08 μs) of rhenium(I) phosphorescence. Additionally,
the complex exhibits high specificity for mitochondria, making it
a valuable tool for specific detection of biothiols in these organelles.
Yi, Liang, and co-workers have developed a self-assembling ruthenium(II)
complex (394), where [Ru(bpy)3]2+ is conjugated with a thiol-responsive self-assembling motif C(StBu)K-CBT (CBT = 2-cyanobenzothiazole), for imaging cancer
cells and tumors with long-lasting emission. Reductive cleavage of the disulfide bond by the high levels of intracellular
biothiols in cancer cells exposes the 1,2-aminothiol group on the
Cys motif, which initiates its intermolecular CBT-Cys click reaction
and leads to the formation of oligomers that self-assemble into NPs.
Thus, the complex shows enhanced retention and persistent emission
in live HepG2 cells and tumor-bearing mice compared to [Ru(bpy)3]2+ ( ).
 Transition metal complexes have also been designed
for the detection
of biothiols based on 1,4-addition reactions. Zhao, Huang, and co-workers
have developed an iridium(III) complex bearing two α,β-unsaturated
ketone moieties (395) ( ) for imaging biothiols in live cells. The complex displays an increase in emission
intensity and lifetime upon incubation with Cys but not in the presence
of Hcy or GSH, enabling the selective visualization of Cys in live
cells using LSCM and PLIM. The photophysical changes are associated
with the suppression of nonradiative decay from the 3LLCT
state upon the addition of Cys to the α,β-unsaturated
ketone unit, as suggested by time-dependent density functional theory
(TD-DFT) calculations. The complex has also been adsorbed onto the
shell of core–shell structured MSNs, where the core is doped
with the thiol-insensitive iridium(III) complex 184a,
to construct a nanoprobe for detecting Cys with ratiometric response.
Zhao and co-workers have developed a luminescent soft salt (396) for ratiometric detection of Cys and Hcy. In the absence of thiols, the soft salt exhibits
long-lived blue-green emission (λem = 485 and 505 nm) originating from the anionic, thiol-insensitive
complex. Incubation of the soft salt with Cys substantially and selectively
increases the emission intensity and lifetime of the cationic thiol-responsive
complex (λem = 560 nm), allowing
the detection of Cys and Hcy in live cells by ratiometric response
and lifetime changes. Related iridium(III) complexes incorporating
the α,β-unsaturated ketone moieties in the diimine ligand
(397) have also been developed for selective imaging
of Cys and Hcy in live cells, zebrafish, and mice. , Li and co-workers have developed two related NIR luminescent iridium(III)
complexes (398) as probes for biothiols. Interestingly, treatment of the complexes with
GSH induces a larger increase in their emission intensities than Cys
and Hcy, enabling the sensitive detection of GSH in live HeLa cells.
The complexes have also been used for PDT due to their increased 1O2 generation efficiencies upon reaction with GSH.
Li and co-workers have reported on an analogous ruthenium(II) complex
(399) that can differentiate Cys from Hcy and GSH in
live cells and zebrafish. Incubation
of the complex with Cys results in a 30-fold increase in emission
intensity, but negligible photophysical changes are observed with
Hcy and GSH. The high selectivity of the complex toward Cys arises
from its relatively smaller steric hindrance and stronger electrostatic
attraction with the complex at physiological pH compared to the other
two biothiols.
 Chao and co-workers have designed iridium(III)
complexes bearing
two α,β-unsaturated nitroolefin moieties as the recognition
sites (400) ( ) for detecting biothiols in live cells. These complexes are weakly emissive due to
PET from [Ir(ppy)2(bpy)]+ to the nitroolefin
unit. However, the addition of thiols to the nitroolefin unit suppresses
the PET process, resulting in substantial emission enhancement. Zhang,
Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) nitroolefin
complex (401) that is selective toward Cys over Hcy and
GSH. Notably, the addition reaction
with Cys triggers the elimination of the quenching nitro group, resulting
in substantial emission enhancement within a short incubation time
(< 30 min). In contrast, exposure of the complex to Hcy and GSH
for more than 2 h only results in a minor increase in emission intensity.
The different response time allows the complex to distinguish Cys
from the other two biothiols, facilitating the selective imaging of
Cys in live cells, D. magna, zebrafish larvae, and
mice. Furthermore, the complex shows high specificity for mitochondria
and has been utilized to examine Cys-mediated mitochondrial redox
activities in murine macrophage J774A.1 cells, revealing a reduction
in the mitochondrial Cys levels during inflammation.
 Transition metal complexes have also been designed
to selectively
detect Cys and Hcy based on their cyclization reaction with aldehydes
that forms thiazolidine and thiazinane derivatives, respectively.
Chen, Li, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex [Ir(pba)2(bpy)]+ (402) ( ) that displays ratiometric
response toward Cys and Hcy. The complex
exhibits a structured emission band at 547 nm with a shoulder at 586
nm in aqueous solution. Upon reaction with Cys and Hcy, the intensity
of the emission band at 547 nm decreases, giving rise to a broad and
structureless band with a maximum at 586 nm. As suggested by TD-DFT
calculations, the cyclization reaction perturbs the p−π
conjugation within the pba ligand, resulting in a conversion of the
excited-state nature from 3IL (π → π*)
(pba) to an admixture of 3MLCT (dπ(Ir) → π*(bpy))
and 3LLCT (π(N∧C) → π*(bpy)).
The ratiometric response has enabled the selective detection of Cys/Hcy
in live KB cells. Related iridium(III) complexes modified with a quaternary
ammonium group (403), a
poly(N-isopropylacrylamide)
(PNIPAM) chain (404 and 405), , or an alkoxysilyl group for conjugation to MSNs (406) to increase their aqueous solubility
and biocompatibility for imaging Cys and Hcy in live cells. Interestingly,
Li and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex (407) that can discriminate between Cys and Hcy by introducing two additional
amino groups to the bpy ligand. Treatment
of the complex with Cys leads to a moderate decrease in the emission
intensity at ca. 585 nm and a small blue shift of ca. 10 nm. In contrast, incubation with Hcy not only reduces
the emission intensity at 585 nm but also substantially increases
the emission intensity at ca. 495 nm, enabling the
ratiometric detection of Hcy in live cells. Hu, Wu, and co-workers
have developed an iridium(III) complex (408) for two-photon
luminescence imaging of Cys and Hcy in living systems. The reaction with Cys and Hcy changes the excited-state
nature of the complex from 3ILCT (π(phenylamine)
→ π*(terpyridine)) to an admixture of 3MLCT
(dπ(Ir) → π*(terpyridine)) and 3LLCT
(π(N∧C) → π*(terpyridine)), resulting
in 12- and 18-fold emission enhancement, respectively. It has been
used to visualize mitochondrial Cys and Hcy in live cells, MCTSs,
zebrafish, and mouse tissue slices due to its high mitochondria specificity.
Shi, Fan, and co-workers have designed an iridium(III) complex (409) that shows emission enhancement in the NIR region (λem = 684 nm) upon reaction with Cys and
Hcy in live cells and mice. Li, Qi,
and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex modified with
an aldehyde unit (410) that is selective toward Hcy over
Cys. The complex displays enhanced emission
upon reaction with Hcy but not with Cys, which is attributed to the
larger pKa of Hcy-derived thiazinane (>
8.0) compared to Cys-derived thiazolidine (> 5.0), which renders
PET
quenching not favorable at physiological pH.
 Zhang and co-workers have developed an AIE-active
platinum(II)
complex (411) ( ) as a Cys-selective theranostic agent. The coordination of the complex with Cys induces
a significant increase in the two-photon (δ900nm increases from 187.3 to 4,140.23 GM) and three-photon
absorption cross-sections (σ1200nm increases from 0 to 3.41 × 10–74 cm6 s2 photon–2) and emission intensity,
which are not observed in the presence of Hcy and GSH. Upon cellular
internalization, the complex specifically resides in the lysosomes
of cells. It has been used to examine Cys-related cellular processes,
indicating that cystine can be absorbed in cells through the cystine/glutamate
transporter and is then converted to Cys by enzymes. Furthermore,
treatment of cancer cells (HeLa and HepG2) with the complex results
in more intense intracellular emission than normal cells (HUVEC and
MH-S) due to the high levels of Cys in cancer cells, indicating that
the complex can distinguish cancer tissues from normal tissues based
on different Cys levels. Thus, the complex can effectively target
the tumor tissues and inhibit the tumor growth in vivo.
 Chao and Zhang have conjugated an iridium(III)
complex with a copper(II)
terpyridine complex for the detection of Hcy in aqueous media. Upon reduction of copper(II) by Hcy to copper(I),
complex 412 ( ) aggregates to form NPs due to reduced aqueous solubility.
This results in 1,500-fold emission enhancement within 2 min, which
is not observed for Cys and GSH. The complex localizes in the cytoplasm
of HeLa cells and the intracellular emission intensity diminishes
when the cells are pretreated with NEM and recovers when the treated
cells are further incubated with Hcy, confirming that the emission
originates from its reaction with Hcy.
 Leung, Ma, and co-workers have developed a MnO2 nanosheet-assisted
sensing platform for label-free detection of GSH. The emission of the iridium(III) complex 413 ( ) is
quenched in the presence of the MnO2 nanosheet, but it
is restored upon reduction of the MnO2 nanosheet by GSH
into Mn2+. The emission response is selective toward GSH
over common cellular reductants (e.g., Cys, Hcy,
NAD(P)H, and ascorbic acid). The nanoplatform has been applied to
monitor the GSH levels in live HeLa cells and zebrafish. Song and
co-workers have designed a nanoprobe, where [Ru(bpy)3]2+ is adsorbed onto the surface of MnO2 nanosheets,
for bimodal luminescence imaging and MRI of GSH. The GSH-induced reduction of MnO2 nanosheets
results in remarkable enhancement in both emission and MR signals
due to the release of [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and Mn2+, respectively, enabling luminescence imaging and MRI of
GSH in live cells, zebrafish, and tumor-bearing mice. Chao and co-workers
have modified AuNPs with an amine-containing ruthenium(II) complex
(414) for two-photon luminescence imaging of thiols in
live cells and tissues. In the presence
of thiols, the complex is displaced from the surface of the AuNPs,
resulting in an increase in the emission intensity (I/Io = ca. 30) and TPA
cross-section (δ800nm = up to 350
GM). These photophysical changes have allowed two-photon luminescence
imaging of thiols in live cells and tissues at depths of 80–170
μm.
 
8.7.

## Gasotransmitters
Gasotransmitters
are a class of gaseous molecules that mediate cellular signaling processes. Nitric oxide (NO), carbon monoxide (CO), and
hydrogen sulfide (H2S) are three endogenous gasotransmitters
that play crucial roles in regulating a wide range of physiological
and pathological processes. These gaseous molecules also possess significant
therapeutic potential, prompting the
development of stimuli-responsive gasotransmitter prodrugs that enable
controlled release at the target site. 
8.7.1.

## Nitric Oxide
Nitric oxide (NO)
is the first identified gasotransmitter and recognized for its role
as an endothelial-derived relaxation factor. In mammalian cells, NO is endogenously produced by three different
isoforms of NO synthases (NOSs), including neuronal NOS, endothelial
NOS, and inducible NOS. These enzymes
catalyze the conversion of l-arginine to l-citrulline
and NO in the presence of O2 and NADPH. At low concentrations,
NO plays an important role as a gasotransmitter in regulating vasodilation,
neurotransmission, and immune response. However, high levels of NO
can trigger the formation of reactive nitrogen species (RNS), which
have been associated with cardiac diseases, inflammation, neurodegenerative
disorders, and cancer. 
Lippard
and co-workers have pioneered the development of copper(II) complexes
as fluorogenic probes for the visualization of NO in live cells. The paramagnetic Cu2+ functions
as both an emission quencher for the fluorescent ligand and a recognition
unit for NO. The reduction of Cu2+ to diamagnetic Cu+ by NO is concomitant with nitrosylation of the ligand, resulting
in ligand dissociation from the reduced copper(I) center and thus
fluorescence turn-on. This seminal work has stimulated the development
of copper(II) complexes as fluorogenic probes for NO, − offering valuable tools for investigating the physiological and
pathological functions of NO in living systems.
Phosphorescent
transition metal complexes have also been developed
as phosphorogenic probes for NO using an indirect approach based on
the reaction of o-phenylenediamine derivatives with
N2O3, the autoxidation product of NO. Ye, Yuan,
and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II) complex (415) ( ) for
imaging NO production in live cells. The introduction of an electron-rich o-diaminophenyl
group effectively quenches the phosphorescence of the complex by PET.
Upon reaction with NO under aerobic conditions, the o-diaminophenyl group is converted to a nonquenching triazole derivative,
resulting in substantial emission enhancement. The emission response
is rapid (< 2 min) and specific toward NO over other ROS and RNS.
The complex has been used to image exogenous NO in live murine macrophage
cells and endogenous NO in gardenia cells. Lo and co-workers have
developed rhenium(I) complexes containing an o-diaminoaromatic
unit (416) for the detection of NO. However, these complexes could not be converted to their
triazole counterparts in the intracellular environment due to their
low reactivity toward NO. Thus, Lo and co-workers have designed another
class of rhenium(I) complexes (417), wherein an electron-donating
methoxy group is introduced to the o-diaminoaromatic
unit to enhance the electron density and thus the reactivity of the
diamino group toward NO. Additionally,
an insulating methylene linker is introduced to separate the o-diaminoaromatic unit from the electron-withdrawing rhenium(I)
center. These modifications have enabled the successful sensing of
exogenous and endogenous NO in live HeLa and RAW264.7 cells, respectively.
Related iridium(III) complexes (418) have also been developed
for intracellular NO sensing. 
 Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers have developed a
ruthenium(II) complex
coordinated with a 5,6-diamino-1,10-phenanthroline ligand (419) ( ) as
