a bimodal probe for NO. The complex
undergoes rapid reaction with NO and the reaction completes within
20 s, inhibiting the PET process and thus switching on both the phosphorescence
and electrochemiluminescence of the complex. The emission enhancement
has enabled the visualization of NO production in live tobacco cells
and in onion inner-layer epidermal peels. Chao and co-workers have
developed an iridium(III) complex (420a) as a two-photon
probe for specific imaging of NO in the mitochondria. The rapid emission response (< 10 s) and
high mitochondria specificity of the complex have allowed real-time
monitoring of NO generation in the mitochondria upon stimulation.
The strong TPA (δ730nm = 65.0 GM)
and intense emission (Φem = 0.149)
of the resultant triazole complex have enabled the visualization of
endogenous NO in live HeLa cells, MCTSs, and zebrafish at an increased
imaging depth using two-photon microscopy (λex = 730 nm). A related iridium(III) complex (420b) has also been developed for imaging NO in live cells. Chao and co-workers have designed a related
iridium(III) complex modified with a lysosome-targeting morpholine
moiety (421) for selective imaging of lysosomal NO. Additionally, the introduction of a benzyl
unit to the o-diaminoaromatic unit increases the
resistance of the complex to changes in pH in the range of 4–10.
Reaction of the complex with NO results in a significant increase
in the emission intensity (I/Io = ca. 350) and lifetime (from 200.1 to 619.6
ns) and the TPA cross-section (δ730nm = 60 GM), enabling the visualization of NO production in live RAW264.7
cells and zebrafish in response to inflammation using LSCM and PLIM.
Furthermore, the use of PLIM can provide a dynamic read-out of the
ratio of the unreacted (100–300 ns) and reacted probe (500–700
ns) in living systems based on their different emission lifetimes,
revealing the continuous generation of NO in inflamed cells and zebrafish.
 Chao and co-workers have designed an NIR luminescent
iridium(III)
complex (422) ( ) that enables the simultaneous detection and differentiation
of N2O3 and peroxynitrite (ONOO–), the two downstream products of endogenous NO. The complex exhibits significant emission enhancement and
lifetime extension upon reaction with N2O3 and
ONOO–. Notably, the emission lifetimes of the complex
(69.87 ns) and its reaction products with N2O3 (697.3 ns) and ONOO– (783.6 ns) differ significantly,
allowing for their facile detection and differentiation in live cells
and zebrafish using PLIM and TGLM ( ).
 
8.7.2.

## Carbon Monoxide
Carbon monoxide
(CO) is endogenously generated in mammalian cells as a by-product
of heme catabolism catalyzed by heme oxygenases. The endogenous CO serves as an important signaling molecule,
particularly in the cardiovascular and nervous systems. Abnormal metabolism
and dysregulated function of CO have been implicated in diseases such
as inflammation, hypertension, heart failure, and neurodegenerative
disorders.
Chang and co-workers have pioneered the design of
palladium(II) complexes as fluorogenic probes for selective imaging
of CO in live cells. , The presence of palladium quenches
the fluorescence of its appended ligands due to the heavy atom effect.
However, the binding of CO to the metal center triggers a carbonylation
reaction, leading to the release of palladium(0) and subsequent fluorescence
turn-on. Palladium(II) complexes have also been developed for imaging
CO in live cells and tissues based on a different sensing mechanism,
where the interaction of palladium with CO results in protonolysis
of the Pd–C bond. − Alongside these developments, Wilton-Ely and co-workers have designed
ruthenium(II) − and osmium(II) vinyl complexes as fluorogenic probes that enable rapid, selective,
and sensitive detection of endogenous CO. The binding of CO to the
metal center dissociates the fluorescent ligand, leading to substantial
fluorescence enhancement. The development of these metal-based probes
offers new opportunities to unveil the roles of CO and its implications
in a wide range of biological processes.
In a therapeutic viewpoint,
CO can serve as a potential treatment
for human diseases such as cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory disorders,
and tissue injury due to its anti-inflammatory and cytoprotective
effects. This has led to the development
of CO-releasing molecules (CORMs) that allow controlled CO release
in living systems. Extensive research
on CORMs has primarily focused on metal carbonyl complexes due to
the strong but modifiable binding of CO to transition metals. Some
of these complexes have been employed as an exogenous source of CO
to examine its biological activity. Of particular interest are photoactivatable
CORMs (photoCORMs) that utilize light to provide precise spatial and
temporal control over CO release. Among
the various photoCORMs, luminescent rhenium(I) tricarbonyl complexes
show significant promise as theranostic photoCORMs as their cellular
uptake and subsequent CO photorelease can be followed by LSCM. − 

8.7.3.

## Hydrogen Sulfide
Hydrogen sulfide
(H2S) is endogenously produced in mammals from the metabolism
of Hcy by cystathionine-β-synthase, cystathionine-γ-lyase,
and 3-mercaptopyruvate sulfurtransferase. It plays an important role in various biological processes such
as vasodilation, neurotransmission, and anti-inflammation. Abnormal levels of H2S have been
implicated in several pathological conditions such as inflammation,
hypertension, diabetes, cirrhosis, neurodegenerative diseases, and
cancer. 
Transition metal complexes
have been developed as probes for H2S by exploiting its
high nucleophilicity under physiological conditions. Zhang, Zhang,
Yuan, and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II) complex modified
with a 2,4-DNP moiety via an ester linkage (423) ( ) for the detection
of H2S. The ester linker
is susceptible to nucleophilic attack by H2S and thus upon
incubation with H2S, it is cleaved through a H2S-mediated thiolysis reaction, resulting in ca.
86-fold emission enhancement due to the separation of the quenching
2,4-DNP moiety from [Ru(bpy)3]2+. The absence
of a similar increase in emission intensity upon treatment of the
complex with biothiols indicates the high selectivity of the complex
toward H2S. The complex has enabled the monitoring of H2S generation in the lysosomes of inflamed J774A.1 cells and
the visualization of H2S in living organisms such as D. magna, zebrafish, and mice ( ). Li, Tang, Zhang, and co-workers have
made similar modifications on a mitochondria-targeting iridium(III)
complex (424a) for imaging H2S in live cells
and mice. A related iridium(III) complex
(424b) has been developed as a mitochondria-targeting,
H2S-responsive theranostic agent. Since H2S can stimulate GSH production and suppress oxidative
stress in mitochondria, the specific reaction of complex 424b with H2S lowers intracellular and mitochondrial GSH levels.
Thus, the complex efficiently catalyzes the oxidation of NADH to NAD+ and generates H2O2 upon continuous
irradiation, resulting in very high photocytotoxic activity (IC50 = 0.011 to 0.016 μM). Lo and co-workers have utilized
the iridium(III) 2,4-DNP complexes 388 that display substantial
emission enhancement upon thiolysis of the 2,4-DNP unit to visualize
H2S in live cells. Yuan,
Zhang, and co-workers have conjugated [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with an NBD unit to develop a phosphorogenic probe (425) for imaging H2S in living systems. The complex is weakly emissive in aqueous solution (Φem = 0.0003) due to efficient quenching by PET. Reaction of
the complex with H2S triggers the thiolysis of the NBD
moiety, resulting in > 65-fold emission enhancement. The complex
has
been used to visualize H2S in live HeLa cells, D. magna, and zebrafish. Additionally, since the complex
is an efficient photosensitizer for 1O2, both
before and after H2S activation, it has been utilized to
self-report changes in H2S levels in response to the PDT
treatment, revealing a gradual increase in intracellular H2S levels during photoinduced cell death. 
 Chao and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex appended
with two acrylate ester units (426) ( ) that enables the sensitive
detection of H2S in live cells. The cleavage of the acrylate ester moieties by H2S induces
a 120-fold increase in the emission intensity of the complex, which
is not observed for Cys, Hcy, and GSH due to the higher pKa and larger size of the biothiols than H2S.
Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have developed a nanosensor for ratiometric
and lifetime imaging of H2S. The nanosensor is constructed by embedding a H2S-responsive
merocyanine derivative (MC) and a H2S-insensitive
iridium(III) complex (427) into MSNs. In the absence
of H2S, the nanosensor exhibits two emission bands at 500
and 596 nm, originating from complex 427 and MC, respectively. Incubation of the nanosensor with H2S
selectively quenches the merocyanine fluorescence as the nucleophilic
addition of HS– to the indolenium C-2 atom of MC disrupts the conjugated system, resulting in a change in
the emission color (from red to blue) and lifetime (from 215.0 to
135.0 ns). These photophysical changes have enabled ratiometric and
lifetime imaging of H2S in live HepG2 cells.
 In addition to its high nucleophilicity, H2S is also
known for its strong ability to reduce electron-withdrawing azide
and nitro groups to electron-donating amino groups. Ma and co-workers
have developed an iridium(III) complex coordinated with a 5-nitro-1,10-phenanthroline
ligand (428) ( ) for detecting H2S through nitro reduction. The complex is nonluminescent due to efficient
quenching through PET from the iridium(III) core to the electron-deficient
nitro unit. However, PET is inhibited upon reduction of the nitro
unit by H2S to an electron-donating amino group, resulting
in a substantial increase in the emission intensity (I/Io = ca. 38). The emission
turn-on has enabled the sensitive detection of H2S in live
HeLa cells and zebrafish.
 Nagano and co-workers have pioneered the exploitation
of copper(II)
chelates as both an emission quencher for fluorescent ligands and
a recognition site for H2S to develop fluorogenic probes
for selective imaging of H2S in live cells. These probes show remarkable fluorescence turn-on
in the presence of H2S due to its high affinity for Cu2+ that leads to CuS precipitation. Based on this metal displacement
approach, a number of copper(II) complexes have been developed as
luminogenic probes for imaging H2S in vitro and in vivo. − Zhang, Xu, and co-workers have designed a luminescent nanosensor
for ratiometric detection of H2S. The nanosensor is constructed by loading the ruthenium(II) DPA complex 325 into the pores of MSNs that are modified with fluorescein
as a reference unit. The introduction of Cu2+ to the NPs
selectively quenches the ruthenium(II) phosphorescence (λem = 600 nm) without affecting the fluorescein fluorescence
(λem = 520 nm), enabling the detection
of H2S with ratiometric response. Treatment of the nanosensor
results in a substantial increase in the ratio I600nm/I520nm within 5 s due to
CuS precipitation. The nanosensor has been used for ratiometric imaging
of H2S in live MCF-7 cells. Monitoring of the H2S levels in cells reveals that the H2S levels increase
upon LPS treatment and then decrease when the inflamed cells are incubated
with dexamethasone, indicating the role of dexamethasone as an anti-inflammation
drug to inhibit cystathionine-γ-lyase expression and H2S production.
Liu, Lee, Leung, Ma, and co-workers have utilized
an iridium(III)
complex (429) ( ) and Fe3+ to develop a sensing platform
for the sensitive and selective detection of H2S with “Off–On–Off”
emission response. The emission of the
complex is quenched in the presence of Fe3+ but restores
upon reaction of Fe3+ with S2– to give
Fe2S3. The probe can detect H2S with
high sensitivity and selectivity even in the presence of a high thiol
background. It has been used to visualize the enzymatic production
of H2S in live HeLa cells and zebrafish using Cys or GSH
as the substrate.
 

8.8.

## Reactive Oxygen and Nitrogen Species
Reactive oxygen and nitrogen species (RONS) constitute a group of
free radicals and reactive small molecules such as O2•–, HO•, alkoxy radicals, peroxy
radicals, H2O2, HClO, 1O2, NO, nitroxyl, and ONOO–. These highly reactive
entities serve diverse roles and functions within cells. However, the excessive production of these
reactive species can lead to severe oxidative stress, which has been
implicated in the development and progression of aging and various diseases. 
8.8.1.

## Superoxide Anion Radical
Superoxide
anion radical (O2•–) is an important
precursor of most RONS. It is generated through one-electron reduction
of O2 and can be converted by superoxide dismutase (SOD)
into H2O2, or react with NO to form ONOO–. It plays an important role as a cellular signaling
molecule involved in various physiological and pathological processes
including cellular defense, metabolic homeostasis, and gene expression
regulation. However, the overproduction
of O2•– can lead to oxidative
stress and is implicated in diseases such as inflammation, neurodegeneration,
and cancer. 
Kim, Kim, and co-workers
have designed a nanosensor for afterglow imaging of acute kidney injury in vivo using an O2•–-responsive iridium(III) complex (430) ( ). The complex is weakly emissive (Φem = 0.01) due to the electron-deficient triflate groups. Upon exposure
to O2•–, the emission intensity
of the complex increases as the triflate groups are susceptible to
nucleophilic attack by O2•–. The
complex has been used to monitor the endogenous O2•– levels in HEK293 cells and mice, revealing
the overproduction of O2•– in
kidney cells upon cisplatin-induced injury. It has also demonstrated
the protective effect of l-carnitine, an amino acid that
can reduce the level of lipid peroxidase and enhance the activities
of GSH, glutathione S-transferase (GST), and SOD,
against cisplatin-induced oxidative stress. Using complex 430 as an afterglow initiator and relay unit, and rubrene as an afterglow
substrate and luminophore, an O2•–-specific nanosensor that displays intense and long-lasting afterglow
luminescence (> 10 min after preexcitation) with deep-tissue penetration
has been developed, enabling the background-free in vivo imaging of O2•– overproduced
in mice with acute kidney injury.
 
8.8.2.

## Hydrogen Peroxide
Hydrogen peroxide
(H2O2) is endogenously generated through various
enzymatic reactions, including the dismutation of O2•– by SOD and the oxidation of various substrates
by oxidases and peroxidases. It is an
important signaling molecule involved in the regulation of cell proliferation,
migration, and differentiation. It also plays a key role in host defense
and immune response. However, excessive production of H2O2 has been linked to various pathological conditions
such as cancer, diabetes, cardiovascular disease, and neurodegenerative
disorders. 
Ma and co-workers have
developed a nanosensor for sensitive detection of H2O2. The nanosensor utilizes AgNPs
as a responsive unit and an iridium(III) complex (431) ( ) as
a signaling unit. The emission of the complex is effectively quenched
upon adsorption onto the AgNPs due to efficient energy transfer. However,
in the presence of H2O2, the AgNPs are oxidized
to Ag+, releasing the complex and thus resulting in emission
enhancement. This nanosensor can be generated in situ in live HeLa cells through simultaneous treatment of the cells with
the AgNPs and the complex. The enhanced intracellular emission upon
increasing the exogenous H2O2 concentration
indicates that the nanosensor can react with H2O2 in the intracellular environment.
 
8.8.3.

## Hypochlorous Acid
Hypochlorous
acid (HClO) is a nonradical ROS that partially dissociates into hypochlorite
(ClO–) at physiological pH. It is endogenously produced
from the oxidation of Cl– by H2O2 catalyzed by myeloperoxidase (MPO), which primarily localizes
in leukocytes such as macrophages, monocytes, and neutrophils in response
to inflammation, playing a pivotal role in host defense against microbial
invasions. However, excessive levels
of HClO can lead to oxidative stress and contribute to the development
of various pathological conditions, including neurodegenerative disorders,
cardiovascular diseases, and cancer.
Zhao, Huang, and co-workers
have utilized two luminescent iridium(III) complexes with different
responsiveness toward HClO and core–shell structured SiNPs
to develop a luminescent nanoprobe for ratiometric detection of HClO. The iridium(III) complex 432 ( ) contains two
oxime moieties that provide a facile nonradiative deactivation pathway
through photoinduced isomerization of the C=N group. Reaction of the
complex with HClO leads to oxidative hydrolysis of the oxime unit
to a carboxyl group, resulting in an increase in the emission intensity
(I/Io = ca. 9.2) and lifetime (from 265 to 410 ns) at 598 nm. This HClO-responsive
complex is incorporated into the mesoporous silica shell of SiNPs
for efficient HClO recognition, whereas the HClO-inert iridium(III)
complex 184a (λem =
479 and 500 nm) is immobilized in the solid core as an internal standard.
Incubation of the nanoprobe with HClO selectively switches on the
red emission of complex 432, resulting in an increase
in the ratio I598nm/I500nm (from 0.42 to 2.40) and the lifetime of the red
emission (from 265 to 406 ns). These photophysical changes have allowed
imaging of exogenous and endogenous HClO in live RAW264.7 cells via
ratiometric and lifetime imaging. Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have
also designed an iridium(III) complex (433) that exhibits
relatively similar distribution in the plasma membrane and cytoplasm,
which not only facilitates the detection of both exogenous and endogenous
HClO but also enables their differentiation from each other. Zhang, Lv, and co-workers have developed an
iridium(III) complex (434) for two-photon luminescence
imaging of endogenous HClO in live cells. Monitoring of endogenous HClO generation has allowed the differentiation
between macrophage RAW264.7 cells and epithelial A549 cells due to
the their different levels of HClO production during inflammation.
This behavior has facilitated the visualization of the spatial distribution
of macrophage cells in tissues obtained from an inflamed mouse model
using two-photon microscopy. Lv and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex (435) that enables multimodal imaging of HClO
in living systems. The complex shows
a substantial increase in emission intensity and lifetime upon reaction
with HClO, which enables the detection of HClO in live cells and tissues
using LSCM and PLIM, respectively. Additionally, the complex displays
strong chemiluminescence due to the generation of high-energy intermediates
during the HClO-induced oxidative deoximation process, enabling the
background-free imaging of endogenous HClO production in live mice
during inflammation. A related iridium(III) complex (436) exhibiting chemiluminescence in the NIR region has also been developed. This complex has been encapsulated into Pluronic
F127 to afford a nanoprobe with extended chemiluminescence time (>
20 min), increased water dispersibility, and enhanced biocompatibility
for chemiluminescence imaging of HClO in live mice.
 Chao and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
complexes functionalized
with a diaminomaleonitrile moiety (437a–437e) ( ) for the detection of HClO based on a de-diaminomaleonitrile
reaction. , Treatment with HClO increases
the emission intensities of the complexes (I/Io = up to 43) due to the oxidative cleavage
of the diaminomaleonitrile unit by HClO, which inhibits emission quenching
associated with photoinduced isomerization of the C=N group. The complexes
show high mitochondria specificity, enabling the selective imaging
of mitochondrial HClO in live cells. The increased TPA cross-section
of complex 437e (δ750nm = 78.1 GM) in the presence of HClO allows for two-photon luminescence
imaging of HClO in zebrafish at increased imaging depth, revealing
increased HClO production in the liver upon LPS-induced liver injury
( ). A related
two-photon-active iridium(III) complex (437f) that exhibits
NIR emission (λem = 663 nm) in the
presence of HClO has been used for imaging of HClO in live cells,
tissues, and mice, revealing increased HClO production in response
to inflammation. A related ruthenium(II)
complex (438) has been used to examine the role of HClO
in AD development. Both in vivo and ex vivo imaging indicate that the complex shows
substantially higher emission intensity in the brain tissues of AD
mice compared to the healthy mice, and the emission intensity increases
when the mice aged, indicating elevated HClO production during AD
development ( ). Furthermore, the complex has been used to develop a cell-based
platform for high-throughput screening of anti-AD drugs using live
human neuroblastoma SH-SY5Y cells as an in vitro AD
model.
 Hou and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
complexes modified
with two tosylhydrazone (439) or isoniazide moieties (440) ( ) for
visualizing HClO in live cells. The presence of HClO induces oxidative
hydrolysis of the hydrazone linkage, leading to a rapid and substantial
increase in the emission intensities of the complexes. Notably, complex 440 specifically accumulates in the mitochondria of 4T1 cells,
enabling the selective imaging of mitochondrial HClO. Hou, Wang, and
co-workers have developed a water-soluble iridium(III) complex (441) for HClO detection. The
hydrolytic cleavage of the methacrylate units by HClO results in efficient
emission quenching with a fast response time. Zhang, Pu, and co-workers
have developed an iridium(III) complex (442) that incorporates
two pentafluorobenzenesulfonyl units as HClO-recognition sites and
a morpholine group as a lysosome-targeting unit. Cleavage of the pentafluorobenzenesulfonyl unit upon nucleophilic
attack by HClO induces efficient emission quenching. Live-cell imaging
indicates that the complex specifically accumulates in the lysosomes
after cellular uptake, facilitating the detection of lysosomal HClO.
 Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II)
complex
modified with a Fc unit via a HClO-sensitive hydrazine linkage (443) ( ) for the detection of HOCl in living systems. The introduction of an electron-rich Fc unit results in
efficient emission quenching through PET. Reaction of the complex
with HClO leads to oxidative hydrolysis of the hydrazine linker, separating
the Fc quencher from the ruthenium(II) core and inducing a up to 60-fold
increase in emission intensity. The complex is taken up by live MDA-MB-231
and human glioblastoma U-343MGa cells via caveolae-mediated endocytosis
and specifically enriched in the lysosomes after internalization,
enabling the selective imaging of lysosomal HClO in live cells. The
complex has also been used to visualize HClO in living organisms such
as D. magna and zebrafish. A related iridium(III)
complex (444a) has also been developed for specific imaging
of mitochondrial HClO . This complex
has been used to visualize the endogenous HClO production in live
HepG2 cells and zebrafish during drug stimulation, as well as in mouse
liver tissues during ischemia-reperfusion (I/R) injury. Qi, Zhang,
and co-workers have utilized the same design approach to develop an
iridium(III) complex (444b) for detecting HClO in live
cells. 
 Ye, Yuan, and co-workers have also designed two
ruthenium(II) complexes
modified with a quenching 2,4-DNP moiety through a HClO-responsive
amide linkage (445a and 445b) ( ) for imaging
HClO. Oxidative cleavage of the linker
by HClO leads to the departure of the 2,4-DNP moiety, resulting in
significant emission enhancement (I/Io = 190 and 1,100, respectively) within seconds. The remarkable
emission enhancement and fast response time of complex 445b have enabled the sensitive detection of HClO in live HeLa and RAW264.7
cells. This complex has been used to monitor HClO production in the
phagosomes of RAW264.7 cells during phagocytosis of zymosan particles.
The complex has also been loaded onto NaYF4:Yb,Tm UCNPs
using an α-CD-assisted self-assembly method to develop a ratiometric
nanoprobe for background-free detection and visualization of HClO
in live cells and mice. The blue UCL
of the UCNPs (λem = 450 and 475
nm) is quenched in the presence of the complex due to its large spectral
overlap with the absorption band of the complex (λabs = 478 nm). The energy transfer from the UCNPs to the
complex can be modulated by the reaction with HClO, which is accompanied
by a blue shift in the absorption band of the complex, resulting in
an increase in the intensity of the blue UCL. This emission enhancement
enables ratiometric detection of HClO using the NIR UCL at 800 nm
as a reference signal. The ratiometric response has allowed the measurement
of HClO levels in live MDA-MB-231 and J774A.1 cells and the visualization
of endogenous HClO production at the inflammation site of live mice
with negligible background interference using NIR-light excitation
(λex = 980 nm). Ye, Yuan, and co-workers
have conjugated [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with a quenching
2,4-DNP moiety through a HClO-sensitive thioether linkage (446). Reaction of the complex with HClO
results in a ca. 190-fold increase in emission intensity,
enabling the sensitive detection of exogenous HClO in HeLa cells and
endogenous HClO generation in macrophage cells.
 Yuan and co-workers have developed a phosphorogenic
probe (447) ( ) for HClO through the modification of a ruthenium(II)
complex with
an electron-rich 4-amino-3-nitrophenyl unit. The reaction with HClO triggers the cleavage of the quenching 4-amino-3-nitrophenyl
unit via the formation of an unstable benzofurazan-1-oxide intermediate,
resulting in a 110-fold increase in the emission intensity of the
complex. The complex has been used to visualize the endogenous HClO
generation in porcine neutrophils. A related iridium(III) complex
(448) has been developed for the detection of HClO in
live HeLa cells. Liu, Huang, Zhao, and
co-workers have encapsulated a related HClO-responsive, red luminescent
iridium(III) complex (449a) (λem = 600 nm) and a HClO-insensitive, NIR luminescent iridium(III)
complex (449b) (λem = 680 nm) with an amphiphilic polymer DSPE-PEG5000 to
develop a dual-luminescent nanoprobe. Incubation of the nanoprobe with HClO selectively increases the
intensity (I/Io = 3.2)
and lifetime (from 280 to 670 ns) of the red emission, facilitating
the detection of HClO in live cells via ratiometric and lifetime imaging.
The nanoprobe has been used to monitor changes in endogenous HClO
levels in HeLa cells and tumor-bearing mice, showing that treatment
with the anticancer drug elesclomol that induces cancer cell apoptosis
through enhancing oxidative stress results in a significant increase
in HClO levels. Song, Yuan, and co-workers have utilized the 4-amino-3-nitrophenyl
moiety as a linker to conjugate [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with a gadolinium(III) complex to afford a heterobimetallic complex
(450) for bimodal imaging of HClO. Reaction of the complex with HClO triggers the cleavage
of the linker and separates the two imaging modalities, resulting
in a remarkable increase in emission intensity (I/Io = 7.4) and a decrease in r1 relaxivity (from 4.65 to 1.87 mM–1 s–1). The complex has been used to monitor the
endogenous HClO production in inflamed RAW264.7 cells and in mice
with rheumatoid arthritis. Furthermore, the complex has been used
to visualize endogenous HClO production in mice with LPS-induced acute
liver and kidney injury via bimodal luminescence imaging and MRI.
The observed significant reduction in T1 signals in the liver and kidney upon LPS stimulation indicates an
elevation in local HClO levels. Ex vivo luminescence
imaging of the main organs harvested from the treated mice reveals
strong emission signals in the liver and kidney, which are up to 6.5-
and 3.3-fold higher in intensity than those of the untreated mice
( a,b). Luminescence
images of the cryosectioned liver and kidney slices from the treated
mice also display increased emission intensities compared to the untreated
mice ( c).
These results suggest that monitoring the HClO level can serve as
a promising approach for the early diagnosis of inflammation-induced
acute kidney and liver injury.
 Sun and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex (451) ( ) that detects HClO based on phenothiazine oxidation. Oxidation of the phenothiazine moiety by HClO
to a sulfoxide derivative inhibits PET quenching, resulting in an
increase in emission intensity. Incubation of the complex with H2S regenerates the phenothiazine unit, leading to a decrease
in the emission intensity. The reversible emission response has allowed
the complex to monitor the HClO/H2S redox cycles in live
mice.
 Sun and co-workers have designed a binuclear
ruthenium(II) complex
(452) ( ), where the two ruthenium(II) units [Ru(phen)3]2+ are conjugated with an azo bridge, for visualizing
HClO in live cells and mice. Incubation
of the complex with HClO induces the oxidative cyclization reaction
of the o-azoaniline ring to a nonquenching benzotriazole
derivative, resulting in a > 50-fold increase in the emission intensity
of the complex. Notably, the emission response is highly selective
toward HClO over other ROS and cations, which is different from its
mononuclear counterpart complex 335 that is more sensitive
to Cu2+ compared to HClO.
 Khatua and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
pyridyl-1,2,3-triazole
complex (453) ( ) for HClO detection. This complex is nonluminescent due to the presence of a low-lying 3MC state. Reaction of the complex with HClO leads to an unusual
C(sp2)–H hydroxylation at the 1,2,3-triazole
ring, which occurs via anti-Markovnikov addition of HClO to the C=C
bond followed by β-elimination of HCl. The hydroxylation of
the triazole ring increases the σ-donation ability of the ligand
and destabilizes the 3MC state, resulting in an increase
in the emission intensity (I/Io = ca. 9) and lifetime (from 14.63 to 79.30
ns) of the complex. These photophysical changes are highly specific
toward HClO over other RONS. The complex has been applied to imaging
HClO in HEK293T cells. A related ruthenium(II) complex (454) has been developed for sensitive, selective, and rapid detection
of HClO in live cells via C(sp2)–H
chlorination. 
 Peng, Huang, and co-workers have developed platinum(II)
complexes
(455) ( ) that can detect HClO through an oxidative addition reaction,
which converts the luminescent platinum(II) complexes into nonluminescent
platinum(IV) derivatives. , The emission of the
complexes can be restored upon reduction of the metal center from
platinum(IV) to platinum(II) by biothiols, suggesting their potential
to monitor the changes of both HClO and biothiol levels in cells and
to examine intracellular redox processes. The complexes have been
used to detect exogenous HClO in live HeLa cells and endogenous HClO
in stimulated RAW264.7 cells.
 
8.8.4.

## Singlet Oxygen
Singlet oxygen (1O2) is the lowest excited electronic state of O2. It can be generated through various processes, including
enzymatic reactions and photochemical
reactions. The oxidation of biomolecules
such as proteins, lipids, or nucleic acids by 1O2 plays a crucial role in cell signaling. However, excessive oxidation
caused by 1O2 can lead to severe oxidative damage
and disrupt cellular homeostasis, ultimately resulting in cell death. The unique properties of 1O2 have made it a key component in PDT where 1O2 is harnessed to induce cell death when the target cells are
exposed to light in the presence of a photosensitizer. 
You and co-workers have designed a molecular
dyad (456) ( ) composed of an iridium(III) complex and a coumarin
314 derivative for efficient photosensitization and visualization
of 1O2. Upon α-dehydrogenative
oxidation of the julolidine unit in coumarin 314 by 1O2 to an iminium form, the characteristic coumarin absorption
(λabs = 445 nm) disappears, leading
to an enhancement in both the coumarin fluorescence (λem = 441 nm) and iridium(III) phosphorescence (λem = 526 nm) due to the suppression of
triplet–triplet energy transfer back to the coumarin unit.
The 1O2-mediated oxidation reaction results
in a significant increase in the ratio I460nm/I561nm from 0.0048 to 2.16. The ratiometric
response of the complex has allowed the visualization of endogenous 1O2 in stimulated RAW264.7 cells and the monitoring
of therapeutic doses of 1O2 during photoinduced
cell death.
 Zhang, Zhao, and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
complexes
functionalized with an anthracene derivative (457) ( ) for the detection
of 1O2. The introduction
of an anthracene unit quenches the iridium(III) emission by PET. Oxidation
of the anthracene unit by 1O2 to an endoperoxide
derivative results in a 20- and 15-fold increase in the emission intensities
of complexes 457a and 457b, respectively,
which is not observed when the complexes are incubated with other
ROS. Complex 457b specifically resides in the mitochondria
after uptake, allowing the monitoring of 1O2 generation in these organelles upon irradiation.
 Tian and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex (458) ( ) that can specifically detect 1O2 in the mitochondria
of cells. The complex is nonluminescent
in solution and its emission is switched on upon oxidation of the
C=C bond linking the 8-hydroxyquinoline and N,N-diethylaniline units by 1O2. The
complex has been used to visualize the endogenous generation of 1O2 in live cells and monitor the morphological
changes of mitochondria during photoinduced cell death.
 
8.8.5.

## Peroxynitrite
Peroxynitrite (ONOO–) is formed through the diffusion-controlled reaction
between NO and O2•–. At physiological
concentrations, it serves as a signaling molecule and an antimicrobial
agent. However, excessive generation
of ONOO– can result in uncontrolled oxidation of
proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids, contributing to the development
of various pathologies such as cardiovascular diseases, neurodegenerative
disorders, and cancer.
Transition metal complexes have been
designed for detecting ONOO– through oxidative hydrolysis.
Zhao and co-workers have developed a series of iridium(III) complexes
containing a phenylboronate ester moiety for the detection of ONOO–. Reaction of the complexes
with ONOO– induces the cleavage of the phenylboronate
ester unit, releasing a pyridinol group that quenches the emission
of the complexes by PET. One of the complexes (459a)
( ) that
exhibits blue emission (λem = 474
nm) and the largest emission quenching has been loaded into the pores
of MSNs embedded with an ONOO–-insensitive, red
luminescent iridium(III) complex (459b) (λem = 605 nm) to develop a nanosensor for ratiometric detection
of ONOO–. Incubation of the nanosensor with ONOO– reduces the ratio I474nm/I605nm by 6 fold and the emission lifetime
at 474 nm from 517 to 275 ns. Notably, the ratiometric emission response
is highly specific toward ONOO– over other RONS,
particularly H2O2 that can also cleave the phenylboronate
ester unit. The photophysical changes have enabled the visualization
of the endogenous production of ONOO– in live RAW264.7
cells, zebrafish, and mice in response to inflammation using LSCM
and PLIM.
 Wu, Fan, Wong, and co-workers have modified an
iridium(III) complex
with a quenching 2,4-DNP unit via an amide linkage to afford a phosphorogenic
probe (460) ( ) for ONOO–. The amide linker is prone to oxidative hydrolysis; thus,
reaction of the complex with ONOO– triggers its
cleavage and separates the quencher from the iridium(III) center,
resulting in substantial emission enhancement (I/Io = 12). The emission response is highly specific
toward ONOO– over other ROS and RNS, particularly
O2•– and HClO (I/Io < 2) that can also trigger the
oxidative cleavage of the amide bond. The complex has been used to
image endogenous ONOO– produced in inflamed MCF-7
cells and mice. Wang, Tang, and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex modified with a malononitrile moiety (461) for
detecting ONOO– in liver cells subjected to drug-induced
liver injury (DILI). Reaction of the
complex with ONOO– induces the oxidative hydrolysis
of the malononitrile moiety to an aldehyde derivative, resulting in
a shift of the excited state character from 3ILCT to 3MLCT and thus substantial emission enhancement. The complex
specifically accumulates in the mitochondria of live HepG2 cells and
has been used to visualize acetaminophen (APAP)- and tolcapone-induced
ONOO– production in cells. Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers
have designed a ruthenium(II) complex conjugated with a fluorescein
derivative (462) for ratiometric imaging of ONOO–. The phenylhydrazine
moiety on the spirolactam ring of fluorescein functions as an ONOO–-specific recognition unit. Oxidative hydrolysis of
the phenylhydrazine moiety by ONOO– induces ring-opening
of the fluorescein spirolactam to give strong green fluorescence (λem = 512 nm), resulting in a 140-fold
increase in the ratio I512nm/I635nm. The complex is specifically enriched in the lysosomes
of live HeLa cells after cellular uptake, enabling the selective and
ratiometric imaging of lysosomal ONOO–.
 Phosphorogenic probes have also been designed
to detect ONOO– through an oxidative N-dearylation
reaction. Chao and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex
modified with an electron-rich N-(4-methylphenyl)amino
group (463) ( ) for visualizing ONOO–. This modification not only imparts the complex
with increased reactivity and selectivity toward ONOO– but also enables efficient emission quenching through PET. The preferential
localization of the complex in the lysosomes has enabled the visualization
of the NO/O2•– crosstalk in this
specific organelle in live HepG2 cells. The complex has also been
used to visualize DILI in live LO2 cells and mice by monitoring changes
in ONOO– levels, showing that excessive intake of
APAP induces hepatotoxicity via increased production of ONOO–. Liu, Zhao, and co-workers have developed iridium(III) complexes
bearing an N-(4-hydroxylphenyl)amino group as phosphorogenic
probes for ONOO–. One
of the complexes, displaying NIR phosphorescence (λem = 680 nm), has been further modified with an electron-donating
methoxy group on the phenol ring to increase its reactivity toward
ONOO– and enhance emission quenching efficiency.
This complex has been incorporated with a red luminescent, ONOO–-insensitive iridium(III) complex (λem = 605 nm) into a PVP backbone to develop a dual-emissive
polymeric probe (464) for ratiometric imaging of ONOO–. Reaction of the polymeric probe with ONOO– selectively enhances the intensity and lifetime of the NIR emission,
enabling the visualization of endogenous ONOO– generation
in live RAW264.7 cells and mice during inflammation via ratiometric
and lifetime imaging. The probe has also been utilized to monitor
changes in the ONOO– levels in vivo, revealing the elevated ONOO– levels in the liver
of mice with APAP- or ketoconazole (KTZ)-induced liver injury and
demonstrating the protective effects of the antioxidant N-acetylcysteine (NAC) on hepatocytes.
 Zhang, Zhang, Yuan, and co-workers have developed
a ratiometric
probe (465) ( ) for ONOO– through conjugating [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and Cy5 with a piperidine linker. Upon photoexcitation (λex = 450 nm), the probe exhibits intense Cy5 fluorescence
(λem = 660 nm) due to efficient
FRET from the [Ru(bpy)3]2+ core to the Cy5 unit.
Reaction of the complex with ONOO– triggers the
oxidative cleavage of the polymethine bridge and interrupts the FRET
process, resulting in a decrease in the Cy5 fluorescence (λem = 660 nm) and an increase in the ruthenium(II)
phosphorescence (λem = 610 nm).
This dual emission response enables the sensitive and selective detection
of ONOO– using a ratiometric approach. The probe
has been used to visualize ONOO– in live HeLa cells,
and its preferential mitochondrial accumulation has enabled the specific
imaging of mitochondrial ONOO–. Using a similar
design approach, an iridium(III) complex conjugated with an ONOO–-responsive benzopyrylium derivative (466) has been developed as a FRET-based ratiometric probe for imaging
ONOO– in the brain of rat models of epilepsy. In the absence of ONOO–,
the complex shows red fluorescence (λem = 633 nm) due to FRET from the iridium(III) core to the benzopyrylium
derivative. Upon reaction with ONOO–, the benzopyrylium
unit is oxidized and cleaved, recovering the green phosphorescence
of the iridium(III) complex (λem = 550 nm) and increasing the ratio I550nm/I633nm by > 100 fold. The probe has
been used to monitor alternations in ONOO– levels
in live SH-SY5Y cells and in the brain of rats, unveiling the elevated
production of ONOO– upon epilepsy induced by lithium
pilocarpine (LI-PC) ( ). Treatment of the epilepsy models with the antiepileptic
drug sodium valproate (VPA) or natural extracts such as curcumin (Cur)
and ginkgo biloba extract (GBE) with strong free radical scavenging
capabilities results in a significant reduction in ONOO– levels. Similar results have been found in two-photon luminescence
imaging of the rat brain slices, highlighting that the probe can be
used for the diagnosis of epileptic disorders and high-throughput
screening of antiepileptic drugs.
 Chao and co-workers have designed an NIR luminescent
iridium(III)
complex functionalized with a redox-responsive hydroquinone moiety
(467) ( ) for monitoring the ONOO–/GSH redox cycles. Oxidation of the hydroquinone moiety by ONOO– results in the formation of a quinone derivative,
which induces emission quenching through PET. However, addition of
GSH to the solution regenerates the hydroquinone moiety, resulting
in a substantial increase in emission intensity. This “On–Off–On”
emission response of the complex has enabled effective monitoring
of the reversible redox cycles between ONOO– and
GSH in living systems.
 

8.9.

## Reactive Carbonyl Species
Reactive
carbonyl species (RCS) is a group of highly reactive aldehydes that
can be present in both endogenous and exogenous sources. While RCS are involved in the regulation of
biological processes such as gene expression, signal transduction,
and immune response, increased levels of RCS can lead to carbonyl
stress, which contributes to the development of several diseases such
as cardiovascular disease and AD. 
8.9.1.

## Formaldehyde
Formaldehyde, recognized
as an environmental toxin, is the simplest RCS. Inhalation or ingestion
of exogenous formaldehyde poses a significant risk to human health.
However, formaldehyde can also be generated endogenously through different
metabolic pathways, including one-carbon metabolism and the demethylation
of proteins and nucleic acids. Elevated levels of endogenous formaldehyde
have been associated with the development of cancer and neurodegenerative
diseases. 
Zhang, Zhang, Yuan,
and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II) complex (468) ( ) using
a “dual-key-and-lock” approach for formaldehyde detection. The ruthenium(II) polypyridine unit is connected
to a 2,4-DNP moiety via an allylamine linker, which plays an important
role in allowing 2-aza-Cope rearrangement to occur after condensation
reaction with formaldehyde (first key). The subsequent hydrolytic
cleavage of the 2,4-DNP moiety under an acidic environment (pH = 3.0–6.0)
(second key) results in significant emission enhancement. This design
has enabled the selective imaging of formaldehyde in the lysosomes
of live cells. Furthermore, the complex can specifically visualize
and monitor endogenous formaldehyde derived from tumors in live mice
( ) due to
the higher intracellular formaldehyde levels and lower lysosomal pH
in cancer cells (pHL = 4.5–5.5) compared to normal
cells (pHL = 5–6). The formaldehyde-responsive allylamine
linker has also been exploited to conjugate a ruthenium(II) complex
with a 7-(diethylamino)coumarin derivative to afford a ratiometric
probe (469) for formaldehyde detection. Both the green fluorescence and red phosphorescence are
restored upon reaction of the probe with formaldehyde under acidic
conditions, resulting in a 419-fold increase in the ratio I505nm/I610nm. The
complex has been used to visualize endogenous lysosomal formaldehyde
in live SH-SY5Y cells and in the brain tissues of AD mice.
 
8.9.2.

## Methylglyoxal
Methylglyoxal is
an RCS generated as a by-product of glycolysis. It can induce the glycation of proteins, nucleotides, and
phospholipids, resulting in the formation of advanced glycation end-products
(AGEs). The increased accumulation of AGEs has been implicated in
various pathological conditions such as aging, diabetes, renal failure,
neurodegenerative diseases, and cancer. 
Zhang, Zhang, and co-workers have utilized ruthenium(II) (419) and iridium(III) 5,6-diamino-1,10-phenanthroline complexes
(470) ( ) for detecting methylglyoxal through the condensation–cyclization
reaction of o-diaminoaromatics with methylglyoxal. The reaction converts 5,6-diamino-1,10-phenanthroline
to 2-methylpyrazino[2,3-f][1,10]phenanthroline, which
inhibits PET and results in substantial emission enhancement (I/Io = 29.4 and 14.1, respectively)
that is greater than those observed with other RCS such as glyoxal.
The complexes have been used to visualize methylglyoxal in live RAW264.7
cells and D. magna. Importantly, while the complexes
also exhibit reactivity toward NO, this reaction is not expected to
interfere with methylglyoxal detection in cellular settings due to
the shorter half-life and lower intracellular concentration of NO
(typically in the low- to mid-nanomolar range) under physiological
conditions.
 

8.10.

## Intracellular Redox Status
Intracellular
redox homeostasis is crucial for maintaining normal cell function. The imbalance between intracellular ROS and
antioxidants has been linked to the development of various diseases.
For example, cancer cells display increased production of antioxidants
to accommodate higher levels of ROS for activating signaling pathways
that regulate cellular proliferation while scavenging excess ROS to
prevent cell death caused by oxidative stress.
Zhao and co-workers
have modified an iridium(III) complex with two 2,2,6,6-tetramethylpiperidine-1-oxyl
(TEMPO) units to afford a redox-sensitive luminescent probe (471) ( ) for assessing the intracellular redox status. The emission intensity and lifetime of the complex increase
upon reduction of the paramagnetic TEMPO radical by GSH or ascorbic
acid to a hydroxylamine derivative. These emission changes have allowed
for the visualization of the intracellular redox environment in HeLa
and 3T3 cells using LSCM and PLIM. The intracellular emission intensity
and lifetime are higher and longer in HeLa cells compared to 3T3 cells,
indicating a more reducing intracellular environment in cancerous
HeLa cells compared to normal 3T3 cells. Cao, Mao, and co-workers
have developed TEMPO-functionalized ruthenium(II) complexes (472) that function as both a ROS photosensitizer and an oxidative
stress indicator. These complexes exhibit
increased emission intensities upon reaction with radical species
such as •CH3 and O2•–, which converts the paramagnetic TEMPO unit into a diamagnetic nonradical
species. Among the two complexes studied, complex 472a shows significantly higher photocytotoxicity due to its more efficient
cellular uptake. Monitoring the intracellular emission of the complex
has revealed the induction of oxidative stress in cells upon exposure
to irradiation. Related iridium(III) complexes (473)
have been developed for simultaneously inducing and monitoring oxidative
stress in cells. These complexes specifically
reside in the mitochondria following cellular uptake and display enhanced
emission intensities and lifetimes when the cells are pretreated with
phorbol myristate acetate or H2O2 to induce
oxidative stress. Remarkably, the cytotoxic activity of the complexes
substantially increases upon irradiation. Monitoring the changes in
intracellular emission intensities and lifetimes of the complexes
shows that the complexes induce mitochondrial oxidative stress during
PDT, which can be attenuated by the use of NAC ( ). Additionally, the enhanced
intracellular emission has enabled the real-time monitoring of morphological
changes in the mitochondria, showing that the mitochondria swell during
photoinduced cell death.
 Yang, Gao, and co-workers have developed a series
of ruthenium(II),
iridium(III), and platinum(II) complexes (474) ( ) that are sensitive
to the intracellular reductive microenvironment. These complexes undergo dissociation of their monodentate
imidazole ligands when exposed to high levels of GSH, resulting in
a substantial decrease in their emission intensities. Among these
complexes, the platinum(II) complex 474c shows the fastest
rate of ligand release, primarily due to its square-planar molecular
geometry that offers smaller steric hindrance. Monitoring the ligand
dissociation process in live A549 cells using LSCM reveals that a
reduction in intracellular GSH levels not only leads to increased
emission intensities of the complexes but also triggers their translocation
from the cytoplasm to the nucleus, offering a potential means to distinguish
cancer cells from normal cells.
 Liang, Liu, Roberts, and co-workers have utilized
three ruthenium(II)
complexes with distinct reactivity toward GSH (382),
HClO (443), and H2O2 (475) ( ) to
construct a two-photon sensing platform for monitoring cellular oxidative
stress in the liver of live mice subjected to APAP-induced liver injury
and hepatic I/R injury. These complexes
display a substantial increase in emission intensities and lifetimes
upon specific reactions with their respective target species, enabling
the simultaneous use of both LSCM and PLIM to provide accurate measurements
of changes in ROS and GSH levels within the liver at single-cell resolution.
 
8.11.

## Nucleic Acids
Nucleic acids are
biomacromolecules that store, transmit, and express genetic information,
playing vital roles in the growth, development, and reproduction of
all living organisms.
8.11.1.

## Deoxyribonucleic Acids
Deoxyribonucleic
acid (DNA) carries the genetic information in the form of a double-stranded
helix. It is predominantly located within the nucleus and mitochondria
of mammalian cells. However, DNA is vulnerable to damage, and defects
in DNA repair mechanisms can lead to an increased risk of developing
various diseases such as neurodegenerative disorders and cancer. 
Ruthenium(II) dppz complexes such as
[Ru(bpy)2(dppz)]2+ and [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ have long been recognized as molecular “light
switches” for DNA due to their significant emission turn-on
upon DNA intercalation. , However, their poor
cellular uptake has limited their use in intracellular DNA sensing. To address this limitation, Zhu and co-workers
have developed an ion-pairing approach for delivering these membrane-impermeable
complexes to the nucleus of live cells. Both the cellular and nuclear uptake of the complexes are significantly
enhanced in the presence of hydrophobic weak acid counteranions such
as perchlorophenolate, enabling the visualization of nuclear DNA in
different cell lines using LSCM and SIM. The enantioselective imaging
of nuclear DNA with the Δ-isomers of the complexes, which exhibit
higher affinities for the right-handed DNA duplex, has been achieved
in live cells for the first time using this approach. This ion-pairing
approach is universal to improve the cellular and nuclear uptake of
other membrane-impermeable ruthenium(II) complexes such as [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and [Ru(phen)3]2+. This
approach has also been used to deliver [Os(phen)2(dppz)]2+ to cells for imaging nuclear DNA, enabling the observation
of chromosome condensation and decondensation during mitosis using
correlative light–electron microscopy. The Λ- and Δ-isomers of the complex can differentiate
the nucleolus from the nucleus in live and fixed cells, respectively,
using PLIM, based on the different emission lifetimes between the
nucleoli and others parts of the nucleus. Monitoring the changes in the emission lifetime of the two enantiomers
has revealed the alterations in the cellular microenvironment upon
treatment with metabolic inhibitors for nuclear protein synthesis
and the dynamic changes in the architecture of the nucleolus, chromosome,
and spindle apparatus during the cell cycle process.
The increased
cellular and nuclear uptake of the membrane-impermeable
complexes is attributed to the formation of charge-neutral, lipophilic,
and relatively stable ion-pair complexes with the hydrophobic weak
acid counteranions, which facilitate cellular internalization via
passive diffusion. Structure–property relationship analysis
reveals that a high binding affinity between the cationic complex
and the counteranion, as well as low lipophilicity of the resultant
ion-pair complex, are both important to minimize nonspecific interactions
with hydrophobic proteins and membranes in the cytoplasm of the cells,
facilitating the nuclear enrichment of the complexes. , However, upon incubation in a fresh culture medium, the complexes
are effluxed out of the cell nucleus by the ATP-binding cassette transporters
and relocated to the mitochondria. High-resolution
imaging with SIM and STED microscopy shows that [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ localizes at the center of the mitochondrion
and intercalates into mitochondrial DNA, revealing that each mitochondrion
contains 1 to 8 DNA molecules distributed throughout the entire mitochondrial
matrix. Furthermore, the complex can
induce enantioselective apoptotic cell death upon continuous visible-light
irradiation, with the Δ-isomer showing higher photocytotoxicity
due to its strong binding with mitochondrial DNA. Notably, the complex
is translocated from the mitochondria back to the nucleus during apoptotic
cell death, offering a direct method for in situ self-monitoring
of cell apoptosis. The ion-pairing approach is also effective to redirect
[Ru(Ph2-phen)2(dppz)]2+ (Ph2-phen = 4,7-diphenyl-1,10-phenanthroline), which can enter cells
via passive diffusion, from the cytoplasm to the cell nucleus. This cytoplasm-to-nucleus translocation is
specific for the Δ-isomer, which enables enantioselective induction
of DNA damage and apoptosis upon visible-light irradiation. The ion-pairing
approach has also been utilized to increase the photocytotoxic activity
of [Ru(Me4-phen)3]2+ (Me4-phen = 3,4,7,8-tetramethyl-1,10-phenanthroline). 
Other strategies have also been developed to enhance
the cellular
uptake of these membrane-impermeable complexes. For example, Feng
and co-workers have encapsulated [Ru(bpy)2(dppz)]2+ and [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ into apoferritin to
improve their cellular uptake and biocompatibility. The uptake of the nanocomposites by MCF-7 cells can be
further improved by surface modification with folic acid, which enables
specific targeting of FRs overexpressed on the cancer cell surface.
Other carriers such as cucurbit[7]uril and MSNs have also been used to facilitate
the transport of these complexes across the cell membrane to stain
DNA. Shen and co-workers have encapsulated [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ into liposomes to develop a theranostic platform that displays
high nuclear uptake and tumor accumulation, allowing for the induction
of efficient DNA damage and cancer cell apoptosis to inhibit tumor
growth in vivo. Wu,
Wang, Mei, and co-workers have leveraged the strong DNA binding affinities
of ruthenium(II) dppz complexes to prepare nanoprobes as tumor-targeting
imaging agents. The binding of [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ and its derivative (476)
( ) to oligonucleotides
induces the formation of NPs with enhanced emission. These NPs exhibit
facile uptake in live MDA-MB-231 cells and selective tumor accumulation
in zebrafish bearing MDA-MB-231 tumor xenografts, suggesting their
potential development as tumor-specific agents for cancer diagnosis
and therapy. Xie, He, and co-workers have utilized [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ to label vaccinia virus nucleic acid. The labeled virions show strong emission and
can be imaged at the single-virion level, enabling the examination
of their invasion into the host cells.
 Metzler-Nolte and co-workers have conjugated
these membrane-impermeable
complexes with the CPP Xentry (LCLRPVG), which is a neutral peptide
derived from the N-terminal region of the X-protein found in the hepatitis
B virus, to increase their cellular
uptake efficiencies. Compared to their
peptide-free counterparts, these peptide conjugates (477a and 477b) ( ) display lower binding affinities for DNA due to the
steric hindrance caused by the peptide on the dppz ligand. However,
these conjugates are efficiently taken up by live HeLa cells, and
the cellular uptake is more efficient than a related Tat49–57 peptide conjugate (477c) that gives negligible intracellular
emission. Keyes and co-workers have developed ruthenium(II) dppz complexes
conjugated with an NLS (VQRKRQKLMP; 478a) and an MPP
(FrFKFrFK; 478b) to enhance its cellular uptake for specific
imaging of nuclear and mitochondrial DNA, respectively. The peptide conjugation increases the binding
affinities of the conjugates for DNA (Kb = 3.6 and 2.8 × 107 M–1, respectively)
by an order of magnitude compared to their peptide-free counterpart
(Kb = 5.04 × 106 M–1) due to enhanced electrostatic interaction. Multimodal
imaging involving LSCM, PLIM, and resonance Raman spectroscopy confirms
the specific staining of nuclear and mitochondrial DNA in HeLa cells
by these conjugates. Furthermore, the high specificity of conjugate 478a for nuclear DNA has enabled the visualization of chromosomes
with high resolution using STED microscopy, facilitating the observation
of the process of cell division ( ). 
 Gunnlaugsson, Williams, and co-workers have studied
the biological
properties of a series of ruthenium(II) complexes bearing an extended
aromatic unit on the dppz ligand (479) ( ). , The phen complexes 479a–479c exhibit
the typical “light switch” effect upon binding to DNA.
Live-cell imaging shows that these complexes primarily localize in
the cytoplasm of cells. In contrast, the 1,4,5,8-tetraazaphenanthrene
(TAP) complexes 479d–479f show emission
quenching when bound to DNA due to photooxidation of the guanine residues,
resulting in significant photodamage to DNA and high photocytotoxicity.
These findings indicate that ruthenium(II) dppz-like complexes can
be developed as either imaging or therapeutic agents, depending on
the specific ancillary ligands used in their design. Simpson, Scattergood,
Elliott, Quinn, and co-workers have developed two osmium(II) complexes
(480) as NIR luminescent metallointercalators for DNA. In contrast to [Ru(TAP)2(dppz)]2+ that displays emission quenching upon binding to guanine-containing
DNA, the osmium(II) complexes 480a and 480b exhibit substantial emission enhancement in the NIR region (λem = 761 and 740 nm, respectively). These
complexes are membrane-impermeable and have been encapsulated into
polymeric micelles derived from Polysorbate 80 to promote their cellular
uptake. Upon cellular internalization, these complexes are enriched
in the nuclear membrane and nucleoli of live HeLa cells. Bhat, Revankar,
and co-workers have incorporated a triptycenyl unit into ruthenium(II)
(481) and iridium(III)
dppz complexes (482) as
a molecular clip to induce DNA condensation, enabling the complexes
to function as gene transfection agents. Ruthenium(II) complexes featuring
a glycoluril unit (483–485) have
also been developed as nonviral DNA carriers. − 
 Yam and co-workers have demonstrated that the
rhenium(I) dppz complex
[Re(dppz)(CO)3(py)]+ (py = pyridine) shows a
lower binding affinity for DNA and a smaller increase in emission
intensity upon DNA binding compared to the ruthenium(II) dppz complexes,
but it effectively induces DNA cleavage upon irradiation. Thomas, de la Serna, and co-workers have conjugated
rhenium(I) and ruthenium(II) dppz complexes, [Re(dppz)(CO)3(py)]+ and [Ru(tpm)(dppz)(py)]2+ (tpm = tris(1-pyrazolyl)methane),
using different linkers. , The emission of the
resultant heterobimetallic complexes (486) ( ) predominantly
originates from the 3MLCT (dπ(Ru) → π*(dppz))
excited state due to energy transfer from the rhenium(I) center to
the ruthenium(II) center, enabling the complexes to exhibit significant
emission turn-on upon binding to DNA. Notably, the choice of linker
plays a significant role in controlling the excited-state properties
of the complexes. While complex 486a shares similar DNA
photocleavage properties to [Re(dppz)(CO)3(py)]+, complex 486b does not induce DNA cleavage even after
prolonged irradiation. Additionally, complex 486b shows
superior photostability compared to complex 486a, allowing
for the precise tracking of its temporal dynamics within cells using
STED microscopy. This nanoscopic imaging technique has unveiled the
rapid migration of the complex from the mitochondria and lysosomes
to the cell nucleus after its cellular internalization. Thomas, Elliott,
and co-workers have developed iridium(III) dppz complexes (487) that display “light switch” effect upon DNA intercalation. In contrast to their ruthenium(II) and osmium(II)
counterparts that are unable to penetrate the plasma membrane, the
iridium(III) complexes exhibit efficient cellular uptake and specific
mitochondrial localization in live MCF-7 and A2780 cells.
 Thomas and co-workers have developed ruthenium(II)
complexes coordinated
with a tetrapyrido[3,2-a:2′,3′-c:3′′,2′′-h:2′′′,3′′′-j]phenazine (tpphz) ligand (488) ( ) that show “light
switch” effect upon intercalative binding to DNA. These complexes enter live MCF-7 cells by active
transport and reside in the cell nucleus, which are different from
their dppz counterparts that display limited cellular and nuclear
uptake. The efficient nuclear accumulation of these complexes has
enabled the visualization of nuclear DNA in a cellular context. Additionally,
these complexes exhibit cytotoxic activity comparable to cisplatin
in various cancer cell lines. Studies on the mechanism of action of
complex 488b in p53-deficient oesophageal squamous cell
carcinoma OE21 cells show that this metallointercalator targets DNA
replication. The tpphz ligand has also
been utilized as a bridging ligand to develop homobinuclear complexes
(489). Compared to the
mononuclear complexes (Kb = 105 to 106 M–1), these binuclear complexes
bind to DNA through a nonintercalative mechanism with stronger affinities
(Kb > 107 M–1). Complex 489b shows specific nuclear staining in both
live and fixed cells due to its binding to nuclear DNA. This is supported
by emission lifetime analysis using TP-PLIM, which reveals that the
emission in the nucleus is longer-lived (> 160 ns) than that in
the
cytoplasm. The complex has been utilized
to monitor structural changes in nuclear DNA during the cell cycle
progression. When coupled with super-resolution
imaging techniques such as SIM and STED microscopy, the complex has
enabled the visualization of chromatin DNA in live cells with enhanced
resolution. Three-dimensional images
of nuclear chromatin have also been obtained using 3D STED microscopy.
In contrast, the lower cellular uptake of complex 489a has limited its use to image nuclear DNA in fixed cells. However, it can be delivered into live cells
using a PEGylated DNA–AuNP conjugate as a nanocarrier, which
displays high stability and resistance against nonspecific adsorption
and enzymatic degradation. Interestingly,
the cellular uptake and localization properties of the two binuclear
complexes can be modulated by encapsulation in polymersomes, which
enter cells via endocytosis and relocate the complexes to the mitochondria
to target mitochondrial DNA. The cellular
uptake of heterobinuclear ruthenium(II)–iridium(III) complexes
(490) have also been examined in both live and fixed
cells. Complexes 490a and 490b exhibit improved cellular uptake without the loss of
nuclear localization compared to the homobinuclear ruthenium(II) complexes
due to the lower formal charge and increased lipophilicity. However,
complex 491 shows limited uptake in live cells with negligible
nuclear staining due to its strong interaction with serum proteins,
which restrains it from targeting the nucleus. Meijer, Dietzek-Ivanšić, Thomas, and co-workers
have also developed a related binuclear osmium(II) complex (492) featuring NIR emission (λem = 780 nm), excellent photostability, and low (photo)cytotoxicity
for imaging nuclear DNA using STED microscopy. 
 Other transition metal complexes containing planar
aromatic ligands
have also been explored for their applications in DNA sensing in live
cells. Bhat, Revankar, and co-workers have examined the photophysical
and biological properties of ruthenium(II) complexes that incorporate
a [1,2,5]-thiadiazolo-[3,4-f]-[1,10]-phenanthroline
ligand (493) ( ). These complexes display
specific nuclear staining in live HeLa cells due to their binding
to nuclear DNA. Wu, Tian, and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex appended with an N-hexyl phenothiazine unit
(494) that enables the specific targeting of mitochondrial
DNA. The complex possesses a large TPA
cross-section (δ760nm = 236.5 GM),
facilitating its visualization in live cells and tissues using two-photon
microscopy. The complex is rapidly internalized into live HepG2 cells
via endocytosis and specifically enriched in the mitochondria due
to its high affinity for mitochondrial DNA. Lu, Che, and co-workers
have studied the DNA-binding and intracellular localization properties
of platinum(II) allenylidene complexes (495). These complexes exhibit a 17- and 5-fold increase
in their emission intensities, respectively, upon binding to DNA.
Live-cell imaging in HeLa cells shows that complex 495a predominantly resides in the cell nucleus, while complex 495b is mainly distributed in the cytoplasm. The smaller emission enhancement
and negligible nuclear uptake of complex 495b are attributed
to the bulky tert-butyl groups that impede the intercalation
of the complex into DNA and refrain its nuclear entry. Mishra and
co-workers have developed a binuclear platinum(II) complex (496) that shows strong binding affinity for DNA, resulting
in its specific accumulation in the nucleus. 
 The selective recognition of mismatched DNA is
important to cancer
diagnosis and therapy. However, most of the luminescent probes display
nondiscriminative intercalative binding interactions with DNA, posing
a significant challenge to the specific detection of intracellular
mismatched DNA. Che and co-workers have developed a series of luminescent
platinum(II) complexes that enable selective recognition of mismatches
in DNA. The incorporation of bulky groups
into the NHC or diphosphine ligand plays a crucial role in introducing
steric hindrance, which weakens the interaction between the complex
and matched DNA while maintaining high affinity for mismatched DNA.
Thus, these complexes exhibit a more significant increase in their
emission intensities upon binding to mismatched DNA than matched DNA,
as the larger pockets present in the mismatched sites provide better
protection to the complex from the surrounding aqueous environment.
Additionally, the complexes can effectively detect abasic sites in
DNA, which are another type of thermodynamically unstable DNA defects.
Among the complexes studied, the binuclear platinum(II) complex 497 ( ) shows up to 13.5-fold higher emission enhancement upon binding
to mismatched DNA compared to matched DNA, taking advantage of its
unique molecular geometry and environment-sensitive emission. It has
been used to detect DNA mismatches in human colorectal carcinoma HCT116
cells, a cell line that is defective in mismatch repair (MMR). Remarkably,
the complex shows substantially higher emission intensity in HCT116
cells than HCT116N cells ( a), a cell line that is modified to restore MMR activity.
The complex can also differentiate between tumor and normal tissues
based on the different levels of mismatches in their DNA extracts
( b).
 Sun, Peng, and co-workers have utilized graphene
oxide as a platform
for loading [Ru(phen)3]2+ for DNA detection. Upon loading onto graphene oxide, the emission
of the complex is quenched. However, upon interaction of the nanosensor
with DNA, the complex is released from the graphene oxide, resulting
in emission turn-on. Live-cell imaging shows that the nanosensor can
enter the nucleus and stain nuclear DNA in MCF-7 cells, indicating
that the graphene oxide serves as a nanovector to direct the complex
to the cell nucleus for DNA detection.

8.11.2.

## Ribonucleic Acids
Ribonucleic
acid (RNA) is a single-stranded nucleic acid structure synthesized
from DNA through transcription. It is primarily present in the nucleoli
and cytoplasm of eukaryotic cells. Within the cell, the nucleolus
serves as the primary site for the synthesis and assembly of ribosomal
RNA (rRNA), which constitutes ca. 70–80% of
the total RNA content.
Turro and co-workers have designed a
ruthenium(II) complex containing a phenanthridine derivative (498) ( ) for the detection of RNA. The two
luminescent moieties are located in close proximity to facilitate
spin-forbidden resonance energy transfer from the 3MLCT
state of [Ru(bpy)2(phen)]2+ to the singlet excited
state of phenanthridine. The delayed phenanthridine fluorescence is
quenched in aqueous solution but restored upon binding to RNA which
shields phenanthridine from the aqueous environment, resulting in
substantial emission enhancement. This complex has been used to visualize
RNA in live MDA-MB-231 cells, with strong emission observed in both
the nucleoli and cytoplasm. Sun, Peng, and co-workers have developed
a dual-emissive heterobimetallic ruthenium(II)–iridium(III)
complex (499) for imaging rRNA in live cells. The complex displays two emission bands at
523 and 615 nm and the ratio I523nm/I615nm increases upon interaction with the secondary
and tertiary structures of RNA but not DNA. Incubation of live MCF-7
cells with the complex results in intense emission in the nucleoli
with weaker emission in the cytoplasm. The nucleolar emission is significantly
reduced in intensity upon treatment with RNase but not with deoxyribonuclease
(DNase), further supporting the specific interaction of the complex
with RNA in the nucleoli.
 Khatua and co-workers have developed an AIE-active
ruthenium(II)
complex (500) ( ) that enables selective detection of rRNA through
an rRNA-induced aggregation mechanism. In the presence of rRNA, the complex exhibits increased emission
intensity due to aggregate formation via π–π stacking
and C–Cl···O halogen bonding interactions with
rRNA. The complex has been used to image rRNA in live HeLa cells,
resulting in specific staining of the nucleolus. An AIE-active iridium(III)
complex coordinated with a benzimidazole-substituted 1,2,3-triazole
ligand (501) has also been reported for the selective
detection of rRNA and nucleolar staining in live cells. The enhanced emission of the complex in the
presence of rRNA is attributed to the supramolecular π–π
interactions between the benzimidazole unit and the secondary structures
of rRNA that facilitate aggregation. A related ruthenium(II) complex
(454) has been demonstrated to show selective detection
of rRNA in live cells in the presence of Ag+. The complex displays ca. 8-fold
emission enhancement in the presence of Ag+ due to the
formation of a silver(I)–triazolide complex. Upon incubation
of the cells with Ag+, intense emission has been observed
in the nucleoli and cytoplasm, which diminishes in intensity upon
the addition of RNase but not DNase. The intracellular emission is
believed to be a consequence of the interaction of the complex with
the duplex structures of rRNA formed upon the binding of Ag+ to the guanine and cytosine bases in single-stranded RNA.
 MicroRNA (miRNA) is a class of small, noncoding
RNAs that regulate
gene expression in a sequence-specific manner. The aberrant expression of specific miRNAs has been implicated
in the initiation and progression of various types of human cancer. Zhang, Jiang, and co-workers have developed
luminescent metal nanoshells for imaging single miRNA in cells. These metal nanoshells consist of a silica
sphere core encapsulating [Ru(bpy)3]2+, and
a thin silver layer serving as the shell. The single-stranded oligonucleotide
5′-CTCGGGGCAGCTCAGTACAGGA-3′
is covalently attached to the metal nanoshells to facilitate specific
recognition of its target miRNA-486 in cells. The metal nanoshells
have been used to examine miRNA-486 expression in various lung cancer
cell lines, revealing elevated levels of miRNA-486 in NCI-H1944 and
NCI-H460 cells compared to A549 cells. Wu and co-workers have developed
a ratiometric nanoprobe for imaging intracellular miRNAs. This nanoprobe is constructed using a SiNP
core encapsulating [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and a polydopamine
shell, with the fuel DNA and fluorescein-labeled signal DNA coassembled
on the outer surface. In the absence of target miRNA, the fluorescence
of fluorescein is effectively quenched by the polydopamine shell in
close proximity and thus the nanoprobe only exhibits the characteristic
ruthenium(II)-based red phosphorescence at 650 nm. However, in the
presence of the target miRNA let-7a, the signal DNA is displaced from
the nanoshell, leading to the recovery of fluorescein fluorescence
at 524 nm. Further amplification of the signal can be achieved upon
NIR irradiation, which promotes the release of the fuel DNA due to
localized temperature increase and thus facilitates the displacement
of let-7a via a strand migration reaction. The nanoprobe has been
used to monitor alternations in let-7a expression in live A549 cells,
revealing the up- and down-regulation of let-7a expression when the
cells are pretreated with let-7a mimics and let-7a inhibitors, respectively.

8.11.3.

## G-Quadruplexes
G-Quadruplexes
are one of the most important higher-order structures formed from
the stacking of guanine tetrads through hydrogen bonding in guanine-rich
DNA and RNA molecules. These noncanonical structures play an important
role in various biological processes, including replication, transcription,
translation, and telomere maintenance. The stabilization of G-quadruplexes in nontelomeric regions, such
as proto-oncogenes and promoter regions, has been regarded as a promising
anticancer strategy. 
Vilar and
co-workers have developed a cyclometalated platinum(II) complex (502) ( ) that acts as a “light switch” for G-quadruplex DNA. The introduction of an ethyl piperidine unit
prevents the complex from intercalating into the base pairs of duplex
DNA and thereby imparts G-quadruplex selectivity to the complex. Thus,
the complex shows substantial emission enhancement in the presence
of G-quadruplex DNA (e.g., c-Myc and 6183 NT) but
not with duplex DNA. However, live-cell imaging shows that the complex
is impermeable to the membrane of U2OS cells. To overcome this limitation,
the complex has been encapsulated into a supramolecular coordination
cage to facilitate its delivery into live cells. Upon cellular internalization,
the complex is released from the cage and enriched in the nucleoli.
A related platinum(II) complex (503) has been developed
for imaging G-quadruplex DNA structures in cells. Binding of the complex to DNA promotes its aggregation,
resulting in a significant increase in its emission lifetime from
75 ns to ca. 0.75 μs (for duplex DNA) and 1.42–1.90
μs (for G-quadruplex DNA), with the magnitude of the increase
depends on the DNA topology. This remarkable feature has enabled the
selective visualization of G-quadruplex DNA in U2OS cells using PLIM.
The longest emission lifetime is detected in the nucleoli, followed
by the whole nucleus, whereas the shortest emission lifetime is observed
in the cytosol ( ). The relatively low amplitude of the long emission lifetime
component (ca. 1–2%) aligns with the low abundance
of G-quadruplex DNA in cells. The complex has been used to develop
a lifetime-based assay for investigating the competitive interactions
of other G-quadruplex DNA binders in cells.
 The interaction of platinum(II) salophen complexes
(504) ( ) with
G-quadruplex DNA has also been examined. Complex 504a contains an additional ethyl piperidine
unit to maximize the interactions with G-quadruplex DNA, while complex 504b is modified with a sulfonate group to reduce nonspecific
interactions with DNA. The cellular uptake and localization of the
complexes vary in different cell lines. Complex 504a localizes
in the cell nucleus and nucleoli in CHO and HeLa cells, respectively,
while residing in small pockets outside the nucleus in HepG2 cells.
In contrast, complex 504b displays slower cellular uptake
due to the negatively charged sulfonate group, and it localizes in
both the cell nucleoli and cytoplasm in all three of these cell lines.
A related platinum(II) complex conjugated with a DOTA ligand has been
radiolabeled with 111In to examine its in vivo biodistribution and clearance using SPECT imaging. The 111In-labeled complex (505)
exhibits rapid renal clearance in mice bearing B16-F10 tumor xenografts,
and it accumulates in the tumor and is retained for a long time (>
72 h). Lewis, Vilar, and co-workers have utilized rotaxanes as a triggerable
cage for platinum(II) salophen complexes (506) to regulate
their binding with G-quadruplex DNA and to aid their controlled release
inside cells. The G-quadruplex binding
activity of complexes 506a and 506b is hindered
in the presence of a bipyridine-based macrocycle due to the significant
steric bulk surrounding them, but it is restored upon enzymatic cleavage
or light irradiation to release the macrocycle. These rotaxanes show
increased cellular uptake compared to the free complex, and display
punctate staining in live U2OS cells. Additionally, the light-responsive
rotaxane exhibits substantially increased cytotoxic activity upon
continuous irradiation, resulting from the translocation of the platinum(II)
salphen complex to the cell nucleus and its binding to DNA. Vázquez,
Vilar, and co-workers have developed dimeric platinum(II) salophen
complexes linked with different peptide sequences (507) for targeting multimeric G-quadruplex DNA. The peptide linker plays an important role in the binding
affinity and selectivity of the complexes toward different G-quadruplex
DNA topologies. Upon cellular internalization, the peptide conjugates
reside in both the nucleoli and cytoplasm of U2OS cells.
 Liu and co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II)
complex (508) ( ) that can target G-quadruplex DNA with higher affinity
and selectivity
than duplex DNA. Both enantiomers of
the complex bind to G-quadruplex DNA, but the Λ-isomer stabilizes
human telomeric G-quadruplex DNA to a greater extent compared to the
Δ-isomer. The Λ-isomer shows selective enrichment in the
nucleus of cancerous HepG2 cells after uptake via an energy-dependent,
nonendocytic pathway but negligible nuclear accumulation in noncancerous
HUVECs. In contrast, the Δ-isomer displays limited nuclear uptake.
Keyes and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II) complex (509) that integrates the DNA “light switch”
properties of [Ru(phen)2(tpphz)]2+ and the G-quadruplex-binding
ability of the G-quadruplex stabilizer phen-DC3 to visualize G-quadruplex
DNA. The complex remains nonluminescent
in aqueous solution but exhibits emission turn-on upon binding to
G-quadruplexes. The complex shows high affinity and specificity for
G-quadruplex DNA over duplex DNA, but it exerts strong disruptive
effect on G-quadruplex structures. The complex enters A549 cells via
an energy-dependent pathway and displays distinctive punctate staining
