due to its association with the membranes of the endosomal/lysosomal
compartments. Although the complex cannot reach the G-quadruplex DNA
in the nucleus, its high affinity and specificity toward G-quadruplex
DNA and strong disruptive effect on G-quadruplex structure indicate
its potential as a theranostic agent targeting G-quadruplexes.
 Shao and co-workers have developed two platinum(II)
complexes coordinated
with an aza-bridged N,N-bis-(1,10-phenanthroline-2-yl)amine
ligand (510) ( ) that exhibit substantial emission enhancement upon
binding to G-quadruplex RNA. Complex 510a has been used to visualize G-quadruplex RNA in live cells,
resulting in luminescent foci in the cytoplasm. Real-time monitoring
of the quantity and folding dynamics of intracellular G-quadruplex
RNA in various cancer and normal cell lines shows that G-quadruplex
RNA is more abundant and less frequently unfold in cancer cells. A
redox-active platinum(IV) complex (511) has been obtained
through oxidation of complex 510a. The axial ligands regulate the G-quadruplex binding activity
of the complex by impeding potential π–π interaction,
allowing the complex to show emission turn-on only upon binding to
G-quadruplexes after reduction by intracellular GSH. Furthermore,
these lipophilic ligands facilitate the cellular and nuclear uptake
of the complex, enabling the simultaneous detection of both G-quadruplex
RNA in the cytoplasm and G-quadruplex DNA in the nucleus. Additionally,
the complex displays potent cytotoxicity after a long incubation time,
highlighting its potential as a theranostic agent.
 Mao, Zhou, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III)
complex
functionalized with two G-quadruplex-binding pyridostatin derivatives
PDP (512) ( ) as an imaging probe for G-quadruplexes. The emission lifetime of the complex increases
upon binding to G-quadruplex DNA and RNA, which are not observed for
single- or double-stranded nucleic acid structures. The emission lifetime
changes have enabled the examination of intracellular dynamics of
G-quadruplex RNA using TP-PLIM, which shows that G-quadruplex RNA
is more active in normal MCF-10A cells than cancerous MCF-7 cells.
These results highlight the different regulatory roles G-quadruplex
RNA plays in cancer and normal cells.
 Moucheron, Demeunynck, Monchaud, and co-workers
have developed
two ruthenium(II) complexes bearing a π-extended acridine-based
ligand (513) ( ) for selective labeling of G-quadruplex DNA and RNA. These complexes exhibit up to 330-fold emission
enhancement in the presence of G-quadruplexes, but less than 60-fold
increase in their emission intensities upon interaction with duplex
DNA. The large difference in their emission intensities has enabled
the sensitive and selective detection of G-quadruplexes even in the
presence of an excess amount of duplex. Treatment of B16-F10 cells
with complex 513b results in luminescent foci in the
nucleolar and perinuclear region, which has been ascribed to the labeling
of G-quadruplex RNA embedded in the nucleoli and cytoplasmic ribonucleoprotein
complexes, respectively.
 

8.12.

## Proteins
Proteins are involved in
numerous cellular functions, and maintaining their homeostasis is
crucial for normal cellular metabolism. Aberrant protein expression
has been implicated in promoting cancer progression and development. Furthermore, the accumulation of misfolded
or aggregated proteins is closely associated with various human diseases
such as diabetes and neurodegenerative disorders like AD and PD. 
8.12.1.

## G-Protein-Coupled Receptors
G-Protein-coupled
receptors (GPCRs) constitute the largest group of integral membrane
proteins in eukaryotic cells. These
proteins are responsible for the transduction of extracellular signals
from agonist binding into intracellular signaling pathways, mediating
cellular processes such as neurotransmission, hormone release, cardiovascular
functions, and immune response. Their aberrant expression and activation
in cancer cells stimulate their associated signaling networks to promote
tumor growth, angiogenesis, and metastasis. 
8.12.1.1.

## Dopamine Receptors
Dopamine
receptors are a class of GPCRs that are prominent in the vertebrate
CNS. These receptors are classified into D1- and D2-like subtypes
according to their biochemical, physiological, and pharmacological
characteristics. The D1-like receptors are involved in the activation
of adenylate cyclase, while the D2-like receptors trigger the inhibition
of adenylyl cyclase, phosphatidyl inositol turnover, and intracellular
Ca2+ mobilization. They are frequently upregulated in different
types of cancer; , for example, the overexpression
of D1-like receptors in breast cancer tissues has been linked to more
advanced tumor stages and grades, as well as a higher incidence of
nodal metastasis. 
Kwong, Tan,
Leung, Ma, and co-workers have modified iridium(III) complexes with
a dopamine agonist to develop luminescent probes (514) ( ) for
imaging intracellular dopamine receptors. Incubation of live A549 cells, a human non-small-cell lung cancer
cell line with dopamine receptor expression, with the complexes results
in intense intracellular emission, which substantially decreases in
intensity when the cells are treated with small interfering RNA (siRNA)
to knockdown dopamine receptor gene expression or pretreated with
an excess of dopamine to compete for the dopamine receptors. These
observations confirm the specific binding of the complexes to the
dopamine receptors inside cells. Complex 514b has been
used to monitor the internalization of dopamine receptors in cells,
a process that is important in maintaining cellular homeostasis.
 
8.12.1.2.

## Gastrin-Releasing Peptide Receptor
Gastrin-releasing peptide (GRP) is involved in many physiological
functions such as regulating gastrin release and subsequent gastric
acid secretion. It has also been recognized as a growth factor for
cancer cells. The receptor for GRP, gastrin-releasing peptide receptor
(GRPR), is overexpressed in a variety of cancer tissues, while its
expression in normal tissues is relatively limited. 
Leung, Ma, and co-workers have conjugated an iridium(III)
complex with the peptide-based GRPR antagonist JMV594 for imaging
GRPR in GRPR-positive A549 cells. The
specific binding of the resulting conjugate (515) ( ) to GRPR on the
cell surface is confirmed by the GRPR antagonist (e.g., JMV594 and RC-3095) competition and GRPR gene knockdown experiments.
Thus, the conjugate can discriminate cancerous A549 cells from normal
LO2 cells due to the different GRPR expression levels. Additionally,
the binding of the conjugate to GRPR in RAW264.7 cells reduces the
secretion of tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-α, indicating that
the conjugate can modulate the functions of GRPR in immune cells.
 
8.12.1.3.

## CXC Chemokine Receptor 4
Chemokine
receptors constitute a family of GPCRs predominantly present on the
surface of leukocytes. Among these receptors, the CXC chemokine receptor
4 (CXCR4) plays a crucial role in various physiological processes
including development, hematopoiesis, organogenesis, and vascularization. It serves as the primary physiological receptor
for stromal cell-derived factor 1 (SDF-1). The interaction between CXCR4 and SDF-1 initiates a cascade of intracellular
signaling transduction pathways that govern essential cellular processes
such as cell survival, proliferation, chemotaxis, migration, and adhesion.
The upregulated expression of CXCR4 in cancer cells has been implicated
in cancer progression and metastasis.
Velders, van Leeuwen,
and co-workers have conjugated [Ir(ppy)3] with the cyclic
peptide Ac-TZ14011, a derivative of the CXCR4 antagonist T140, for
targeting CXCR4. The resultant peptide
conjugates (516) ( ) bind to CXCR4 with high affinity (Kd = 66.3 to 254.4 nM), which enables them to
visualize CXCR4 expression in cells. Live-cell imaging demonstrates
that upon incubation at 4°C to inhibit the occurrence of endocytosis,
the conjugates are enriched in the plasma membrane of MDA-MB-231 cells
that are transfected to overexpress CXCR4 (MDA-MB-231CXCR4+). Furthermore, the conjugates show increased accumulation in MDA-MB-231CXCR4+ cells compared to MDA-MB-231 cells that express CXCR4
at basal levels. These findings illustrate the high specificity of
the conjugates toward CXCR4 expressed on the cell surface.
 
8.12.1.4.

## Gonadotropin-Releasing Hormone Receptors
Gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) receptors, also known as
luteinizing hormone-releasing hormone receptors, are GPCRs that play
a key role in reproductive function. The binding of GnRH, which is released from the hypothalamus, to
these receptors in the anterior pituitary regulates the synthesis
and secretion of luteinizing hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone.
There has been increasing evidence that GnRH receptors are overexpressed
in various cancer tissues, which makes them an attractive molecular
target for the treatment of endocrine-related cancers. 
Tzakos and co-workers have examined the
cellular uptake of a ruthenium(II) complex conjugated with a GnRH-derived
decapeptide (517) ( ) in bladder cancer T24 cells, which displays
a high expression level of GnRH receptors. The cellular internalization of the conjugate remains low for an
incubation period of 4 h, but it gradually increases upon extending
the incubation time to 24 h. In contrast, the cellular uptake of the
conjugate is negligible in HeLa cells, which lack endogenous expression
of GnRH receptors, highlighting the role of the receptors in mediating
the cellular internalization of the conjugate.
 
8.12.1.5.

## Formyl Peptide Receptor 2
Formyl
peptide receptors (FPRs) constitute a small group of GPCRs that recognize
chemotactic signals to mediate inflammatory and immune response. Within the human FPR family, FPR2 is the most
intriguing member due to its interaction with a wide range of endogenous
or exogenous ligands. It plays an important role in regulating angiogenesis,
which is crucial for wound healing and tissue regeneration. However,
uncontrolled angiogenesis is central for tumor growth, progression,
and metastasis.
Leung, Ma, and co-workers have developed a luminescent
probe (518) ( ) for imaging FPR2 in live cells. The probe features the conjugation of an iridium(III) complex
with a synthetic hexapeptide WKYMVm that acts an agonist of FPR2.
The conjugate exhibits specific binding to FPR2, which has enabled
the visualization of FPR2 in live HUVECs. Competition assays with
the FPR2 antagonist, WRW4, and siRNA knockdown experiments confirm
that the observed intense intracellular emission is a result of the
specific binding of the conjugate to FPR2. Additionally, the conjugate
reduces the motility of HUVECs both in the absence and presence of
the FPR2 agonist lipoxin A4, indicating that the conjugate can regulate
FPR2 signaling in live cells.
 

8.12.2.

## Integrins
Integrins are a large
family of heterodimeric transmembrane receptors composed of an α-subunit
and a β-subunit. They are responsible
for mediating biological processes involving cell–cell and
cell–extracellular matrix adhesion, playing a key role in various
diseases. One prominent member is the
platelet integrin αIIbβ3, which
is expressed at high levels on circulating platelets. Activation of
integrin αIIbβ3 plays a vital role
in platelet aggregation, leading to thrombosis. Another member sharing
the same β3 subunit is integrin αvβ3, which is often overexpressed in cancer cells,
contributing to the promotion of tumor growth, invasion, and metastasis.
Keyes and co-workers have conjugated ruthenium(II) complexes with
an RGD-containing peptide that shows a high affinity for integrin
αIIbβ3. The resultant conjugates (519) ( ) display substantial emission
enhancement upon binding to integrin αIIbβ3, which are attributed to the changes in the local environment
surrounding the metal complexes. Among the two conjugates, conjugate 519a binds to a single site on the protein with high affinity
(Kd = 0.09 μM), whereas conjugate 519b binds to the protein via a two-step binding mechanism
involving hydrophobic interaction along with RGD recognition. However,
the binding affinity of conjugate 519b is much lower
(Kd = 0.25 and 4.37 μM) due to its
larger size and higher lipophilicity. The conjugates have been used
to visualize integrin αIIbβ3 in
CHO cells that are transfected to increase their expression of this
transmembrane protein. The conjugates reside in the plasma membrane
of the cells and colocalize with the αIIb-specific
monoclonal antibody SZ22 ( ), confirming the specific binding of the conjugates
to integrin αIIbβ3 expressed on
the cell surface. Fei and co-workers have utilized an iridium(III)
solvato complex [Ir(ppy)2(OH2)2]+ to staple a modified RGD peptide with two additional His
residues at its two ends to produce a luminescent cyclic RGD peptide
conjugate (520). The cyclic
RGD peptide exhibits substantially increased stability and binding
affinity for integrin αvβ3 due to
its structural constraint, resulting in more efficient internalization
in αvβ3-positive A549 cells compared
to its linear counterpart. In contrast, there is no comparable uptake
observed for the conjugate in MCF-7 cells that express αvβ3 at lower levels. The conjugate has been
further tethered with the apoptosis-inducing peptide KLA at the C-terminus.
This modification has enabled the conjugate to show cancer-selective
cytotoxic activity, particularly toward A549 cells (IC50 = 4.5 μM) compared to MCF-7 cells (IC50 = 37.7
μM) due to their different levels of αvβ3 expression.
 
8.12.3.

## Epidermal Growth Factor Receptor
Human epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) is a transmembrane
glycoprotein that belongs to the ErbB family of receptor tyrosine
kinases. The binding of epidermal growth
factor (EGF) to this receptor results in receptor dimerization and
autophosphorylation, activating the signaling pathways that are involved
in cell differentiation and proliferation. It is overexpressed on
the plasma membrane of various types of cancer cells to promote tumor
growth, invasion, and metastasis. 
Leung, Ma, and co-workers have conducted a cell-based screening of
a series of iridium(III) complexes modified with the EGFR kinase inhibitor
tyrphostin 1, which identifies complex 521 ( ) as a theranostic
agent that enables both the visualization and inhibition of EGFR in
live cells. Incubation of human epidermoid
carcinoma A431 cells that display a high level of EGFR expression
with the complex results in strong intracellular emission. The intracellular
emission intensity significantly decreases when the cells are pretreated
with tyrphostin 1 to inhibit EGFR activity or siRNA to suppress EGFR
gene expression, indicative of the specific binding of the complex
to EGFR. The complex has also been used to examine EGFR activity in
LO2 cells that exhibit a lower level of EGFR expression, and the negligible
emission observed indicates that the complex can discriminate cancer
cells from normal cells based on their different levels of EGFR expression.
Furthermore, the complex shows higher cytotoxicity toward A431 cells
(IC50 = 37.4 μM) compared to LO2 cells (IC50 > 50 μM) due to effective EGFR inhibition (EC50 = 1.18 μM), which deactivates its downstream targets such
as extracellular signal-regulated kinase, mitogen-activated protein
kinase, and transcription factor activator protein-1.
 Odysseos, Georgiades, and co-workers have developed
two ruthenium(II)
complexes (522) ( ) as EGFR probes through the incorporation
of an EGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor selected from a focused library
of aminoquinazoline derivatives. The bis-quinazoline conjugate 522b displays specific
enrichment in the mitochondria of U-87 MG cells after uptake, which
has been attributed to its association with EGFR that undergoes translocation
to the mitochondria. Wang and co-workers have developed platinum(II)
quinazoline complexes (523) that serve as both EGFR probes
and anticancer agents. Interestingly,
the quinazoline pendant not only allows complexes 523a and 523b to exhibit potent EGFR inhibitory activity
(IC50 = 103.3 and 86.2 nM, respectively) but also increased
DNA binding affinity. As revealed by secondary-ion mass spectrometry
(SIMS) imaging and inductively coupled plasma-mass spectrometry (ICP-MS)
measurements, these complexes localize in both the plasma membrane
and nucleus of HeLa cells due to their binding to EGFR and DNA, respectively.
Remarkably, the complexes show high cytotoxic activity toward various
cancer cell lines including A431, A549, HeLa, and MCF-7 cells, particularly
demonstrating the selective inhibition of EGF-stimulated growth in
MCF-7 cells. Related ruthenium(II) quinazoline complexes (524) have also been developed as cancer theranostics. Multimodal imaging with LSCM and SIMS shows that complex 524a primarily resides in the cytoplasm, while complex 524b predominantly accumulates in the nucleus. These complexes
display enhanced cytotoxicity toward cancer cells upon irradiation
due to 1O2 generation.
 Lam, Lo, and co-workers have developed iridium(III)
complexes bearing
a heteroaromatic methylsulfone moiety for selective modification of
Cys-containing peptides and proteins. One of these complexes has been conjugated with the dodecapeptide
GE11, which binds to EGFR with high affinity and selectivity. The
resultant conjugate (525) ( ) exhibits selective enrichment in the
plasma membrane of cancerous MDA-MB-231 and A431 cells but not in
noncancerous HEK293T cells due to its specific binding to the EGFRs
expressed on the surface of cancer cells. The cancer-selective accumulation
of the conjugate results in potent photocytotoxic activity toward
MDA-MB-231 and A431 cells (IC50 = 1.01 and 0.49 μM,
respectively) compared to HEK293T cells (IC50 = 10.09 μM).
 
8.12.4.

## Prostate-Specific Membrane Antigen
Prostate-specific membrane antigen (PSMA) is a type II transmembrane
glycoprotein expressed in all types of prostatic tissue. Notably, it shows increased expression in most
prostate cancer cells, making it a valuable target for diagnosing
and treating prostate cancer. 
Liu, Leung, Wang, and co-workers have designed an iridium(III) complex
conjugated with a PSMA inhibitor (526) ( ) for imaging PSMA in prostate
cancer microenvironments. The complex
displays high binding affinity for PSMA (Kd = 9.49 nM), which is over 1,000-fold stronger than a Glu–urea-based
PSMA inhibitor (Kd = 12.4 μM). The
complex has been used to visualize LNCaP cells in both 2D and 3D cultures,
resulting in intense emission on the plasma membrane of the cells.
The membrane staining results from the binding of the complex to PSMA
overexpressed on the cell surface, as evidenced by the significantly
reduced emission intensity in cells pretreated with siRNA to knockdown
PSMA gene expression. The strong and specific binding of the complex
to PSMA has enabled facile discrimination of prostatic cancer cells
expressing high (LNCaP), medium-to-low (22RV1), and negligible levels
of PSMA (PC3). The high specificity of the complex toward PSMA has
also enabled the visualization of 22Rv1 tumors in live mice.
 
8.12.5.

## Bromodomain-Containing Protein 4
Bromodomain-containing protein 4 (BRD4) belongs to the mammalian
bromodomain and extra-terminal domain (BET) family of proteins. It functions as an epigenetic reader that specifically
recognizes acetylated histone and nonhistone proteins to regulate
gene transcription. It also plays a crucial role in cell cycle progression,
embryogenesis, and immune response. The
dysregulation of BRD4 functions has been associated with various diseases
such as inflammation, viral infection, neurological disorders, and
cancer. 
Ma, Wang, Leung, and co-workers
have developed a luminescent probe for BRD4 (527) ( ) by conjugating
an iridium(III) complex to the BRD4 inhibitor K197. The complex specifically binds to BRD4 in the cytoplasm
of A375 cells over other BET family members such as BRD2 and BRD3.
The complex has been used to establish a cell-based platform for screening
a library of FDA-approved drugs for BRD4 inhibitors. This approach
has led to the identification of Azelastine, an FDA-approved histamine
1 antagonist, as a potential BRD4 inhibitor that prevents BRD4 translocation
into the nucleus, and suppresses cancer cell growth and migration.
 
8.12.6.

## Nuclear Factor-κB
Nuclear
factor-κB (NF-κB) is a ubiquitous transcription factor
that regulates the expression of genes involved in inflammatory and
immune response, as well as those in cell survival, proliferation,
and differentiation. It exists as a
homodimer or heterodimer that is located in the cytoplasm in an inactive
form, and upon stimulation, it translocates to the nucleus to regulate
gene expression.
Tan, Leung, Ma, and co-workers have developed
a luminescent probe (528) ( ) for NF-κB by conjugating an iridium(III)
complex with the natural product oridonin that specifically binds
to active NF-κB dimers (i.e., p50/p50 homodimer
or p50/p65 heterodimer). The complex
shows specific binding to intracellular NF-κB in live HeLa cells.
Monitoring of NF-κB trafficking shows that it translocates from
the cytoplasm to the nucleus upon stimulation with TNF-α. Additionally,
the complex displays minimal inhibitory effects on the NF-κB
transcriptional activity and cell viability (IC50 >
100
μM), suggesting that the complex can be used to monitor NF-κB
activity in live cells without influencing its normal functions.
 Tian, Tian, and co-workers have developed a theranostic
platinum(II)
complex (529) ( ) that can hijack endogenous NF-κB translocation
to induce cancer cell death. The complex
is nonluminescent in aqueous solution but exhibits intense emission
upon binding to NF-κB. The specific binding of the complex to
NF-κB has enabled the visualization of the intracellular localization
of NF-κB in live cells, revealing that it localizes in the nucleus
of cancer cells (HepG2, HSS5787, HeLa, MCF-7, H460, SH-SY5Y, MDA-MB-231,
A549, and U2O3), but resides in the cytoplasm of normal cells (HELF,
mouse Astrocyte, mouse brain endothelium bEND3, HEK293T, and NIH/3T3).
It has also revealed the translocation of NF-κB from the cytoplasm
to the nucleus in RAW264.7 cells upon inflammation. The distinctive
intracellular localization of NF-κB has allowed the complex
to hijack endogenous NF-κB translocation to enter the nucleus
of cancer cells to induce apoptosis, while remaining inactive in the
cytoplasm of normal cells. Thus, the complex exhibits significantly
higher cytotoxic activity toward HepG2 cells (IC50 = 0.28
μM) compared to HELF cells (IC50 > 50 μM).
This cancer-selective cytotoxicity has led to effective inhibition
of tumor growth in mice bearing 4T1 tumor xenografts with negligible
side effects.
 
8.12.7.

## Nucleolin
Nucleolin is one of
the most abundant nonribosomal phosphorylated proteins in the nucleolus. It is involved in cellular processes such as
DNA and RNA metabolism, chromatin remodeling, ribosome biogenesis,
and nucleocytoplasmic transport. This
protein is also found in the cytoplasm and on the plasma membrane,
and its expression on the surface of cancer cells is upregulated to
promote tumor growth and angiogenesis. 
Chen, Chen, and co-workers have modified an iridium(III) complex
(530) ( ) with the aptamer AS1411, a 26-mer guanosine-rich DNA oligonucleotide
(5′-d(GGTGGTGGTGGTTGTGGTGGTGGTGG)-3′) that has a high
affinity for nucleolin, for cancer-selective imaging and therapy. The conjugate is efficiently taken up by nucleolin-overexpressing
cancer cells (HeLa, MCF-7, A549, and HepG2) through endocytosis and
enriched in the mitochondria, while showing minimal uptake in normal
cells (LO2 and HEK293T) ( ). The selective uptake has allowed the conjugate to
induce mitochondrial dysfunction and caspase-3/7-dependent apoptosis
in cancer cells while displaying much lower cytotoxicity toward normal
cells. Furthermore, the conjugate can specifically target MCTSs, demonstrating
excellent tumor penetration and effective inhibition of cancer cell
proliferation and tumor regrowth. Zeng, Wang, Mei, and co-workers
have utilized the interaction between a ruthenium(II) complex (531) and AS1411 to develop a nucleolin-targeting nanoprobe. The interaction between the complex and AS1411
induces the self-assembly of AS1411 through liquid–liquid phase
separation (LLPS). The resulting nanoprobe recognizes nucleolin overexpressed
on the membrane of cancerous MDA-MB-231 and MCF-7 cells and enters
the cell nucleus via an ATP-dependent endocytic pathway. In contrast,
it exhibits lower cellular uptake in normal MCF-10A cells where nucleolin
is mainly confined to the cell nucleus and deficient in the plasma
membrane. This differential uptake has allowed for the rapid and selective
differentiation of cancer cells from normal cells. The nanoprobe also
shows efficient accumulation and retention in tumor tissues in transgenic
MMTV-PyMT mouse models of breast cancer for an extended period (>
48 h). Furthermore, it can distinguish different grades of invasive
ductal carcinoma in human breast cancer patient pathological sections
based on different levels of nucleolin expression, providing a convenient
and effective method for breast cancer diagnosis in a clinical setting.
Zhao, Chen, and co-workers have loaded a luminescent ruthenium(II)
complex (532) into a cervical cancer-targeted DNA cage
for the rapid diagnosis of cervical and endocervical cancer tissues. The DNA cage is constructed using the aptamer
AS1411 in combination with another aptamer that targets mucin-1, which
is overexpressed in patients with cervical and endocervical cancer.
The resulting nanoprobe specifically distinguishes cancer tissues
from noncancer specimens and diagnoses different grades of cervical
cancer, demonstrating high potential for clinical pathological grading
and surgical judgment in cervical and endocervical cancer.
 
8.12.8.

## Amyloid β
Amyloid precursor
protein (APP) is a transmembrane glycoprotein that plays an important
role in synaptic structure and functions. The proteolytic cleavage of APP by β- and γ-secretases
leads to the production of amyloid β (Aβ) peptides, primarily
consisting of 40 residues (Aβ1–40) and, to
a lesser extent, 42 residues (Aβ1–42). The accumulation of Aβ, particularly
as plaques in the brain parenchyma, plays a key role in AD pathogenesis.
Yu, Wang, Yu, and co-workers have developed a luminescent ruthenium(II)
complex (533) ( ) that binds to all forms of Aβ, including monomers,
oligomers, and fibrils, without disrupting their aggregation behavior. This unique binding behavior has allowed for
the visualization of the whole process of Aβ self-aggregation,
including nucleation, oligomerization, and fibrillation, at the single-fibril
level. This observation is unattainable with the fluorescent Aβ
probe thioflavin T that only stains Aβ fibrils. Real-time monitoring
of fibril growth reveals that Cu2+ accelerates Aβ
self-aggregation, promoting the formation of amorphous oligomers at
high Aβ concentrations and fibers at low Aβ concentrations.
Remarkably, the complex can specifically detect Aβ1–40 in live PC-12 cells ( ). Histological staining shows that the complex can
also differentiate Aβ plaques in brain slices obtained from
APP/PS1 transgenic mice. Notably, the complex can induce effective
photocleavage of Aβ aggregates upon continuous irradiation.
Carlos and co-workers have also developed two luminescent ruthenium(II)
complexes (534) for monitoring Aβ self-aggregation. The long emission lifetimes of the complexes
have enabled real-time imaging of Aβ self-aggregation using
PLIM, allowing the direct observation of the structural evolution
of Aβ monomers to protofibrils (Aβ1–40) and globular oligomers (Aβ15–21) in aqueous
solution. Live-cell imaging shows that these complexes display efficient
uptake and cytoplasmic localization in live Neuro-2a cells.
 Sarantseva, Tunik, and co-workers have conjugated
platinum(II)
complexes with a bifunctional peptide that contains a CPP sequence
(R9) and an Aβ aggregation-inhibiting peptide sequence
(RHVLPKVQA) through His coordination. One of the resulting luminescent peptide conjugates (535) ( ) exhibits
efficient blood-brain barrier penetration and homogenous distribution
in the brains of Drosophila melanogaster showing
the major clinical and pathomorphological characteristics of AD, highlighting
its potential in AD diagnosis and treatment.
 
8.12.9.

## α-Synuclein
α-Synuclein
is an abundant neuronal protein that is predominantly expressed at
presynaptic terminals, where it interacts with synaptic vesicles and
participates in neurotransmitter release, synaptic vesicle pool homeostasis,
and vesicle trafficking. However, the
aberrant aggregation of α-synuclein is associated with PD and
other neurodegenerative diseases such as dementia with Lewy bodies,
multiple system atrophy, and pure autonomic failure. 
Martí and co-workers have
utilized [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+ to detect α-synuclein
both in vitro and in cells. The complex displays an 18-fold increase in emission intensity
upon binding to fibrillar α-synuclein, which enables the real-time
monitoring of the formation of α-synuclein fibrils. The complex
has been used to detect αS aggregation in human neuroglioma
H4 cells. Increased intracellular emission has been observed in cells
that are treated with the proteasomal degradation inhibitor MG-132
to induce α-synuclein aggregation. The emission enhancement
becomes more dramatic in H4/α-syn-GFP cells, where the cells
are transfected to overexpress α-synuclein that is fused with
green fluorescent protein (GFP) as a reporter. The large overlap of
the ruthenium(II) emission with GFP fluorescence further confirms
the binding of the complex to α-synuclein aggregates.

8.13.

## Enzymes
Enzymes are essential for
catalyzing biological transformations and maintaining cellular homeostasis.
Aberrant enzyme activity is often implicated in diseases such as cancer,
which exhibits different enzyme activity profiles compared to normal
cells for promoting cell proliferation, invasion, and metastasis. , 
8.13.1.

## Cyclooxygenases
Cyclooxygenases
(COXs) are membrane-bound, heme-containing peroxidases that catalyze
the conversion of arachidonic acid to prostaglandins, which play a
key role in inflammatory response. They
exist in two distinct isoforms: the constitutive isoform COX-1 that
is ubiquitously expressed in mammalian tissues and is responsible
for the production of prostaglandins with homeostatic function, and
the inducible isoform COX-2 that plays the predominant role in prostaglandins
production during pathological states such as inflammation and tumorigenesis. The expression of COX-2 is usually upregulated
in inflamed and neoplastic tissues. 
Tan, Leung, Ma, and co-workers have conjugated an iridium(III)
complex with a structural analogue of the COX-2 inhibitor indomethacin
to develop a luminescent probe (536) ( ) for monitoring COX-2 activity
in live cells. Incubation of HeLa cells
that show a high level of COX-2 expression with the complex gives
intense emission, which is not observed in LO2 cells with minimal
COX-2 expression. The intracellular emission intensity substantially
decreases when the cells are treated with curcumin to inhibit COX-2
expression, indicating that the complex can report COX-2 expression
in cells due to its high specificity toward COX-2.
 
8.13.2.

## Tyrosinase
Tyrosinase is a copper-containing
oxidase that is crucial for melanin biosynthesis. It catalyzes the hydroxylation of l-tyrosine to
3,4-dihydroxyphenylalanine and its oxidation
to dopaquinone. However, overproduction and accumulation of melanin
can lead to various hyperpigmentation-related dermatological disorders
such as melasma, lentigines, excessive freckles, and nevi. The overexpression of tyrosinase is an important
biomarker for melanoma. Abnormal expression
levels of tyrosinase are also related to other diseases such as vitiligo
and PD.
Zhang, Yoon, and co-workers have modified a platinum(II)
complex with a 3-hydroxybenzyloxy moiety to
develop a tyrosinase-activatable theranostic agent (537) ( ) for
imaging and PDT of melanoma. This modification
facilitates hydroxylation at the 4-position vacancy by tyrosinase,
which is followed by the removal of the resultant hydroxylation product
via a spontaneous 1,6-rearragement–elimination reaction, resulting
in an increase in the emission intensity and 1O2 generation efficiency of the complex. The complex has been used
to image A375 cells that overexpress tyrosinase, and it induces apoptosis
upon continuous white-light irradiation. Remarkably, it can efficiently
detect early-stage melanoma in A375 tumor-bearing mice and suppress
tumor growth upon irradiation with no obvious side effects.
 
8.13.3.

## Quinone Reductases, Nitroreductases, and
Azoreductases
Hypoxia, a condition characterized by low O2 levels, has been implicated in the development of several
human diseases, including metabolic disorders, chronic kidney diseases,
cardiovascular diseases, and cancer. In solid tumors, the reduced
O2 concentration leads to an increase in reductive stress,
which is accompanied by the upregulation of various reductases such
as quinone reductases, nitroreductases, and azoreductases. Thus, the
overexpression of these enzymes has been recognized as an important
biomarker for cancer diagnosis and
therapy. 
Quinone reductases are
enzymes that catalyze the reduction of quinones to their hydroquinone
derivatives using NAD(P)H as an electron donor. Chao and co-workers
have developed three ruthenium(II) complexes modified with a redox-sensitive
anthraquinone moiety (538) ( ) for hypoxia imaging. These complexes display extremely weak emission due to
efficient quenching through PET. However, upon incubation with reductases
and NADPH under hypoxic conditions, the emission intensities of the
complexes substantially increase, resulting from the enzymatic reduction
of the anthraquinone moiety to a nonquenching dihydroanthraquinone
derivative. Furthermore, the dihydroanthraquinone
complexes can be readily reoxidized back to their anthraquinone counterparts
upon exposure to air, resulting in efficient emission quenching. This
reversible emission response has allowed for two-photon luminescence
imaging of cycling hypoxia in live cells and zebrafish. Related ruthenium(II)
(539) and iridium(III)
complexes (540) have been
developed as theranostic agents that enable the visualization of hypoxic
cancer cells and inhibition of their growth in both 2D monolayer and
3D spheroid cell cultures. Iridium(III) complexes incorporating two
anthraquinone moieties (541) have also been developed
for hypoxia imaging. The high mitochondria
specificity of the complexes has enabled the visualization of hypoxic
mitochondria in live A549 cells and MCTSs as well as in zebrafish
using two-photon microscopy.
 Nitroreductases are flavin-containing enzymes
that catalyze the
reduction of nitroaromatics to their corresponding hydroxylamines
or amines using NAD(P)H as a cofactor. Ma and co-workers have utilized
the iridium(III) 5-nitro-1,10-phenanthroline complex 428 as a phosphorogenic probe for nitroreductase. The emission intensity of the complex increases in the
presence of nitroreductase and NADH due to the reduction of the quenching
nitro unit to a nonquenching amine derivative. The complex has been
used to examine the nitroreductase activity in live HeLa cells, showing
that the enzyme activity substantially increases upon hypoxia induction.
Additionally, the complex has been utilized to monitor hypoxia in
live zebrafish. Suzenet, Brindell, and co-workers have utilized the
ruthenium(II) 2-nitroimidazole complex 20e for cancer
imaging and treatment. The reduction
of the 2-nitroimidazole unit by intracellular nitroreductases results
in the formation of a hydroxylamine derivative. This derivative reacts
with biomolecules inside the cells, leading to increased retention
of the complex in hypoxia cancer cells. Thus, the complex exhibits
higher cytotoxic activity toward hypoxic A549 cells compared to the
normoxic cells. Donnelly and co-workers have developed rhenium(I)
and technetium-99m complexes modified with a nitrobenzyl or nitroimidazole
unit (542–545) ( ) for targeting tumor hypoxia. The luminescent rhenium(I) complexes 542a–545a show substantially higher accumulation
in hypoxic SH-SY5Y cells compared to normoxic cells, highlighting
their potential as hypoxia-selective imaging and therapeutic agents.
The radioactive technetium-99m complex 545b has been
used to visualize hypoxic tumors in mice bearing human renal cell
carcinoma SK-RC-52 tumor xenografts with different volumes (400, 600,
and 1,100 mm3). Among the three mouse models, the complex
displays the most significant accumulation in the tumor with a volume
of 600 mm3.
 Azoreductases are enzymes that catalyze the reductive
cleavage
of azo compounds into their corresponding amines using NAD(P)H as
an electron source. Chao and co-workers have developed a series of
iridium(III) complexes incorporating an azo group as an azoreductase-responsive
unit (546) ( ) for monitoring azoreductase activity in living systems. These complexes are nonluminescent due to
the photoinduced isomerization of the azo group that causes efficient
emission quenching. However, when incubated with reductases and NADPH
under hypoxic conditions, the complexes exhibit a substantial increase
in their emission intensities (I/Io = 11.4 to 58.8) due to the reduction of the azo unit
to a nonquenching amine derivative. No similar increase in emission
intensity is observed upon incubation of the complexes with biothiols.
The complexes have been utilized to image hypoxic HeLa cells ( ). The intense
intracellular emission indicates increased azoreductase activity under
hypoxic conditions (1% O2). However, the enzyme activity
remains low when the cells are pretreated with the flavoprotein inhibitor
diphenyliodonium chloride (DPI) or incubated under normoxic
conditions (21% O2). Furthermore, the complex has been
used to visualize hypoxia in HeLa MCTSs, revealing a hypoxic core
in tumor spheroids. A related iridium(III) azo complex modified with
a thiol-terminated alkyl chain (547) has been conjugated
to gold nanorods (AuNRs) to develop a mitochondria-targeting theranostic
nanoagent for hypoxia imaging and PTT. Enzymatic cleavage of the azo linker separates the complex and AuNRs,
resulting in substantial emission enhancement (I/Io = 80) within 30 s due to inhibited FRET. Additionally,
the nanoagent induces a significant increase in temperature from ca. 25 to 60°C upon NIR-light irradiation (808 nm)
due to the photothermal effect of AuNRs. These properties have enabled
the successful visualization of hypoxic tumor and effective inhibition
of tumor growth in mice bearing A549 tumor xenografts.
 
8.13.4.

## Glutathione S-Transferases
Glutathione S-transferases (GSTs) are a class
of enzymes with an important function in cellular metabolism and xenobiotic
detoxification by catalyzing the conjugation of GSH to electrophilic
substrates. These enzymes are abundant
in the liver where they are responsible for drug metabolism and elimination.
However, abnormal expression levels of GSTs have been linked to various
liver diseases, neurodegenerative disorders, and cancer. 
Zhang and co-workers have developed
a ruthenium(II) complex appended with a 3,4-DNP moiety (548) ( ) as
an activity-based probe for detecting GST activity in mice with DILI. The complex is very weakly emissive due to
efficient PET quenching. However, treatment of the complex with GST
in the presence of GSH results in substantial emission enhancement,
as GST catalyzes a nucleophilic aromatic substitution reaction whereby
GSH substitutes one of the quenching nitro groups on the 3,4-DNP unit.
This emission response has enabled the sensitive and selective detection
of GST activity in live HepG2 cells. The large TPA cross-sections
of both the complex (δ752nm = 118
GM) and its GSH adduct (δ765nm =
248 GM) have enabled the use of two-photon microscopy to examine GST
activity in mouse liver tissue slices, which shows that mice treated
with brufen, APAP, and diclofenac sodium (SDDL) show substantially
lower GST activity in the liver than untreated mice ( a). The decrease in GST concentration
in liver tissues upon drug treatment is accompanied by an increase
in GST concentration in the sera ( b), corresponding to the release of GSTs
from liver cells into the blood plasma during DILI due to increased
liver cell membrane permeability. Ex vivo luminescence
imaging of the main organs of mice supports that GSTs are abundant
in mammalian livers ( c), and their activity decreases upon DILI ( d). These findings suggest
that monitoring of GST activity in liver tissues is a promising approach
for diagnosing DILI.
 Wang, Leung, and co-workers have conjugated an
iridium(III) complex
with the GST inhibitor ethacrynic acid to afford an affinity-based
probe (549) ( ) for GSTs. This complex
displays a ca. 4-fold increase in emission intensity
upon incubation with GST-pi, the most abundant member of cytosolic
GSTs in mammalian cells that is also overexpressed in a wide range
of cancers. The complex has been used to visualize GST-pi in MDA-MB-231
cells, a triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC) cell line characterized
by elevated GST-pi expression. Remarkably, the complex specifically
resides in the mitochondria of the cells and binds to mitochondrial
GST-pi. Notably, the interaction of the complex with intracellular
GST-pi is independent of the presence of GSH in live cells, which
is different to most existing GST-pi probes that rely on enzyme-catalyzed
nucleophilic substitution reaction and require the presence of GSH
for the reaction to occur. The complex has been utilized to examine
GST-pi expression in four TNBC cell lines (MDA-MB-231, MDA-MB-468,
4T1, and BT549), a non-TNBC breast cancer cell line (MCF-7), and a
normal breast cell line (MCF-10A) ( ). The different emission intensities of
the complex in different breast cell lines correlate with their respective
GST-pi expression levels at both the transcriptional and translational
levels, indicating that the complex can differentiate between various
types of breast cells and tissues based on their GST-pi expression
levels.
 
8.13.5.

## β-Galactosidase
β-Galactosidase
is a glycoside hydrolase that catalyzes the hydrolysis of the glycosidic
bond in β-galactosides. Its overexpression is an important biomarker
for primary ovarian cancer cells and
senescent cells. 
Leung, Ma, and
co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex bearing a galactose
moiety (550) ( ) for monitoring β-galactosidase activity. The emission intensity of the complex increases
upon enzymatic cleavage of the galactose unit since the binding of
the resultant cleavage product to the enzyme offers protection from
the aqueous surrounding. The complex has been used to examine β-galactosidase
activity in different cell lines and it can differentiate between
ovarian cancer cells (SKOV3 and OVCAR3) and noncancerous cells (HUVEC
and HEK293T) based on different β-galactosidase expression levels
( ).
 
8.13.6.

## Carboxylesterases
Carboxylesterases,
belonging to the serine hydrolase superfamily, catalyze the hydrolysis
of ester, thioester, carbamate, and amide bonds in substrates. They play a crucial role in maintaining lipid
balance and metabolizing xenobiotics. The two major isoforms expressed
in human tissues are human carboxylesterase 1 (hCE1) and human carboxylesterase
2 (hCE2), which exhibit different tissue distribution and substrate
specificity. Abnormal levels of hCE1 and hCE2 have been linked to
various metabolic disorders such as hyperlipidemia, obesity, nonalcoholic
steatohepatitis, diabetes, atherosclerosis, and cancer.
Yam
and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II) complex functionalized
with a coumarin 343 derivative (551) ( ) for sensing esterases. The complex shows both coumarin fluorescence
(λem = 485 nm) and ruthenium(II)
phosphorescence (λem = 620 nm) in
aqueous solution. Cleavage of the ester linkage by esterases separates
the two luminescent moieties, suppressing FRET and thereby leading
to a substantial increase in the intensity of coumarin fluorescence.
The complex is readily taken up by HepG2 cells and reacts with intracellular
esterases, resulting in a change in the emission color from blue to
red. Qiao, Zhang, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex
(552) for specific detection and imaging of hCE2 in live
cells. Upon cleavage of the ester bond
by hCE2, the emission lifetime of the complex increases from 338 to
625 ns due to a reduction in nonradiative decay pathways associated
with intramolecular vibrational relaxation. However, when the complex
is incubated with other enzymes such as hCE1, acetylcholinesterase,
and butyrylcholinesterase, there are only
minor changes in the emission lifetime. Monitoring hCE2 activity in
live HepG2 cells using PLIM shows that hCE2 activity decreases when
the cells are treated with the hCE inhibitor bis(p-nitrophenyl)phosphate (BNPP) ( ).
 
8.13.7.

## Caspases
Caspases are a family
of aspartate-specific cysteine proteases that hydrolyze peptide bonds
at the C-terminus of aspartic acid residues in substrates, with the
reaction requiring the catalytic Cys residue in the active site. They
play a critical role in regulating cell death and inflammation. They also contribute to cell differentiation,
cell proliferation, tissue regeneration, tumorigenesis, aging, and
neural development.
Lee, Lo, and co-workers have developed a
phosphorogenic probe (553) ( ) for caspase-3/7, which are key executors
in apoptosis. The probe is comprised
of the emission quencher QSY-7 and a rhenium(I) complex conjugated
to the N-terminus and the π-clamp sequence, respectively, of
a caspase-3/7-cleavable peptide DEVDFCPF. The probe is weakly emissive
in aqueous solution due to FRET; however, the specific cleavage of
the DEVD sequence by caspase-3/7 separates the quencher from the rhenium(I)
unit, resulting in a remarkable increase in the emission intensity.
It has been used to examine caspase-3/7 activity in esophageal squamous
cell carcinoma KYSE-510 cells, demonstrating that the enzyme activity
increases when the cells are treated with cisplatin to induce apoptosis
( ).
 
8.13.8.

## Matrix Metalloproteinases
Matrix
metalloproteinases (MMPs) are a group of zinc-dependent endopeptidases
responsible for the turnover and degradation of the extracellular
matrix. They play an important role in the regulation of tissue remodeling
to maintain tissue homeostasis. However,
MMPs are overexpressed in many types of cancer and implicated in tumor
progression, invasion, metastasis, and angiogenesis. 
van Leeuwen and co-workers have developed
a luminogenic probe (554) ( ) for MMP-2/9, which is overexpressed in
breast cancer tissues and plays a key role in lymph node metastasis. The probe is comprised of [Ir(ppy)3] and a Cy5 derivative conjugated to the two ends of an MMP-2/9-cleavable
peptide eeeeeGPLGLArrrrrrrrk. The Glu and Arg residues in the peptide
sequence facilitate a hairpin conformation that brings the two luminescent
moieties into close proximity, allowing efficient quenching of the
iridium(III) phosphorescence and the Cy5 fluorescence by FRET and
heavy atom effect, respectively. The use of d-amino acids,
except for the cleavage sequence, prevents nonspecific cleavage by
other proteases. Cleavage of the peptide conjugate by MMP-2/9 separates
the two luminescent moieties, resulting in an increase in the intensity
of both iridium(III) and Cy5 emission, as well as an increase in the
lifetime of iridium(III) phosphorescence from 30 to 94 ns. The conjugate
has been used to examine MMP-2/9 activity in SKOV3 cells that overexpress
MMP-2/9. The observation of both iridium(III) and Cy5 emission in
SKOV3 cells confirms the successful cleavage of the conjugate by MMP-2/9.
 
8.13.9.

## Carbonic Anhydrases
Carbonic anhydrases
(CAs) are metalloenzymes that catalyze the reversible hydration of
carbon dioxide to bicarbonate and proton, which is crucial in regulating
intra- and extracellular pH and ion transport. Among the various active isoforms expressed in human tissues,
the membrane-associated isoforms CA-IX and CA-XII are overexpressed
in cancer cells in response to hypoxia to counteract acidosis through
regulating intracellular pH for promoting cell survival and growth. 
Delsuc, Policar, and co-workers have
modified a rhenium(I) complex with the CA inhibitor benzenesulfonamide
via an N-acyl imidazole linker to afford a SCoMPI
probe (555) ( ) for CA detection. This
design allows for the traceless labeling of endogenous CAs through
ligand-directed acyl imidazole chemistry, where the binding of the
benzenesulfonamide unit to the specific enzymes facilitates acyl transfer
to a nucleophilic amino acid in the vicinity on the enzyme surface,
resulting in the covalent attachment of the complex to the enzyme.
The complex has been used to label endogenous CAs in A549 cells. As
revealed by SXRF microspectroscopy, the rhenium signal derived from
the SCoMPI is concentrated in the cell nucleus, which disappears when
the cells are treated with the competitive CA inhibitor ethoxyzolamide,
indicative of specific labeling of intracellular CAs. Yang, Gao, and
co-workers have developed NIR luminescent platinum(II) complexes modified
with a benzenesulfonamide unit (556) as theranostic agents. Live-cell imaging demonstrates that the intracellular
localization of complex 556b varies with the cell lines
used. In particular, the complex gradually translocates from the cytoplasm
to the nucleus in HeLa cells after uptake, while it is predominantly
enriched in the lysosomes of MDA-MB-231 cells. Notably, the complex
displays higher cytotoxicity toward cancer cells (HeLa, A549, and
MDA-MB-231) (IC50 = 3.37 to 5.72 μM) compared to
normal cells (HLF and LO2 cells) (IC50 = 9.21 and 61.37
μM, respectively).
 

9.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Probes
for Microenvironment
The intracellular microenvironment is
governed by various factors,
including O2 levels, pH, polarity, viscosity, and temperature.
These factors play a crucial role in controlling the physical and
chemical behaviors of cellular components such as their diffusion,
transportation, and intermolecular interactions. Disturbances in these
factors can lead to cellular dysfunction and are closely linked to
the development of severe diseases. Thus, the development of luminescent
probes capable of providing insights into the pathophysiological microenvironments
holds great potential for aiding in the diagnosis and treatment of
clinical diseases. , 
9.1.

## Oxygen
The intracellular O2 level is a key indicator of cellular metabolism and health status.
Inadequate O2 supply can lead to hypoxia, which is a characteristic
feature of many diseases including solid tumors. Conversely, excessive inhalation of O2 can result
in hyperoxia, which generates an excessive amount of ROS. The accumulation of ROS can cause oxidative
damage to cells, tissues, and organs, leading to their dysfunction.
Phosphorescent transition metal complexes are promising candidates
for monitoring O2 levels in a nondestructive, real-time,
and reversible manner. The emission
intensities and lifetimes of these complexes are highly sensitive
to the O2 levels of their local surroundings, allowing
for accurate and precise measurement of O2 levels in living
systems. This particular feature is challenging to achieve with organic
fluorophores.
9.1.1.

## Palladium(II) and Platinum(II) Porphyrins
Phosphorescent palladium(II) and platinum(II) porphyrins represent
an important class of O2 sensors due to their intense and
long-lived red to NIR emission. Their long emission lifetimes (in
the range of 10–1000 μs) have allowed for the use of
various lifetime-based imaging techniques to examine changes in O2 levels in vitro and in vivo. For example, Becker and co-workers have developed a lifetime imaging
approach that combines FLIM and PLIM for simultaneous detection of
both fluorescence and phosphorescence lifetime changes in cells. Monitoring of the fluorescence of NAD(P)H
and the phosphorescence of PdTCPP (H2TCPP = meso-tetra(4-carboxyphenyl)porphyrin) (557) ( ) in HEK293 cells
indicates a decrease in the fluorescence lifetime upon hypoxia induction
due to reduced oxidative phosphorylation. Savitsky and co-workers
have utilized the time-correlated single photon counting method to
measure the dynamics of tissue oxygenation in vivo. Monitoring of the emission lifetime
of a palladium(II) porphyrin (558) in live mice bearing
sarcoma S37 tumors indicates that the O2 level in tumors
is lower compared to normal tissues, and the intratumoral O2 level decreases upon PDT due to O2 consumption during
the treatment.
 However, most of the metalloporphyrins typically
suffer from poor
aqueous solubility due to their hydrophobic nature, limiting their
applications in biological systems. Wilson and co-workers have incorporated
PdTPP and PdTPTBP into a poly(l-glutamic acid) dendrimer
to afford two water-soluble O2 probes, Oxyphor R2 (559a) and G2 (559b) ( ), which have
been used to examine the O2 profiles in different murine
tumor models. , Related dendritic probes featuring
a higher generation of dendritic skeleton with surface coating with
PEG (560 and 561) have also been developed. − The dendrimer encapsulation allows a high degree of control of O2 diffusion and interaction with the triplet excited states
of the complexes, while the peripheral PEGylation imparts high aqueous
solubility and minimizes interactions with albumin and other biomolecules.
These dendritic complexes have been used to measure the O2 distribution in various areas of live mice, including the interstitial
space of normal skeletal muscle, the
cortical vasculature, and tumors. Kamachi and co-workers have examined the O2 sensitivity and biomolecular interactions of a series of
dendritic platinum(II) porphyrins, which consist of PtTPP as the core,
poly(l-lysine) as the dendrons, and various amino acids as
the surface functional groups. The
dendritic skeleton plays an important role in suppressing the nonspecific
interactions between the complex and biomolecules, facilitating their
accumulation in cells for intracellular O2 sensing.
 Pogue and co-workers have introduced a new in vivo O2 imaging approach termed Cherenkov-excited
luminescence
imaging (CELI) that utilizes X-ray radiation to generate Cherenkov
radiation in tissues to excite the administrated O2 probe. − In CELI, the platinum(II) derivative of the dendritic probe 561b is used as its long emission lifetime (τo = 47 μs) is amendable to the time-gated techniques
used to record the Cherenkov radiation-induced luminescence. The CELI approach has allowed for in vivo mapping of tumor O2 distribution with
spatial resolution close to 0.1 mm at tissue depths of several millimeters
even with nanomolar concentrations of the probe. The high spatial resolution is crucial to accurate quantification
of the hypoxic fraction in tumors. Since
this imaging technique enables the evaluation of spatial O2 distribution in tumors at the time of the radiation dose delivery,
it offers a noninvasive method to track the response of tumors to
fractionated radiotherapy. The probe
can also be loaded into an agarose matrix to afford an implantable
O2 sensor for local CELI of intratumoral O2 during
the course of radiotherapy. 
Considering
that palladium(II) and platinum(II) porphyrins typically
possess small TPA cross-sections, Vinogradov and co-workers have modified
a dendritic platinum(II) porphyrin with multiple coumarin 343 moieties
as a two-photon light-harvesting antenna for sensitizing PtTPP phosphorescence
under two-photon excitation through FRET. The resultant probe (562) ( ) is compatible with TP-PLIM, which allows
noninvasive high-resolution imaging of O2 gradients in vivo. Remarkably, the probe has enabled the direct measurement
of local O2 concentration in the bone marrow of live mice,
revealing a unique hypoxic environment mediated by its dense vascularity
and high cellularity. The balance between
O2 supply and consumption can be altered by stress such
as radiation and chemotherapy. The imaging approach has also been
applied to measure the O2 levels in mouse cerebral vasculature
and tissues, − offering exciting opportunities to examine brain metabolism. A related
probe (563) with improved performance including increased
FRET efficiency, emission quantum yield, and signal dynamic range
has been developed using platinum(II) tetracyclohexenoporphyrin as
the core and coumarin 307 moieties as the antenna. The probe has been utilized with TP-PLIM to measure the
intravascular O2 level in the bone marrow of live mice.
Weber, Vinogradov, and co-workers have also designed an antenna-free,
two-photon-active O2 probe Oxyphor 2P (564) with a larger TPA cross-section (δ960nm = ca. 650 GM), longer emission wavelength (λem = 758 nm), and higher brightness, enabling
O2 imaging at higher speeds and deeper depths. This complex has been used to monitor O2 levels in the cortex of awake mice using TP-PLIM. − 
 Although these metalloporphyrins are useful for
examining O2 concentration in blood as their large molecular
sizes prevent
them from sequestering into surrounding cells or tissues, their poor
cellular uptake hinders their applications in determining intracellular
O2 levels. Papkovsky and co-workers have conjugated platinum(II)
coproporphyrin I derivatives with CPPs to afford cell-permeable probes
for intracellular O2 sensing. Among the various CPP conjugates, the nonaarginine conjugate (565a) ( ) displays the most efficient cellular uptake. The conjugate
has been used to examine the intracellular O2 levels in
various cell lines. A related conjugate bearing a bactenecin 7 peptide
fragment (565b) has also been developed and utilized
for the measurement of intracellular O2 levels, revealing
significant cell deoxygenation in response to mitochondrial uncoupling
and external hypoxia. Studies on a
series of oligoarginine conjugates of platinum(II) coproporphyrin
I shows that the tetrasubstituted conjugate (566a) exhibits
uniform distribution across the cell including the nucleus, enabling
efficient measurement of cell oxygenation and metabolic response. Its palladium(II) counterpart (566b), which shows a longer emission lifetime and higher O2 sensitivity, has also been prepared and used for monitoring changes
in intracellular O2 levels under hypoxia. Papkovsky and
co-workers have also modified PtTPFPP with glucose moieties to yield
a water-soluble and cell-permeable O2 probe (567) for the detection of O2 distribution in live neurospheres
and brain tissue slices. Heterosubstituted
bifunctional probes (568) which contain one glucose unit
for cell targeting and three polar cysteamine or PEG moieties for
forming a hydrophilic shell with variable formal charges have also
been prepared. Structure–property
relationship analysis shows that the positive charge of cysteamine
can enhance the membrane penetration of the complexes for intracellular
O2 sensing, while PEGylation prevents the complexes from
entering the cells due to the negative charges and large molecular
size, facilitating extracellular O2 sensing.
 The encapsulation of palladium(II) and platinum(II)
porphyrins
into NPs has proven to be an effective means to prepare optical O2 nanosensors. Li and co-workers have designed an O2 nanoprobe by encapsulating PdTPTBP into phospholipid micelles. The substantial changes in the emission lifetime
of the nanoprobe in response to O2 levels allow for quantitative
mapping of O2 distribution in vivo via
wide-field phosphorescence lifetime imaging, which offers a faster
imaging rate than traditional PLIM. This imaging approach has been
used for real-time quantification of changes in O2 concentration
in the livers of mice afflicted with hepatic I/R injury and mice bearing
orthotopic liver tumors. Papkovsky and co-workers have formulated
PtTPFPP into a cationic polymer to afford cell-penetrating PNPs for
intracellular O2 sensing. , The PNPs
have been used for monitoring metabolic response of cells under ambient
and hypoxic microenvironment. Further introduction of an O2-insensitive fluorophore polydioctylfluorene (PFO) as both an energy
donor and a two-photon light-harvesting antenna for PtTPFPP affords
a new nanosensor for ratiometric and lifetime imaging of intracellular
O2 levels under one and two-photon excitation. The nanosensor has been used for time-lapse
imaging of intracellular O2 levels at the single-cell level
in response to hypoxia, metabolic inhibition, and mitochondrial cytochrome-c release. Related nanoprobes
developed using an anionic polymer have been used for sensing O2 levels in neural cells and tissues, uncovering heterogeneous
O2 distribution in embryonic brain tissues that correlates
with the localization of specific progenitor cell populations. Papkovsky and co-workers have utilized platinum(II) meso-tetra(4-fluorophenyl)tetrabenzoporphyrin (569) ( ), which
can be excited at a longer wavelength compared to PtTPFPP, to develop
a cell-penetrating nanoprobe for in vivo imaging
of O2 in the mouse brain following peripheral sensory stimulation,
showing that sensory stimulation leads to changes in oxygenation of
activated areas of the barrel cortex. 
 The O2-insensitive fluorescence of
polyfluorene derivatives
renders them ideal candidates for integration with phosphorescent
palladium(II) and platinum(II) porphyrins to develop ratiometric O2 sensors. McNeill and co-workers have doped PtOEP into polyfluorene-based
Pdots for O2 sensing. The
resulting Pdots display efficient cellular uptake in live J774A.1
cells, suggesting their potential applications in imaging intracellular
O2 concentration. Rao and co-workers have developed an
O2 nanoprobe, composed of PdTPTBP and a semiconducting
polymer poly[2,7-(9,9-dioctylfluorene)-alt-4,7-bis(thiophen-2-yl)benzo-2,1,3-thiadiazole]
(PFO-DBT), for quantitative, longitudinal imaging of tumor hypoxia
dynamics during radiotherapy. Upon
red-light irradiation (λex = 630
nm), the nanoprobe exhibits both PFO-DBT fluorescence (λem = 685 nm) and PdTPTBP phosphorescence (λem = 795 nm), allowing ratiometric O2 imaging
in the NIR region. The nanoprobe shows efficient tumor accumulation in vivo and effective penetration into the tumor including
the hypoxic region, enabling quantitative mapping of hypoxia across
the tumor. Monitoring tumor hypoxia dynamics over 7 days during fractionated
radiotherapy reveals that a high fractional dose (10 Gy) is more effective
in improving tumor reoxygenation than a low dose (2 Gy), and the effect
tends to persist longer in smaller or more radiosensitive tumors.
Chen and co-workers have developed functionalized Pdots that enable
simultaneous imaging of intracellular O2 levels and pH
through the coassembly of PtTPFPP and PFO with fluorescein-modified
polystyrene using a nanoprecipitation method. The resultant Pdots display triple emission upon single-wavelength
excitation, enabling the ratiometric sensing of O2 and
pH in live cells.
Wu and co-workers have incorporated PtTPFPP
into the PFO backbone
to afford a dual-emissive polymer (570) ( ), which forms compact Pdots
upon nanoprecipitation with poly(styrene-co-maleic
anhydride). These Pdots are highly
sensitive to O2 levels at the single-particle level, enabling
the imaging of hypoxia–normoxia cycles in live MCF-7 cells
and tumor hypoxia in live mice bearing MCF-7 tumor xenografts. Zhao,
Li, Huang, and co-workers have incorporated PtTPP into the backbone
of a fluorene-based CPE, which yields an amphiphilic dual-emissive
CPE (571) that self-assembles into ultrasmall Pdots (size
= ca. 5 nm) in aqueous solution. These Pdots have been used for ratiometric and lifetime
imaging of intracellular O2 levels in live HepG2 cells
and tumor-bearing mice. Dmitriev, Borisov, and co-workers have also
developed conjugated polymer nanoparticles (CPNs) using dual-emissive
CPEs (572) for high-resolution imaging of O2in vitro and in vivo. The CPNs formed are of different cationic,
anionic, or zwitterionic structures, and all of them exhibit efficient
staining in a broad range of 2D and 3D cell and tissue models. Notably,
the zwitterionic CPNs exhibit efficient intracellular staining and
deep penetration across multiple cell layers in 3D models of HCT116
and PC-12 cells. Furthermore, Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have developed
branched CPEs (573 and 574) for tumor hypoxia
imaging and PDT. , The branched structures of
the CPEs can reduce their aggregation, which in turns enhances their
O2 sensitivity and 1O2 generation
efficiency. These features have enabled efficient tumor detection in vivo and almost complete tumor ablation upon irradiation.
 Guo, Zhu, and co-workers have incorporated PtTPP
into a PVP backbone,
which gives an unprecedented monochromophoric ratiometric probe (575) ( ) for hypoxia imaging. The amphiphilic
polymer self-assembles to form micelle-like NPs with an average diameter
of 118 nm in aqueous solution. In addition to the characteristic long-lived
phosphorescence (λem = 760 nm, τo = ca. 40 μs)
from the PtTPP unit, the polymeric probe shows a short-lived fluorescence
band at 660 nm (τo = ca. 7 ns) originating from the assembled clusters of the PVP chain.
The probe specifically accumulates in the tumor region of tumor-bearing
mice due to the EPR effect, enabling the visualization of the hypoxic
tumor tissues with high spatiotemporal resolution. Ye, Fan, Wong,
and co-workers have developed an NIR-II luminescent platinum(II) porphyrin
(576) for imaging tumor hypoxia. The complex displays intense emission at > 1,000 nm
in
the solid state due to the formation of J-aggregates. Encapsulation
of the complex into Pluronic F127 induces J-aggregation within the
resulting PNPs, giving rise to O2-sensitive NIR-II phosphorescence
with a long emission lifetime of 35.33 μs when excited at 500
nm. However, upon excitation at 808 nm, the PNPs exhibit O2-insensitive NIR-II fluorescence with a short lifetime of 2.89 ns,
arising from the J-aggregates of the ligand. The PNPs have been used
for imaging hypoxia in live 4T1 cells and tumor-bearing mice. Monitoring
of both NIR-II fluorescence and phosphorescence using different excitation
wavelengths shows that the PNPs are preferentially enriched in the
tumor and liver tissues in vivo, with the NIR-II
phosphorescence signal mainly observed in the tumor tissues.
 Chen and co-workers have designed water-dispersible
supramolecular
polymer nanoparticles (SPNPs) for ratiometric O2 sensing
in live cells. The SPNPs are prepared
from the coassembly of three ureidopyrimidinone-based
quadruple hydrogen-bonded supramolecular polymers, which contain a
palladium(II) porphyrin (577) ( ) as a phosphorescent O2 indicator,
a difluoroboron diketonate derivative as a fluorescent reference dye
(BF2-bis-UPy), and a 9,10-diphenyl
anthracene derivative (DPA-bis-UPy)
as a skeleton scaffold. Related SPNPs comprising a ureidopyrimidinone-modified
platinum(II) porphyrin (578) as the energy acceptor and
the ureidopyrimidinone-modified 9,10-diphenyl
anthracene derivative DPA-bis-UPy as
the energy donor have been developed for ratiometric hypoxia imaging
and PDT. The efficient energy transfer
between the two dyes in the SPNPs leads to emission enhancement and
lifetime extension, facilitating O2 detection and 1O2 photosensitization. Fukumura, Bawendi, Jain,
Duda, Nocera, and co-workers have utilized lipid micelles to encapsulate
the supramolecular assemblies of a palladium(II) porphyrin (579) with quantum dots (Qdots) for the quantification of O2 levels in vivo. Since Qdots possess a large TPA cross-section, two-photon excitation
of the Qdots results in efficient FRET to the palladium(II) porphyrin
bound to the surface of Qdots, enabling ratiometric O2 sensing.
The micelle-encapsulated supramolecular assemblies have been used
for multiphoton imaging and lifetime-based O2 measurements
in mice with chronic dorsal skinfold chambers or cranial windows.
 Kopelman and co-workers have developed luminescent
PNPs as O2 nanosensors for intracellular O2 measurements. The nanosensors are prepared by the incorporation
of Oxyphor G2 and a fluorescent dye into polyacrylamide hydrogel NPs,
followed by surface modification with CPPs (Tat) or tumor-specific
peptides (F3). The resulting nanosensors have enabled real-time monitoring
of O2 levels in live cancer cells under normoxic and hypoxic
conditions. Alves, Resch-Genger, Schäferling, and co-workers
have developed tumor-targeting polystyrene NPs for imaging tumor hypoxia in vivo. The polystyrene
NPs are doped with PdTPTBP and an O2-insensitive cyanine
dye, with the NP surface coated with PEG and functionalized with the
human epidermal growth receptor 2 (HER2)-targeting monoclonal antibody
trastuzumab. This design enables the NPs to show selective accumulation
in cancer cells overexpressing HER2, facilitating ratiometric imaging
of tumor hypoxia in live mice bearing human pancreatic adenocarcinoma
AsPC-1 tumor xenografts. Peng, Teng, and co-workers have utilized
PNPs doped with PtTPFPP to develop three different kinds of phosphorescent
O2 nanosensors that specifically detect extracellular,
intracellular, and mitochondrial O2 levels. Monitoring of the mitochondrial O2 levels in HepG2 and LO2 cells reveals that the mitochondria in cancer
cells display reduced activity compared to normal cells due to their
altered glucose metabolism and physical defects in the respiratory
chain. Related mitochondria-targeting NPs have been developed as theranostic
agents for both O2 sensing and PDT. Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have developed a core–shell
structured silica-based nanoprobe for ratiometric and lifetime imaging
of O2 levels. The red luminescent
platinum(II) porphyrin (580) ( ) is physically entrapped in the porous
shell to increase its interaction with O2, whereas the
blue luminescent iridium(III) complex 184a is covalently
immobilized in the solid core to protect it from O2 quenching.
This design enables sensitive detection and quantitative measurement
of intracellular O2 levels. Additionally, the long emission
lifetime of the nanoprobe allows the use of time-resolved techniques
to improve the signal-to-noise ratio.
 Wang, Wolfbeis, and co-workers have designed
a dual-emissive nanosensor
for imaging intracellular O2 levels and pH. This nanosensor features a core–shell
structure, where the O2-sensitive probe PtTPTBP and the
reference dye H2TPFPP are encapsulated in the hydrophobic
core, while the pH-sensitive probe fluorescein is covalently attached
to the PEG end-group on the shell. The nanosensor exhibits excellent
stability in aqueous solution and shows rapid response toward both
O2 and pH in the physiological range. Tian and co-workers
have developed core–shell structured PNPs as a ratiometric
sensor for simultaneous monitoring of intracellular O2 and
glucose concentration. A PtTPP derivative
(581) and a rhodamine B derivative (Rh-MA) ( ) are
incorporated into the hydrophobic core using a microemulsion polymerization
approach, while the glucose sensor (AnBA-NHS) is grafted
onto the hydrophilic shell. The nanosensors are distributed in the
cell cytoplasm, enabling effective intracellular O2 and
glucose measurements even at the single-cell level. Xie and co-workers
have utilized poly(dimethylsiloxane) as a host material for PdTPFPP
and a Cl– sensor to produce a dual functional nanoprobe
capable of simultaneous monitoring of O2 and Cl–. Upon cellular internalization, the
probe specifically localizes in the lysosomes of live HeLa cells.
Measurement of the intracellular emission intensity reveals an average
Cl– concentration of ca. 85.7 mM
in the lysosomes, and the Cl– level decreases upon
O2 and glucose deprivation. Wang and co-workers have designed
a biodegradable nanosensor that displays excellent response to O2 levels, pH, and temperature in the physiological range. The nanosensor is constructed using BSA as
a scaffold for the covalent immobilization of four different luminophores,
including PtTCPP as an O2 probe, fluorescein as a pH indicator,
a europium(III) complex as a temperature sensor, and rhodamine B as
a reference dye. The nanosensor has been used for real-time imaging
of intracellular O2 levels, pH, and temperature in live
HeLa cells.
 Babilas and co-workers have immobilized PtOEP
in a polystyrene
matrix to afford a transparent planar sensor for imaging O2 distribution in the tumor and normal tissues based on a dorsal skinfold
chamber model of live mice with amelanotic melanoma. This method has enabled the mapping of surface
O2 distribution with high temporal (ca. 100 ms) and spatial resolution (> 25 μm). Furthermore,
the
transparent sensor has allowed the simultaneous visualization of the
underlying microvasculature and the corresponding O2 levels.
Liu, Austin, Qu, and co-workers have utilized poly(perfluoroether)
as a gas-permeable polymer matrix for loading PtTPFPP to fabricate
a lifetime-based O2 sensor film. The sensor has been used for real-time monitoring of O2 levels in microfluidic or conventional in vitro cell culture. Ingram and co-workers have embedded a PtOEP derivative
(582) ( ) and nanocrystal Qdots into an inert polymer matrix to develop
a biocompatible nanosensor that enables real-time and quantitative
measurements of extracellular O2 levels in living tissues. The sensor has been used to measure the interstitial
O2 concentration in various microdomains of active brain
tissues with temporal resolution of several hundred milliseconds .
 Zhang and co-workers have utilized PtTCPP and
Zr6 cluster
as the building blocks to construct O2-responsive NMOFs. Functionalization of the NMOFs with a cancer
cell membrane endows them with homotypic targeting and immune escape
abilities. This modification has enabled real-time monitoring of O2 fluctuation in cancer cells, offering opportunities for cancer
treatment evaluation. Lin and co-workers have developed a dual-emissive
NMOF for ratiometric O2 sensing in live cells. The NMOF is constructed using platinum(II)
5,15-di(4-carboxyphenyl)porphyrin (583) and quaterphenyldicarboxylic
acid (H2QPDC-NH2) ( ) as the bridging units for
Hf4+, followed by modification with rhodamine B isothiocyanate.
The NMOF exhibits excellent ratiometric emission response toward O2 and efficient cellular uptake, allowing quantitative measurement
of O2 in live CT26 cells with ratiometric readouts. Wang,
Yu, Huang, Stang, and co-workers have designed a dual-emissive platinum(II)
metallacage using PtTCPP, 9,10-di(pyridin-4-yl)-anthracene,
and cis-(PEt3)2Pt(OTf)2 as the building blocks. The
metallacage has been encapsulated into PNPs to improve its circulation
time and tumor accumulation. The resultant PNPs can not only sensitively
detect tumor hypoxia in vivo but also effectively
inhibit the tumor growth, highlighting its potential as a cancer theranostic.
 
9.1.2.

## Ruthenium(II) Complexes
Phosphorescent
ruthenium(II) complexes such as [Ru(bpy)3]2+ and [Ru(phen)3]2+ have been established as
promising candidates for O2 measurements in live cells − and tissues. − These complexes show negligible interaction
with serum albumin, which allows reliable measurement of O2 levels in the blood stream, in the intra- and extracellular space,
and in live cells. Kalinina, Rück,
and co-workers have demonstrated the simultaneous imaging of [Ru(bpy)3]2+ with PLIM and NAD(P)H with FLIM in cells under
different hypoxic conditions, which provides valuable information
about the correlation of intracellular O2 tension and cell
metabolism. This correlated FLIM–PLIM
imaging approach has also been used to monitor the cellular response
upon PDT treatment with TLD1433. , Hille and
co-workers have utilized the FLIM–PLIM technique to monitor
changes in the emission lifetimes of oxidized flavin adenine dinucleotide
and a ruthenium(II) complex (584) ( ) in insect salivary glands
to characterize the metabolic state, showing that dopamine stimulation
increases the cellular metabolic activities and thus pericellular
O2 consumption. Compared
to palladium(II) and platinum(II) porphyrins, ruthenium(II) complexes
display significantly lower photocytotoxicity. The low photocytotoxic activity of [Ru(phen)3]2+ toward endothelial cells has been ascribed to the
fact that the complex is internalized into the cells via endocytosis
and then redistributed toward the peroxisomes and other endosomal
and secretory vesicles before elimination via exocytosis. 
 Various strategies have been adopted to improve
the photophysical
and biological behavior of ruthenium(II) complexes for O2 sensing. Modification of [Ru(bpy)2(phen)]2+ with Hoechst 33258 and an MPP sequence (FrFKFrFK) has afforded O2 probes 585( ) and 586( ) ( ) that specifically target the nucleus
and the mitochondria of cells, respectively, for monitoring variations
in the O2 levels in these organelles in response to stimulation
with mitochondrial uncouplers and inhibitors. Rosenzweig and co-workers
have utilized a ruthenium(II) complex bearing a pyrene pendant (587) for intracellular O2 measurements. The incorporation of a pyrene unit not only
enhances the O2 sensitivity of the complex by prolonging
its triplet excited-state lifetime but also increases the cellular
uptake efficiency of the complex due to its high hydrophobicity. Thus,
the complex is cell permeable and has been used to examine changes
in intracellular O2 levels in live J774 cells in response
to hypoxia induced by glucose oxidase. Tanabe and co-workers have
also developed pyrene-appended ruthenium(II) complexes (588) for imaging hypoxia in live cells. , One of these
complexes has been utilized to visualize ischemia-induced hypoxia
in live mice. Furthermore, Tanabe and
co-workers have developed a ruthenium(II) complex modified with a
hypoxia-sensitive 2-nitroimidazole moiety (589) for examining
the O2 status in tumor tissues. The increased accumulation of the complex in hypoxic cells has facilitated
the real-time monitoring of the fluctuations in O2 levels
in hypoxic cancer cells and tumor tissues. A maleimide-functionalized
ruthenium(II) complex (590) has been incorporated into
an oligodeoxynucleotide modified with pyrene and nitroimidazole units
for imaging O2 levels in tumor tissues. The resulting conjugates form aggregates exhibiting enhanced
cellular uptake and accumulation in a hypoxia-selective manner, facilitating
the imaging of O2 fluctuation in tumor tissues.
 Lloyd and co-workers have embedded [Ru(bpds)3]4– (bpds = bathophenanthroline
disulfonate)
in polyacrylamide-coated NPs for biological imaging of intracellular
O2 tension. − Wolfbeis and co-workers have
encapsulated [Ru(Ph2-phen)3]2+ into
polystyrene NPs for two-photon luminescence imaging of O2 levels in live MCF-7 cells and tissues. This complex has also been doped into nonporous solid SiNPs. The nonporous silica matrix of the NPs has
good O2 permeability, while the surface of the NPs can
been functionalized with a morpholine or TPP+ unit for
targeting the lysosomes and mitochondria, respectively. The resultant
NPs show uniform performance in different cell lines, including cancerous
HeLa and MCF-7 cells and normal MCF-10A cells. The mitochondria-targeting
nanoprobe has also been utilized to monitor intracellular O2 concentration over time using PLIM. Kondo, Tanabe, and co-workers
have functionalized MSNs with complex 590 for monitoring
O2 levels in live cells and in vivo. , The covalent immobilization of the ruthenium(II) complex in the
pore of MSNs attenuates the dark cytotoxicity of the complex by reducing
its interaction with intracellular biomolecules as well as its photocytotoxic
