activity by deactivating the photogenerated 1O2 back to O2 before leaking out from the pores. Dutta and
co-workers have incorporated [Ru(bpy)3]2+ into
siliceous zeolite for sensing O2 in macrophage cells. It shows that the intracellular O2 concentration decreases upon stimulation with zymosan to induce
oxidative burst.
Tanabe and co-workers have developed a dual-emissive
ruthenium(II)
complex (591) ( ), where [Ru(bpy)2(phen)]2+ is
conjugated with an O2-insensitive fluorescent coumarin
unit via an alkyl linker. The alkyl
linker plays an important role in increasing the cellular uptake of
the complex for ratiometric sensing of O2 levels in live
cells. The ratiometric response has allowed the visualization of oxygenation
and deoxygenation inside cells induced by valinomycin and antimycin
A, respectively. Keyes and co-workers have developed a bichromophoric
ruthenium(II)–BODIPY complex (592) that displays
solvent-switchable emission. Both ruthenium(II)
phosphorescence and BODIPY fluorescence are observed from the complex
in organic solvents; however, the BODIPY fluorescence is switched
off in aqueous media, indicating that the complex is useful for ratiometric
O2 sensing in nonaqueous environments such as lipid membranes.
As revealed by LSCM, the complex is readily taken up by CHO cells
and enriched in the cytoplasm and nucleoli.
 Kopelman and co-workers have immobilized [Ru(Ph2-phen)3]2+ and an O2-insensitive
fluorophore
Oregon Green 488–dextran into a sol–gel matrix to prepare
an optical nanosensor for ratiometric O2 imaging in live
rat glioma C6 cells. The entrapment
of the luminophores in the inert matrix can protect them from nonspecific
interactions with proteins inside cells and minimize their cytotoxicity,
enabling reliable measurement of intracellular O2 levels.
Choi, Stroock, and co-workers have incorporated a ruthenium(II) complex
(593) ( ) and coumarin 540 into PNPs for quantitative measurements
of O2 concentration in 3D microfluidic tumor models and
in mouse vasculature. A ruthenium(II)
complex (594) and a BODIPY derivative (BODIPY-NaPy) have been coencapsulated into the core of poly(l-lysine)-coated
polystyrene NPs to protect them from external environment effects. Both luminophores can be directly excited
at a single wavelength for ratiometric O2 measurement without
the requirement for FRET. The PNPs are internalized into cancer cells
such as HeLa and A549 cells via an energy-dependent pathway but impermeable
to normal CHO cells. Remarkably, the PNPs remain intact in the intracellular
environment with no sign of probe leakage, facilitating the ratiometric
detection of late-endosomal O2 levels. Heise, Keyes, and
co-workers have developed a core–shell structured PNPs using
a ruthenium(II)-containing block copolymer (595) that
acts as an emulsifier in the miniemulsion polymerization of BODIPY-loaded
styrene NPs. The spatial separation
of the ruthenium(II) complex and BODIPY into the poly(l-lysine)
shell and polystyrene core of the NPs, respectively, ensures a stable
reference signal unaffected by the external environment. The PNPs
adhere to the plasma membrane of live CHO cells, and their uptake
increases when the cells are treated with a cationic surfactant, resulting
in their localization in the lysosomes. Peng and co-workers have developed
an O2 nanosensor based on a ruthenium(II)-containing metallopolymer
(596a) for real-time monitoring of intracellular oxygenation
and deoxygenation. The metallopolymer
is doped into PNPs using a reprecipitation method, followed by surface
modification with TPP+ moieties to target the mitochondria.
The polymer has also been mixed with an O2-insensitive,
terbium(III)-based metallopolymer to afford a ratiometric nanoprobe
for intracellular imaging in live HeLa cells and MCTSs. Related PNPs have been developed with metallopolymers
bearing extended π-conjugated ligands (596b and 596c). The increased π-conjugation
of the diimine ligands of the ruthenium(II) unit increases the O2 sensitivity of the PNPs, facilitating the visualization of
O2 levels in 2D and 3D cell cultures.
 Guan, Xie, Qu, and co-workers have conjugated
a carboxyl-modified
ruthenium(II) complex (597) ( ) to O2-insensitive Cdots, followed
by PEGylation to induce aggregation of the resultant polymeric probe
in aqueous solution to from a nanosized O2 sensor. This design enables excitation of the complex
with NIR light via the UCL of Cdots. The nanosensor is distributed
in the cytoplasm of live HeLa cells and has been used to monitor changes
in the intracellular O2 levels. It has also been utilized
to detect hypoxia in vivo and image tumor in mice
bearing HeLa tumor xenografts. Leach and co-workers have developed
SiNPs containing [Ru(Ph2-phen)3]2+ and the O2-insensitive fluorophore Nile blue for mapping
O2 concentration in live bacterial biofilms and 3D microfabricated cell cultures. Bu, Shi, and co-workers have designed an ultrasensitive
O2 nanosensor based on NaYF4:Yb/Tm@NaYF4 UCNPs core–hollow mesoporous silica shell NPs. The encapsulation of [Ru(Ph2-phen)3]2+ into the hollow cavity of the NPs enables efficient
energy transfer from the UCNPs to [Ru(Ph2-phen)3]2+ upon NIR-light excitation (λex = 980 nm). The nanosensor has been used to detect changes
in O2 levels in live cells and zebrafish. A related nanoprobe
that allows for dual imaging of O2 and temperature in live
cells has also been developed using NaYF4:Yb,Er@NaYF4 UCNPs. Lin and co-workers
have designed luminescent nanoscale metal–organic layers (NMOLs)
for ratiometric O2 and pH imaging. As a monolayered version of NMOFs, NMOLs allow hierarchical
functionalization and facile access to analytes. The NMOLs are built
from the solvothermal reaction of Hf12 secondary building
units with a dicarboxylic acid-functionalized ruthenium(II) complex
(598), followed by surface modification with pH-sensitive
fluorescein and pH-independent rhodamine B. After cellular internalization,
the nanosensor escapes from the endosomes/lysosomes and accumulates
in the mitochondria, enabling real-time quantitative measurements
of O2 and pH in the mitochondria of live CT26 cells.
 
9.1.3.

## Cyclometalated Iridium(III) and Platinum(II)
Complexes
Cyclometalated iridium(III) and platinum(II) complexes
have emerged as a new class of O2 sensors. One notable
example is the cyclometalated iridium(III) β-diketonate complex
[Ir(btp)2(acac)] (599) ( ) developed by Tobita and
co-workers, which exhibits intense phosphorescence at 616 nm with
a lifetime of 5.8 μs. The complex
shows intense emission in a range of cell lines including CHO, HeLa,
mouse oral squamous carcinoma SCC-7, and human glioblastoma U251 cells
that are incubated at low O2 levels (5%) but not in those
under normoxic conditions (20%). Costaining experiments indicate that
the complex is preferentially enriched in the ER of the cells. Real-time
monitoring of changes in the intracellular O2 levels with
complex 599 indicates that mitochondrial respiration
determines the induction of intracellular hypoxia and the activation of prenylated proteins. Additionally, complex 599 specifically
localizes in the LDs of 3T3-L1 adipocytes due to its neutral and lipophilic
character, allowing real-time monitoring of O2 tension
in LDs upon different metabolic stimulations. The complex has been used for simultaneous imaging of the
LD structure and O2 status in vivo, revealing
that hepatic tissue hypoxia in mice with nonalcoholic fatty liver
is associated with the structural distortions of the sinusoidal microvasculature
caused by the increased and enlarged LDs. Furthermore, complex 599 has been used to image tumor hypoxia in live mice bearing
tumor xenografts. Intense emission
is only observed in the tumor region after intravenous injection of
the complex due to the hypoxic nature of tumor tissues. The possibility
of selective accumulation of the complex in the tumor sites is ruled
out on the basis of the longer emission lifetime detected in the tumor
region over the extratumoral tissues. The hypoxia-sensitive emission
has enabled the monitoring of the tumor hypoxic microenvironment in vivo to evaluate the chemotherapeutic effects of cisplatin. The complex has also been coencapsulated with
AuNPs and poly(iohexol) to give a nanoprobe for bimodal imaging. The AuNPs can enhance the phosphorescence
of the complex and improve the image contrast due to the metal-enhanced
luminescence effect, while poly(iohexol) functions as a CT contrast
agent, enabling imaging with dual luminescence and CT outputs. Wu
and co-workers have developed a related iridium(III) complex coordinated
with poly(n-butyl cyanoacrylate) (600) for the fabrication of core–shell structured NPs with chitosan. Remarkably, these NPs display prolonged retention
in tumor tissues in vivo for over 24 h, surpassing
complex 599 that only remains in tumor tissues for 4
h. This extended retention significantly enhances the effectiveness
of tumor imaging while minimizing nonspecific accumulation in normal
tissues.
 To realize quantitative measurement of intracellular
O2 concentration, Tobita and co-workers have conjugated
iridium(III)
complexes with coumarin 343 , or 7-diethylaminocoumarin via a peptide linker to construct ratiometric
probes (601 and 602) ( ) for O2 sensing
in live cells. Xiao and co-workers have also developed a ratiometric
O2 probe (603) using green fluorescent naphthalimide
as the energy donor. The complex exhibits
high specificity for the ER, which enables the visualization of changes
in the O2 levels in this organelle in live MCF-7 cells,
showing that the O2 levels in the ER increases upon stimulation
with rotenone (an inhibitor of the mitochondrial respiratory chain)
or thapsigargin (an irreversible inhibitor of calcium ATPase in the
ER).
 Tobita and co-workers have modified complex 599 with
a TPP+ moiety to afford a mitochondria-targeting O2 probe (604) ( ) for detecting changes in O2 levels in this organelle. Iridium(III)
complexes modified with a basic amino or dimethylamino group (605) have also been developed for intracellular O2 sensing and hypoxia imaging. These
structural modifications facilitate the cellular uptake of the complexes
and direct them to the lysosomes of the cells. Additionally, these
modifications change the in vivo biodistribution
of the complexes; complex 605b predominantly localizes
in the liver and kidney of tumor-bearing mice, while complex 599 is mainly enriched in the spleen and lung. Complex 605b has been employed for the visualization of intracellular
O2 distribution in the hepatic lobules and on the renal surface of live mice with high spatiotemporal resolution using PLIM. Quantitative
analysis shows that the intracellular O2 concentration
in renal tissues decreases in response to acute ischemia, hypoxemia,
and anemia. Additionally, a lower level
of O2 is detected in the kidney subjected to I/R injury
compared to the contralateral kidney in live mice. The complex has
also been used to interrogate the metabolic and O2 states
of tumors in mice bearing CT26 tumor xenografts via FLIM–PLIM
using an implantable fiber-optic probe, revealing that the reduced
O2 concentration in tumors increases glycolytic metabolism. A related green luminescent iridium(III) complex
(606a) has been utilized with a red luminescent iridium(III)
complex (606b) for multicolor imaging of intracellular
and intravascular O2 levels in pancreatic tissues. Measurements of the emission lifetimes of
the two complexes with PLIM indicate that the average pO2 in pancreatic acinar cells and capillaries is almost equal at ca. 30 mmHg.
 To facilitate O2 imaging at deeper
depths, Tobita and
co-workers have developed an NIR luminescent iridium(III) complex
(607) ( ) (λem = 720 nm) by increasing
the π-conjugation of the btp ligand. This complex can image tumors located 6–7 mm below the surface
of the abdominal skin. Additionally, the complex shows TPA across
800–1,200 nm, rendering it compatible with two-photon excitation
using a femtosecond Yb-doped fiber laser operating at 1,030 nm. Huang, Gao, and co-workers have encapsulated
the complex within the hydrophobic pocket of β-CD labeled with
cyanine 7 to construct a ratiometric O2 probe. The probe not only allows quantitative measurement
of O2 levels in live cells and in vivo but also noninvasive mapping of the hypoxia microenvironment of
solid tumors. Jiang and co-workers have modified the complex with
a PVP pendant to increase its tumor retention by the EPR effect. The resultant conjugate (608)
can accurately report the hypoxic tumor microenvironment in various
cancer models including metastatic tumors in vivo, and even detect a small amount of cancer cells (> 103 cells) before tumor formation based on the increased O2 consumption during cancer cell proliferation. The PVP conjugate
has been further functionalized with a rhodamine or cyanine derivative
to yield dual-emissive probes 609a and 609b, respectively, where the organic dye functions as both an internal
standard for assessing the local probe concentration and an O2-insensitive fluorophore for ratiometric O2 measurement.
These findings indicate that the conjugates can be used for monitoring
tumor development and treatment response involving hypoxia-targeted
therapeutics. The PVP conjugate 608 has also been combined
with an amphiphilic block copolymer poly(ε-caprolactone)-block-poly(N-vinylpyrrolidone) to generate
nanosized comicelles for tracking cancer cell metastasis in
vivo. A related PVP conjugate
(610) that can differentiate inflammatory and healthy
tissues based on different O2 levels has been used to detect
hypoxia-associated inflammation. It
has been incorporated into a polyurethane thin film for long-term
monitoring of would inflammation and healing in mice. Qiu and co-workers
have developed a nanomicelle that consists of an iridium(III) complex
(611) as a hypoxia-sensitive probe and an indocyanine
green derivative (ICG-PEG) as a PTT agent. Sequential excitation of these two components
allows for precise tumor diagnosis and treatment.
 Tobita and co-workers have developed a series
of low-energy-emitting
iridium(III) complexes (612) ( ) for O2 sensing in live cells. Among these complexes, complexes 612c, 612d, 612g, and 612h display
intense NIR emission (λem = 686
to 711 nm) with relatively high emission quantum yields (0.14 to 0.24).
All the complexes exhibit efficient cellular uptake and specific mitochondrial
localization in live HeLa cells. Related NIR luminescent iridium(III)
complexes (613) have also been developed for intracellular
O2 sensing. These complexes
either reside in the mitochondria (613a) or lysosomes
(613b) of live CHO-K1 cells after cellular internalization.
Yoshihara and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex coordinated
with a π-extended meso-mesityl dipyrromethene
ligand (614) that shows long-lived emission in the NIR
region (λem = 773 nm, τo = 18.5 μs). The
complex is efficiently taken up by live cells and enriched in the
ER. The O2-sensitive emission lifetime of the complex has
enabled O2 imaging in tissues up to ca. 100 μm from the liver surface of anesthetized mice and quantification
of O2 gradients in hepatic lobules with cellular-level
resolution using PLIM. Tao, Wang, Wong, Zhao, and co-workers have
developed an isomer engineering approach to manipulate the excited-state
properties of lepidine-based iridium(III) complexes. This method has led to the identification
of complex 615 featuring far-red emission (λem = 660 nm) with a long emission lifetime (τo = 1.60 μs) for hypoxia imaging in live HeLa cells,
zebrafish, and mice. The complex also displays efficient 1O2 photosensitization (ΦΔ = 0.73), resulting in efficient cancer cell death upon irradiation.
The construction of binuclear complexes is another strategy to lower
the emission energy of the complexes. Shi, Yuan, Zhao, and co-workers
have utilized 2-pyrimidine-benzimidazole as a bridging ligand to afford
a single-anion-based cationic binuclear iridium(III) complex (616) for intracellular O2 sensing. This complex exhibits emission at a longer
wavelength with a higher emission quantum yield and a longer emission
lifetime (λem = 619 and 659 nm, Φem = 0.35, τo = 806.4 ns) compared to its mononuclear counterpart (λem = 574 nm, Φem = 0.29, τo = 511.8 ns).
Zhao and co-workers have developed a binuclear iridium(III) complex
(617) for intracellular O2 imaging. Compared to its mononuclear counterpart (λem = 540 nm, τo = 591.6 ns), the complex shows red-shifted and longer-lived
emission (λem = 626 nm, τo = 1076.8 ns). Chou, Chelushkin, and
co-workers have leveraged the self-assembly behavior of a square-planar
platinum(II) complex (618) to construct an NIR luminescent
probe for O2 sensing. Encapsulation
of the complex into the hydrophobic core of polymeric micelles promotes
close intermolecular Pt···Pt contact and thus induces 3MMLCT emission in the NIR region (λem = ca. 790 nm). The luminescent micelles
have been used to monitor the intracellular O2 levels in
CHO-K1 cells under normoxic and hypoxic conditions. Papkovsky and
co-workers have modified an iridium(III) porphyrin with two CPPs to
afford cell-penetrating NIR probes for intracellular O2 sensing. In particular, the bis-tetraarginine conjugate (619) displays
efficient cellular internalization and accumulation in a wide range
of cell lines, facilitating the monitoring of changes in intracellular
O2 levels in response to hypoxic conditions and metabolic
stimulation.
 A long emission lifetime is advantageous to monitoring
intracellular
O2 levels as it can not only increase the O2 sensitivity of the probes but also allow the use of time-resolved
techniques to eliminate the short-lived autofluorescence background
to improve the signal-to-noise ratio. Liu, Zhao, Huang, and co-workers
have tuned the excited-state properties of iridium(III) complexes
through the incorporation of a carbazole or 1,2,3-trifluorobenzene
group into the diimine ligand. This
approach has led to the identification of complex 620 ( ) that
exhibits emission originating from a long-lived 3ILCT excited
state, which endows the complex with increased O2 sensitivity
and thus enables more sensitive O2 detection in live cells
via lifetime imaging. Yan, Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have demonstrated
that the introduction of an electron-withdrawing o-carborane unit to iridium(III) complexes induces a significant blue
shift of over 70 nm in the emission maxima concomitant with a substantial
increase in the emission quantum yields and lifetimes. For example, the iridium(III) complex functionalized
with a hydrophilic nido-o-carborane
moiety (621) shows intense emission (Φem = 0.57) with a long lifetime (τ = 4.38 μs) in aqueous solution and high sensitivity toward
O2, enabling its application in intracellular O2 imaging. Related iridium(III) o-carborane complexes
(622 and 623) have also been used for endocellular
hypoxia imaging. , 
 In addition to the emission properties, the aqueous
solubility
of the complexes is an important consideration. Chou, Tunik, and co-workers
have developed a water-soluble iridium(III) complex containing a sulfonated
diphosphine ligand (624) ( ) that is suitable for O2 mapping. The complex is efficiently taken up by live
HeLa cells despite its negative net charge. The O2-sensitive
emission lifetime and the large TPA cross-section of the complex (δ800 nm = 627 GM) have allowed the
monitoring of changes in intracellular O2 levels in vitro and in vivo using TP-PLIM, revealing
a lower level of oxygenation in tumors compared to the surrounding
muscle tissues. Tunik and co-workers have also developed iridium(III)
complexes modified with short “double-tail” OEG pendants
(625–627) as water-soluble and biocompatible
O2 probes. – The OEG corona surrounding the complexes
increases their aqueous solubility and shields them from side interaction
with components in biological media while preserving their high cellular
internalization efficiencies, enabling mapping of O2 distribution
in live cells and mice.
 The encapsulation of transition metal complexes
into PNPs is another
effective approach to protect them from interacting with the components
of physiological media while maintaining their sensitive lifetime
response toward O2 variations. Chelushkin, Chou, Tunik,
and co-workers have embedded an NIR luminescent iridium(III) complex
(628) ( ) into polymeric micelles to develop a luminescent nanoprobe
for O2 sensing in live CHO-K1 cells. Xu, Zhu, Zhao, Yang, and co-workers have utilized a TADF
polymer (PCzDP-20) to encapsulate iridium(III) complexes
(629) for constructing luminescent PNPs with enhanced
emission quantum yields and lifetimes for intracellular O2 sensing. The use of a TADF polymer
as an energy donor to sensitize the emission of iridium(III) complexes
allows the exploitation of both singlet and triplet excitons, enabling
efficient FRET (> 80%) even at a low doping ratio of the iridium(III)
complexes (2.5%). Bochkarev and co-workers have developed norbornene-modified
iridium(III) complexes for the preparation of water-soluble luminescent
polymeric probes (630) for imaging O2 levels
in biological tissues in vivo. A related membrane-permeable polymeric probe (631) has been used to examine the correlation between metabolic state
and O2 levels in live cancer cells and tumor-bearing mice,
revealing a high level of cellular heterogeneity in both metabolism
and O2 distribution in vivo. 
 Zhao and co-workers have developed three anionic
iridium(III) complexes
(632) ( ) for monitoring intracellular O2 levels. The red luminescent complex 632c has been paired with a cationic, blue fluorescent, and O2-insensitive polyfluorene-based CPE (633) or zinc(II) complex (634) to give water-soluble dual-emissive soft salts
for ratiometric and lifetime imaging of O2 in live cells.
Iridium(III) and platinum(II) complexes have also been covalently
conjugated with polyfluorene to develop dual-emissive CPEs (635 and 636). , These amphiphilic
polymers self-assemble into ultrasmall Pdots with a particle size
down to 10 nm in aqueous solution. The resulting Pdots have been used
for monitoring O2 levels in live cells via ratiometric
and lifetime imaging. Wang, Zhao, and co-workers have developed a
luminescent hyperbranched conjugated polymer (637) with
an iridium(III) complex as the core and polyfluorene units as the
branches. Coprecipitation with poly(styrene-co-maleic anhydride) endows the resulting Pdots with high
aqueous solubility and biocompatibility. The Pdots have been used
for ratiometric and lifetime imaging of intracellular O2 variations. Furthermore, the Pdots can induce effective cancer cell
death upon continuous irradiation due to 1O2 generation, realizing image-guided PDT.
 Zhao, Li, Huang, and co-workers have developed
a phosphorescent
nanoprobe for hypoxia imaging, where an iridium(III) complex (638) ( ) is attached to the outer silica shell of mesoporous silica-coated
lanthanide-doped UCNPs. This design
allows the use of both NIR light excitation and time-resolved techniques
to effectively minimize cell autofluorescence interference, enabling
the sensitive detection of hypoxia in live cells. Liu, Huang, Zhao,
and co-workers have utilized emissive gold nanoclusters as both an
O2-insensitive luminophore and a nanocarrier for an iridium(III)
complex (639) to construct a nanoprobe for ratiometric
and lifetime imaging of changes in O2 levels in live cells
and zebrafish. Wu and co-workers have
utilized glycerol monoolein cubic-phase NPs as a platform to prepare
a nanoprobe for ratiometric O2 imaging. A red phosphorescent iridium(III) complex (640) and O2-insensitive, NIR fluorescent Qdots are incorporated
into the hydrophobic and hydrophilic channels of the NPs, respectively,
to avoid interference between the two components. Notably, the nanoprobe
exhibits prolonged tumor accumulation time compared to the free complex,
enabling long-term monitoring of O2 level changes in tumors in vivo. Chelushkin, Chou, Tunik, and co-workers have developed
a dual O2- and pH-responsive sensor through covalent modification
of HSA with an iridium(III) complex (641) and FITC. The drastic changes in the phosphorescence
and fluorescence lifetimes of the protein conjugate in response to
O2 and pH, respectively, have allowed the spatial and temporal
tracking of both O2 levels and pH in live cells using a
dual FLIM–PLIM imaging technique. The conjugate has also been
used for O2 and pH sensing inside the endolysosomes of
human endometrial mesenchymal stromal cells in both 2D and 3D cultures. 
 Zhao, Lo, Huang, and co-workers reported the
first molecular probe
(642) ( ) that can differentiate hypoxia, normoxia, and hyperoxia
in living systems. The nonconjugated
amino groups introduce a new 3NLCT (n →
π*) excited state that inhibits internal conversion between
the 3IL and 3CT states, resulting in dual emission
at ca. 520 and 620 nm, respectively. Owing to the
different O2 sensitivity of the two triplet emissive states,
the complex shows naked-eye distinguishable green, orange, and red
emission in aqueous solution under an atmosphere of 0, 21, and 100%
O2, respectively ( ). The sensitive spectral response has enabled the
detection of both hypoxia and hyperoxia in live cells, zebrafish,
and mice. This design strategy has further been exploited to develop
a dual-emissive complex (643) that allows for the simultaneous
detection of O2 and Cu2+. The increase in local O2 concentration results
in the selective quenching of the longer-lived 3IL emission
at 490 nm, whereas Cu2+ binding induces the selective quenching
of the 3CT emission at 620 nm. The orthogonal emission
response has enabled the sensitive and independent detection of changes
in intracellular O2 and Cu2+ concentrations
in live HeLa cells without crosstalk. Shi, Li, Zhao, Yuan, and co-workers
have developed a platinum(II) complex (644) that displays
dual emission under hypoxia. The introduction
of a strong electron-donating N-embedded π-conjugation unit
leads to weak SOC (ξ = 5.26 cm–1) and thus an inadequate ISC process, resulting in both fluorescence
and phosphorescence at 552 and 610 nm, respectively. The complex has
been used to examine intracellular O2 levels by monitoring
the ratiometric emission response and phosphorescence lifetime.
 In the aforementioned wavelength-based ratiometric
probes, the
luminophores are designed to emit at different wavelengths for spectral
separation. However, it is difficult to avoid spectral overlap and
possible energy transfer between the luminophores. Additionally, variations
in the absorption and scattering of light of different wavelengths
in biological samples can significantly reduce the detection efficiency.
Notably, the use of time-resolved techniques allows the separation
of multiple emission signals with varying emission lifetimes at the
same wavelength, enabling the analysis of the individual emission
spectrum of the luminophores. Zhao, Zhang, and co-workers have developed
a lifetime-based polymeric probe (645) ( ) for time-resolved ratiometric
O2 imaging. The probe is
composed of a fluorescent phenoxazine derivative and a phosphorescent
iridium(III) complex, both of which emit at ca. 550
nm in deaerated aqueous buffer but with lifetimes of ca. 2.9 and 931 ns, respectively. Upon increasing the O2 content to 100%, the phosphorescence lifetime is reduced to 251
ns, with the phosphorescence/fluorescence ratio decreasing from 4.49
to 1.08. The different emission lifetimes of the two luminophores
allow spectral separation using time-resolved techniques, enabling
quantitative measurement and mapping of intracellular O2 levels in live HeLa cells. This design strategy also opens up the
possibilities of multiplex imaging and sensing. Zhang, Huang, Zhao,
and co-workers have designed a polymeric probe (646)
for simultaneous sensing of O2 and HClO via time-resolved
analysis. The probe is comprised of
an O2-sensitive phosphorescent iridium(III) complex and
a HClO-responsive fluorescent phenothiazine derivative. The phosphorescence
of the probe is efficiently quenched upon increasing the O2 level, while the fluorescence is substantially enhanced upon oxidation
of phenothiazine by HClO to its sulfoxide derivative. The probe has
been applied to cellular imaging with PLIM, and its independent emission
response toward O2 and HClO in separated time intervals
has enabled simultaneous quantitative determination of O2 and HClO levels in cells. These findings illustrate that the long
emission lifetimes of transition metal complexes are not only advantageous
to background-free imaging but also provide exciting opportunities
for multiplex imaging and sensing.
 

9.2.

## pH
Intracellular pH plays an important
role in regulating cell survival, metabolism, and proliferation. It varies significantly among different compartments,
ranging from ca. 4.7 in lysosomes to 8.0 in mitochondria
in normal mammalian cells. Dysregulation of pH homeostasis in both
the cytoplasm and intracellular organelles has been implicated in
various pathological conditions such as cancer and AD. 
Zhou and Zhang have designed an NIR
luminescent iridium(III) complex conjugated with a fluorescein diacetate
unit (647) ( ) for ratiometric imaging of intracellular pH. Upon cellular internalization, fluorescein
diacetate, which is nonfluorescent, is converted by intracellular
esterase into fluorescein that shows pH-sensitive fluorescence. The
ratio Igreen/Ired decreases upon decreasing intracellular pH, enabling quantitative
pH measurements. Zhao, Wong, and co-workers have developed a luminescent
soft salt (648) for ratiometric and lifetime imaging
of intracellular pH variations. This
soft salt is comprised of a cationic, pH-sensitive iridium(III) complex
appended with two pyridyl moieties (λem = 625 nm) and an anionic, pH-insensitive iridium(III) cyanide complex
(λem = 451 and 475 nm). Increasing
the pH from 2.03 to 7.94 increases the ratio I625nm/I451nm from 0.18 to 2.86
due to selective quenching of the emission of the cationic complex
upon protonation of the pyridyl units. Additionally, the lifetime
of the red emission increases from 73 ns (at pH 3.99) to 328 ns (at
pH 7.94). These photophysical changes have enabled the detection and
quantitative measurements of intracellular pH fluctuations induced
by oxidative stress ( ). Liu, Zhao, and co-workers have designed a water-soluble
polymeric probe (649) for ratiometric pH sensing in vitro and in vivo. This probe is composed of a pH-responsive iridium(III)
complex (λem = 580 nm) and a pH-insensitive
iridium(III) complex (λem = 470
nm) tethered on a hydrophilic PVP backbone. Under acidic conditions,
both the uncoordinated pyridine ring on the cyclometalating ligand
and the amide group on the diimine ligand of the pH-responsive complex
are protonated, inhibiting intramolecular oxidative quenching. Thus,
upon changing the pH from 7.34 to 3.01, the ratio I470nm/I580nm increases from
0.36 to 0.87, and the emission lifetime at 580 nm decreases from 1,073
to 530 ns. The probe has been used to measure pH fluctuations in subcellular
organelles and examine the effects of pH modifiers on the pH of the
intestinal juice in zebrafish digestive tracts. Zhang, Zhao, and co-workers
have developed a dual-emissive polymeric probe (650)
for simultaneous and independent sensing of intracellular pH and HClO. This probe is comprised of a pH-sensitive
phosphorescent iridium(III) complex and a HClO-responsive fluorescent
anthracene carboxamide derivative. The phosphorescence of the probe
is quenched upon protonation of the pyridyl groups on the iridium(III)
complex under acidic conditions, while the fluorescence is quenched
upon oxidation of the thioether group on the anthracene carboxamide
by HClO. Time-resolved luminescence imaging with the probe has allowed
for independent, simultaneous, and quantitative measurement of both
phosphorescence and fluorescence in cells and their response toward
pH and HClO due to different emission lifetimes.
 Diao, Chao, and co-workers have developed mitochondria-targeting
iridium(III) complexes modified with two morpholine moieties (651) ( ) for monitoring pH variations in the mitochondria. Protonation of the morpholine groups under
acidic conditions results in efficient emission quenching through
PET. These complexes have been utilized to track changes in the mitochondrial
pH in HeLa cells upon CCCP treatment, revealing that the mitochondrial
pH decreases from ca. 8.0 to 7.1 during apoptosis
( ). Hao,
Li, and co-workers have developed an iridium(III) complex (652) capable of simultaneously inducing mitophagy and quantitatively
monitoring mitochondrial pH changes. The emission intensity and lifetime of the complex increase with
decreasing pH due to protonation of the imidazole moieties. Upon specific
accumulation in the mitochondria, the complex induces mitochondrial
depolarization and mitophagy. Monitoring the emission lifetime of
the complex using TP-PLIM has allowed quantitative measurement of
mitochondrial pH changes in a real-time manner, revealing mitochondrial
acidification during mitophagy due to the fusion of mitochondria with
autophagosomes and lysosomes. Zhang, Zhang and co-workers have developed
two pairs of isomeric iridium(III) complexes (653) that
show different emission response upon protonation of the benzimidazole
ring and carboxyl group. In particular,
complexes 653a and 653c display a more significant
decrease in emission intensities (23- and 25-fold, respectively) and
a considerable shift in emission maxima (41.4 and 40.9 nm, respectively)
compared to complexes 653b and 653d. The
higher pH sensitivity of complexes 653a and 653c has enabled them to self-report mitochondrial pH changes during
apoptosis caused by the photogenerated 1O2.
Qi and co-workers have utilized an aldehyde-modified iridium(III)
complex (654) to monitor intracellular pH fluctuations
under external stimuli. The emission
intensity of the complex decreases upon decreasing pH due to protonation
of the aldehyde groups. The complex resides in the mitochondria of
HeLa cells and has been utilized to monitor pH fluctuations upon stimulation
with phorbol myristate acetate and ultrasound.
 Yu, Yu, and co-workers have developed an NIR luminescent
ruthenium(II)
complex (655) ( ) that can detect pH fluctuations in the lysosomes. The emission of the complex is pH-sensitive
due to the deprotonation/protonation of the imidazole rings. This
complex is nonluminescent under neutral and basic conditions; however,
upon decreasing the pH from 7.16 to 1.88, it exhibits substantial
emission enhancement (I/Io = ca. 400) at 680 nm. The complex specifically
localizes in the lysosomes of live U251 cells and has been utilized
to monitor lysosomal pH fluctuations upon treatment with bafilomycin
A1. A related binuclear ruthenium(II) complex (656) also
shows intense NIR emission at ca. 700 nm under acidic
conditions. Remarkably, the complex
selectively labels the acidic lysosomes of cancerous HeLa cells over
normal HEK293 cells even under a coculture environment ( ), indicating that
the complex can differentiate between cancer and normal cells based
on pH and can be used for cancer diagnosis. Leung and co-workers have
utilized an iridium(III) complex that can detect lysosomal pH changes
(657) to establish a cell-based platform for time-resolved
high-throughput screening of lysosomotropic compounds. Screening from 400 FDA-approved drugs has
revealed five compounds that can modulate the lysosomal pH in cancer
cells, with mitoxantrone identified as the most potent lysosomotropic
agent.
 Bacteria have evolved various mechanisms to maintain
their cytoplasmic
pH within a narrow range. , The pH homeostasis
is crucial for their survival and growth under various forms of environmental
stress. One notable example is E. coli, a kind of
enteric bacteria that can thrive and colonize in the acidic environment
of human gastrointestinal tracts through regulating their cytoplasmic
pH. Mallick, Gupta, and co-workers have developed three water-soluble,
pH-responsive heterobimetallic iridium(III)–platinum(II) complexes
(658) ( ) for staining bacterial cells. The square-planar platinum(II) unit is crucial to the aggregation
behavior of the complexes in aqueous solution. The propensity of the
complexes to aggregate increases upon changing the pH from 7.08 to
1.25, resulting in a significant increase in the 3MMLCT
emission intensity at 666 nm (I/Io = ca. 175 to 220). These complexes
have been applied to measure the intracellular pH of E. coli grown in the pH range of 1.2 to 6.8. Complexes 658a and 658b exhibit efficient staining of E. coli at pH = 1.2 and 3, while complex 658c shows staining
at pH < 7. Qi, Qi, and co-workers have developed a carboxyl-modified
iridium(III) complex (659) as a dual photoluminescent
and electrochemiluminescent probe for quantitative pH measurements
under acidic conditions. Upon decreasing
the pH from 7.0 to 2.0, the complex displays a substantial decrease
in its emission quantum yield and lifetime, facilitating the detection
of intracellular pH fluctuations in E. coli under
different external pH conditions.
 Shakirova, Tunik, and co-workers have designed
a polymeric pH probe
(660) ( ) that can overcome the inherent sensitivity of phosphorescent
transition metal complexes toward O2, which can result
in crosstalk between pH and O2 measurements. The pH-sensitive iridium(III) complex is embedded
in a polymer that is permeable to H+ but impermeable to
O2. This is achieved by conjugating the iridium(III) complex
to a water-soluble block copolymer poly(N-vinylpyrrolidone)-block-poly(vinylamine), followed by crosslinking
the residual amino groups on the polymer chains with glutaraldehyde.
This design protects the complex from O2 quenching while
retaining the protonation/deprotonation equilibrium, eliminating the
crosstalk between pH and O2 measurements. The emission
lifetime of the polymeric probe is highly sensitive to pH variations
in the physiological range, allowing the monitoring of changes in
both intra- and extracellular pH in CHO-K1 cells using PLIM.
 Nevertheless, the simultaneous measurement of
pH and O2 levels can provide valuable insights into cellular
processes such
as cellular growth, metabolism, and signaling. Jiang and co-workers
have designed an iridium(III) complex (661a) ( ) for imaging
tumor acidity and hypoxia. The emission
of this complex is activated upon successive exposure to two distinct
stimuli: (1) an acidic environment, which triggers the cleavage of
the imine bond, leading to a significant red shift in the absorption
(from 410 to 520 nm) and emission bands (from 610 to 705 nm); and
(2) hypoxia, which results in significant emission enhancement. The
dual-sensitive emission of the complex has enabled in vivo imaging of primary tumors with a high tumor-to-normal tissue ratio.
Furthermore, the complex can detect metastatic liver lesions as small
as 1 mm in mice with orthotopic breast tumors and monitor cancer cell
metabolism in vivo. A related iridium(III) complex
with emission extended to the NIR region (661b) has been
developed. The specific response of
the complex toward pH and O2 has allowed the sensitive
detection of tumors of different cancer types in vivo. Notably, the extension of the imaging window to the NIR region
significantly improves the tumor-to-normal tissue ratio, enabling
the ultrasensitive visualization of hundreds of tumor cells and small
tumors with a size of 260 μm in vivo as well
as metastatic lesions in the lungs with sizes down to 115 μm.
 Chen, He, Guo, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III) complex
(662) ( ) for synergistic imaging of tumor acidity and hypoxia. The phenol group conjugated to the complex
via an ethynyl linker renders the emission of the complex pH-sensitive,
and its deprotonation under alkaline conditions results in efficient
emission quenching through PET. Upon decreasing the pH from 7.4 to
6.4 and lowering the O2 concentration from 21 to 0%, the
emission intensity of the complex increases by 3.13 fold and the emission
lifetime increases from 85 to 161 ns. The complex has been used to
monitor pH and O2 levels in live MCF-7 cells. Incubation
of the cells under an acidic (pH 6.4) and hypoxic (0% O2) environment results in a substantial increase in the emission intensity
and lifetime of the complex compared to those under normal physiological
conditions (pH 7.4 and 21% O2). The complex has also been
used to visualize the microenvironment of MCF-7 MCTSs using PLIM.
The emission lifetime detected at the center of the spheroids is longer
(ca. 120 ns) than that at the edge (ca. 90 ns), and decreasing the pH of the culture medium from 7.4 to
6.4 extends the emission lifetime of the whole spheroids to ca. 120–140 ns. These findings highlight the potential
of the complex in in vivo imaging of solid tumors
based on its synergistic response toward both tumor acidity and hypoxia.
 
9.3.

## Polarity
Cellular polarity plays
an important role in controlling the physical and chemical properties
of surrounding molecules, regulating protein folding, enzyme activities,
and membrane dynamics. Each organelle has an optimal polarity for
its specific functions. Aberrant changes in polarity can lead to organelle
dysfunction and contribute to pathological conditions such as cancer. 
Yan, Lu, Zhao, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III) complex featuring an o-carborane
moiety (663) ( ) for monitoring mitochondrial polarity. The electron-deficient o-carborane
unit increases the transition dipole moment of the complex, which
renders the emission of the complex highly sensitive to solvent polarity.
Thus, the complex exhibits a considerable red shift in the emission
maximum from 535 to 577 nm and a substantial decrease in the emission
quantum yield and lifetime from 0.80 to 0.21 and from 794.5 to 185.2
ns, respectively, upon changing the solvent from less polar toluene
(0.36 D) to more polar DMSO (3.96 D). The complex has been used to
examine the difference in mitochondrial polarity between cancer and
normal cells. The more intense and longer-lived emission observed
in cancerous HepG2 cells compared to noncancerous human liver HL-7702
cells indicates that cancer cells are characterized by a less polar
intracellular environment. Furthermore, the complex has been applied
to monitor changes in mitochondrial polarity during apoptosis using
PLIM. The emission lifetime of the complex varies with the health
status of cells, allowing the differentiation of live, apoptotic,
and dead cells based on mitochondrial polarity ( ).
 Huang, Zhang, Zhang, and co-workers have developed
an iridium(III)
complex (664) ( ) for real-time monitoring of changes in ER polarity. This complex shows substantial emission enhancement
upon decreasing solvent polarity, with the emission quantum yield
increasing from 0.001 to 0.243 upon changing the solvent from water
to CHCl3. The complex is specifically enriched in the ER
in a range of mammalian cell lines, including both cancerous (A549
and HepG2) and normal (MRC-5 and HL-7702) cells. Real-time monitoring
of changes in the ER polarity in cells indicates that treatment with
tunicamycin induces a rapid increase in ER polarity during ER stress.
Furthermore, the complex has been used to examine variations in polarity
between fresh blood samples from normal and diabetic mice. The results
indicate that the polarity of blood in diabetic mice is lower than
normal mice, serving as a useful indicator for diabetes detection.
 
9.4.

## Viscosity
The viscosity inside cells
plays an important role in the regulation of diffusion-mediated cellular
processes that depend on intracellular hydrodynamics, such as interactions
between macromolecules, transport of nutrients and metabolites, and
intra- and intercellular transmission of signals. Abnormalities in intracellular viscosity have been linked
to various disorders and diseases, such as diabetes, cardiovascular
diseases, neurodegenerative diseases, and cancer. 
Liu, Chen, Sun, and co-workers have designed a binuclear
iridium(III) complex (665) ( ) for ratiometric detection of viscosity. This complex features a long and flexible
linker to connect the two iridium(III) moieties with different emission
colors. Upon increasing the viscosity of the solution from 1.1 to
950 cP, the intensities of the emission bands at 521 and 711 nm increase
by 16.9 and 2.5 fold, respectively, due to the restriction of molecular
rotation that minimizes nonradiative decay. The complex has been used
for ratiometric monitoring of intracellular viscosity, which reveals
higher viscosity in cancerous HepG2 cells compared to normal HL-7702
cells ( ).
Similar observations have also been found between other cancerous
(MCF-7 and A549) and noncancerous (HEK293T) cell lines, indicating
that the complex can distinguish between cancer and normal cells based
on intracellular viscosity. Additionally, the complex has been used
for real-time monitoring of changes in viscosity in cells in response
to the etoposide treatment, revealing an increase in intracellular
viscosity during apoptosis.
 Tan, Mao, and co-workers have developed six iridium(III)
diphosphine
complexes (666) ( ) as viscosity probes for monitoring mitochondrial
viscoisty. Among these complexes, complex 666f displays the largest increase in emission intensity (I/Io = 6.4) and lifetime (from
0.54 to 2.17 μs) upon increasing the viscosity of a methanolic
glycerol solution from 0.55 to 259 cP, resulting from the inhibited
free rotation of the phenyl rings on the diphosphine ligand and the
aldehyde group on the cyclometalating ligands in viscous media. Upon
cellular internalization, the complex specifically localizes in the
mitochondria, inducing apoptosis through causing mitochondrial dysfunction.
On the basis of its viscosity-sensitive emission lifetime and large
TPA cross-section (δ750nm = 444
GM), complex 666f has been utilized for real-time monitoring
and quantitative measurements of changes in mitochondrial viscosity
using TP-PLIM, which shows that dysfunctional mitochondria experience
a significant but heterogeneous increase in viscosity ( ). Tian and co-workers have
developed an iridium(III) complex bearing a long and flexible alkyl
chain (667) that can monitor apoptosis by tracking mitochondrial
viscosity. Additionally, the complex
has been used to examine the differences in the microenvironment of
tumor and normal tissues in a mouse model, revealing a higher viscosity
level in the tumor microenvironment. Zhang, Zhang, and co-workers
have developed two mitochondria-targeting viscosity-responsive iridium(III)
complexes (668) for PDT applications. Complex 668b exhibits a significant increase
in emission intensity (I/Io = 1,311.6)
and lifetime (from 0.27 to 4.47 μs) upon increasing the glycerol
content in an aqueous solution from 0 to 99%. Both complexes show
higher photocytotoxic activity toward cancer cells (A549 and HepG2)
compared to normal cells (MRC-5 and LO2) due to more efficient 1O2 photosensitization in viscous media. Tian and
co-workers have developed a viscosity-responsive iridium(III) complex
(669) for lifetime image-guided PDT. The complex possesses large two-photon (δ725nm = 369 GM) and three-photon absorption cross-sections
(σ1200nm = 9 × 10–81 cm6 s2 photon–2) in the
NIR region, offering opportunities to monitor viscosity changes in
complex cellular environment using multiphoton microscopy. The complex
has been used to monitor changes in mitochondrial viscosity during
apoptosis via lifetime imaging, which enables the real-time self-assessment
of PDT outcomes. The high 1O2 generation efficiency
of the complex has enabled effective inhibition of tumor growth in vivo upon exposure to NIR-light irradiation (730 nm).
A viscosity-sensitive iridium(III) complex featuring uncoordinated,
rotatable pyridine rings (670) has also been developed
for lifetime image-guided PDT. Remarkably,
the complex can differentiate cancer stem cells from the surrounding
cancer cells in the same tumor tissue due to its longer emission lifetime
in cancer stem cells. This feature highlights its potential in imaging-guided
PDT for eradicating cancer stem cells.
 Zhang, Chao, Zhang, and co-workers have developed
a binuclear iridium(III)
complex (671) ( ) that can measure lysosomal viscosity in live cells. The emission intensity and lifetime of the
complex increase with the viscosity of the medium due to the restriction
of free rotation of the aliphatic linkers between the two iridium(III)
units. As revealed by PLIM, the complex displays a longer emission
lifetime in cancerous HepG2 and A549 cells compared to normal HL-7702
cells ( a– c, g), indicative of a
more viscous intracellular environment in cancer cells. The complex
has also been used to monitor changes in lysosomal viscosity under
different stimulations. The lysosomal viscosity in HepG2 cells increases
upon treatment with dexamethasone that stabilizes the lysosomal membrane
and inhibits the release of lysosomal enzymes, but remains relatively
unaffected by chloroquine that increases lysosomal pH ( d– f, h). Additionally, the complex
has been employed to detect variations in viscosity between fresh
blood samples collected from normal mice and those with diabetes,
revealing that the blood of diabetic mice has a higher viscosity level.
Tao and co-workers have reported an iridium(III) complex coordinated
with a bithiazole ligand (672) that can detect viscosity. The high lysosome specificity of the complex
suggests its potential for monitoring changes in lysosomal viscosity.
 Li, Yu, and co-workers have designed a purine-based
iridium(III)
complex (673) ( ) that allows for selective determination of ER viscosity. The complex exhibits an increase in the emission
intensity (I/Io = 22.6)
and lifetime (from 380 to 1,120 ns) at 550 nm upon increasing the
viscosity of the medium from 1.01 to 76.78 cP. Monitoring the changes
in ER viscosity in A549 cells via lifetime imaging shows that the
ER viscosity increases under ER stress induced by tunicamycin due
to the generation of unfolded proteins. Tan, Mao, and co-workers have
developed a neutral iridium(III) complex featuring two rotatable aldehyde
groups (674) for quantitative tracking of ER viscosity. Real-time tracking of changes in ER viscosity
in MCF-7 cells using TP-PLIM reveals that the viscosity increases
during erastin-induced ferroptosis, which is attributed to the accumulation
of lipid peroxides in the ER. A rhenium(I) complex coordinated with
a BODIPY-based molecular rotor (675) has been reported
as both a photoinducer and tracker of ER-to-lysosome-associated degradation
(ERLAD), a pathway that targets misfolded proteins from the ER to
the lysosomes for degradation. This
complex can induce ERLAD upon continuous irradiation and monitor morphological
changes in the ER during ERLAD. Real-time monitoring of changes in
the viscosity of ER buds during ERLAD with TP-PLIM shows that ER viscosity
initially increases due to the gradual accumulation of unfolded proteins
in the ER buds and then decreases, which is not observed when the
cells are treated with 3-methyladenine to inhibit autophagy by blocking
autophagosome formation, indicating that ERLAD is responsible for
the removal of misfolded proteins to alleviate ER stress.
 Xu and co-workers have developed a viscosity-sensitive
ruthenium(II)
complex (676) ( ) for monitoring viscosity changes in LDs. The complex shows substantial emission enchantment
with increasing viscosity due to the inhibited rotation of the C=C
bonds. The high specificity of the complex toward LDs has enabled
real-time monitoring of viscosity changes in these organelles in live
cells and zebrafish, which shows that the LDs viscosity increases
under ER stress.
 The development of viscosity probes has allowed
for the examination
of LLPS in cells, a process that is responsible for the formation
of membraneless organelles, paving the way to understand its roles
in human health and disease. Qiao,
Li, Zhang, and co-workers have developed a viscosity-sensitive iridium(III)
complex (677) ( ) to monitor LLPS in live cells. Upon increasing the viscosity of the solution from 1.45
to 165.47 cP, the emission lifetime of the complex substantially increases
from 163.39 to 1,023.90 ns. The drastic changes in the emission lifetime
have enabled the monitoring of fused in sarcoma (FUS) phase separation
in live NIH/3T3 cells using PLIM. In cells that are transfected to
express modified FUS protein that undergoes light-activated phase
transition, the emission lifetime of the complex progressively increases
with the time of irradiation, indicating an increase in size and viscosity
of the photogenerated protein droplets, which ultimately leads to
the formation of solidified aggregates. Tan, Zhang, Li, Mao, and co-workers
have developed a ruthenium(II) dppz complex modified with two TPP+ units (678) that can induce and monitor DNA
phase separation. The cationic and
lipophilic TPP+ moieties increase the interactions of the
complex with DNA, leading to DNA aggregation. The emission lifetime
of the complex increases upon DNA condensation ( ), allowing the real-time
visualization of DNA phase separation in the nucleus of live A549
cells using TP-PLIM. Additionally, the complex can alter the gene
expression profile and the chromatin state of the cells, resulting
in potent anticancer activity in vitro and in vivo.
 
9.5.

## Temperature
Temperature is an important
physical parameter that plays a crucial role in regulating biochemical
reactions within live cells to maintain intracellular homeostasis
and normal cellular functions. The temperature distribution within
a cell provides valuable insights into the thermodynamics and functions
of cellular components. Intracellular temperature can also serve as
an indicator of pathological conditions, which often lead to higher
temperature due to increased metabolic activities. Thus, intracellular
temperature mapping is important to understanding cellular events
and diagnosing diseases. However, this is a challenging task since
it necessitates a thermoresponsive probe with both high temperature
resolution and spatial resolution.
Poly(N-isopropylacrylamide)
(PNIPAM) is a temperature-sensitive polymer that undergoes reversible
phase transition in aqueous media in response to temperature. Sun,
Huang, and co-workers have incorporated an AIE-active platinum(II)
complex into the PNIPAM backbone to afford a luminescent polymeric
thermometer (679) ( ) for intracellular temperature sensing. Upon increasing the temperature, the polymer
changes from an extended structure to an aggregated state. This conformational
rearrangement increases the hydrophobicity and rigidity of the local
environment of the complex, resulting in substantial emission enhancement.
The probe has been used to visualize the intracellular temperature
distribution in HepG2 cells, revealing variations in temperature in
different regions of the cell. Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have introduced
two iridium(III) complexes with different emission colors to the PNIPAM
backbone to construct a dual-emissive thermoresponsive probe (680) for temperature mapping. Upon increasing the temperature from 10 to 40°C, the high-energy
emission (λem = 470 nm) experiences
a more significant increase in its intensity and lifetime compared
to the low-energy emission (λem =
590 nm), resulting in a ca. 18.2-fold increase in
the ratio I470nm/I590nm. The ratiometric response enables temperature sensing
with high temperature resolution of ca. 0.4–0.6°C
over a wide temperature range. The probe has been used for temperature
sensing in live cells and zebrafish ( a,b). Additionally, the long emission lifetime
of the probe has enabled more accurate temperature detection in vivo while minimizing interference from autofluorescence
via time-resolved luminescence imaging ( c,d). A related dual-emissive probe (681) featuring an iridium(III) complex as the response unit
and a europium(III) complex as the reference unit has been developed
for in vitro and in vivo temperature
sensing. The presence of the iridium(III)
complex is critical to the efficient sensitization of the europium(III)
complex under visible-light excitation, allowing temperature sensing
with ratiometric response. Additionally, the long emission lifetime
of the europium(III) complex in the millisecond time regime makes
the probe more favorable for lifetime-based temperature sensing.
 

10.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Bioorthogonal
Probes
Bioorthogonal chemistry, pioneered by Carolyn R. Bertozzi
in the
early 2000s, encompasses a class of high-yielding chemical reactions
that can occur rapidly and selectively in biological settings without
disrupting natural biological processes. This strategy has been honored with the Nobel Prize in Chemistry
in 2022 as a recognition of its significant impact and potential in
various fields. One of the key applications of these chemoselective
reactions is the labeling of specific biomolecules in living systems,
paving the way for studying the localization, dynamics, and functions
of the biomolecules in their native environments. Bioorthogonal chemistry has also been successfully applied
in activity-based protein profiling for drug discovery and the development of in vivo pretargeting strategies for imaging and therapy. These achievements have stimulated considerable interest
in the development of photofunctional transition metal complexes as
bioorthogonal reagents for bioimaging and therapeutic applications. 
10.1.

## Copper(I)-Catalyzed and Strain-Promoted Azide–Alkyne
Cycloaddition
Azides and alkynes are useful bioorthogonal
handles due to their small size, bioinertness, and nonnative nature,
which make them ideal for incorporation into biomolecules without
interfering with the normal functions of the biomolecules. However,
the traditional Huisgen 1,3-dipolar cycloaddition reaction between
azides and alkynes is often too slow for practical use in biological
systems. To overcome this limitation, two strategies have been developed
to accelerate the reaction kinetics, which involve the introduction
of a copper(I) catalyst (CuAAC) , or the incorporation
of ring strain into the alkynes (strain-promoted azide–alkyne
cycloaddition; SPAAC). These modifications
enable efficient and selective formation of triazoles under physiological
conditions, providing versatile approaches for a wide range of applications
such as bioconjugation, imaging, and drug delivery. , 
Lo and co-workers have developed the first phosphorescent
bioorthogonal probes based on iridium(III) complexes appended with
a dibenzocyclooctyne (DIBO) moiety (682) ( ). These complexes have been used to label CHO
cells that are pretreated with 1,3,4,6-tetra-O-acetyl-N-azidoacetyl-d-mannosamine (Ac4ManNAz),
a metabolic substrate that introduces azide units into the terminal
sialic acid residues of membrane-bound glycans. While complexes 682a and 682b are efficiently taken up by the
cells and enriched in the mitochondria, incubation of the cells with
complex 682c leads to intense emission on the cell surface,
indicating successful labeling of the azide-modified sialoglycans.
This highlights the significance of the ionized carboxylate groups
in suppressing the internalization of the complex and facilitating
its SPAAC reaction with the membrane-bound azide moieties. Related
rhenium(I) (683) and ruthenium(II)
DIBO complexes (684) have
also been developed for imaging cell-surface sialoglycans. Monitoring
the intracellular trafficking of the labeled glycans has revealed
the involvement of endosomes, the Golgi apparatus, and lysosomes in
their internalization and subsequent recycling processes. 
 Zhang, Zhao, Huang, and co-workers have introduced
a novel “labeling
after recognition” (LaR) approach to visualize caspase-3 activity
in cells. This method involves three
major components: an iridium(III) DIBO complex (685),
a rhodamine–tetrazine conjugate (Rh–Tz), and a caspase-3-cleavable
peptide (Az-DEVDGK-Nor) ( ). The recognition and cleavage of the peptide substrate
by caspase-3 produces two fragments, Az-DEVD and GK-Nor, which are
then labeled with complex 685 and Rh–Tz, respectively,
via two independent bioorthogonal reactions. As a result, the enzymatic
reaction separates the two luminophores and inhibits FRET between
them, resulting in an increase in the emission lifetime of the iridium(III)
complex. Compared to the traditional “labeling before recognition”
(LbR) approach where the two luminophores are attached to the peptide
substrate before enzyme recognition, the LaR approach generates faster
and more significant emission lifetime response due to the smaller
size of Az-DEVDGK-Nor, which effectively reduces steric hindrance
and/or nonspecific interaction and facilitates its recognition by
caspase-3. The LaR sensing approach has enabled the visualization
of caspase-3 activity in apoptotic HeLa cells with high spatial resolution
using PLIM ( ), offering new opportunities to examine the roles of caspase-3 in
cellular processes related to apoptosis.
 Qiao, Zhang, and co-workers have utilized an alkyne-bearing
iridium(III)
complex (686) ( ) for imaging newly synthesized proteins in cells. The emission lifetime of the complex decreases
from 530 to 116 ns upon the CuAAC reaction with l-azidohomoalanine
(AHA). Since AHA serves as a surrogate for methionine and can be incorporated
into proteins during protein synthesis, the substantial difference
in the emission lifetime before and after the bioorthogonal reaction
has allowed the use of PLIM for wash-free and real-time tracking of
newly synthesized proteins in live HeLa cells ( ), providing valuable insights into the
dynamics of protein synthesis in a cellular context.
 Ball and co-workers have developed three iridium(III)
azide complexes
(687) ( ) as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal probes for alkyne-modified
biomolecules. The emission of these
complexes in solution is very weak (Φem = 0.0013 to 0.0018) due to effective quenching by the azide unit.
However, upon the CuAAC or SPAAC reaction with alkynes, the complexes
display a substantial increase in their emission intensities, resulting
from the conversion of the quenching azide moiety to a nonquenching
triazole derivative. The complexes have been used to image U2OS cells
that are modified with the strained alkyne (1R,8S,9s)-bicyclo[6.1.0]non-4-yne (BCN). Their
phosphorogenic properties have enabled the clear visualization of
the labeled cells while minimizing background interference from the
unreacted probes.
 
10.2.

## Strain-Promoted Alkyne–Nitrone Cycloaddition
Nitrones are a class of 1,3-dipoles that can undergo strain-promoted
alkyne–nitrone cycloaddition (SPANC) reaction with cyclooctynes
under physiological conditions, resulting in the formation of isoxazolines. In contrast to azides, nitrones offer multiple
sites for modification, providing greater control over their electronic
properties and reaction kinetics and allowing for the incorporation
of various functional handles.
Lo and co-workers have leveraged
nitrones, for the first time, as both a bioorthogonal handle and an
emission quencher to develop phosphorogenic probes (688) ( ) for
bioorthogonal labeling and imaging. , The incorporation
of the nitrone moiety into iridium(III) complexes leads to significant
emission quenching (Φem ≤
0.023), primarily due to the photoinduced isomerization of the nitrone
unit that provides an efficient nonradiative decay pathway for the
complexes. However, upon reaction of the complexes with BCN, the nitrone
moiety is transformed into a nonquenching isoxazoline derivative,
resulting in a substantial increase in their emission intensities
(I/Io = up to 135). Additionally,
the direct coordination of the nitrone ligand (k2 = 4.0 × 10–2 M–1 s–1) to the cationic iridium(III) center greatly
enhances its reactivity toward BCN (k2 = 11.6 to 27.0 × 10–2 M–1 s–1). The high phosphorogenicity and bioorthogonal
reactivity of the nitrone complexes have enabled selective imaging
of BCN-modified decane (BCN-C10) and
SNAP-tag proteins using BCN-modified O6-benzylguanine (BCN-BG) as a substrate in live CHO-K1 cells. Notably, the nitrone unit also suppresses
the 1O2 photosensitization efficiencies of the
complexes. Thus, upon the SPANC reaction,
the 1O2 generation quantum yields of complexes 688d–688g increase substantially from
0.19–0.26 to 0.42–0.91. Through the combination of protein
tag technology and bioorthogonal chemistry, the iridium(III) complexes
selectively accumulate in cells that are transfected to express the
SNAP-tag protein and labeled with BCN-BG, enabling targeted photoinduced
cell death upon irradiation.
 Related ruthenium(II) nitrone complexes (689) ( ) have also been
developed as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal reagents for cellular labeling. Notably, the reactivity of the N-phenyl nitrone complex 689e toward BCN (k2 = 3.06 M–1 s–1)
is significantly higher compared to its N-methyl
nitrone counterparts 689a–689d (k2 = 7.8 to 8.6 × 10–2 M–1 s–1) due to the presence
of an electron-deficient phenyl ring at the N position of the nitrone
moiety. These complexes have been utilized to image live HeLa cells
that are pretreated with BCN-C10. Interestingly, the different lipophilicity
and bioorthogonal reactivity of the complexes have led to regioselective
cellular labeling ( ). In particular, complexes 689a and 689c with low lipophilicity (log Po/w = −1.84
and −1.49, respectively) exhibit limited cellular uptake and
punctate staining on the plasma membrane, whereas the most lipophilic
complex 689d (log Po/w =
1.40) is efficiently internalized into the cells and diffusely distributed
in the cytoplasm. However, complexes 689b and 689e are exclusively enriched in the cell surface despite their different
lipophilicity (log Po/w = 1.32 and −1.16,
respectively). The membrane accumulation of complex 689b is ascribed to the presence of n-butyl chains,
which assist in hydrophobic interaction with the phospholipid bilayers
of the plasma membrane and hence facilitate reaction with the membrane-bound
BCN-C10; while that of complex 689e is due to the high
bioorthogonal reactivity of the complex, which allows efficient labeling
of the cell surface without significant internalization. Notably,
complex 689d shows higher cytotoxicity toward BCN-C10-pretreated
cells compared to other complexes in the series, attributed to the
generation of a more lipophilic isoxazoline adduct that induces extensive
cell death through enhanced nonspecific interactions with intracellular
components. These findings illustrate the significance of the cellular
localization of the complexes in determining their cytotoxic activity.
 
10.3.

## Strain-Promoted Sydnone–Alkyne Cycloaddition
Sydnones are stable mesoionic 5-membered heterocyclic compounds
that can undergo strain-promoted sydnone–alkyne cycloaddition
(SPSAC) reaction with cyclooctynes under physiological conditions,
resulting in the formation of pyrazoles. The use of sydnones as a bioorthogonal handle offers advantages
over azides. One distinctive feature is their tunable reactivity,
which can be easily modulated by modifying the substituents at the N-3 and C-4 positions of the mesoionic
ring without compromising their stability in biological media.
Lo and co-workers have developed iridium(III) complexes modified
with a sydnone moiety (690) ( ) as a new class of phosphorescent bioorthogonal
probes. These complexes display increased
emission intensities and lifetimes (I/Io = up to 7.9; τ = 0.10 to 0.25
μs) with a blue shift in their emission maxima upon the SPSAC
reaction with BCN in aqueous solution, as the conversion of the dipolar
mesoionic sydnone ring to a less polar pyrazole derivative increases
the hydrophobicity of the local environment. Complex 690b, which exhibits the largest emission changes, has been utilized
to visualize BCN-modified ceramide (BCN-Cer) in live HeLa cells. The
intracellular SPSAC reaction produces a luminescent pyrazole adduct
that specifically localizes in the mitochondria. This bioorthogonal
reaction also enhances the photocytotoxicity of the complex by increasing
its efficiency in generating 1O2. Rhenium(I)
complexes coordinated with a 4-(4-pyridyl)sydnone ligand (691) have also been developed as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal probes. The increased π-conjugation between
the sydnone unit and the pyridine ligand leads to significant emission
quenching (Φem ≤ 0.0017),
allowing the complexes to show more substantial emission enhancement
(I/Io = 8.8 to 17.3)
upon the SPSAC reaction with BCN. Complex 691a has been
used to detect BCN-modified morpholine (BCN-Mor) in live HeLa cells,
which enables the selective staining of lysosomes with phosphorogenic
response.
 
10.4.

## Inverse Electron-Demand Diels–Alder
Reaction
The inverse electron-demand Diels–Alder (IEDDA)
reaction between 1,2,4,5-tetrazine and strained alkenes or alkynes
has emerged as a highly promising bioorthogonal reaction due to its
rapid kinetics, high selectivity, and excellent biocompatibility. Additionally, tetrazines possess the unique
ability to quench the emission of attached luminophores, allowing
for the development of luminogenic bioorthogonal probes for labeling
and imaging applications both in cells and in vivo.
Lo and co-workers have modified rhenium(I) complexes with
a tetrazine moiety to afford phosphorogenic bioorthogonal probes (692) ( ) for dienophile-labeled biomolecules. The close proximity and large spectral overlap between the rhenium(I)
center (λem = 513 to 556 nm) and
the tetrazine unit (λabs = ca. 520 nm) enable efficient quenching of the rhenium(I)
emission through FRET, resulting in the low emission quantum yields
of the complexes (Φem ≤ 0.049).
However, the complexes display a remarkable increase in their emission
intensities upon the IEDDA reaction with dienophiles such as 5-norbornen-2-ol
(NBO; I/Io = 1.1 to 8.0)
and BCN (I/Io = 8.0 to
181.1). The smaller emission enhancement observed for NBO is due to
the formation of a dihydropyridazine moiety that effectively quenches
the emission of the complexes through PET. Notably, the direct coordination
of the tetrazine ligand to the rhenium(I) complexes not only perturbs
their emission properties but also enhances their bioorthogonal reactivity.
The binuclear complexes 692e and 692f exhibit
significantly higher reactivity toward BCN (k2 = 407.2 and 537.0 M–1 s–1, respectively) compared to the mononuclear complexes 692a–692d (k2 = 52.4
to 69.7 M–1 s–1) and the free
tetrazine ligands (k2 = 20.1 to 50.3 M–1 s–1) due to the cationic rhenium(I)
center. Complex 692f has been utilized to track the intracellular
distribution of BCN-modified BSA in live HeLa cells, demonstrating
the potential applications of these complexes in examining the biological
functions and spatiotemporal dynamics of biomolecules in living cells
and organisms.
 Related iridium(III) tetrazine complexes (693) ( ) have also been
developed as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal probes for biological labeling
and imaging. These complexes, including
complexes 693c and 693d that emit in the
orange-red to red region, show very weak emission in solution (Φem ≤ 0.010), primarily due to PET
acting as an additional emission quenching pathway alongside FRET.
However, upon reaction with BCN, the complexes display substantial
emission enhancement (I/Io = 19.5 to 121.9). Complex 693c has been used to visualize
azide-modified sialoglycans on the surface of live CHO-K1 cells using
a homobifunctional crosslinker bearing two BCN moieties (bis-BCN). Tetrazine has also been incorporated into iridium(III) complexes
through direct conjugation to the diimine ligand. The weak emission of the resultant tetrazine complexes
(694) in solution (Φem ≤ 0.0202) is due to a dual quenching mechanism involving
FRET and PET. Additionally, the reactivity of the complexes toward
BCN (k2 = 20.9 to 83.1 M–1 s–1) and trans-cyclooct-4-en-1-ol
(TCO; k2 = 634.6 to 1,471.3 M–1 s–1) is remarkably higher compared to the free
tetrazine ligand (k2 = 29.1 and 188.0
M–1 s–1, respectively) due to
the cationic iridium(III) center. Although the reaction of the tetrazine
complexes with TCO is fast, the resulting dihydropyridazine products
exhibit lower emission intensities, shorter emission lifetimes, and
lower 1O2 generation efficiencies than the pyridazine
products formed in the reaction with BCN due to PET quenching, indicating
that the emission and 1O2-photosensitization
properties of the tetrazine complexes can be controlled by the use
of different bioorthogonal reaction partners. Complex 694b has been used for phosphorogenic labeling of lysosomes and the ER
in live HeLa cells using BCN-Mor and BCN-modified phospholipid (BCN-DPPE),
respectively. It has also been utilized for selective imaging and
photoactivated killing of HeLa cells transfected with HaloTag proteins
using a BCN-modified chloroalkane substrate (BCN-C6-Cl).
 The phosphorogenic tetrazine complexes mentioned
above are designed
based on a bichromophoric approach, where the luminophore is connected
to the tetrazine unit through either an aliphatic or π-conjugated
linker. However, the efficiency of emission quenching in these systems
is significantly influenced by factors such as the donor–acceptor
separation and spectral overlap integrals in FRET, and the thermodynamics
of electron transfer in PET. To overcome these limitations, Lo and
co-workers have developed monochromophoric iridium(III) complexes
with a built-in tetrazine motif (695) ( ) as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal
probes. These complexes are very weakly
emissive in solution (Φem ≤
0.0084) due to the electron-deficient tetrazine ligand, but exhibit
substantial enhancement in their emission intensities (I/Io = 23.1 to 96.1) upon the IEDDA reaction
with BCN. Notably, the metal coordination significantly enhances the
reactivity of the tetrazine moiety (k2 = 23.0 to 780.4 M–1 s–1) compared
to the uncoordinated tetrazine ligand (k2 = 12.9 M–1 s–1). Complex 695c has been used for labeling and visualizing BCN-modified
HaloTag proteins in transfected HeLa cells. Lo and co-workers have
also designed a two-step targeting approach that allows for selective
accumulation and controllable activation of related ruthenium(II)
tetrazine complexes (696) in cancer cells. This strategy involves the use of a BCN-modified
self-assembling peptide Nap-FFK(BCN)Yp, which forms large supramolecular
assemblies upon dephosphorylation by alkaline phosphatase (ALP). The
enzyme-instructed self-assembly of the peptide enables the accumulation
of BCN moieties in ALP-overexpressing cancer cells for the IEDDA reaction
with the tetrazine complexes, switching on their emission and 1O2-photosensitization properties for cancer-selective
imaging and therapeutic applications. Interestingly, the cellular
localization of the ruthenium(II) complexes can be controlled by varying
the peptide concentration, which dictates the site of formation of
the supramolecular assemblies in cells from the plasma membrane (at
[Nap-FFK(BCN)Yp] = 200 μM) to the mitochondria (at [Nap-FFK(BCN)Yp]
= 100 μM) ( ), offering opportunities to track different extracellular
and intracellular events. Notably, the photocytotoxicity of the ruthenium(II)-containing
supramolecular assemblies is higher in intracellular regions as the
photogenerated 1O2 causes direct oxidative damage
to proximal organelles instead of diffusing across the plasma membrane
or extracellular space, indicating the importance of cellular localization
in determining their photocytotoxic activity. Iridium(III) complexes
featuring two tetrazine units (697) have also been developed
as phosphorogenic bioorthogonal probes for bis-cyclooctynylated
substrates. These complexes show extremely
weak emission (Φem = 0.0001 to 0.0009)
and 1O2 generation (ΦΔ = 0.02 to 0.07). However, upon reaction with bis-BCN, these complexes are converted to their respective bis-pyridazine counterparts, resulting in a significant
enhancement in their emission intensities (I/Io = 1,008.2 to 3,885.7) and 1O2 photosensitization efficiencies (ΦΔ = 0.62 to 0.77). These changes have enabled selective
imaging and photocytotoxicity toward cells pretreated with bis-BCN. Additionally, one of the complexes has been utilized
for constructing luminescent nanogels for cargo delivery applications.
 Bioorthogonal cleavage reactions have emerged as
a new subfield
of bioorthogonal chemistry that goes beyond typical ligation reactions. , These dissociative reactions have allowed the in situ manipulation of protein activities and controlled activation of
small-molecule prodrugs in live cells and animals. One example is
the IEDDA reaction between a methylene carbamate-substituted tetrazine
and a TCO, which induces carbamate cleavage and liberates a secondary
amine. Lo and co-workers have utilized
this “click-to-release” strategy to develop rhenium(I)
tetrazine complexes (698) ( ) as bioorthogonally activatable photosensitizers. These complexes display weak emission (Φem = 0.029 to 0.039) and 1O2 photosensitization (ΦΔ = 0.22 to 0.48) due to the quenching tetrazine moiety. Incubation
of the complexes with TCO induces the bioorthogonal cleavage reaction,
increasing both the emission intensities and 1O2 generation efficiencies of the complexes. Notably, the PEG complex 698d exhibits relatively low dark cytotoxicity (IC50 = 51.1 to 73.7 μM) but substantially enhanced cytotoxic activity
toward TCO-pretreated cells upon irradiation (IC50 = 0.6
to 4.6 μM), resulting from the synergistic release of the more
cytotoxic rhenium(I) aminomethylpyridine complex and increased 1O2 generation. The treatment efficiently induces
lysosomal dysfunction, autophagy suppression, and immunogenic cell
death. This is the first example of using bioorthogonal cleavage reactions
to realize photoinduced immunogenic cell death, opening up new avenues
for the development of innovative therapeutic agents for photoimmunotherapy.
 

11.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Probes
for Bacterial Cells
Bacterial infections have long presented
a significant global public
health challenge, being the second leading cause of death worldwide.
Although antibiotics have been effective in treating these infections,
the excessive and widespread use of antibiotics has led to the emergence
of antibiotic-resistant bacteria. This has made the treatment of bacterial
infections, especially multidrug-resistant strains, increasingly challenging.
Thus, the selective detection of pathogenic bacteria is of high clinical
significance for accurate diagnosis and treatment of bacterial infections. 
11.1.

## Bacterial Envelope
Bacteria can
be categorized into two groups: Gram-negative bacteria, characterized
by a thin layer of peptidoglycans and an outer membrane containing
LPS, and Gram-positive bacteria, distinguished by a thick layer of
peptidoglycans decorated with teichoic acids and lipoteichoic acid
(LTA) but lacking an outer membrane. The cell membrane of bacteria is also rich in anionic phospholipids,
which are primarily located in the inner leaflet of the plasma membrane
of mammalian cells. The abundance of anionic components in the bacterial
cell envelope sets it apart from mammalian cell membranes, which are
predominantly zwitterionic. This distinction in membrane composition
offers a straightforward and efficient method for detecting and differentiating
bacteria.
Ding, Yu, and co-workers have utilized a cationic
platinum(II) complex (699) ( ) for LPS detection and rapid discrimination
of pathogenic bacteria. The binding
of the complex to LPS enhances intermolecular Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions and switches on the 3MMLCT emission in the deep-red region (λem = 650 nm). The specific recognition of LPS has allowed for
