the rapid and wash-free differentiation between Gram-negative E. coli and Gram-positive S. aureus and Bacillus subtilis (B. subtilis). Cationic
AIE-active iridium(III) complexes appended with a quaternary ammonium
group (700) have also been used for the rapid detection
of LPS and wash-free imaging of bacteria. 
 Xu, Wang, and co-workers have designed three
AIE-active iridium(III)
complexes (701) ( ) that enable the selective discrimination,
imaging, and elimination of Gram-positive bacteria. These complexes form large luminescent aggregates upon
binding to LTA, giving rise to strong emission in Gram-positive bacteria
such as B. subtilis, S. aureus,
and methicillin-resistant S. aureus (MRSA) ( a,b). The specific emission turn-on has allowed for the selective
discrimination of Gram-positive S. aureus from Gram-negative E. coli and the fungus Candida albicans (C. albicans) ( c). Furthermore, complex 701a displays potent antibacterial activity toward Gram-positive bacteria
with minimal inhibitory concentrations ranging from 0.9 to 1.9 μg
mL–1. The activity is substantially enhanced upon
irradiation due to the generation of 1O2. Notably,
the complex is highly biocompatible with mammalian cells with minimal
cytotoxic and hemolytic activity under both dark and light conditions.
These remarkable properties have led to its successful application
in treating S. aureus-infected wounds on mice, resulting
in almost complete wound healing and scar disappearance after the
treatment. A related ruthenium(II) complex (702) also
exhibits specific interaction with LTA, enabling the simultaneous
detection and photodynamic inactivation of Gram-positive bacteria in vitro and in vivo. 
 Sasmal and co-workers have developed three cationic
AIE-active
iridium(III) complexes (703) ( ) for simultaneous detection and elimination
of drug-resistant bacteria. In the
presence of bacteria, these complexes bind to LTA or LPS through electrostatic
and hydrophobic interactions, resulting in blue-shifted and enhanced
emission. The complexes also show potent antibacterial activity toward
antibiotic-resistant bacteria such as carbapenem-resistant Acinetobacter baumannii and MRSA. Other AIE-active iridium(III)
complexes (704 and 705) have also been reported
for simultaneous imaging and inactivation of bacteria. , Strassert, Vida, Perez-Inestrosa, and co-workers have developed
a dendritic platinum(II) complex (706) for labeling bacteria. The terminal amino groups of the dendritic
skeleton are critical to the binding of the complex to the anionic
bacterial cell surface via hydrogen bonding and electrostatic interactions.
 Wang, Zhou, and co-workers have designed a ruthenium(II)
complex
bearing two alkyne moieties (707) ( ) for targeted imaging and
PDT of Gram-positive bacteria. This
complex can specifically recognize aminophospholipids such as phosphatidylethanolamine
and lysyl-phosphatidylglycerol that are abundant on the outer membrane
of Gram-positive bacteria via the amine–yne click reaction.
The specific targeting of aminophospholipids has enabled the efficient
labeling and photodynamic inactivation of Gram-positive bacteria such
as S. aureus and MRSA while leaving mammalian cells
unaffected.
 
11.2.

## Bacterial Enzymes
Bacterial enzymes
play an important role in the development of antibiotic resistance. For example, β-lactamases are often
expressed at high levels in antibiotic-resistant bacteria. These enzymes
can cleave the β-lactam ring present in antibiotics such as
penicillin and cephalosporin, rendering them ineffective against the
bacteria. Thus, the development of luminescent probes that can specifically
target bacterial enzymes will provide a valuable tool for the detection
of pathogenic bacteria.
Xing and Shao have designed a β-lactamase-responsive
ruthenium(II) complex (708) ( ) for selective imaging and photodynamic
inactivation of antibiotic-resistant bacteria. This complex incorporates cephalosporin as a linker to
connect the ruthenium(II) unit and the emission quencher BHQ3. Hydrolytic
cleavage of the cephalosporin linker by β-lactamases separates
the two moieties and disrupts FRET, resulting in an increase in both
the emission intensity and 1O2 photosensitization
efficiency of the complex. The high specificity of the complex toward
β-lactamase has allowed the selective discrimination of drug-resistant
bacteria such as penicillin G-resistant B. cereus and two clinical isolates of MRSA (BAA39 and BAA44) over S. aureus ( ), and enabled effective photodynamic inactivation of these
bacteria due to their higher expression levels of β-lactamase.
 
11.3.

## Bacterial Biofilms
Bacterial biofilms
are communities of bacteria that are attached to a surface and embedded
in a self-generated matrix composed of extracellular polymeric substances
including proteins, polysaccharides, and nucleic acids. This matrix protects the bacterial cells against
adverse conditions such as pH changes, oxidative stress, and antibiotic
treatment. Since bacteria in biofilms are more resistant to antibiotics
than their planktonic counterparts, the rapid and selective detection
of biofilms is crucial for effective treatment of biofilm-associated
infections.
Strassert, Faust, and co-workers have developed
a series of platinum(II) complexes (709) ( ) as luminescent
probes to examine the microenvironment of bacterial biofilms. These complexes possess various structural
features that facilitate their interaction with bacteria. In particular,
complex 709a is modified with two adamantane units to
enhance its affinity for bacteria through nonspecific hydrophobic
interaction, while complexes 709b–709d are functionalized with a maltohexaose moiety to promote their specific
recognition by maltodextrin transporters on the bacterial membrane.
In S. aureus biofilms, complexes 709a and 709b display increased emission intensities and
lifetimes due to their interactions with the extracellular matrix
surrounding the bacteria, resulting in the selective staining of the
biofilms over their planktonic counterparts. However, in E.
coli biofilms, only complex 709d stains the
agglomerated bacteria in the biofilms, while complexes 709a–709c preferentially interact with individual
cells dispersed throughout the biofilms. The differential staining
behavior suggests that the substituents on both the tridentate and
monodentate ligands play a significant role in dictating the interactions
of the complexes with bacterial biofilms.
 

12.

## Luminescent Transition Metal Complexes as Probes
for Cell Viability
Cell death is a complex process that can
occur through different
mechanisms such as apoptosis, necrosis, and autophagy, depending on
the specific conditions. Evaluating cell viability is an important
aspect of biological, pathological, and medical research. While traditional
methods rely on the use of colorimetric reagents, fluorescent probes
can offer more precise and comprehensive information about the health
status of cells. These probes can be
coupled with fluorescence microscopy for real-time monitoring of cell
viability at the single-cell level, or with flow cytometry for quantitative
measurement of cell viability and identification of sub-populations
of cells with different health statuses. These techniques have enabled
the facile differentiation between live, apoptotic/necrotic, and dead
cells, providing valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms
of cell death and aiding in the development of new therapeutic strategies
for various diseases.
Orellana and co-workers have developed
a luminescent ruthenium(II)
complex (710) ( ) that can distinguish between live and dead cells. This complex cannot pass through the intact
membrane of live cells, but it can penetrate the compromised membrane
of dead cells and accumulate in the nucleus. The differential cellular
uptake behavior has allowed the complex to serve as a specific indicator
of dead cells within a cell population. The staining performance of
the complex is comparable to the commercial dead cell stain PI when
applied in cell viability measurement using flow cytometry. Additionally,
the emission lifetime of the complex is sensitive to the health status
of cells, which increases from 0.45 μs in viable cells to 0.82
μs in dead cells. This interesting feature can be exploited
to develop novel time-resolved assays for cell viability measurement
with minimal autofluorescence interference. Selective staining of
the nucleus of dead cells has also been observed for [Ru(phen)2(dppz)]2+, which has enabled its use as a nuclear
stain in the comet assay to measure DNA damage in cells. A related ruthenium(II) complex (711) is also impermeable to live cells but can stain both the cytoplasm
and nucleus of dead cells, allowing the facile differentiation between
live and dead cell populations. Vaidyanathan,
Nair, and co-workers have developed a membrane-impermeable platinum(II)
complex (712) that selectively stains the nucleus of
cells with compromised membranes. This
complex has been utilized to image cells undergoing apoptosis induced
by plumbagin, visualizing various apoptotic features such as membrane
blebbing and nuclear fragmentation.
 Li and co-workers have designed an iridium(III)
solvato complex
(713) ( ) as a luminescent indicator of cell death. This complex displays selective labeling of the nucleus
of dead cells as a result of its increased cellular and nuclear uptake
when the membranes are permeabilized, allowing the differentiation
between live, early apoptotic, and dead cells using LSCM and flow
cytometry based on their distinct plasma and nuclear membrane permeability
( ). Notably,
the complex outperforms conventional commercial apoptosis kits, which
typically require the combined use of two different fluorescent stains
such as Annexin V–FITC and PI to differentiate apoptotic cells
from dead cells.
 Chao and co-workers have developed a dual-emissive
platinum(II)
complex (714) ( ) for two-photon bioimaging. This complex exhibits both fluorescence (λem = 445 nm) and phosphorescence (λem = 573 nm) in aqueous solution, originating from the
monomeric species and the supramolecular self-assembled nanostructures,
respectively. The loss of plasma membrane integrity during cell death
promotes the cellular uptake and accumulation of the complex, inducing
its intracellular self-assembly and increasing the ratio I573nm/I445nm. This ratiometric
response can reflect the degree of membrane damage, enabling the differentiation
between live, apoptotic, and necrotic cells and the monitoring of
the inflammation process in zebrafish. Li, Yam, and co-workers have
designed a platinum(II) complex (715) that allows for
the monitoring of cell viability based on its distinct subcellular
localization in live and dead cells. At increasing concentrations, the complex undergoes supramolecular
self-assembly into well-defined aggregated nanostructures via Pt···Pt
and π–π interactions, resulting in a change in
the emission color from green (λem = 490 and 520 nm) to red (λem =
550–800 nm). In healthy cells, the complex is internalized
and enriched in the lysosomes as monomeric species with green emission.
However, when the nuclear membrane permeability is lost during cell
death, the complex readily migrates to the nucleus and self-assembles
into red-emissive aggregates. This unique lysosome-to-nucleus translocation,
along with the different emission response, has enabled the real-time
tracking of acid-induced necrosis in HeLa cells ( ).
 
13.

## Conclusions and Outlook
In this Review,
we provide a comprehensive overview of the recent
development of luminescent transition metal complexes with a d6, d8, and d10 electronic configuration
for applications in bioimaging and biosensing. The metal–ligand
coordination allows these complexes to establish new excited states
such as MLCT, LLCT, and MMLCT states that are not accessible to organic
fluorophores. The presence of a heavy metal center in these complexes
enhances ISC, leading to the efficient population of the triplet manifolds
that contribute to their interesting photophysical properties. Most
of these complexes display phosphorescence with long emission lifetimes,
rendering them compatible with time-resolved luminescence imaging
microscopy. The facile differentiation between long-lived and short-lived
emission signals not only enables the effective elimination of interference
from background autofluorescence but also allows their simultaneous
analysis for multiplex imaging and sensing. Furthermore, the CT excited
states of these complexes enhance their NLO properties, making them
suitable for multiphoton microscopy that enables high-resolution imaging
at increased tissue depths. Luminescent transition metal complexes
have also been used for super-resolution microscopy, allowing the
visualization of cellular structures with nanometer resolution. Compared
to the organic fluorophores that are commonly used in super-resolution
imaging such as rhodamine and its derivatives, transition metal complexes
exhibit superior photostability, making them advantageous for super-resolution
microscopy such as SIM and STED microscopy. Additionally, their larger
Stokes’ shifts allow for multicolor super-resolution imaging
when combined with other luminescent probes. However, most of these
complexes are not suitable for single-molecule localization microscopy
such as STORM that utilizes the stochastic blinking of luminescent
molecules to improve spatial resolution. Thus, the development of
transition metal complexes with photoswitchable luminescence properties
(i.e., reversible switching between a dark state
and an emissive state) is anticipated to strengthen their applications
in super-resolution imaging. The presence of a transition metal center
in these complexes also confers unique coordination chemistry and
molecular geometry, offering new sensing mechanisms that involve changes
in the metal coordination environment and metal–metal and ligand–ligand
interactions, which are not typically observed in organic fluorophores.
The strategic manipulation of the intriguing properties of transition
metal complexes has led to their successful applications in studying
biological processes in various cell cultures and small animal models,
enhancing our understanding of cell physiology and pathology. These
advancements have also contributed to the identification of important
biomarkers and disease hallmarks, aiding in the diagnosis of diseases
such as bacterial infections, inflammation, kidney and liver diseases,
neurodegenerative diseases, and cancer. Furthermore, the long-lived
triplet excited states of the complexes allow them to show efficient
ROS photosensitization. The controlled activation of their ROS generation
behavior in response to specific biomarkers has enabled them to function
as theranostic agents to “see” and to “treat”
the diseases in a more precise and personalized manner.
However,
to translate these exciting results into clinical settings,
several major challenges need to be addressed. One of the primary
challenges is sensitivity, as most of the complexes developed to date
display absorption and emission profiles in the visible region, which
are susceptible to interference from cell autofluorescence and light
scattering in biological tissues. Thus, it would be highly advantageous
to focus on the development of new complexes with absorption and emission
profiles centered in the far-red to NIR region or compatible with
multiphoton excitation techniques. Long-wavelength light offers benefits
such as reduced autofluorescence background and increased penetration
depth, enabling deep-tissue imaging in vivo. However,
the design of luminescent transition metal complexes exhibiting red
to NIR emission with high quantum efficiencies is a longstanding challenge
due to the higher nonradiative decay rates governed by the energy
gap law. A judicious choice of metal
centers and ligands with different electronic properties can lead
to the development of more effective NIR luminescent probes. For example,
the use of an electron-rich nitrogen-containing ancillary ligand for
cyclometalated iridium(III) complexes has demonstrated an effective
means to destabilize the dπ orbital of the iridium(III) center
and reduce the energies of the MLCT excited states, resulting in red
to NIR luminescent iridium(III) complexes but still with very high
emission quantum yields. The exploration
of transition metal complexes as chemiluminescent and afterglow luminescent
probes also holds promise for background-free imaging in vivo.
Another challenge is specificity and selectivity of the complexes
toward their targets within complex biological environments. One approach
to address this issue is to incorporate targeting moieties into the
complexes for recognizing specific molecular signatures present on
target analytes, organelles, and cell surfaces to minimize their interactions
with nontargeted components. For example, transition metal complexes
can be conjugated to biomolecules such as peptides and antibodies
(active targeting) or formulated into nanomaterials (passive targeting)
to selectively target cancer cells. The use of bioorthogonal chemistry
as a pretargeting strategy has become a valuable approach in achieving
target specificity and selectivity within living systems, as it can
not only facilitate the accumulation of transition metal complexes
at the desired target sites but also enable controlled activation
of their phosphorescence and ROS-photosensitization properties. Furthermore,
masking of transition metal complexes with responsive moieties using
a dual-locked approach can lead to the development of probes that
can only be activated in the copresence of two target analytes, precluding
nonspecific activation and improving detection accuracy and reliability.
While these new design strategies hold promise for the development
of more effective reagents, structural optimization is often required
to fine-tune their properties for clinical applications, which can
be a tedious and laborious process. Studies on the structure–property
relationships in luminescent transition metal complexes have revealed
that both the metal center and ligands play crucial roles not only
in the photophysical and photochemical properties of the complexes
but also in their lipophilicity, formal charge, and molecular size,
which strongly influence their biological behavior such as cellular
uptake, localization, biocompatibility, pharmacokinetics, and biodistribution.
Recent advancements in artificial intelligence, machine learning,
and deep learning offer cutting-edge computational tools that are
expected to enhance our understanding of the structure–property
relationships of these complexes and provide new insights to guide
the design of novel complexes and facilitate high-throughput screening
of lead compounds, thereby accelerating the discovery of complexes
with desirable properties for clinical applications.
In conclusion,
luminescent transition metal complexes hold great
promise for bioimaging and biosensing applications. Their intriguing
photophysical and photochemical properties offer exciting prospects
for the development of innovative theranostics. Further research and
advancements in their design will overcome current challenges and
pave the way for their successful translation from the laboratory
to clinical settings.

## Biographies
Lawrence Cho-Cheung Lee obtained his BSc and PhD
degrees at City
University of Hong Kong in 2014 and 2020, respectively. He is currently
working as a Postdoctoral Fellow under the supervision of Prof. Kenneth
Lo. His research interests focus on the design of photofunctional
transition metal complexes as biological reagents for bioconjugation,
bioimaging, and (photo)therapeutic applications.

Kenneth Kam-Wing Lo obtained his BSc and PhD degrees
at The University
of Hong Kong in 1993 and 1997, respectively. He then worked as a Croucher
Foundation Postdoctoral Research Fellow in the Inorganic Chemistry
Laboratory of the University of Oxford. In 1999, he joined the Department
of Biology and Chemistry (currently Department of Chemistry) of City
University of Hong Kong as an Assistant Professor and has been a Chair
Professor since 2023. His research interest is the utilization of
luminescent inorganic and organometallic transition metal complexes
as biomolecular probes, cellular imaging reagents, and photocytotoxic
agents.

The authors
declare no competing financial interest.
